Sum-of-Squares & Gaussian Processes I: Certification Thanks: This project has received funding from the European Research Council (ERC) award No. 834861 (“SO-ReCoDi”).
Juspreet Singh Sandhu, Jonathan Shi

Abstract
We introduce a class of distributions which may be considered as a smoothed probabilistic version of the ultrametric property that famously characterizes the Gibbs distributions of various spin glass models [ Pan13a ] . This class of high-entropy step (HES) distributions is expressive enough to capture a distribution achieving near-optimal average energy on spin glass models in the so-called full Replica-Symmetry Breaking (fRSB) regime. Simultaneously, with high probability, there are polynomial-size certificates on the average energy achievable by any HES distribution which are tight within a constant factor. These certificates can be found in polynomial time by a semidefinite program corresponding to a sum-of-squares (SoS) hierarchy we introduce, termed the HES SoS hierarchy. This improves over classical sum-of-squares certificates which are loose by a factor of n ⌊ p / 2 − 1 ⌋ / 2 n^{\lfloor p/2-1\rfloor/2} [ BGL17 , HKP+17 ] .



1
Introduction

In the Sum-of-Squares (SoS) meta-algorithm, the existence of a certain type of proof (known as a low-degree SoS proof) characterizing the solutions of a problem instance automatically gives rise to a corresponding polynomial-time algorithm for that problem
[
Par04
,
BS14
]
. In various average-case settings, however, no low-degree proof exists due to known lower bounds against the standard SoS hierarchy
[
BGL17
,
HKP+17
,
JPR+22
]
. Therefore, the central question that motivates this paper is:

What can SoS-type proof systems certify for large ensembles of average-case optimization problems?

To understand this question, we propose a new SoS hierarchy to certify the value of a polynomial over points on the sphere that can be output by a parameterized family of distributions (
Theorem

1.1
).
These distributions are termed high-entropy (HES) step processes (
Definition

1.2
).
Thus, the HES SoS hierarchy certifies the maximum value that can be achieved by
any
algorithm whose output distribution on a fixed instance can be characterized by some HES process.
To demonstrate the power of this hierarchy, we prove that for the spherical spin glass problem (
Definition

2.1
) the HES SoS hierarchy provides polynomial-sized certificates with value that is only a constant factor off the true optimum (
Theorem

1.4
).
This improves over prior SoS certificates, which are off of the true optimum by superlinear factors at any constant level of the hierarchy
[
BGL17
,
HKP+17
]
.
A celebrated result of Subag
[
Sub21
]
provides a Hessian ascent algorithm for the spherical spin glass problem with behavior that is conjecturally optimal among all polynomial time algorithms
[
HS22
]
. Indeed, we demonstrate that a randomized version of Subag’s algorithm (
Algorithm

2
) is a HES process (
Lemma

7.1
) and then certify that the value achieved by
any
HES process is the same up to constant factors (
Theorem

1.4
).
Continuing further, we show that the HES SoS hierarchy diverges from standard Hessian ascent on modified ensembles of the spherical spin glass problem (
Section

8
) and performs better than standard Hessian ascent, though it remains open to prove its performance is optimal.
While a result of Huang and Sellke
[
HS23
,
HS24
]
provides a conjecturally-optimal approximate-message passing (AMP) algorithm for the models from which these modified ensembles are chosen, the striking fact is that the HES SoS hierarchy works
without
being reprogrammed or changed.
This is in stark constrast to the AMP algorithm
[
HS23
,
HS24
]
which is not in any obvious way relatable to the Hessian ascent algorithm
[
Sub21
]
.
This leads to a conjecture that the HES SoS hierarchy is optimal among
all
polynomial-time algorithms for optimizing a large family of Gaussian processes (
1.6
); these processes trivially subsume the spherical spin glass problem.

The HES SoS hierarchy is based on three key ideas.

•

The first key insight is to permit
distributions over
solutions to be objects of optimization in their own right
[
GLR18
,
MR20
,
JKO98
]
.
The upshot of this is that SoS proofs may refer to the moments of a hypothetical distribution over solutions, and prove polynomial relationships between these moments, as well as constraints on those moments.

•

The second key insight is giving the hierarchy access to the
interior
of the solution domain to parameterize such distributions via their low-degree moments.
This idea is inspired by a line of work initiated by Subag
[
Sub18
,
CPS18
]
that considers an extension of the original polynomial into the interior of the solution domain, and tracks how a smoothened version of the maximum changes as you move from the center outwards to the domain.

•

The third key insight is to impose constraints on the moments of the distributions that maximize some notion of entropy over the near-optimizers.
This is inspired by the well-known fact that the Gibbs distribution is the maximum entropy distribution with a given energy.

In keeping with the insights mentioned above, the HES SoS hierarchy solves the following algorithmic task:

Certify the value of a problem instance with low-degree SoS proofs over distributions of points that are generated by a “high-entropy” process.

Critically, note that this task is
not
a relaxation of the original problem but a different problem that asks for the value of the instance achieved by some family of distributions having large entropy.
Informally, HES processes are defined as a sum of sequential vector-valued steps, with each step conditionally distributed as some Gaussian with large effective dimension whose covariance varies smoothly under the values of the previous steps.
This family of distributions is sufficiently well-behaved to enable powerful SoS proofs on its properties, and yet it is also expressive enough to capture near-extrema of (for instance) spherial spin glasses on the sphere (
Theorem

1.4
).

In order to get a better understanding of the capabilities of this hierarchy, we briefly contrast it with three (broad) algorithmic approaches that have been used previously in the literature to study average-case optimization problems.

HES SoS hierarchy vs. Hessian ascent.

The Hessian ascent algorithm proposed by Subag
[
Sub21
]
follows the largest eigenvector of the Hessian of the instance from the origin outwards, eventually reaching a point on the sphere.
On spherical spin glasses, the HES SoS hierarchy
automatically
finds a near-optimal HES distribution which is also output by a randomized variant of the prior Hessian ascent algorithm
[
Sub18
,
Sub21
]
. In doing so, it captures the Hessian ascent algorithm
without simulating
it.
This allows the HES SoS hierarchy to perform better on modified input ensembles that are anisotropic (see
Section

8
and
1.6
).

HES SoS hierarchy vs. Local-Statistics hierarchy.

The HES SoS hierarchy is stronger than the previously proposed local-statistics hierarchy (LStH)
[
BMR21
,
IS23
]
in the sense that the structure of the LStH precludes any scope of meaningful certification on an
instance
from the relevant average-case ensemble.
In contrast, the SoS HES hierarchy is a certification algorithm that
directly
searches and optimizes over certain distributions over solutions.

HES SoS hierarchy vs. Low-degree polynomial method.

Previous algorithms that have successfully been applied to spin-glasses and random Max-CSPs are either some form of local algorithms
[
AMS23
,
CHM23
]
or low-degree polynomial (LDP) algorithms
[
GJW20
,
HS22
]
.
For certification problems, there are natural ways to set up the standard SoS hierarchy
[
Par04
]
. However, as is evidenced by the certification lower bounds against the standard SoS hierarchy
[
BGL17
,
HKP+17
,
JPR+22
]
the relevant question becomes what can be achieved when certification is harder than optimization
[
Sub21
,
Mon19
]
for such problems. In such a situation, the HES distributions provide a natural way to set up a SoS hierarchy that may be universal.

We conjecture (
1.6
) that the SoS HES hierarchy subsumes these algorithms on a wide class of average-case optimization problems. The HES distributions considered by the hierarchy are not necessarily LDPs of the instance (although some parameters of the distributions are constrained to be near-LDPs of
each other
): thus, the LDP method is not known to be equivalent to the SoS HES hierarchy.

In this paper, for technical simplicity, we study optimization problems over the sphere.
Provided a similar HES SoS hierarchy can be instantiated on the cube, it would very likely have similar findings for random optimization problems over the hypercube (see
1.8
).

A natural question, then, is the following:

What is the advantage of the HES SoS hierarchy over prior approaches?

The answer to this question is threefold, as detailed below.

Lower bounds computable in
\mathsf{poly}(n)
-time.

The HES distributions correspond to entire families of stochastic processes and, naively, it is not even possible to enumerate them in finite time. Even then,
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.4
demonstrate that the HES SoS hierarchy can find efficient certificates in
\mathsf{poly}(n)
time that give limitations on
any
algorithm whose output distribution can be parameterized by some HES process. This is a non-standard and somewhat surprising use of semidefinite programs, and can be thought of as an “algorithmic lower bound” against HES processes on certain families of inputs.

Computing Parisi-like constants in low-degree SoS.

Prior certification lower bounds for the standard SoS hierarchy
[
BGL17
,
HKP+17
,
JPR+22
]
demonstrate it cannot even certify the correct
scaling
of the maxima of random low-degree polynomials until the underlying proofs have near-linear degree. This reflects the reality that the existing proofs of the Parisi-like formula
[
CS92
]
that captures the maximum value are extremely technical and deep, making heavy use of concentration of measure and the analytic toolkit used to analyze Gaussian processes
[
Tal06
,
Sub18
]
. However, we demonstrate that there exist low-degree SoS proofs over HES distributions that obtain the correct
scaling
with a value that approximates the maximum up to an explicit constant factor (
Theorem

1.4
).

Ensemble agnosticism.

We demonstrate that the HES hierarchy handles some anisotropic variants of the spherical spin glass ensemble (
Section

8
) where direct Hessian ascent takes suboptimal steps.
While it is an open problem to provide low-degree HES SoS certificates that match the conjectural optimal value
[
HS23
,
HS24
]
on such anisotropic ensembles (
1.6
), the striking feature is that the HES hierarchy does not require any such tweaking and works as-is without being told which ensemble the instance was drawn from. Au contraire, the standard Hessian ascent algorithm becomes provably suboptimal, as is shown in
Section

8
, and a completely new AMP algorithm needs to be designed for this different anisotropic ensemble
[
HS23
,
HS24
]
.

1.6
and
1.8
also assert that there exist rounding schemes for the HES SoS hierarchy which achieves optimal value among all polynomial-time algorithms on these ensembles of problems. The rounding aspect of the conjecture is discussed further in the companion paper
[
SS24
]
, which introduces the rounding scheme for the spherical spin glass problem.

To complete the picture, it would be interesting to know what natural families of algorithms are captured by HES processes:
Lemma

7.1
shows that this includes standard Hessian ascent, and a recent result
[
JSS24
]
shows that a Hessian ascent algorithm modified with a potential function achieves equivalent performance with AMP
[
Mon19
]
, suggesting that the performance of AMP may also be generically captured by HES processes in certain settings. It may be possible to demonstrate a connection between sufficiently “smooth” algorithms—such as overlap-concentrated algorithms
[
HS22
]
—and HES processes on various families of problems.

This work makes progress towards a program proposed by Barak
[
Bar14
, Chapter 9, p. 105]
to understand the optimality of SoS on average-case optimization problems.

The remaining parts of the paper are structured as follows:

1.

In
Section

1.1
we state our technical contributions, main results, the “ensemble-agnosticism” conjecture and an immediate open question, followed by an interpretation of the implications of these for SoS relaxations in
Section

1.2
and a summary of the related literature in
Section

1.3
.

2.

In
Section

2.1
we introduce the spherical spin-glass hamiltonian. In
Section

2.2
and
Section

2.3
we motivate, state and explain the HES SoS hierarchy and its proof system.
We conclude in
Section

2.5
by giving an overview of the proof of
Theorem

1.4
and the construction of the modified ensembles in
Section

8
where the HES SoS hierarchy does better than the direct Hessian ascent algorithm.

3.

In
Section

3
we introduce various technical preliminaries.
These critically include a description of the Hessian ascent algorithm (
Section

3.2.4
), the algorithmic threshold for spherical spin glasses (
Section

3.2.5
), graph matrices (
Section

3.7.1
), some important facts from Hermite analysis (
Section

3.5
), and customized matrix representations of polynomials (
Section

3.4.2
) that heavily feature in the analysis.

4.

In
Section

4
we formally introduce the grammar that generates the conditional moments of a filtrated space as the atoms of the SDP corresponding to the HES SoS hierarchy (
Section

4.2.2
).
We explicitly explain how to encode conditional constraints into atoms of the SDP (
Section

4.2.4
), briefly mentioning the connection of the grammar with replicas (
Section

4.2.9
) and how its encoding includes the low-degree Wiener-Hermite decomposition for functions at a particular level of the filtration (
Section

4.2.7
).

5.

In
Section

5
we use harmonic analysis over multi-variate Hermite polynomials to develop low-degree SoS proofs to control the nuclear norms of the joint moments of HES distributions (
Theorem

5.8
,
Theorem

5.9
).

6.

In
Section

6
we demonstrate, using low-degree HES SoS proofs, that the spectral moments of the Hessian of the spherical spin glass ensemble converge to those of a scaling of the
\mathsf{GOE}(n)
ensemble by a function that depends purely on the distance from the origin (
Lemma

6.1
).
We also demonstrate that the energy of the spin-glass hamiltonian is certifiably upper bounded by the value that comes directly from the contribution of the Hessian (
Lemma

6.4
).

7.

In
Section

7
we show that the HES SoS hierarchy is feasible for the spherical spin-glass model by proving that its constraints are satisfied by a
randomized
version of the Hessian ascent algorithm that has (provably) equivalent performance (
Lemma

7.1
).

8.

In
Section

8
we show that the HES SoS hierarchy does better than the Hessian ascent algorithm on certain ensembles that come from anisotropic modifications of spherical spin glasses.

9.

In
Section

9
we conclude with discussions about the possibility of obtaining meaningful lower-bounds against larger families of SDPs (
Section

9.1.1
), the possibility (or lack thereof) of certifying relaxed versions of the Ghirlanda-Guerra identities (
Section

9.1.3
), the ability to create a SoS HES hierarchy on the cube (
Section

9.1.2
), and other future directions (
Section

9.2
).

Various auxiliary technical definitions and proofs used throughout the paper are delegated to the appendices.

1.1
Contributions

We formalize a system of proofs that extends the SoS system to have the capability to reason about the moments of distributions over steps
\sigma_{i}:=v_{1}+\dots+v_{i}
.
Each step
v_{j}
is sampled one at a time, so that, for example, the moments of
v_{i}
can be functions of
v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}
. The objects of this proof system are recursive polynomials of moments, for example,
(\E_{v_{1}}v_{1}(\E_{v_{2}}\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}\right\rangle)^{2})^{3}
. An inequality
p\geqslant 0
can be proved by writing
p=\sumop\displaylimits_{i}q_{i}^{2}
as a sum of squares of polynomial expressions in the proof system (
Section

4.2
).

This set of proofs is convex
[
BS14
]
and, therefore, it is the dual proof system to some primal space.
We analyze the SoS meta-algorithm for this proof system, formulated by optimizing some objective function over the primal space and then rounding the result.
Given below is a statement of the key properties that must permit low-degree SoS proofs under HES distributions to allow for certificates with a precise value.

Essentially, SoS can globally search over all low-degree polynomials
\tilde{H}
on the ball
\{\sigma:\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant 1\}
that certifiably upper-bound the original objective
H
on the sphere
\{\sigma:\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=1\}
to find one so that an (appropriately designed) local iterative algorithm run on
\tilde{H}
provably optimizes
H
.
We remark that this is only one way to analyze the certification, and that in general there need not be any pre-existing local algorithm.

Theorem 1.1

(Certifiable properties of HES distributions)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be some set of polynomial axioms that can be satisfied by the moments of a sequence of step variables
\sigma_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\left(v_{1}+\dots+v_{i}\right)
.

Let
H
be a function on the sphere
\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, for which there exists an extension
\tilde{H}
defined on the
n
-dimensional ball such that
\nabla\tilde{H}(0)=0
and so that there is a SoS proof that
H(\sigma)\leqslant\tilde{H}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1)
.
Suppose that
\tilde{H}
additionally satisfies the following properties:

Certifiably bounded higher-order derivatives

There is an integer
k
and a SoS proof that for every sequence of steps
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
satisfying
\mathcal{A}
and every
i\in[k]
and every
r\in\{3,4,\dots,d_{H}\}
and every integer
p\leqslant d_{H}/\varepsilon
,

\E_{v_{1},\dots,v_{i}}\;\left(\frac{1}{r!\,k^{r/2}}\left\langle\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{r}\tilde{H}(\sigma_{i-1}),v_{i}^{\otimes r}\right\rangle\right)^{p}\leqslant\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2k}\right)^{p}\,,

Certifiable stepwise boundedness

There is a SoS proof that for every sequence of steps
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
satisfying
\mathcal{A}
and every
i\in[k]
,

\frac{1}{n}\E_{v_{1},\dots,v_{i}}\tilde{H}(\sigma_{i})\leqslant(1-\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^{2}))\frac{1}{k}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{i}\mathcal{E}_{i}\,,

where
\mathcal{E}_{i}
only depends on
i
.

Agreement with
H
near optima

There is a SoS proof that if

\frac{1}{n}\E_{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}}\tilde{H}(\sigma)\geqslant(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})\frac{1}{k}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{j}

then

\frac{1}{n}\E_{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}}\tilde{H}(\sigma)\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\E_{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}}H(\sigma)+\varepsilon\varepsilon^{\prime}\,.

Then the HES SoS hierarchy outputs a certificate of value
(1-O(\varepsilon))e^{O(k^{2})}(\mathcal{E}_{1}+\dots+\mathcal{E}_{k})n/k
and size
\mathsf{poly}\left(n,\exp\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)
.

In this paper we prove that the spherical spin glass satisfies these properties for some explicit choice of
\mathcal{E}_{i}
with a degree-
O\left(\exp\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)
proof over the HES SoS hierarchy. In the companion paper
[
SS24
]
, we provide a rounding algorithm that (with high probability) outputs configurations with performance equivalent to Subag’s algorithm
[
Sub21
]
.

SoS proofs for HES distributions

The first of the conditions in
Theorem

1.1
is
a priori
the most difficult to verify, as bounding the higher-order derivatives requires reasoning about injective tensor norms, and it is impossible to give a constant-degree pointwise SoS proof doing so due to known lower bounds
[
BGL17
,
HKP+17
]
.
However, a fundamental lemma about the moments of HES distributions (
Lemma

1.3
) allows us to circumvent this problem by reasoning about the spectral norm of a matrix reshaping of the tensor instead, which in particular is satisfied by polynomials with independent random coefficients.
We first give a precise definition of high-entropy steps:

Definition 1.2

(High-Entropy Step (HES) Distributions)
.

The random vectors
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
are a sequence of
high-entropy steps
(HES) and
\sigma:=v_{1}+\dots+v_{k}
is
HES-distributed
if each
v_{i}
is Gaussian conditioned on the values of all
v_{j}
for
j\in[i-1]
and

f_{i}(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}):=\E_{v_{i}}[v_{i}v_{i}^{T}\mid v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}]\,,

(so that
f_{i}
is a matrix-valued function), then

\E_{v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}}\left\lVert f_{i}(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1})-f_{i}^{\leqslant d_{1}}(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1})\right\rVert_{F}^{d_{2}}\leqslant o_{n}(1)\,,

for some
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
, where
{f_{i}}^{\leqslant d_{1}}
is the degree-at-most-
d_{1}
part of
f_{i}
(e.g. if
f_{i}
is decomposed into Hermite polynomials or any other family of weakly orthogonal polynomials
[
Chi11
]
), and

\left\lVert f_{i}(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1})\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\delta n}\,,

for some
\delta>0
.

We now state the bound on the nuclear norm of (polynomials of) joint moments of high-entropy steps.

Lemma 1.3

(Nuclear norm property of HES moments (informal))
.

Let
p
be a homogeneous degree-
d_{p}
scalar-valued polynomial over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and let
\mat(p)
be the matrix whose row indices are degree-
\left\lfloor\frac{d_{p}}{2}\right\rfloor
monomials and whose column indices are degree-
\left\lceil\frac{d_{p}}{2}\right\rceil
monomials and whose entries are

\mat(p)_{x^{\alpha},x^{\beta}}=\frac{1}{C_{\alpha+\beta}}p_{\alpha+\beta}\,,

where
p_{\alpha+\beta}
is the coefficient of
p(x)
in front of
x^{\alpha+\beta}
and
C_{\alpha+\beta}
is the number of entries of
\mat(p)
that represent the same coefficient.
Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{i}
be a sequence of high-entropy steps with the (conditional) covariance of each step bounded by
\nu
in spectral norm.

Then, there is a sum-of-squares proof that

\E[p(v_{1}+\dots+v_{i})]^{\ell}\leqslant e^{O(i^{2})}(\nu^{d_{p}/2}n^{\lfloor d_{p}/2+1\rfloor/2}\left\lVert\mat(p)\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}})^{\ell}\,,

where the
O(1)
is hiding dependencies on
i
,
d_{p}
,
\ell
, and the smoothness parameter of the high-entropy steps.

This informal lemma is a special case of a more general lemma (
Theorem

5.8
) where
p
can be a multi-argument polynomial whose arguments are each high-entropy step vectors.

While the spectral norm of
\mat(p)
may appear at first to be an opaque quantity, we can interpret it as the maximum over all low-degree polynomials
\phi:\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1)\to\mathbb{R}
of the quantity

\frac{1}{n^{d_{p}/2}}\cdot\frac{|\E_{\sigma\sim S^{n-1}(1)}p(\sigma)\phi(\sigma)^{2}|}{\E_{\sigma\sim S^{n-1}(1)}\phi(\sigma)^{2}}\,.

In other words,
\left\lVert\mat(p)\right\rVert{}_{\mathrm{op}}
is small if the average value of
p
is small on every low-degree polynomial reweighing of the uniform distribution over the sphere.

Note that when
p:\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1)\to\mathbb{R}
is a polynomial where each possible coefficient is random, zero-mean, variance
O(n)
, and bounded in its higher moments, the value of
\left\lVert\mat(p)\right\rVert{}_{\mathrm{op}}
will be on the order of
n^{\lceil d_{p}/2+1\rceil/2}

[
Ver18
, Theorem 4.4.5]
and we can choose
\nu^{d_{p}/2}
to be on the order of
n^{-d_{p}/2}
, so that the order-
n
contributions in
Lemma

1.3
precisely cancel each other out.
The resulting bound on
\E p(\sigma)
is
O(n)
, which is of the same order as the true value of
\max_{\sigma}p(\sigma)
with high probability
[
ASZ20
, Lemma 58, Eq. B.6]
.

Lemma

1.3
complements the graph matrix norm bound framework
[
AMP21
]
; graph matrix norm bounds provide upper bounds on the
spectral norms
of matrices that can be used to represent
polynomials
, and
Lemma

1.3
provides upper bounds on the
nuclear norms
of matrices that represent
moments
.
Consequently, the bounds provided by
Lemma

1.3
are on the dual norm (
1
-norm) in the dual space to those provided by graph matrix norm bounds (
\infty
-norm), allowing us to apply each technique to its corresponding term in a
(1,\infty)
-Hölder’s inequality.

This serves as a template for future work bounding the spectral statistics of other random matrices and tensors via low-degree SoS proofs, with the critical idea being to parameterize the set of step-distributions in a way that the nuclear norm of the joint moments can be made to “fight” the typical spectral norm of the appropriate reshaping of the tensor, thereby allowing for a smooth transfer of bounds from random matrix theory and free probability to be SoS-compliant.

Spherical spin glass model

Finally, we show that the spherical spin glass model (defined in
Definition

2.1
) satisfies these sufficient conditions, and therefore the following algorithm exists:

Theorem 1.4
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
and
n
, there exists a set of SDP constraints
R_{\varepsilon}
(
Equation

P.0
) of polynomial size, such that with high probability with respect to a spin glass Hamiltonian
H
over
n
variables, there exist
O\left(\mathsf{poly}\left(n,\exp\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)\right)
-sized SoS certificates over this SDP as well as actual HES distributions which certify

e^{O(1/\varepsilon^{2})}\,\mathsf{ALG}\geqslant\E_{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}}\left[H\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}v_{j}\right)\right]\geqslant(1-\varepsilon-O(\varepsilon^{2}))\,\mathsf{ALG}\,,

where
\mathsf{ALG}
is a conjectured upper limit on the quality of solution achievable by polynomial-time algorithms, elaborated on in
Section

3.2.5
.

Furthermore, in the fRSB regime of the spherical spin glass model (
Proposition

3.6
), we have

e^{O(1/\varepsilon^{2})}\sup_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}}H(\sigma)\geqslant\E_{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}}\left[H\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}v_{j}\right)\right]\geqslant(1-\varepsilon-O(\varepsilon^{2}))\sup_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}}H(\sigma)\,.

For the spherical spin glass problem, the Crisanti-Sommers formula
[
CS92
]
is known to give an explicit expression for the typical optimal value
[
Tal06
,
Sub18
]
. The
\mathcal{E}_{i}
value achieved for spherical spin glass Hamiltonians in
Theorem

1.1
(formally shown in
Lemma

6.1
) and the bounds on the higher-order derivatives provided by
Lemma

1.3
(formally shown in
Theorem

5.8
) combine to show that the HES SoS program gives
low-degree certificates
on the expected value of the Hamiltonian under any HES distribution. The quality of the bound of this certificate is equivalent (up to constant factors) to that of a “relaxation” of the Crisanti-Sommers formula (
Proposition

3.8
), a result of possibly independent interest. As mentioned earlier, the rigorous proofs of the Crisanti-Sommers formula by Talagrand
[
Tal06
]
and Subag
[
Sub18
]
make heavy use of concentration of measure and the analytical part of the toolkit used to analyze Gaussian processes (such as smooth interpolations and chaining) to characterize the optimum value, neither of which are accessible to low-degree SoS proofs. This provides further evidence for the low-degree hardness of certifying the expected
true
optimal value. The “relaxed” value is the conjectured maximum attainable by any polynomial time algorithm
[
HS22
,
JMSS23
]
.

The constant factors from
\mathsf{ALG}
which the HES SoS certificates are off by
may
be removed by a more judicious analysis of
Lemma

5.26
. One way to do this is to attempt a proof which uses a particular low-degree representation for the conditional covariance
\fE_{i}\left[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mid v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}\right]
which satisfies the HES constraints, but is also near-optimal. It is not hard to verify that this can be achieved by a matrix Bernstein polynomial that approximates the projector into the top-eigenspace of the Hessian (
Corollary

6.7
), or a choice of a sufficiently large power of a “shifted” Hessian that tightens the Hölder inequality applied to bound the contribution of the Hessian in the analysis (
Corollary

6.3
). The remaining technical challenge then becomes to understand the
derivatives
of the near-optimal choice of conditional covariance, which seems to require deeper techniques from the study of asymptotically free tensors
[
BB24
]
.

Ensemble-agnosticism

The HES hierarchy makes no assumptions specific to the spherical spin glass model:
its structure is more closely related to the smoothness and correlational structure of low-degree Gaussian processes.
While the HES hierarchy does not inherently encode any local algorithm, we analyze its behavior via one, and in that view, it is able to search over all possible low-degree extensions of the original input problem to find one where a local algorithm works.

In
Section

8
, we provide evidence for this viewpoint by considering anisotropic modifications of the spherical spin glass problem.

Lemma 1.5

(HES hierarchy on ansiotropic spherical spin-glasses)
.

There exist ansisotropic modifications to the spherical spin-glass problem, such as those specified in
Example

8.1
,
Example

8.2
and
Example

8.4
, for which there exist HES distributions achieving better value than direct Hessian ascent.

The idea behind these anisotropic modifications is to “confuse” the Hessian ascent algorithm by putting differing amounts of weight in different subspaces for some extension of the original function
\tilde{H}
inside the ball.
We conjecture that this gives this SoS relaxation a robustness against model misspecification that cannot be attained by any purely local algorithm.

Conjecture 1.6

(Ensemble-agnosticism)
.

Let
H_{n}:\mathcal{S}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
be a random sequence of polynomials of degree at most some constant
d
. Suppose that
\{H_{n}\}
is an asymptotically Gaussian process on
\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
, in the sense that for every large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
, for every
r\in\mathbb{N}
, and every fixed sequence
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{r}\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
, the
\ell
th cumulant tensor (
Definition

3.24
) where
\ell\geqslant 3
of the joint distribution on
H_{n}(\sigma_{1}),\dots,H_{n}(\sigma_{r})
is bounded by at most
o_{n}(r^{r/2}\left\lVert C\right\rVert_{F}^{r/2})
in 2-norm, where
C\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
is the covariance matrix between
H_{n}(\sigma_{1}),\dots,H_{n}(\sigma_{r})
.

Suppose that for every
n
and every fixed
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
,

\E_{H_{n}}H_{n}(\sigma)=0\qquad\quad\text{and}\quad\qquad\E_{H_{n}}H_{n}(\sigma)^{2}\leqslant O(1)\E_{H_{n}}\,\left\lVert H_{n}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{n-1})}^{2}\,,

where the expectations are over the randomness of
H_{n}
and
\left\lVert H_{n}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{n-1})}^{2}:=\E_{\sigma\sim\mathcal{S}^{n-1}}H_{n}(\sigma)^{2}
.
Suppose also that

\E_{\sigma\sim\mathcal{S}^{n-1}}\sigma H_{n}(\sigma)=0

(meaning that
H_{n}
has no linear part).
Then there is a rounding algorithm for the HES relaxation that, with high probability, achieves value on
H
that is an arbitrarily good multiplicative approximation to optimal among polynomial-time algorithms as
n
grows large.

Two examples of
H_{n}
meeting these criteria include:

•

Subspace-projected spin-glasses
: A linear combination of a constant number of spherical spin glass models (
Section

2.1
) in different (not necessarily mutually orthogonal) subspaces of dimension
\Omega(n)
.

•

Random spherical CSPs
: Given a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\E_{x}f(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=0
and
\E_{x}xf(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=0
when
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Id_{k})
, taking
H_{n}(\sigma)
to be the sum over
j\in[m]
of
f(s_{1,j}\sigma_{i_{1,j}},\dots,s_{k,j}\sigma_{i_{k,j}})
, where
s_{1,j}
, …,
s_{k,j}
are independent random signs,
i_{1,j}
, …,
i_{k,j}
are independent random elements of
[n]
, and
m
is superlinear in
n
.

The law of large numbers can be applied to show that this second example is an asymptotically Gaussian process. One example that is not an asymptotically Gaussian process is the random rank-one tensor where
H_{n}(\sigma)=\left\langle a,\sigma\right\rangle^{3}
for a uniformly random
a\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
.

Proving the conjecture in full generality is a daunting technical challenge.
A potentially more tractable first step is to prove that the Hessians of LDPs that are approximate Gaussian processes have a spectrum whose low degree moments can be specified via a SoS proof under the HES constraints.

Conjecture 1.7

(Hessians of smooth Gaussian processes)
.

Suppose that
H_{n}:\mathcal{S}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
is a random sequence of polynomials that is an asymptotically Gaussian process satisfying the constraints mentioned in
1.6
. Then, for every low-degree polynomial extension
\tilde{H}:\mathcal{B}^{n-1}(1)\to\mathbb{R}
of
H
satisfying
\tilde{H}(\sigma)=H(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}
, there exists some bounded function
f_{\tilde{H}}:\mathcal{B}^{n-1}(1)\to\mathbb{R}
which
does not depend on the input randomness
, such that, for every
\sigma_{i}\in\mathcal{B}^{n-1}(1)
, it is the case that,

\{\text{HES constraints}\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\fE_{1}\left(\frac{1}{n}\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{p}^{p}-f_{\tilde{H}}(\sigma_{i})\right)^{2}\leqslant o_{n}(1)\,,

for every constant
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{even}}
(see
Section

4.2.2
for notational definitions).

SoS HES hierarchy on the hypercube

It is likely possible to instantiate a version of the SoS HES hierarchy on the hypercube. This would entail some extra technical work in proving versions of
Lemma

5.13
and
Lemma

6.1
under the changed HES axioms.

Open Question 1.8

(HES SoS hierarchy for the hypercube)
.

There exists a HES SoS hierarchy over the hypercube which yields low-degree SoS certificates up to the
\mathsf{ALG}
threshold for mixed spin-glasses on the hypercube
[
HS22
]
. Furthermore, this new hierarchy has exactly the same “ensemble-agnosticism“ for random low-degree polynomials
H_{n}:\{\pm 1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
which are an asymptotically Gaussian process, and moments that satisfy the conditions stated in
1.6
.

SoS proofs for analytic inequalities

We give a collection of SoS proofs for certain inequalities from commutative and non-commutative analysis during the course of analyzing the relaxation, which may be of independent interest (
Appendix

C
,
Appendix

D
). These include a proof for a matrix Hölder’s inequality with dyadic norms (
Lemma

D.6
) and a proof of strong convexity of the map
x\to x^{q}
with
q\in(1,2]
.

1.2
Implications for Sum-of-Squares relaxations

We briefly describe the immediate implications of our work for SoS relaxations.

Convex relaxations over distributions

In sampling, a now-popular perspective considers the problem of sampling from a Gibbs distribution as equivalently the problem of optimizing a regularized objective over the set of all distributions
[
GLR18
,
MR20
,
JKO98
]
.
This view gives primacy to the
probability distribution
over algorithm outputs as itself an object of optimization.
We extend this view to the realm of convex relaxations.

Concentration of measure in SoS

A common difficulty in previous SoS work was that concentration of measure and union bound proofs could not be converted into SoS proofs
[
BS16
, Lec. 1.2, Marley Paradigm]
.
For example, bounding each point in an exponentially large epsilon-net with a Chernoff bound and then applying the union bound to add up the probabilities at each point usually requires access to linearly large degree pseudo-distributions.

This feature presented a gap between powerful SoS techniques and relatively simple iterative local-update techniques.
These local update techniques, in contrast, can be
enabled
by concentration arguments, allowing them to find iterative improvements upon a rough first guess with high probability
[
Mon19
,
BMO+15
,
Sub21
,
AMS23
]
.

This is where it helps to lift to a convex relaxation over distributions.
A good choice of constraints on the rounded distributions drastically reduces the degree needed to show these concentration bounds by drastically curtailing the set of primal objects that the bounds need to apply to.

We believe this is the first time that a low-degree SoS proof of the existence of
many
potential solutions (possessing some regularity in their occurrence) can be provided (while still enabling a roudning scheme) rather than a proof that there are a limited number of solutions
[
BKS14
,
BRS11
,
MSS16
,
KKK19
,
RY20
]
or of product distributions of solutions
[
BRS11
,
JKR19
]
.

Robustness in average-case

The SoS proofs-to-algorithms framework has demonstrated remarkable adversarial/outlier robustness in learning settings
[
KSS18
,
HL18
,
DKK+22
,
BDH+20
,
LM21
]
. We provide evidence that the HES SoS hierarchy exhibits a kind of “robustness” to model-misspecification (
Section

8
) that allows it to be applied in an ensemble-agnostic manner that prior algorithms used for such average-case problems cannot be.
A general SoS approach to average-case problems could extend these robustness properties to these settings as well, as has already been done for certain robustness properties for any problem with a low-degree AMP algorithm
[
IS23
]
.

Entropy-energy variational trade-off

The HES distribution features a tunable parameter
\delta
, which can be understood as a form of entropy constraint: setting
\delta
to 1 results in the largest possible entropy, the uniform distribution over the sphere, whereas
\delta=1/n
does not constrain the solution at all.

Similarly to the entropy-energy trade-off in statistical physics, the HES SoS program searches for the highest-value HES distribution at a given value
\delta
.
Unlike previous uses of pseudo-entropy constraints
[
MSS16
,
KKK19
,
RY20
]
, this program fails to be a relaxation of the original maximization problem, since the HES entropy constraint reduces the achievable value to
below
the true optimum.
This is in keeping with the thermodynamic analogy, where temperatures above zero give progressively softer soft-minima of the energy.
This gives the HES distribution family a trade-off between value and entropy, similar to the Gibbs distribution.

Average case optimization in SoS

This work advances understanding toward a program of Barak
[
Bar14
, Chapter 9, p. 105]
regarding the universality of SoS programs in average-case problems.
Average-case problems are usually where concentration of measure is the primary characterization of solutions, and so they are resistant to techniques based on low-degree pointwise proofs.

1.3
Related work

Analytic & geometric properties of spin glasses

The Crisanti-Sommers formula
[
CS92
]
gives a variational principle for the limiting free energy density of spherical spin glasses.
An alternative characterization of the free energy density over the
ball
(and not just the sphere) was given by a generalized TAP free energy formula due to Subag
[
Sub18
]
.
This approach was then strengthened and adapted to the setting of mean-field spin glasses over the hypercube by Chen, Panchenko and Subag
[
CPS18
,
CPS21
]
.
In the spherical case, Subag’s characterization was independent of the Parisi framework, but in the setting over the hypercube the generalized TAP free energy still relies on the Fenchel dual of a solution to the Parisi PDE on restricted intervals.
In addition to the generalized TAP free energy, Subag
[
Sub18
]
gave a complete characterization of the free energy landscape of spherical spin glasses by appealing to the study of thin spherical bands induced by the orthogonal subspace of points in the unit ball that maximize the generalized TAP free energy at a specific distance.
This approach inspired Subag’s Hessian-based optimization algorithm (
Section

3.2.4
) under fRSB
[
Sub21
]
.

Algorithmic results for optimization of mean-field spin glasses

Along with the local spectral algorithm of Subag
[
Sub21
]
, there has been an equivalent class of results that use an AMP algorithm to find near-ground states of mean-field spin glasses over the hypercube
[
Mon19
,
AMS21
]
.
In a work of Huang and Sellke
[
HS22
]
, it is established that both these families of algorithms achieve a particular threshold of approximation (called
\mathsf{ALG}
) which is given by the infimum of a “relaxed” version of the Parisi formula. In fact, this is the best approximation ratio achievable by any “overlap-concentrated” algorithm.
It is known that on spin-glasses, Langevin dynamics and certain families of AMP algorithms are overlap-concentrated
[
HS22
]
.
Certain low-degree polynomials are also conjectured to be overlap-concentrated
[
JMSS23
]
, but this has not yet been formally proved (
Section

9.1.1
).

Hardness results for optimization of mean-field spin glasses

Complementing the algorithmic side of the results, it is well established that when the unique minimizer
[
AC15
]
of the Parisi formula (which has value
\mathsf{OPT}
) is not strictly increasing, there is a gap as
\mathsf{ALG}<\mathsf{OPT}

[
HS22
]
.
This situation corresponds to the existence of a so-called
overlap-gap property
and there is abundant work in the literature defining various variants of this property
[
GS14
,
RV+17
,
CGPR19
,
HS22
]
and then using it to obtain varying strengths of hardness results for many problems
[
RV+17
,
CGPR19
,
CLSS22
,
Wei22
,
GJW20
,
Hua22
]
including spin-glasses
[
GJW20
,
GJ21
,
HS22
]
against a large collection of algorithms.

SoS certificate complexity for spin glasses

There is now a fairly complete body of work showing that certifying
\mathsf{OPT}
to
\pm\varepsilon
-precision using the standard SoS relaxation requires
\Omega(n^{f(\varepsilon)})
-degree on the sphere
[
BGL17
,
HKP+17
]
and the hypercube
[
KB21
,
GJJ+20
]
.
The certification lower bound on the sphere is for
pure
spherical-spin glasses with
d_{H}\geqslant 3
, but can be extended by the same trace-moment method to
mixed
spherical spin glasses as each degree term in the mixed setting makes a
\Theta(n)
contribution.
The lower bounds on the hypercube are explicitly against the SK model
[
SK75
]
, but can likely also be extended to the mixed setting as well for similar reasons.

SoS algorithms, entropy, and rounding

Previous work has added various forms of high-entropy constraints to the standard SoS hierarchy
[
MSS16
,
KKK19
,
RY20
]
, serving as a precursor to the full idea of writing an SoS program over distributions rather than individual solutions.
Correlation rounding has been proven optimal for convex relaxations with pointwise proofs optimizing degree-2 spin glass polynomials over the discrete hypercube
[
JKR19
,
Ris16
]
.
The approximation ratio of the standard SoS hierarchy has been studied on average-case CSPs
[
BKS13
]
.

2
Technical Overview

2.1
The spherical spin glass problem

The Hamiltonian: Dense random low-degree polynomials

The goal is to optimize a random polynomial of degree
d_{H}
over the hypersphere
\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1)
of radius
1
. A spherical spin glass corresponds to the
low temperature
limit of a material whose Hamiltonian is given by the very same random polynomial.

Definition 2.1

(Mixed Spherical
p
-Spin Glass)
.

The hamiltonian of a mixed spherical
d_{H}
-spin glass is given as,

H_{n}(\sigma)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=1}^{d_{H}}\gamma_{k}\sqrt{n}\!\!\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in[n]^{k}}g_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\sigma_{i_{1}}\dots\sigma_{i_{k}}\,,

(2.1)

where
g_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
for every
k\in[d_{H}]
. Furthermore, the coefficients
\left\{\gamma_{k}\right\}_{k=2}^{p}
are non-negative, real valued and independent of
n
.
1
1

1

Although we do not handle a linear term
k=1
in this manuscript, such a term can be accommodated with a modification of the SoS-encoded HES distributions in order to allow for a
drift
term. We implement this in forthcoming work addressing the SK model and its generalizations.

The goal of the optimization task is to maximize the hamiltonian above over
\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1)
,

\sup_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1)}H_{n}(\sigma)\,.

This quantity is called the
ground state energy
of the spherical spin glass and is equivalent to its so-called free energy at zero temperature.

Note that we formulate the problem on the unit sphere, but have adjusted the Hamiltonian to be equivalent to previous formulations on the radius-
\sqrt{n}
sphere
[
Sub18
,
Sub21
]
.

The mixture function

The solution quality achieved by the algorithm on this optimization problem depends on the structure of the mixture coefficient
\{\gamma_{k}\}_{k=2}^{d_{H}}
. Under a specific assumption (
Proposition

3.6
) on these, termed “full-Replica Symmetry Breaking” (fRSB), our algorithm will output a solution that is arbitrarily close to the true optimal value.

Definition 2.2

(Mixture Polynomial)
.

The mixture polynomial of a hamiltonian
H_{n}(\sigma)
is given as,

\nu(q)=\sumop\displaylimits_{k=2}^{d_{H}}\gamma_{k}^{2}q^{k}\,,

and is defined for
q\in[0,1]
.

2.2
Pseudo-distributions over filtered distributions

A SoS relaxation
[
BS14
]
takes an optimization problem of the form

\displaystyle\max_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\;

\displaystyle p(x)

subject to

\displaystyle{}q_{1}(x)\geqslant 0

.

.

.

\displaystyle q_{m}(x)\geqslant 0

(where
p
,
q_{1}
, …,
q_{m}
are polynomials)
and augments the domain of the problem to a higher-dimensional space, so that both the constraint set and the objective function are convex in the new augmented domain.
This convex program is then solvable in polynomial time, and rounding techniques are used to convert the solution found in this augmented domain back to a solution in the original domain without losing too much of the objective value.

The augmentation adds variables (
\pE x^{\alpha}
for each monomial
x^{\alpha}
up to some degree limit, where the entire expression “
\pE x^{\alpha}
” is a single SDP variable) representing the low-degree moments of a distribution over the original solution domain. This converts the objective into one of optimizing the
pseudo-expectation

\pE p(x)
of the original polynomial objective
p(x)
.
Now
\pE p(x)
is a linear objective in the
pseudo-moments
, since the expectation of a polynomial is simply a linear combination of moments.
Similarly, the original polynomial constraints
q_{i}(x)\geqslant 0
each become a family of linear constraints on the pseudo-moments.

Finally, the SoS relaxation adds consistency constraints stating that the pseudo-expectation of every square polynomial
\pE r(x)^{2}
is non-negative.
The non-negativity of all square polynomials (up to a degree limit) can be realized as a single convex constraint simply requiring a certain matrix of pseudo-moments to be positive semi-definite
[
BS14
]
.

To this basic framework, we introduce symbols
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
each representing a random variable taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
The convex program variables are now not pseudo-moments of the
v_{i}
, but pseudo-moments
of the moments of

v_{i}
, for example
\pE(\E(v_{i})_{j}^{3})^{2}
.
For this to be non-trivial as a relaxation, we add non-convex polynomial constraints on those moments, restricting the set of possible distributions represented by
v_{i}
, before taking the pseudo-moment relaxation.
This turns those polynomial constraints on moments into linear constraints on pseudo-moments of moments.
For pseudo-distributions over filtered distributions such as the HES distributions, the distribution of each
v_{i}
depends on all previous
v_{1}
, …,
v_{i-1}
, so that, for example, if
\fE_{i}
represents the expectation conditioned on
v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}
, then
\pE(\E_{1}(v_{1})_{4}(\E_{2}(v_{2})_{3})^{2})^{3}
is a SDP variable in the program
2
2

2

Note that this term is not expressible as a joint moment, making this set-up strictly stronger than using joint moments as program variables. This is elaborated on, and the equivalence with replicas explained, in
Section

4.2.9
.

In an extention to the axiom notation of standard SoS
[
BS14
]
, we also state axiomatic constraints as, for example,
\{p(v_{1})\geqslant 0\}
and
\{p(v_{1},v_{2})\geqslant 0\}
, which intuitively mean that
p(v_{1})\geqslant 0
and
p(v_{1},v_{2})\geqslant 0
for all
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
in the support of the filtered distribution.
These may be formally defined in the SDP as meaning
\pE\E_{1}[p(v_{1})q(v_{1})^{2}]\geqslant 0
and
\pE\E_{1}\left[\E_{2}[p(v_{1},v_{2})q(v_{2})^{2}]\cdot r(v_{1},\E_{2}[v_{2}],\E_{2}[v_{2}^{2}],\dots)^{2}\right]\geqslant 0
for all polynomial expressions
q
and
r
for which those pseudo-expectations are defined.
This system is explained in detail in
Section

4.2
.

2.3
The HES SoS hierarchy

We state our program and briefly explain and motivate the constraints.

The actual solution is represented implicitly as a sum of
k
equally-sized components
\sigma=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}/\sqrt{k}
.
The variables
k
,
\delta
,
d
, and
d^{\prime}
are adjustable parameters.

\displaystyle\;\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\max\pE\fE_{1}\left[H\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{k}\frac{v_{j}}{\sqrt{k}}\right)\right]\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad

(P.0)

s.t.

\displaystyle\qquad\forall i\in[k]\mathrel{.}{}

(P.1)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left\{\left\lVert v_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=1\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\forall i\in[k]\mathrel{.}{}

(P.2)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left\{\left\langle v_{i},\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{i-1}v_{j}\right\rangle=0\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\forall i\in[k]\mathrel{.}{}\forall j\in[d]{\setminus}\{2\}\mathrel{.}{}

(P.3)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left\{\left\lVert\kappa_{j}\left[v_{i}\mid v_{i-1}\right]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\frac{o_{n}(1)}{n^{j/2}}\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\forall i\in[k]\mathrel{.}{}

(P.4)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left\{\left\lVert\fE_{i}\left[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\right]\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\leqslant\frac{1+o_{n}(1)}{\delta n}\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\forall i\in[k]\mathrel{.}{}

(P.5)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left\{\left\lVert\left(\fE_{i}\left[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\right]^{>d}\right)^{\otimes d^{k-i}}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\leqslant\frac{o_{n}(1)}{n^{d^{k-i}}}\right\}\,.

1.

The normalization constraint ensures that steps are of equal length.

2.

The orthogonality constraint ensures that we can compute the length of the sum of the steps via Pythagorean theorem.
3
3

3

This orthogonality constraint is approximately implied to high precision by the other constraints, but we keep it for convenience so that the norms of the sums of the steps are always known exactly. Changing the constraint to be an upper and lower bound up to
\pm o_{n}(1)
factors allows many computations to become more convenient, but does not affect the quantitative strength of the results. Therefore, in
Section

6.3
and the companion paper, we invoke arguments that only preserve the constraint up to small fluctuations.

3.

The stepwise Gaussianity constraint says that each step is close to a zero-mean Gaussian in its low-degree moments, conditioned on the
(i-1)
th step of the filtration.
It enforces this by matching the cumulants (and therefore moments) of
v_{i}
to those of a Gaussian (
Sections

3.4.3
and
E
).
This constraint enables the use of the Weiner-Hermite polynomial basis (
Section

3.5.2
), whose weak orthogonality is equivalent to vanishing higher cumulants.

4.

The spectral high-entropy constraint forces the Gaussian to be spread out over at least
\delta n
directions.
This ensures that the distribution of
v_{i}
does not put too much weight into any single direction, allowing us to avoid pointwise proofs.

5.

The low-degreeness constraint asserts that the dependence of the distribution of
v_{i}
on the previous iterates should be close to a low-degree polynomial. Here,
f^{>d^{\prime}}
for a function
f
refers to the projection of
f
to its degree-more-than-
d^{\prime}
part (up to the degree limit of the pseudo-distribution).
This constraint is defined in terms of the Fourier basis given by the Weiner-Hermite polynomials in
Appendix

E
.

Note that while it is rigorously established that the support of the Gibbs distribution for these models resides in a very complicated approximately ultrametric structure, only a
trivial
approximately ultrametric structure can be inferred from the HES constraints.
4
4

4

The fact that the support of the Gibbs measure should reside in an ultrametric space was first predicted by Parisi
[
Par80
]
.
Ruelle then explicitly characterized the Gibbs via a construction of a random measure supported on the leaves of an ultrametric tree, defined by a family of cascading independent Poisson-Dirichlet processes on the inner nodes - This construction was termed the Ruelle Probability Cascades
[
Rue87
,
Pan13b
]
and it gave an object on which the Parisi formula could be equivalently re-written.
It was later shown by Panchenko
[
Pan13a
]
that the so-called Ghirlanda-Guerra identities
[
GG98
]

implied
the ultrametricity of the solution-space.
It is an interesting question as to whether there even exist SoS proofs (under some modified step constraints) that can certify that solutions reside in a hierarchically organized ultrametric space consistent with the RSB framework, and this is discussed further in
Section

9.1.3
.

Rounding higher pseudo-moments

The HES structure was partly motivated by the following concrete question:

What rounding strategies can round pseudo-distributions by matching higher pseudo-moments?

Rounding by matching degree-2 moments is well understood due to the quadratic sampling lemma (
Lemma

3.5
), but in general, the question of whether a set of degree-3 pseudo-moments even corresponds to the moments of an actual distribution is NP-hard
[
Gri01
]
.

As a Gaussian vector’s third moments are a symmetrization of the tensor product of its mean with its covariance, this distribution can only match the order-3 moments of a pseudo-distribution to the extent that it matches the first and second moments.
But consider the sum of two vectors,
u+v
, where
u
is Gaussian and
v
is also Gaussian but only when conditioned on
u
, so that the covariance of
v
may depend on
u
.
Then the third-order moments of this sum includes a
\E_{u,v}u\otimes v\otimes v
term, which can be expressed as
\E u\otimes f(u)
for some arbitrary function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes 2}
.
This is much more expressive: for example, when
f
is degree-
3
,
\E u\otimes f(u)
may have full tensor rank, whereas pure Gaussian third moments cannot.

Rounding to HES distributions would therefore allow much greater expressivity than rounding to Gaussians. While random reweighing
[
BKS14
]
can improve the expressiveness of Gaussian rounding, it is limited by only being able to output a statistical mixture of Gaussians.

2.4
Technical challenges

The spectral conditions for the success of the HES hierarchy stated in
Theorem

1.1
are based on finding algebraic structure compatible with a SoS proof that the dominant certifiable contributions are from the Hessian.
This amounts to certifying the Taylor expansion of the Hamiltonian under HES distributions, which recovers Subag’s Hessian ascent algorithm
[
Sub21
]
on spherical spin glasses.
We briefly overview, for SoS experts, some of the technical challenges involved in approaching this task
5
5

5

Readers that are not familiar with average-case SoS lower bounds may freely skip this subsection without any loss of ability to follow along the remaining subsections in
Section

2
.
.

Reweighed iterative rounding along a path in the ultrametric tree.

It is natural to consider explicitly representing steps in the ultrametric tree as program variables (
Equations

P.1
and
P.2
), as this follows the path of existing iterative algorithms.
It then becomes apparent that previous methods of rounding fail to make progress due to the impossibility of proving that there are few good solutions
[
BKS14
,
MSS16
,
KKK19
,
RY20
]
or few good product distributions
[
BRS11
,
JKR19
]
of solutions (since, in fact, there are many good product distributions of solutions, corresponding in this case to “pure states” in the statistical physics literature
[
Tal10
]
).

Eigendecompositions and edge statistics for the eigenspectrum.

Progressing in this direction, one runs into further problems if one attempts to follow the iterative spectral algorithm of Subag too closely: the proofs and algorithm of Subag make heavy use of high-degree SoS operations and objects, such as concentration inequalities with exponentially decaying tails
[
Sub21
, Lemma 3]
and eigendecompositions
[
Sub21
, Eq 3.10 & Eq. 3.11]
.

Bounding higher-order derivatives via Dudley-style entropy bounds.

When bounding the contribution of the higher-order derivatives, Subag
[
Sub21
, Lemma 11]
invokes a bound
[
ASZ20
, Lemma 58]
on the injective norm of random tensors.
This uses Dudley-style entropy bounds
[
Lat06
]
(or chaining arguments) to upper-bound the expected norm, and then invokes the Borell-TIS inequality
[
AT09
, Theorem 2.1.1]
to guarantee strong concentration around the bounded value.
These techniques are not amenable to pointwise low-degree SoS proofs
[
BGL17
,
HKP+17
]
.

Disorder chaos.

To compound the technical challenge, we believe it is not possible to capture the
true
low-temperature Gibbs distribution with a low-degree SoS program due to
disorder chaos
: small perturbations in the Hamiltonian lead to large fluctuations in the moments of the Gibbs distribution
[
CS17
, Theorem 3]
[
AMS22
, Eq. 2.11, Eq. 5.16 & Eq. 5.22]
, whereas SoS is generally believed to excel when solutions are stable to input perturbations.

The difficulties involving concentration or too many good solutions are averted by introducing pseudo-distributions over HES distributions.

2.5
Structure of main argument

The main structure of the argument for the sufficient conditions in
Theorem

1.1
will be to certify the Taylor expansion of the objective function achieves good value when run on the extension
\tilde{H}
to the ball of the original problem
H
on the sphere.

2.5.1
Certifiable Taylor expansion

This argument is based on a Taylor expansion (
Section

6.2
), from which we derive strict upper bounds on the expected objective value achieved by each step.

The gradient term is automatically suppressed by the HES constraint that
\E_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}
is bounded in spectral norm.
6
6

6

As in Subag’s analysis
[
Sub21
]
, it is crucial that we
do not
follow the gradient—briefly, while following the gradient can gain more objective value
per distance travelled
in the step, it will also reduce the
number of steps
we can take, due to the gradients being aligned with the radial direction and thereby reaching the unit sphere more quickly.

The third- and higher-order derivatives of
\tilde{H}(\sigma_{i-1})
are bounded when the step size is small enough,
\sigma_{i-1}
is drawn from a HES distribution, and
\tilde{H}
is well behaved enough, as discussed in the next section.
Therefore the dominant contributions are from the Hessian term at each step.

2.5.2
SoS proofs about HES distributions

To show upper bounds on
\E p(\sigma)
for a degree-
d
homogeneous polynomial
p
(suppose
d
is even for simplicity), we represent both the polynomial
p
and the moments of
\sigma
as matrices

\E p(\sigma)=\left\langle\mat(p),\E\sigma^{\otimes d/2}(\sigma^{\otimes d/2})^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rangle\,,

where
\mat(p)\in(\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})^{\otimes d/2}
is the matrix representation of the coefficients of
p
satisfying
the tensor mode permutation symmetries

\mat(p)_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{d/2}),(i_{d/2+1},\dots,i_{d})}=\mat(p)_{(i_{\pi(1)},\dots,i_{\pi(d/2)}),(i_{\pi(d/2+1)},\dots,i_{\pi(d)})}

for every permutation
\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{d}
in the symmetric group.
This representation always exists since
\E p(\sigma)
is a linear function of the degree-
d
moments of
\sigma
, each of which is represented in at least one entry of
\E\sigma^{\otimes d/2}(\sigma^{\otimes d/2})^{\mathsf{T}}
.

Now observe that since
\mat(p)
is mode-symmetric, for any matrix
V
such that
\E\sigma^{\otimes d/2}(\sigma^{\otimes d/2})^{\mathsf{T}}
is the mode-symmetrization of
V
, we have

\E p(\sigma)=\left\langle\mat(p),V\right\rangle\,.

For many average-case ensembles of polynomials over the unit sphere, we will have tight concentration on
\left\lVert\mat(p)\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}
, so that
\left\lVert\mat(p)\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}
will be at most
O(n^{d/4-1/2})
times larger than
\max_{\sigma}p(\sigma)
with high probability, so we can consider by Hölder’s inequality

\E p(\sigma)\leqslant\left\lVert\mat(p)\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{1}\,.

Then, for a given distribution over
\sigma
, all it takes is to find some
V
whose mode-symmetrization is
\E\sigma^{\otimes d/2}(\sigma^{\otimes d/2})^{\mathsf{T}}
and whose nuclear norm is at most
O(n^{-d/4+1/2})
in order to bound
\E p(\sigma)
to within a constant of
\max_{\sigma}p(\sigma)
with high probability.

To illustrate the broad approach to this, we can look at the simple case where
\sigma
is drawn from a Gaussian distribution with mean
0
and covariance
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
.
By Isserlis’s theorem (
Lemma

3.11
),
\E\sigma^{\otimes d/2}(\sigma^{\otimes d/2})^{\mathsf{T}}
is equal to a sum of mode-permutations of
\Sigma^{\otimes d/2}
.
Letting
\Phi\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes 2}
be the tensor whose entries are
\Phi_{(i,j)}=\Sigma_{i,j}
, we can choose
V
to be one of those mode-permutations

V:=(\Phi\Phi^{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes\lfloor d/4\rfloor}\otimes\Sigma^{\otimes(d/2-2\lfloor d/4\rfloor)}\,.

Then the mode-symmetrization of
V
is equal to
\E\sigma^{\otimes d/2}(\sigma^{\otimes d/2})^{\mathsf{T}}
and
\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{1}=\left\lVert\Phi\right\rVert_{2}^{2\lfloor d/4\rfloor}\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{1}=\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{F}^{2\lfloor d/4\rfloor}
since
\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{1}=1
.
If we assume
\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
, we get
\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant O(n^{-d/4+1/2})
as desired.

To generalize this so that
\sigma
can be HES-distributed, we do similar manipulations inductively over the steps
\sigma=v_{1}+\dots+v_{k}
, this time using the moment-cumulant formula (
Definition

3.24
), which generalizes Isserlis’s theorem to the case of non-Gaussian distributions.
It takes a fair amount of care here to characterize all the cumulants that arise and how to reshape them to matrices with low nuclear norm.

Through a more careful analysis of mode symmetries, this generalizes to the case where
p
is a power of a polynomial whose matrix representation satisfies a spectral norm bound (similar to the technique in the upper bound proof of
[
BGL17
]
), and then further on to cases where
p
can be represented as a graph matrix whose components have spectrally bounded matrix representations, as these are also relevant in
Section

2.5.3
.

This also generalizes to the case when
p
is a polynomial of both
\sigma
and
v
, where
v
is a high-entropy step with
\sigma
the sum of the previous iterates, and captures the case when
p
is a third- or higher-order derivative.

2.5.3
Certifiable spectral moments for the Hessian

The only missing certification now is for the value achieved/achievable by the Hessian term of the Taylor expansion, which is done in
Section

6.1
.

The Taylor expansion showed that the change
\E\tilde{H}(\sigma_{i})-\E\tilde{H}(\sigma_{i-1})
is dominated by the Hessian term
\frac{1}{2k}\E\left\langle\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}(\sigma_{i-1}),\E_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rangle
.
For
v_{i}
a high-entropy step, the value attainable will be determined by the
p
-norms of
\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}(\sigma_{i-1})
, by a direct matrix generalization of the analytic sparsity arguments of
[
ABS15
,
BBH+12
]
.
Therefore, we need to certifiably bound these Schatten
p
-norms for all HES distribution over
\sigma_{i-1}
.

We do this by borrowing the non-backtracking walk argument from random matrix theory.
The
p
th power of the Schatten
p
-norm of a matrix
M
is equal to
\Tr M^{p}
, which can be expressed as a sum over length-
p
closed walks on a graph where the edge
\{i,j\}
is labelled by
M_{i,j}
and the value to be summed for each walk is the product of all the edge labels on the walk.

For the spherical spin glass Hamiltonian, it will turn out via the non-backtracking walk argument that the expectation term
\E_{g}\Tr[\nabla^{2}H(\sigma_{i-1})]^{p}
(with expectation over randomness of the instance) is essentially independent of
\sigma_{i-1}
, as nearly all of the expectation terms will factor out into a multiple of a power of
\left\lVert\sigma_{i-1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
(which is a constant) yielding an (upto
o_{n}(1)
factors) exact expression.

We then represent the coefficients of the polynomial expressing the non-expectation terms
\Tr[\nabla^{2}H(\sigma_{i-1})]^{p}-\E_{g}\Tr[\nabla^{2}H(\sigma_{i-1})]^{p}
as a sum of graph matrices
[
AMP21
]
.
This allows for the invocation of the graph matrix norm bound framework on the spectral norms of the matrix representations (
Theorem

3.51
) of those polynomials.
At this point, by a (
1,\infty
)-Hölder’s inequality, one can upper bound the contribution of each non-expectation term,

\E\left[\Tr[\nabla^{2}H(\sigma_{i-1})]^{p}-\E_{g}\Tr[\nabla^{2}H(\sigma_{i-1})]^{p}\right]=\sumop\displaylimits_{\text{noise}}\E[p_{\text{noise}}]\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{\text{noise}}\left\lVert\mat{(p)_{\text{noise}}}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\left\lVert V(\sigma)\right\rVert_{1}\,.

The exact extraction of the expected trace component and bound on the non-expected components gives a certificate for the
p
-Schatten norms of the Hessian consistent with the low-degree moments of a scaled
\mathsf{GOE}(n)
matrix. An upper bound on
\frac{1}{2k}\E\left\langle\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}(\sigma_{i-1}),\E_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rangle
now follows by a
(p,q)
-Hölder’s inequality, where the choice of
(p,q)
conjugates is ascertained by a judicious use of Catalan combinatorics and analysis (
Corollary

6.3
), and the bound can be invoked with high probability (over the randomness of instance) due to standard implications of Gaussian hypercontractivity (
Lemma

3.12
).

2.5.4
Ensemble-agnosticism

We provide a few simple examples of modifications to the spherical spin glass ensemble for which the HES hierarchy would provably outperform the local Hessian ascent algorithm (
Section

8
),
conditioned
on the existence of a rounding scheme mentioned in
1.6
.
This is done in an ensemble-agnostic way, so that the SoS-based algorithm is exactly the same one run for the plain spherical spin glass model, with no parameter tuning needed.

The first one is motivated by considering a homogeneous degree-4 spherical spin glass, except that the entries of the random tensor
g
that have repeated indices (for example
g_{i,i,j,j}
or
g_{i,j,i,j}
) have
\alpha n
times larger variance than the other entries for some constant
\alpha
.
On the sphere, this is equivalent to sum of a degree-2 spin glass
\alpha H_{2}(\sigma)
and a degree-4 spin glass model
H_{4}(\sigma)
, but on the ball, it is
\alpha\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}H_{2}(\sigma)+H_{4}(\sigma)
.
This makes a local algorithm neglect the degree-2 term in the beginning when
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}
is small.

The second is the vector space direct sum of a pure degree-2 spherical spin glass and a pure degree-4 spherical spin glass,
\alpha H_{2}(\Pi\sigma)+H_{4}(\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)
for
\Pi
and
\Pi^{\perp}
the projectors to the two subspaces of the direct sum. Here, the local algorithm will follow the degree-2 term and get stuck in that subspace, even if following the degree-4 term eventually attains a higher value.

The third example adds a random bilinear interaction between the two subspaces of the second example, so that the objective is
\alpha H_{2}(\Pi\sigma)+H_{4}(\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)+2\beta H_{2,\mathrm{off}}(\Pi\sigma,\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)
.
This starts to make the SoS proof for the “stepwise upper bound” condition of
Theorem

1.1
fairly non-trivial, requiring a bound for all possible values of the “state variables”
\left\lVert\Pi\sigma_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
and
\left\lVert\Pi^{\perp}\sigma_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
over all steps
i
.
For this example, we give a proof sketch with an assumption about SoS proofs of a certain type of matrix concentration, rather than a full proof.

3
Preliminaries

3.1
Sum-of-squares optimization: proofs to algorithms

3.1.1
Sum-of-squares certificates and pseudo-expectations

We first define formally the notion of an “axiom”
\mathcal{A}
which will be a collection of low-degree polynomial equalities and inequalities
[
BS14
,
MSS16
]
.

Definition 3.1

(SoS Axioms
\mathcal{A}
)
.

A set of
axioms

\mathcal{A}
is a collection of polynomial equalities and inequalities, formally written as,

\mathcal{A}=\left\{f_{i}=0\right\}_{i=1}^{M}\cup\,\left\{g_{j}\geqslant 0\right\}_{j=1}^{K}\,,

where,
f_{i},g_{j}\in\mathbb{R}_{\leqslant d}[x_{1}\dots,x_{n}]
are real-valued polynomials over some domain.

Critical to the notion of efficient SoS algorithms are low-degree proofs of non-negativity of polynomials over the underlying semi-algebraic set
D
specified by being exactly the set
x
of points satisfying the axioms
\mathcal{A}
.
A proof of non-negativity over
D
will require the degree to be at least at large as that of the polynomial whose non-negativity is being shown.

Definition 3.2

(SoS Non-Negativity Proof of Degree-
k
)
.

Given polynomials
p(x),q(x)
, a degree-
k
SoS proof of non-negativity of
p-q
is given as

p(x)-q(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{r}(r_{i}(x))^{2}\prodop\displaylimits_{j\in\alpha_{i}}g_{j}(x)+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{M}s_{i}(x)f_{i}(x)\,,

for some choices of
r_{i},s_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\leqslant d}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
and
\alpha_{i}\subseteq[K]
and where the total degree of any term is at most
k
.

For certain domains, such as over the boolean hypercube (
D=\{\pm 1\}^{n}
), a low-degree proof implies that
r\leqslant n^{k/2}
and this further means that a low-degree SoS proof of negativity is efficiently verifiable, provided the coefficients can be represented with reasonable bit complexity
[
RW17
,
BR22
]
.
When a low-degree SoS proof of deg-
k
exists, it is written as,

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{k}\{p\geqslant q\}\,.

If the proof exists modulo some SoS axioms
\mathcal{A}
, then it is written as,

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{k}\{p\geqslant q\}\,.

The set of low-degree SoS proofs is a convex set: a convex combination of sums of squares
g_{i}(x)^{2}
is another sum of squares.
This implies the existence of a
\mathsf{poly}(n)
time algorithm to output such a proof if it exists and has polynomial bit complexity.
This involves solving a particular
\mathsf{poly}(n)
-sized SDP. Specifically, this means that

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{k}\{p\geqslant q\}\implies O(MKn)^{O(k)}\text{ algorithm to output $r_{i}$, $s_{i}$, and $\alpha_{i}$ according to \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:sos-proof}.}

By strong duality, an important consequence of the above is that whenever there is no low-degree SoS proof that
p\geqslant q
, there is a linear operator
\pE
called a
pseudo-expectation
that operates on polynomials, satisfies
\pE p(x)<\pE q(x)
, respects the axioms
\mathcal{A}
, is normalized, and respects non-negativity of squared polynomials of not too-large degree.
This operator is the output of the underlying semi-definite program (SDP) whenever it fails to find a SoS proof.

Definition 3.3

(Pseudo-distribution of degree
k
)
.

A pseudo-distribution
\mathcal{D}
of degree
k
with respect to SoS axioms
\mathcal{A}
corresponds to a linear functional,

\pE_{\mathcal{D}}:\mathbb{R}_{\leqslant k}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]\to\mathbb{R}\,,

that satisfies the following propositions,

Scaling
:
\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[1]=1
.

Non-negativity
:
\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[g^{2}]\geqslant 0\,,\,\forall
polynomials
g
with deg(
g
)
\,\leqslant k/2
.

Satisfies Axioms
: The following holds for the polynomials in the axioms
\mathcal{A}
:

1.

\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[a_{i}f_{i}]=0
,
\forall a_{i}
such that deg(
a_{i}f_{i}
)
\,\leqslant k
.

2.

\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[b^{2}_{i}g_{i}]\geqslant 0
,
\forall b_{i}
such that deg(
b_{i}g^{2}_{i}
)
\,\leqslant k
.

We write
\mathcal{D}\models\mathcal{A}
whenever the pseudo-expectation
\pE_{\mathcal{D}}
satisfies the axioms given by
\mathcal{A}
.

Note that the Riesz representation theorem implies that every pseudo-distribution
\mathcal{D}
can be associated with a linear functional
\pE_{\mathcal{D}}
that satisfies the above axioms.
The SDP formally outputs a pseudo-moment matrix of
\mathsf{deg}(k)
(when it is feasible), and the linear functional acts on polynomials in ways that respect certain inequalities, such as Jensen’s inequality (
Lemma

C.2
) and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

Lemma 3.4

(Pseudo Cauchy-Schwarz Inequality,
[
BS16
, 21. Theorem]
)
.

Given a degree-
d
pseudo-distribution
\mathcal{D}
and polynomials
p,q
of degree
\leqslant d/2
, the following holds:

\bigg(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}pq\bigg)^{2}\leqslant\bigg(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}p^{2}\bigg)\bigg(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}q^{2}\bigg)\,.

(3.1)

3.1.2
Rounding & quadratic sampling lemma

Critical to a relaxation of a problem to a space where it is tractable to solve, is the ability to “round” back the relaxed solution into the original solution space with (hopefully) minimal loss in solution quality.
For SoS relaxations, since pseudo-moment matrices are positive semi-definite (PSD), a natural candidate for rounding is to use these as moment matrices for some underlying distribution that is chosen appropriate to the problem at hand
[
BKS14
,
BS16
]
.
The second-most basic rounding algorithm is to use the degree-1-and-2 pseudo-moments as the moments of a Gaussian.
The fact that this can always be done is known as the
quadratic sampling lemma
.

Lemma 3.5

(Quadratic Sampling Lemma,
[
BS16
, 23. Lemma]
)
.

For every degree-2 pseudo-distribution
\mathcal{D}
, there exists a gaussian
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, such that,

\mu=\pE_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}[x]\,,

and,

\Sigma+\mu\mu^{\mathsf{T}}=\pE_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}[xx^{\mathsf{T}}]\,.

3.2
Mixed spherical spin glasses

3.2.1
Replica symmetry breaking

Full-Replica Symmetry Breaking is an imposition on the allowed overlaps in the solution space.
The condition implies that the normalized overlap of two configurations sampled i.i.d. from the Gibbs distribution can take any value between
0
and
1
.
More precisely, given
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\sim G_{n,\beta}^{\otimes 2}
,

\textsf{supp}\left(\frac{1}{n}|\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rangle|\right)=[0,q_{\beta}]\,,

where
q_{\beta}\to 1
as
\beta\to\infty
.
In fact, this condition can be used to ascertain finer properties about the input hamiltonian
H_{n}
when it is an instance of a Spherical Spin Glass, and the following proposition from Subag
[
Sub21
]
outlines these (equivalent) properties.

Proposition 3.6

(full-RSB Models
[
Sub21
, Proposition 1]
)
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

Given the mixture polynomial
\nu
for the input hamiltonian
H_{n}
,
\nu^{\prime\prime}(q)^{-1/2}
is concave for
q\in(0,1]
.

2.

The Parisi Measure
\mu_{\beta}
has
full support

[0,q_{\beta}]
for any
\beta>0
.

3.

The following can be said about the support of the Parisi measure based on the mixture polynomial
\mu
’s behavior at
0
:

(a)

If
\beta\leqslant\nu(0)^{\prime\prime-1/2}
, then
\textsf{supp}(\mu_{\beta})=\{0\}
.

(b)

If
\beta>\nu(0)^{\prime\prime-1/2}
, then
\textsf{supp}(\mu_{\beta})=[0,q_{\beta}]
where
q_{\beta}
is the unique solution of

\nu^{\prime\prime}(q)^{-1/2}=\beta(1-q)\,.

Additionally, the CDF of the overlap distribution
\mu_{\beta}
has the following form,

\mu_{\beta}(q)=\begin{cases}\frac{\mu^{\prime\prime\prime}(q)}{2\beta v^{\prime\prime}(q)^{3/2}},&\text{ if $q\in[0,q_{\beta})$}\\
1,&\text{ if $q\in[q_{\beta},1]\,.$}\end{cases}

The first condition of
Proposition

3.6
asserts the concavity of the negative square-root of the second derivative of the mixture polynomial under the fRSB assumption, and the third one allows us to have an explicit characterization of the CDF of the overlap distribution.

3.2.2
The generalized TAP free energy

There are two formulations of the limiting free energy density for typical instances of spherical hamiltonians, where the first is the so-called
Crisanti-Sommers
representation which is the spherical analogue of the infamous Parisi-Variational Principle
[
CS92
,
Tal06
]
and the second is an explicit expression proved by Subag
[
Sub18
]
via a generalized TAP formula. Under the fRSB assumption, the generalized TAP free energy formula will be computable as an explicit function of the mixture polynomial, and so we work with the this representation as introduced by Subag
[
Sub18
]
.

A result of Chen and Sen
[
CS17
]
in conjunction with the fact that the mixture polynomial continues to be concave under re-scaling as,

s\to\nu(qs)\,,

gives a clean expression for
E_{*}(q)
(which is the ground state energy at radius
q
) as a function of the mixture polynomial in the fRSB regime.

Lemma 3.7

(fRSB Ground State Energy,
[
CS17
]
)
.

The ground state energy of the mixed spherical spin glass under fRSB on the ball
\mathcal{B}^{n}
at radius
q
is given as,

\displaystyle E_{*}(q)=E_{\nabla^{2}}(q)=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{\ q}\nu^{\prime\prime}(t)^{1/2}dt\,.

(3.2)

Restating the points above for
q=1
corresponds to the case that
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(\hskip-2.84526pt\sqrt{n})
, and yields that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\max_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(\hskip-1.42262pt\sqrt{n})}H_{n}(\sigma)\overset{a.s.}{=}E_{*}(1)=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime\prime}(t)^{1/2}dt\,,

(3.3)

provided the coefficients
\{\gamma_{k}\}_{k=2}^{d_{H}}
satisfy
Proposition

3.6
.

3.2.3
Eigenvalue distribution of the Hessian

As is shown by Subag
[
Sub21
]
, under the fRSB condition (
Proposition

3.6
) it suffices to look at the top-
\delta n
eigenvectors of the hessian (
\nabla^{2}H
) at every point in the unit ball and follow iteratively perpendicular directions with infinitesmal updates.
The reason for the perpendicular updates corresponds to the ultrametric structure of the solution space
[
Pan13a
]
, but the reason for following the top eigenspace is built upon two key observations:

1.

The eigenvalues of the hessian (
\nabla^{2}H
) are distributed as a mixture polynomial dependent scaling of the Wigner Law for
\textsf{GOE}(n-1)
matrices,

\nabla^{2}H\sim\sqrt{n/(n-1)}\cdot\nu^{\prime\prime}(q)^{1/2}\textsf{GOE}(n-1)\,.

Furthermore, with uniform control over every point
\sigma\in\mathcal{B}^{n}
that maximizes the generalized TAP Free Energy at distance
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}
, there are (with high probability) at least
\delta n
eigenvectors with eigenvalue at least
2\nu^{\prime\prime}(q)^{1/2}-\varepsilon

[
Sub21
, Lemma 3]
.

2.

It then follows by a Taylor expansion argument (given small contributions from the higher-order derivatives) and some algebra that the last iterate will have energy given by a truncated sum of the Chen and Sen formula (
Lemma

3.7
) and, under the fRSB assumption, this will be arbitrarily close to optimal. More specifically, the algorithm will output a value,

\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{k}(1-\varepsilon(q_{i}))\cdot\nu^{\prime\prime}(q_{i})^{1/2}\approx\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime\prime}(q)^{1/2}dq\,,

where
q_{i}
is the
\ell^{2}
-norm of the
i
-th iterate and
\varepsilon(q_{i})
is some arbitrarily small number that depends only on choosing a sufficiently large (but constant) number of iterates.
