The most critical observation in the argumentation above is to notice that the maximum eigenvalue of
\nabla^{2}H
will be a scaling of the Wigner law by a factor that will exactly correspond to the optimum in the fRSB setting.
One can then construct an algorithm that starts at the origin, iteratively makes infinitesmal perpendicular updates that also maximize
\nabla^{2}H
, and then outputs the last iterate.

3.2.4
Hessian ascent

We state here, for completeness, the pseudo-code of the algorithm proposed by Subag
[
Sub21
]
. The
\perp
sign denotes that the gradient
\nabla_{\perp}H(v_{i-1})
and hessian
\nabla^{2}_{\perp}H(v_{i-1})
are projected to be orthogonal to
v_{i-1}
itself.

v_{0}=(\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}},0,\dots,0)
.

i=1
.

while

i\in[k]

do

Choose
x\perp v_{i-1}
with
\left\lVert x\right\rVert=1
, such that,

•

\langle x,\nabla_{\perp}H(v_{i-1})\rangle=0
.

•

\langle x,\nabla^{2}_{\perp}H(v_{i-1})x\rangle\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)2\nu^{\prime\prime}(v_{i})
.

v_{i}=v_{i-1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}x
.

i\leftarrow i+1
.

end

while

Output
v_{k}
.

Algorithm 1

Hessian-Based Optimization for Spherical Spin Glasses
[
Sub21
, Pg 12]

3.2.5
The algorithmic threshold of Huang and Sellke
[
HS22
]

The value achieved by the HES SoS relaxation is certifiably
\varepsilon
-close to
\mathsf{ALG}_{Sp}
for any
\varepsilon>0
.
\mathsf{ALG}_{Sp}
is precisely characterized by
[
HS22
, Proposition 2.2 and Equation 2.5]
and given by a
relaxed
version of the Crisanti-Sommers formula
[
CS92
]
.
When
h=0
(which is the case for the hamiltonian given in
Equation

2.1
) the infimum is given by
Equation

3.4
.

Proposition 3.8

(Variational Algorithmic Threshold for Spherical Spin Glasses,
[
HS22
, Equations 1.3-2.5]
)
.

The value achieved by Subag’s algorithm is given by the following variational principle,

\mathsf{ALG}_{Sp}=\inf_{(B,\mu)\in\mathcal{V}(\nu)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{h^{2}}{B_{\mu}(0)}+\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\left(\frac{\nu^{\prime\prime}(t)}{B_{\mu}(t)}+B_{\mu}(t)\right)dt\right]\right)\,,

(3.4)

where,
B_{\mu}(t)=B-\intop\nolimits_{t}^{1}\nu^{\prime\prime}(q)\mu(q)dq
and the optimizing set is the following collection of tuples,

\mathcal{V}(\nu)=\left\{(B,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\,\times\mathcal{L}([0,1])\,:\,B>\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime\prime}(t)\mu(t)dt\right\}\,,

(3.5)

with the set
\mathcal{L}
denoting a relaxation of the set of all CDFs over the interval
[0,1]
specifically defined as,

\mathcal{L}([0,1])=\left\{\mu:[0,1)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\,:\,\mu\,\text{is right-continuous},\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime\prime}(t)\mu(t)dt<\infty\right\}\,.

(3.6)

By
[
HS22
, Corollary 1.8]
the above variational representation can be simplified to an explicit formula. We restate the proposition, restricting to the case that
h=0
and the hamiltonian only has terms with with degree
\in[2,d_{H}]
.

Proposition 3.9

(Explicit Algorithmic Threshold,
[
HS22
, Corollary 1.8]
)
.

The algorithmic threshold is given as a function of the mixture polynomial as,

\mathsf{ALG}_{Sp}=q_{1}\nu^{\prime\prime}(q_{1})^{1/2}+\intop\nolimits_{q_{1}}^{1}\nu^{\prime\prime}(q)^{1/2}dq\,,

(3.7)

where
q_{1}\in[0,1)
is the unique solution to,

\nu^{\prime}(q_{1})=q_{1}\nu^{\prime\prime}(q_{1})\,.

(3.8)

When
h=0
and
\gamma_{1}=0
, the only way to satisfy
Equation

3.8
is when
q_{1}=0
, which immediately implies that for the family of hamiltonians detailed in
Equation

2.1
,

\mathsf{ALG}_{Sp}=\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime\prime}(q)^{1/2}dq\,.

(3.9)

For the solution to
Equation

3.8
in the case that
\gamma_{1}=0
and
h=0
, observe that

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}(q_{1})=q_{\nu}^{\prime\prime}(q_{1})

\displaystyle\implies\sumop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{d_{H}}i\cdot\gamma^{2}_{i}q^{i-1}_{1}=q_{1}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{d_{H}}i(i-1)\cdot\gamma^{2}_{i}q^{i-2}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\implies\sumop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{d_{H}}i\cdot\gamma^{2}_{i}q_{1}^{i-1}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{d_{H}}i(i-1)\cdot\gamma^{2}_{i}q^{i-1}_{1}

\displaystyle\implies\sumop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{d_{H}}i(2-i)\cdot\gamma^{2}_{i}q^{i-1}_{1}=0

\displaystyle\implies\sumop\displaylimits_{i=3}^{i}(2-i)\gamma^{2}_{i}q_{1}^{i-1}=0\,,

and since
(2-i)<0
for
i>2
, the above term can
only
be made
0
at
q_{1}=0
. This immediately justifies the algorithmic threshold in
Equation

3.9
and one notices that this threshold coincides with the ground state energy under fRSB in the Chen and Sen formula (
Lemma

3.7
).

3.3
High-dimensional Gaussian distributions

3.3.1
Moments of Gaussian distributions

We state below two elementary facts about the moments of Gaussian distributions.

Lemma 3.10

(Moments of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
)
.

Given a random variable
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, its moments are given as follows,

\E[X^{m}]=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }m\text{ is odd}\,,\\
\frac{m!}{(m/2)!}\frac{1}{2^{m}}&\text{if }m\text{ is even}\,.\end{cases}

The above lemma can be proved by a straightforward integration-by-parts argument.

Lemma 3.11

(Isserlis’ Lemma)
.

Given
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\,\Sigma_{m})
, the following holds,

\displaystyle\E\left[\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}\right]=\sumop\displaylimits_{p\in P^{2}_{m}}\prodop\displaylimits_{\{i,j\}\in p}\mathsf{Cov}[x_{i},x_{j}]\,,

(3.10)

where
P^{2}_{m}
is the set of partitions of
\{1,\dots,m\}
into pairs of indices.

3.3.2
Hypercontractivity of low-degree polynomials of Gaussians

It is a standard result by now that low-degree polynomial functions of gaussians are “stable” (in the
\ell^{2}
-norm) to small perturbations in the input.
This critically relies on a standard hyper-contractive inequality that applies to smooth functions of gaussians with bounded-degree.

Lemma 3.12

(Hypercontractivity of LDPs of Gaussians,
[
GJW20
, Proposition 3.3]
)
.

Given a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
with
f\in L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mu^{\otimes n})
where
\mu\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
f
is a multi-variate polynomial with
\mathsf{deg}(f)\leqslant d
, the following statement holds,

\ProbOp_{x\sim\mu^{\otimes n}}\left[f(x)^{2}\geqslant t\E_{x\sim\mu^{\otimes n}}\left[f(x)^{2}\right]\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{dt^{1/d}}{3e}\right)\,,

(3.11)

for any
t\geqslant(6e)^{d}
.

The lemma above is used in the SoS certification of the Schatten norms of the Hessian (
\nabla^{2}H
) (
Lemma

6.1
) to obtain strong concentration statements when bounding the so-called “noise” term.

3.4
Real polynomials: linear representations and smooth approximation

3.4.1
Multi-index notation

A
multi-index

\alpha
over
\{0\}\cup[n]
is a tuple
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})
of non-negative integers, representing a multi-set of indices, such that

x^{\alpha}=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}x_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}

for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We have the following notation:

\alpha!=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}!

\alpha!!=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}!!

|\alpha|=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}

\left\lVert\alpha\right\rVert_{\infty}=\max_{i\in[n]}\alpha_{i}

\alpha+\beta=(\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}+\beta_{n})

\alpha+k=(\alpha_{1}+k,\dots,\alpha_{n}+k)\,.

We often treat multi-indices as if they were multi-sets containing those indices and vice-versa.

3.4.2
Polynomials represented as coefficient matrices

In order to leverage the full power of matrix Holder’s inequality on the polynomials that appear in our proofs, we represent those polynomials and the corresponding pseudo-moments as matrices.
Recall that actual moments are a special case of pseudo-moments, so all of the definitions here can be applied using the moments of an actual distribution.

Definition 3.13
.

A matrix
M
is a
matrix representation
of a homogeneous polynomial
p(x)
if there are
a
and
b
such that

p(x)\equiv(x^{\otimes a})^{\mathsf{T}}\,M\,x^{\otimes b}\,.

Given a pseudo-expectation operator
\pE
over a vector-valued variable
x
, a matrix
V
is a
matrix representation
of its degree-
d
pseudo-moments if

\sumop\displaylimits_{\{i_{1},\dots,i_{a},j_{1},\dots,j_{b}\}=S}V_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{a}),(j_{1},\dots,j_{b})}=\pE_{x}\prodop\displaylimits_{s\in S}x_{s}\,,

for all multisets
S
of indices.

These definitions also apply to polynomials and pseudo-moments of more than one variable, albeit with an additional requirement to keep track of which tensor modes belong to which variables.

Definition 3.14
.

A matrix
M
is a
matrix representation
of a homogeneous multivariate polynomial
p(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})

with shape

(A,B)
if

p(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})\equiv\left(\bigotimesop\displaylimits_{x\in A}x\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\,M\,\left(\bigotimesop\displaylimits_{x\in B}x\right)\,,

where
A
and
B
are tuples of the variables
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
so that the number of times a particular variable
u_{i}
shows up in
A
and
B
is equal to the degree of
p
in
u_{i}
.
Note that the tensor products here are taken in order of the tuples.

Definition 3.15
.

Given a pseudo-expectation operator
\pE
over several vector-valued variables
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
and a multiset
S
of those variables, a matrix
V
is a
matrix representation
of its
S
-pseudo-moments
with shape

(A,B)
where
A
and
B
are tuples of the variables
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
if, for all sequences
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}
of multi-indices with
|\gamma_{m}|
equal to the multiplicity of
u_{m}
in
S
,

\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\dots,i_{a})\in\operatorname{ind}(A,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k})\\
(j_{1},\dots,j_{b})\in\operatorname{ind}(B,\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k})\\
\alpha_{m}+\beta_{m}=\gamma_{m}\,\forall m\in[k]\end{subarray}}V_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{a}),(j_{1},\dots,j_{b})}=\pE_{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}}\prodop\displaylimits_{m\in[k]}u_{m}^{\gamma_{m}}\,,

where
a
is the number of elements in
A
,
b
is the number of elements in
B
,
\operatorname{ind}(A,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k})
is the set of all tuples
(i_{1},\dots,i_{a})
so that
i_{k}
is a valid index into the
k
th variable in
A
and
\alpha_{m}
counts the number of occurrences of each index associated with
u_{m}
among
(i_{1},\dots,i_{a})
, and
S
is the multiset of all elements of
A
and
B
with multiplicity.

A
re-representation
of a matrix is another matrix representation of the same polynomial or pseudo-moments.

Definition 3.16
.

A matrix representation
M
of a polynomial or a set of pseudo-moments is
mode-symmetric
if

M_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{a}),(i_{a+1},\dots,i_{a+b})}=M_{(i_{\sigma(1)},\dots,i_{\sigma(a)}),(i_{\sigma(a+1)},\dots,i_{\sigma(a+b)})}

for every permutation
\sigma\in S_{a+b}
.

Definition 3.17
.

A matrix representation
M
of a multivariate polynomial or a set of
S
-pseudo-moments with shape
(A,B)
is
variable-mode-symmetric
if

M_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{a}),(i_{a+1},\dots,i_{a+b})}=M_{(i_{\sigma(1)},\dots,i_{\sigma(a)}),(i_{\sigma(a+1)},\dots,i_{\sigma(a+b)})}

for every permutation
\sigma\in S_{a+b}
that fixes the concatenation
C
of
A
and
B
.
To be more precise,

C:=(A_{1},\dots,A_{a},B_{1},\dots,B_{b})

and the permutation
\sigma
is required to satisfy

(C_{1},\dots,C_{a+b})=(C_{\sigma(1)},\dots,C_{\sigma(a+b)})\,.

We can now specify the pseudo-expectation of a polynomial as a Hilbert-Schmidt inner product between two matrix representations.

Proposition 3.18
.

If
M
is a matrix representation of a multivariate polynomial
p(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})
with shape
(A,B)
and
V
is a matrix representation of the
S
-pseudo-moments of
\pE
also with shape
(A,B)
, and
at least one
of
M
or
V
is variable-mode-symmetric, then

\left\langle M,V\right\rangle=\pE_{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}}p(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})\,.

Definition 3.19
.

A
reshaping
of a matrix
M:(U_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes U_{a})\to(U_{a+1}\otimes\dots\otimes U_{a+b})
is another matrix
M^{\prime}:(V_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes V_{a^{\prime}})\to(V_{a^{\prime}+1}\otimes\dots\otimes V_{a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}})
with
a+b=a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}
so that there is a permutation
\pi:[a+b]\to[a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}]
so that
U_{k}=V_{\pi(k)}
for all
k\in[a+b]
and

M^{\prime}_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{a^{\prime}}),(i_{a^{\prime}+1},\dots,i_{a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}})}=M_{(i_{\pi(1)},\dots,i_{\pi(a)}),(i_{\pi(a+1)},\dots,i_{\pi(a+b)})}\,

for all
i_{1}\in V_{1},\dots,i_{a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}}\in V_{a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}}
.

A matrix representation is
squaremost
if there is no reshaping of it whose row dimension is closer to its column dimension.

Definition 3.20

(Canonical Coefficient Matrices of Polynomials)
.

Given a homogeneous degree-
d
polynomial
p\in\mathbb{R}[x]
in a single vector-valued variable
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
written in the monomial basis as

p(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha,|\alpha|=d}C_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\,,

the following matrix
M_{p}:(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\lfloor d/2\rfloor}\to(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\lceil d/2\rceil}
constitutes its canonical coefficient representation:

\displaystyle M_{p}(u,v):=\frac{|S(u)+S(v)|}{(S(u)+S(v))!}C_{S(u)+S(v)}\,,

(3.12)

where
u\in[n]^{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}
and
v\in[n]^{\lceil d/2\rceil}
and
S(u)
is the multi-index which represents the same collection of indices as the tuple
u
.

This is the unique square-most mode-symmetric matrix representation of
p
.

3.4.3
Gaussian cumulants: symmetric invariant representations and partition lattices

Due to the structure of the coefficient matrix representation of polynomials of finite degree over
\mathbb{R}
, we will invoke projectors into the symmetric subspace (
\vee^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
).
As it turns out, this is also the natural class of subspaces to restrict to when evaluating the eigendecomposition of moment matrices of the multi-variate normal distribution.

Definition 3.21

(Symmetric Subspace
\vee^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
)
.

The symmetric subspace
\vee^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a subspace of
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes d}
which is stabilized under the action of the linear representation of the symmetric group
S_{d}
as,

\vee^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}:=\left\{v\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes d}\,|\,P_{d}(\pi)\cdot v=v\,,\forall\pi\in S_{d}\right\}\,,

(3.13)

where the linear representation
P_{d}(\pi)
of
\pi\in S_{d}
is given by,

P_{d}(\pi)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}\in[n-1]}\left(e_{i_{\pi^{-1}(1)}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{\pi^{-1}(d)}}\right)(e_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{d}})^{\mathsf{T}}\,.

(3.14)

The symmetric projector, as defined below, simply “averages” over all permutations of the multi-index of degrees.

Definition 3.22

(Orthogonal projector
\mathsf{sym}
into
\vee^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
)
.

The projector
\mathsf{sym}:(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes d}\to\vee^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a linear map defined as follows,

\mathsf{sym}:=\frac{1}{|S_{d}|}\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi\in S_{d}}P_{d}(\pi)\,.

(3.15)

Proving orthogonality relies on showing that
\mathsf{sym}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathsf{sym}=\mathsf{sym}
.
A standard argument for this can be found in, for instance,
[
Har13
, Proposition 1]
.
One can use Isserlis’ lemma (
Lemma

3.11
) in conjunction with elementary properties of
Definition

3.22
to observe the following fact about moments of tensor powers of multi-variate random vectors,

Proposition 3.23

(Isserlis’s formula for tensor moments,
[
Har13
, Eq. 14]
)
.

Given a multi-variate normal vector
x\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Id_{n}\right)
, the following holds for every
d\geqslant 2
,

\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Id)}\left[x^{\otimes d}\right]=\frac{1}{n^{d}}\sumop\displaylimits_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{2d}}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{2d})\,,I\in S(M)}\left(e_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{d}}\right)\left(e_{i_{d+1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{2d}}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\,,

(3.16)

where
\mathcal{M}_{2d}
denotes the set of all possible perfect matchings over
2d
vertices,
M
is a perfect matching in
\mathcal{M}_{2d}
, and
S(M)
denotes a choice of indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{2d}\in[n]^{2d}
where
i_{j}=i_{k}
for every
(j,k)\in M
.

The representation above can be used to compute an explicit eigendecomposition of
\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Id_{n})}\left[(xx^{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes d}\right]
.

Extending the above idea further, the actual entropy constraints constrain the cumulants in
any

\delta
n-dimensional subspace to fall off akin to a specific sub-gaussian distribution. This necessitates the introduction of a cumulant tensor.

Definition 3.24

(Cumulant Tensor
\kappa^{d}
of
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
)
.

Given a degree parameter
d>0
and a jointly distributed family of random vectors
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
, the
d
-th cumulant tensor
\kappa^{d}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\in\vee^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
is described as,

\kappa^{d}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})}(|\pi|-1)!(-1)^{|\pi|-1}\prodop\displaylimits_{B\in b(\pi)}\E\left[\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in B}X_{i}\right]\,,

(3.17)

where
\mathcal{P}(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})
denotes the lattice of partitions of the set
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}\}
,
\pi
denotes a particular partition from the lattice,
b(\pi)
denotes the collection of disjoint subsets of
\pi
, and
|\pi|
denotes the size of the partition.

We will be interested in the regime where
d=O_{\varepsilon}(1)
and, therefore, the number of summands for each entry in the degree-
d
cumulant matrix will be
O(d^{d})=O_{\varepsilon}(1)
. The number of entries in the tensor will be
O(n^{d})=n^{O(1/\varepsilon)}=\mathsf{poly}(n)
. We would like the cumulants (of large but constant degree) of the conditional pseudo-distributions in the program to match those of a multi-variate gaussian in
\delta
n-dimensions (scaled appropriately). The cumulants of a multi-variate normal distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\Id_{n})
beyond the second degree are zero.

Fact 3.25

(Cumulants of
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\Id_{n})
)
.

The first and second cumulants of
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\Id_{n})
are,

\displaystyle\kappa^{1}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\Id_{n}))=\mathbf{0}\,,

(3.18)

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\Id_{n}))=\Id_{n}\,,

(3.19)

and the higher cumulants are zero.

Definition 3.26
.

Let
x^{(1)}
, …,
x^{(m)}
be a sequence of vector-valued random variables.
The
m
th
joint cumulant tensor

\kappa_{m}(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(m)})
of these variables is the tensor whose entries are given by

\left(\kappa_{m}(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(m)})\right)_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}}:=\kappa_{m}(x^{(1)}_{i_{1}},\dots,x^{(m)}_{i_{m}})\,.

Suppose that
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
is a sequence of vectors and that
\alpha:[m]\to[k]
is a function so that
x^{(j)}=v_{\alpha(j)}
for all
j\in[m]
.
Then we may also denote

\kappa_{m}(v^{\otimes\alpha}):=\kappa_{m}(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(m)})\,.

3.4.4
Weierstrass approximation and Bernstein polynomials

We briefly introduce the Bernstein polynomial and state a version of the Weierstrass Approximation theorem
[
DB59
]
using the Bernstein polynomials
[
Ber12
]
.
Specifically, we state the precise quantification of the desired degree of the Bernstein polynomial required to approximate any Lipschitz continuous function
f
on some compact interval
[a,b]
.

Definition 3.27

(Lipschitz Continuity)
.

A function
f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
is
C
-Lipschitz continuous if,

\displaystyle\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{|x-y|}\leqslant C\,,\,\forall x,\,y\in[a,b]\,.

(3.20)

Definition 3.28

(Bernstein Polynomials)
.

Given a continuous function
f\in C([a,b],\mathbb{R})
, its
k
-th Bernstein Polynomial
B_{k}
is defined as,

\displaystyle B_{k}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{k}f\left(a+\frac{i(b-a)}{k}\right)\binom{k}{i}\frac{(x-a)^{i}(b-x)^{k-i}}{(b-a)^{k}}\,.

(3.21)

Bernstein polynomials can be used to approximate any
C
-Lipschitz continuous function with maximum error at most
\varepsilon

Theorem 3.29

(Weierstrass approximation Theorem via Bernstein Polynomials)
.

Given any continuous function
f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
that is
C
-lipschitz, it can be approximated with maximum error
\varepsilon
using a Bernstein polynomial
B_{k}(x)
with degree
k=\frac{C(b-a)^{3}}{2\varepsilon^{3}}
.

The ability of a low-degree (univariate) polynomial to approximate the step-function (a continuous version of which we approximate in
Corollary

6.7
) is well established by, for instance,
[
DGJ+10
, Theorem 4.4 and Lemma 4.5]
.
Furthermore, this low-degree polynomial approximation permits a low-degree SoS proof, provided the inputs are over some compact interval
[a,b]
where
a
and
b
are chosen independent of
n

[
BBK+21
, Theorem 3.2]
.
It is permissible to use the Bernstein polynomials as they are the
unique
minimizers of the maximum error, and therefore, the low-degree SoS proof can be made to apply to them.
We require a version of
Theorem

3.29
that is applied to
matrix
(univariate) polynomials with respect to their Frobenius norm, and such a theorem can be proved to hold for matrices in a compact subspace of the underlying manifold of matrices on the Loewner order (
Proposition

A.2
).

3.5
Fourier analysis of Gaussian
L^{2}
-spaces

The design of the cumulant constraints (
Equation

P.3
) in the relaxation critically uses the insight that such a constraint implies an orthogonal (Fourier) decomposition (in the low-degree moments) for a current iterate as a function of the prior iterate. As the cumulant constraints are consistent with the low-degree cumulants of a gaussian, they allow the use of the Hermite polynomials as the Fourier basis. This decomposition, in conjunction with gaussian cumulant combinatorics and matrix reshaping arguments, permits a sum-of-squares proof to control the nuclear norm of the joint moments of the HES distribution. Introduced below are key definitions and properties of Hermite polynomials, including combinatorial formulae for algebraic manipulation of the Hermite polynomials.

3.5.1
Univariate Hermite polynomials

The standard gaussian measure on
(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}))
will be denoted as
dg=e^{-x^{2}/2}dx
leading to a probability space
(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}),dg)
.

Definition 3.30

(Univariate Hermite polynomials)
.

The univariate hermite polynomials are defined in the following equivalent ways:

1.

Appell Sequence

\displaystyle\mathsf{He}_{0}(x)=1\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\mathsf{He}_{d}(x)=d\mathsf{He}_{d-1}(x)\,.

2.

Rodrigues’ formula

\displaystyle\mathsf{He}_{d}(x)e^{-x^{2}/2}=(-1)^{d}\partial^{d}_{x}e^{-x^{2}}\,.

3.

Generating Function

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{d=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{d}}{d!}\mathsf{He}_{d}(x)=e^{ax-a^{2}/2}\,.

Over the set of all measurable functions of
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, the vector space denoted
L^{2}(X)
denotes those that are square-integrable. With the choice of inner-product,

\langle f,g\rangle=\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x)g(x)dg(x)\leqslant_{\text{Cauchy-Schwarz}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{2}\,,

under the gaussian measure, this space becomes a Hilbert space. It is not hard to check that,

\langle\mathsf{He}_{d}(x),\mathsf{He}_{e}(x)\rangle=d!\,\delta_{de}\,,

which implies that Hermites form an orthogonal basis for the set of all functions that are square-integrable. Since the Hermites form an orthogonal basis, any function
f\in L^{2}(dg)
can be expressed as,

f(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{d=0}^{\infty}\frac{f_{d}}{d!}\mathsf{He}_{d}(x)\,,

where,

f_{d}=\langle f,\mathsf{He}_{d}\rangle=\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}[f(x)\mathsf{He}_{d}(x)]=\mathsf{Cov}(f,\mathsf{He}_{d})\,.

Since the vector space is complete, one can always increase the degree of the Hermites till one actually converges to the desired function (in the
L^{2}
-sense),

\lim_{d\to\infty}\left\langle f-\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{d}\frac{f_{k}}{k!}\mathsf{He}_{k},f-\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{d}\frac{f_{k}}{k!}\mathsf{He}_{k}(x)\right\rangle=\lim_{d\to\infty}\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(f(x)-\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{d}\frac{f_{k}}{k!}\mathsf{He}_{k}(x)\right)^{2}dg(x)=0\,.

3.5.2
Multivariate Hermite polynomials

Given a
centered
gaussian process
\mathcal{X}=\{X_{k}\mid 1\leqslant k\leqslant n\}
with some covariance structure, the Hermite basis is given by the multivariate Hermite polynomials. A straightforward generalization of the generating function in the univariate case using the covariance matrix defined as,

\left\{C_{ij}=\E[X_{i}X_{j}]\right\}_{i,j=1}^{n}\,,

suffices to obtain the required definition.

Definition 3.31

(Multivariate Hermite Polynomials
[
Ter21
, Section 4.2]
)
.

The generating function for the multivariate Hermite polynomials of the space
(L^{2}(\mathcal{X}),\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),d\vec{g})
is defined as,

\Psi(\mathcal{X},a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=e^{\langle\vec{a},\mathcal{X}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{X},C\mathcal{X}\rangle}\,,

where we are abusing notation so that
\mathcal{X}
is interpreted as a vector. The multivariate Hermite polynomials of
\mathcal{X}
are then,

\displaystyle\mathsf{He}_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\mathsf{He}_{m}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m})|_{(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m_{1}})\to X_{1},\dots,(Y_{m_{n-1}+1},\dots,Y_{m_{n}})\to X_{n}}

(3.22)

\displaystyle=\partial_{a_{1}}\dots\partial_{a_{m}}\Psi(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{m})\,,

where
m=m_{1}+\dots+m_{n}
and
Y
is a multivariate Gaussian independent of
X
with covariances
\mathsf{Cov}(Y_{i},Y_{j})=\mathsf{Cov}(X_{\alpha(i)},X_{\alpha(j)})
with
\alpha:[m]\to[n]
mapping the indices as shown in
Equation 3.22
.

We may sometimes write
\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(X)
with
\beta
a multi-index over the coordinates of
X
to mean
\mathsf{He}_{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
.
We may also write
\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(X\mid C)
to make explicit the covariance matrix that generates the polynomial.

Listed below are some properties of multivariate Hermite polynomials. As the above mapping trick shows, we need not worry about non-distinct indices as we can do a simple substitution after taking the derivatives. Therefore, we only define the multivariate Hermite polynomials in the regime that the number of variables is equivalent to the degree.

Proposition 3.32

(Properties of multivariate Hermite polynomials
[
Ter21
, Section 4.2]
)
.

The multivariate Hermite polynomials
\{\mathsf{He}_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\}_{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}
satisfy the following:

1.

Appell Sequence

\displaystyle\mathsf{He}_{0}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=1\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{X_{k}}\mathsf{He}_{n}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\mathsf{He}_{n-1}(X_{1},\dots,X_{k-1},X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n})\,.

2.

Recurrence Formula

\displaystyle\mathsf{He}_{0}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=1\,,\mathsf{He}_{1}(X_{i})=X_{i}\,\forall i\in[n]\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{He}_{n}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=X_{n}\mathsf{He}_{n-1}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n-1})-\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n-1}C_{jn}\mathsf{He}_{n-2}(X_{1},\dots,X_{j-1},X_{j+1},\dots,X_{n-1})\,.\

This formula makes it clear that the coefficients of the Hermites are themselves polynomials in the covariance matrix
C
, enabling the use of Hermite polynomials over indeterminate covariances in sum-of-square proofs.

3.

Conditional Expectation

Let
Y
denote a multivariate Gaussian with the same covariance as
X
but independent of it. Then

\displaystyle\mathsf{He}_{n}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\E\left[\prodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}(x_{j}+\iota Y_{j})\right]|_{x_{1}=X_{1},\dots,x_{n}=X_{n}}\,.

4.

Independent Factorization

\displaystyle\text{If }X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\perp X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n}\text{, then,}

\displaystyle\mathsf{He}_{n}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\mathsf{He}_{k}(X_{1},\dots,X_{k})\mathsf{He}_{n-k}(X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n})\,.

5.

Invariance under
S_{n}

If
C=c\,I
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, then

\displaystyle\forall\pi\in S_{n}\,,\,\mathsf{He}_{n}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\mathsf{He}_{n}(X_{\pi(1)},\dots,X_{\pi(n)})\,.

6.

Multilinearity

\displaystyle\forall a,b\in\mathbb{R},\,\mathsf{He}_{n+1}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n},aY+bZ)=a\mathsf{He}_{n+1}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n},Y)+b\mathsf{He}_{n+1}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n},Z)\,.

The proof for the properties is omitted, but can be derived relatively straightforwardly from the generating function definition of the Hermites using basic properties about the derivative operator and some elementary combinatorics.

In the multivariate case with arbitrary covariance structure, the Hermites are no longer necessarily orthogonal to each other under the
L^{2}(C)
inner product
\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{L^{2}(C)}:=\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)}f(x)g(x)
.
However, they are still
weakly orthogonal
, meaning that
\left\langle\mathsf{He}_{\alpha},\mathsf{He}_{\beta}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(C)}=0
if
|\alpha|\neq|\beta|
, and it is still possible to decompose functions in
L^{2}(C)
into what is known as a
generalized polynomial chaos expansion

[
Rah17
]
:

Definition 3.33
.

Let
f\in(L^{2}(C))^{n}
.
Then the
generalized Wiener-Hermite polynomial expansion
is given by
\hat{f}(\alpha)
over the set of multi-indices
\alpha
, so that

f(X)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha}\hat{f}(\alpha)\,\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(X\mid C)\,,

where
\hat{f}(\alpha)
satisfy the linear system of equations: for each
\beta
,

\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\end{subarray}}\hat{f}(\alpha)\E_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(X\mid C)\,\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(X\mid C)=\E_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)}f(X)\,\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(X\mid C)\,.

We then have Bessel’s inequality (Plancherel’s theorem in the limit as the degree of the expansion goes to infinity):

Theorem 3.34

(Bessel’s inequality and Plancherel’s theorem
[
Rah17
, Proof of Theorem 14]
)
.

For every
d\in\mathbb{N}
,

\E_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\{0\}\cup[d]}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\\
|\alpha|=k\end{subarray}}\hat{f}(\alpha)\,\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(X\mid C)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\E_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)}\left\lVert f(X)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

Furthermore, for every
f\in L^{2}(C)
,

\E_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\\
|\alpha|=k\end{subarray}}\hat{f}(\alpha)\,\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(X\mid C)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\E_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)}\left\lVert f(X)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

To observe how to encode these statements (as well as constraints that utilize these polynomials) as valid sum-of-squares statements in the underlying polynomial grammar (
Section

4.2.4
), refer to
Section

E.2
and
Appendix

E
.

Finally, there is an explicit expression for the inner products of the Hermites

Lemma 3.35

(
[
Ter21
, Equation (4.11)]
)
.

Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be multi-indices over
[n]
.
Let
S(\gamma)
for
\gamma
a multi-index be the multi-set which contains each
i\in[n]
with multiplicity
\gamma_{i}
.
Let
L(\gamma)
be an arbitrary ordering of
S(\gamma)
accessed through square brackets so that
L(\gamma)[1],\dots,L(\gamma)[|\gamma|]
is a list of all elements (with multiplicity) of
S(\gamma)
.
If
|\alpha|=|\beta|=\ell
, then

\E_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(X\mid C)\,\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(X\mid C)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi\in S_{\ell}}\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[\ell]}C_{L(\alpha)[i],L(\beta)[\pi(i)]}\,.

If
|\alpha|\neq|\beta|
then the inner product is
0
.

The
cumulants
of a distribution are polynomial transforms of the moments that possess convenient additive properties. For instance, the cumulants of the sum of two independent random variables add, whereas this is not true for the moments (and knowing the moments would typically require doing some complicated Fourier convolution).

3.6
Random walks and the semi-circle law

We are interested in random walks over a complete hypergraph with i.i.d. gaussian weights.
Specifically, we critically use the the set of “Dyck-shaped” closed walks to compute a “signal” term when certifying even trace powers of the Hessian (
\nabla^{2}H
).
As is proven in
Lemma

6.1
, the signal term will be stronger than the corresponding “noise” term, yielding sums-of-squares proofs for both a lower bound and an upper bound for the Schatten norm of
\nabla^{2}H
.

3.6.1
Dyck-shaped walks

We introduce the notions of a Dyck-shaped walk, followed by the notation used to indicate Dyck paths and indices into specific nodes involved in the walk.
We borrow notation from
[
CST22
, Section 4.2]
, and redefine it here for completeness.

Definition 3.36

(
q
-Dyck Paths)
.

The set
\mathcal{T}_{q}
of
q
-Dyck paths is the set of all zero-indexed length-
2q

integer
walks
\tau
that are:

Closed

\tau_{0}=\tau_{2q}=0
.

Non-negative

\tau_{i}\geqslant 0
for all
i\in[2q]
.

Unit Monotone Indices

|\tau_{i}-\tau_{i-1}|=1
for all
i\in[2q]
.

We think of a
q
-Dyck path
\tau=(\tau_{0},\dots,\tau_{2q})
as a set of indices that represent a valid Dyck sequence that can be
instantiated
by a sequence
\omega
of
(q+1)
vertices in
[n]
.

Definition 3.37

(
2q
-walk instantiation of a
q
-Dyck path)
.

Given a
q
-Dyck path
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{q}
, a walk
w\in[n]^{2q}
is an instantiation of
\tau
specified by a zero-indexed vector
\omega\in[n]^{q+1}
if:

Root Vertex

w_{0}=\omega_{0}
.

Ascending Transition

If
\tau_{i}-\tau_{i-1}
is equal to 1, and this is the
j
th time it has been equal to
1
, then
w_{i}=\omega_{j}
.

Descending Transition

If
\tau_{i}-\tau_{i-1}
is equal to
-1
, and
j
is the largest value less than
i
such that
\tau_{i}=\tau_{j}
, then
w_{i}=w_{j}
.

Unique Shape

\omega_{i}\neq\omega_{i-1}
for any
i
.

Fact 3.38
.

The number of
q
-Dyck paths is
|\mathcal{T}_{q}|=C_{q}
, where
C_{q}
is the
q
th Catalan number.
The number of instantiations of a path
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{q}
is
n(n-1)^{q}
.

3.6.2
Catalan numbers and the semi-circle law

It is a standard result in random matrix theory that the limiting spectral distribution of the
\mathsf{GOE}(n)
ensemble is given by the semi-circle law.
Since we give low-degree SoS certificates of the Schatten norms of the hessian
\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)
converging to moments of this distribution, certain facts about the integral representation and asymptotic approximation are abundantly useful.

Definition 3.39

(Catalan Numbers)
.

For any
n>0
, the
n
-th Catalan number is given by the following formula,

C_{n}=\frac{\binom{2n}{n}}{n+1}\,,

(3.23)

which represents the number of ways to have
n
balanced parentheses in a sequence of
2n
spots.

We will use the fact that the
n
-th Catalan number
C_{n}
is the
2n
-th moment of the semi-circle law to give a low-degree matrix SoS certifying closeness of a particular matrix Bernstein polynomial to the appropriate moment of the semi-circle law.

Fact 3.40

(Integral Representation for Catalan Numbers)
.

For any
n>0
,

C_{n}=4^{n}\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1}x^{2n}\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}dx=\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}[x^{2n}]\,,

(3.24)

where
\mu_{sc}=\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}
represents the density function for the semi-circle law.

3.6.3
Truncated moments of the semi-circle law

We require explicit representations for integrals that evaluate various quantities that are related to the
\mathsf{CDF}
of the semi-circle law.

Proposition 3.41

(Integrals related to
\mu_{sc}
)
.

The following (real) integral identities hold (up to constants of integration):

\displaystyle\intop\nolimits\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}dx=\mathsf{CDF}(x)=\frac{x\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}{\pi}+\frac{\arcsin{x}}{\pi}\,,

\displaystyle\intop\nolimits x\cdot\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}dx=-\frac{2(1-x^{2})^{3/2}}{3\pi}\,.

When computing the expected correlation between a Bernstein polynomial that approximates the “ramp function” and the value of an element chosen under the semi-circle measure, it will be crucial to evaluate multiple truncated moments on different compact intervals.
The integrals summarized above are used extensively therein (
Corollary

6.7
).

3.7
Graph matrices and Schatten norm bounds

3.7.1
Graph matrices

Invoking the trace power method on a
random
matrix
M
to bound its Schatten norm
\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{p}
is by now a mainstay in random matrix theory as well as proofs for certifying these norms for moments of certain random matrices
[
AGZ10
,
AMP21
,
RT23
]
. As such, we use the machinery of the “graph matrix” framework
[
AMP21
,
RT23
]
. The definitions below capture the minimal concepts used in the building-block of arbitrary graph matrices. For a comprehensive treatment, see
[
AMP21
, Section 2 & Section 7]
. This framework will prove critical in bounding the “noise” term to be of order
o(
“signal”
)
when certifying the
p
-Schatten norm of the Hessian.

Let
G=(V,E)
denote some (possibly random) graph. We first introduce the notion of an “index” shape which is just a
m
-tuple of distinct variables that can be concretely fixed by a “realization” to be any
m
-tuple of vertices in
V(G)
.

Definition 3.42

(Index Shape)
.

An index shape
U=(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})
is a tuple of
distinct
variables, which induces a set of (unrealized) vertices
V(U)=\{u_{1},\dots,u_{m}\}
.

The notion of an index shape is sufficient to describe a shape
\alpha
, which can be seen as a representation of a subgraph of
G
with a specific edge structure.

Definition 3.43

(Shapes)
.

Given some graph
G=(V,E)
, a shape
\alpha
is represented by a
4
-tuple
(U_{\alpha},V_{\alpha},W_{\alpha},E(\alpha))
where,

•

U_{\alpha}\subset V(G)
is the set of “left” index shapes.

•

V_{\alpha}\subset V(G)
is the set of “right” index shapes.

•

W_{\alpha}\subset V(G)
satisfies
W_{\alpha}\cap(U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha})=\emptyset
, and is termed the set of “middle” index shapes.

•

E(\alpha)
is a set of (hyper)edges that are induced by the index shapes in
U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha}\cup W_{\alpha}
where the vertices are
distinct
.

Given any shape
\alpha
, we can get a “realization” of the shape by associating some fixed set of vertices to every index shape in
\alpha
. A realization of a shape
\alpha
is termed a “ribbon”.

Definition 3.44

(Ribbons)
.

Given some graph
G=([n],E)
, a shape
\alpha=(U_{\alpha},V_{\alpha},W_{\alpha},E(\alpha))
and a realization
\sigma:V(\alpha)=U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha}\cup W_{\alpha}\hookrightarrow[n]
, a ribbon
R=\sigma(\alpha)
is given as,

R=\sigma(\alpha):=(\sigma(U_{\alpha}),\sigma(V_{\alpha}),\sigma(W_{\alpha}),\sigma(E(\alpha)))\,,

(3.25)

where,
\sigma(U_{\alpha})=(\sigma(u_{1}),\dots,\sigma(u_{m}))
and
\sigma(V_{\alpha})
and
\sigma(W_{\alpha})
are defined similarly. The action on the edge set
\sigma(E(\alpha))
is obtained by applying
\sigma
to every
(u,v)\in E(\alpha)
as (
\sigma(u),\sigma(v)
).

A “realization” of the left index shape
U_{\alpha}
and right index shape
V_{\alpha}
indexes into a matrix
M_{\alpha}
(
Definition

3.45
) whose value is then given by a generalized Fourier coefficient that also depends on the middle index shape
W_{\alpha}
. This intuitively implies that a graph matrix for some shape
\alpha
consists of specifying the entries of
M_{\alpha}
at every possible ribbon induced by
\alpha
.

Definition 3.45

(Graph Matrix)
.

Given a graph
G=(V=[n],E)
and a shape
\alpha
, the graph matrix associated
M_{\alpha}
is defined for any entry by indices
A,B\subset[n]
that satisfy
|A|=|U_{\alpha}|
and
|B|=|V_{\alpha}|
as,

\displaystyle M_{\alpha}(A,B):=\sumop\displaylimits_{\sigma,\sigma(U_{\alpha})=A,\sigma(V_{\alpha})=B}\chi_{\sigma(E(\alpha))}\,,

(3.26)

where,

\displaystyle\chi_{\sigma(E(\alpha))}:=\prodop\displaylimits_{e\hskip 1.42262pt\in\hskip 1.42262pt\sigma(E(\alpha))}\chi(e)\,,

(3.27)

and
\chi(e)=1
if
e\in E(G)
and
-1
otherwise.

Note that the coefficients
\chi(e):E(G)\to\{\pm 1\}
. Therefore, for a random graph
G=(V,E)
chosen according to some product measure
\mu^{\otimes\tbinom{|V(G)|}{2}}
, they can be chosen to be the Fourier coefficients of an appropriate Fourier basis.

Definition 3.46

(Generalized Matrix Index Pieces)
.

A matrix index piece
A=((a_{1},\dots,a_{m}),p)
corresponds to the monomial

p_{A}:=\prodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{m}x^{p}_{a_{j}}\,,

(3.28)

where
x_{a_{j}}
is the
a_{j}
-th variable
and
p
is the degree of every element in the monomial.

Once again, for the purposes of this work,
p_{A}
will be a monomial over
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of degree
p\in[d_{H}-2]
, where we implicitly use the convention that
\sigma_{0}^{p}=1
. The monomial itself will be now be associated with a vertex in a hypergraph (along with the indices
\{i,j\}
that represent the action of the derivative operator). Therefore, a matrix index
A
will correspond to a set of
distinct
monomials
\{p_{A_{i}}\}_{i\in[m]}
.

Definition 3.47

(Generalized Matrix Index)
.

A generalized matrix index
A=\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
is a collection of
distinct
matrix index pieces, where distinctness is enforced by requiring that
p_{i}<p_{j}
.

The size of the generalized matrix index
|A|=m
is the number of index pieces it consists of, and the vertex set induced by it is
V(A)=\cup_{i\in[m]}V(A_{i})=\cup_{i\in[m]}\cup_{j\in|A_{i}|}\{(a_{j},i)\}
.
Together with a Fourier coefficient
\chi(e)
defined for every hyper-edge
e\in E
, this yields a natural definition for generalized graph matrices
M_{\alpha}
with the fourier basis for
\chi(e)
being the rescaled hermite polynomials
[
AMP21
, Section 7.2.2.]
.

3.7.2
Minimal weight vertex separators and Schatten norm bounds

Before giving an explicit bound on the Schatten norms of generalized graph matrices
M_{\alpha}
, we need to introduce the notion of a
minimum weight vertex separator
. To do this, we must first associate a weight
w(v)
to every vertex
v\in V(\alpha)
of a shape
\alpha
.

Definition 3.48

(Vertex weight)
.

Given a shape
\alpha
, the weight of a vertex
v\in V(\alpha)
is

1
.

Note that this immediately implies that
w(\sigma_{0})=0
and
w(\sigma)=1
.

Definition 3.49

(Weight of a shape)
.

For any
q>0
and shape
\alpha
, the weight of any subset of vertices
V\subseteq V(\alpha)
is given by,

\displaystyle w(V)=\sumop\displaylimits_{v\in V}w(v)\,.

The above definitions suffice to precisely state the notion of a minimal weight vertex separator
S_{\min}
, which corresponds to a subset of vertices of
V(\alpha)
that separate
U_{\alpha}
and
V_{\alpha}
with minimal weight.

Definition 3.50

(Minimal weight vertex separator)
.

A minimal weight vertex separator
S_{min}
for a shape
\alpha
is a subset
S_{min}\subset V(\alpha)
that separates
U_{\alpha}
and
V_{\alpha}
with the condition that
w(S_{min})\leqslant w(S)
for all other separators
S
.

The bound below is critical in bounding the contribution of the “noise” term in the proof of
Lemma

6.1
in conjunction with
Theorem

5.8
.

Theorem 3.51

(Spectral Norm Bounds on Shapes,
[
AMP21
, Lemma 8.6]
)
.

Given a shape
\alpha
, the minimal weight vertex separator
S_{min}
of
\alpha
, the following holds with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
when
M
is a matrix of independent Gaussian entries and
M_{\alpha}
denotes a generalized graph matrix where each entry is a sum over Fourier coefficients of ribbons induced by realizations
\sigma
:

\left\lVert M_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\leqslant 2m^{m}n^{[w(V(\alpha))-w(S_{min})+w(S_{iso})]/2}\left(6e\left\lceil\frac{w(S_{min})\log n-\log\varepsilon}{6(|V(\alpha)\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha})|+l(\alpha))}\right\rceil\right)^{l(\alpha)+|V(\alpha)\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha})|},

(3.29)

where
l(\alpha)
is the sum of labels of all hyperedges in
\alpha
, which we might also denote as
\deg(\alpha):=l(\alpha)
and
m
denotes the number of vertices that are not in
U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha}
.

4
High-Entropy Step Distributions as Solution Concepts in Sum-of-Squares Programs

4.1
Matrix constraints: Operator norms of the conditional covariance

4.1.1
Sum-of-Squares proofs for matrix polynomials

A HES spectral entropy constraint (
Equation

P.4
) will be of the form
\left\{\left\lVert\fE_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\leqslant c\right\}
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
.
We briefly introduce the framework for matrix polynomial constraints on pseudo-distributions and matrix sum-of-squares proofs, so as to reason about the encoding and consequences of such a constraint.

Matrix sum-of-squares proofs generalize those of scalar sum-of-squares
[
Cim12
]
:
a scalar polynomial constraint
\{f\geqslant 0\}
in the matrix sum-of-squares setting is notational shorthand for an
1\times 1
matrix polynomial constraint
\{[f]\geqslant 0\}
.
Matrix polynomials used in constraints will always be symmetric as matrices.

Square positivity of matrices under pseudodistributions

We write
D\models_{\ell}\{M_{1}(x)\succeq 0,\dots,M_{m}(x)\succeq 0\}
if
\pE_{D}H\left(\bigotimesop\displaylimits_{i\in S}M_{i}\right)H^{\mathsf{T}}\succeq 0
, for every multiset
S
consisting of elements of
[m]
and every matrix polynomial
H
that validly left-multiplies
\bigotimesop\displaylimits_{i\in S}M_{i}
such that
|S|\ell+2\deg(H)\leqslant d
.
The tensor product may be taken in any order since the different orderings are related to each other via conjugation by a permutation matrix.

Matrix sum-of-squares proof

For a
d\times d
matrix
A(x)
with entries that are polynomials in
x
,
we write
\{M_{1}(x)\succeq 0,\ldots,M_{m}(x)\succeq 0\}\vdash_{\ell}\{A\succeq 0\}
if
there exists some
k
and a sequence of matrix polynomials
(P_{S,j}(x))_{S\subseteq[r],j\in[m]}
for multisets
S

such that

A=\sumop\displaylimits_{S\subseteq[m],j\in[k]}P_{S,j}\left(\bigotimesop\displaylimits_{i\in S}M_{i}\right){P_{S,j}}^{\mathsf{T}}\,,

where each of the summands have degree at most
\ell
,
and where the empty tensor product is defined as the one-dimensional identity matrix.
Some special cases are
\vdash_{0}\{\Id\succeq 0\}
,
\{A\succeq 0\}\vdash_{\deg(PAP^{\mathsf{T}})}\{PAP^{\mathsf{T}}\succeq 0\}
for every matrix
P
that validly conjugates
A
, and

\{f\geqslant 0,M\succeq 0\}\vdash_{\deg(fM)}\{fM\succeq 0\}
.
The valid form of a matrix SoS proof is used in
Section

4.2
.

Matrix sum-of-squares proofs satisfy all of the same inference rules as those of polynomial sum-of-squares proofs, except that the multiplication rule is replaced by tensor products:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{A}\vdash_{\ell}\{A\succeq 0\},\qquad\mathcal{A}\vdash_{\ell^{\prime}}\{B\succeq 0\}}{\mathcal{A}\vdash_{\ell+\ell^{\prime}}\{A\otimes B\succeq 0\}}\,.

(tensor product)

This rule follows from the observation that
A=MFM^{\mathsf{T}}
and
B=NGN^{\mathsf{T}}
implies
A\otimes B=(M\otimes N)(F\otimes G)(M\otimes N)^{\mathsf{T}}
.

The same complexity and soundness properties hold for the matrix case as in the scalar case, and furthermore the proofs are of identical substance.

Theorem 4.1

(
\mathsf{poly}(n)
-time SDPs for bounded matrix SoS proofs)
.

There exists a
(n+\lvert\mathcal{A}\rvert)^{O(d)}
-time algorithm that, given any explicitly bounded and satisfiable system
\mathcal{A}
of matrix polynomial constraints in
n
variables, outputs (up to arbitrary accuracy) a degree-
d
pseudo-distribution that satisfies
\mathcal{A}
.

Theorem

4.1
immediately implies that provided the degree of the scalar polynomials in the SoS HES hierarchy is constant and the matrices are polynomial in size, there exists a
\mathsf{poly}(n)
-sized SDP for the feasibility problem, just as in the scalar case.

Lemma 4.2

(Soundness)
.

If
D\models_{\ell}\mathcal{A}
for
D
a pseudo-distribution and
\mathcal{A}
a system of matrix polynomial constraints, and there exists a matrix sum-of-squares proof
\mathcal{A}\vdash_{\ell^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}
, then
D\models_{\ell\cdot\ell^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}
.

4.1.2
Spectral norms of matrix formal variables

We may sometimes write a constraint as
\left\{\|M\|\leqslant\alpha\right\}
, which is understood as equivalent to the pair of constraints
\left\{M\preceq\alpha\Id,-M\preceq\alpha\Id\right\}
. This gives the encoding for the constraint,

\left\lVert\E_{i}[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}]\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\delta n}\,,

in the SoS HES hierarchy, and this is explained further in
Appendix

E
.

4.2
Pseudo-expectations over moments of filtered probability spaces

We formalize filtered probability distributions in SoS-compatible language, laying the groundwork to define an SoS program to optimize over them.

The formal variables of the pseudo-distribution will be the
moments of a filtered distribution
over
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
rather than the variables
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
themselves.
In this way, the program is a relaxation of optimizing a function over some family of distributions.

This allows us to refer to polynomials of moments in a sum-of-squares proof, rather than just polynomials of the variables (e.g.
\pE(\E x)^{2}(\E y)
instead of
\pE x^{2}y
).
In this way, we can impose the constraint that the distribution over a pseudo-distribution variable
v_{1}
is Gaussian without imposing any specific Gaussian (by constraining the pseudo-moments of the cumulants of
v_{1}
).
We impose a hierarchical structure on
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
, with a filtration so that
v_{1}
is sampled first, then
v_{2}
, and so on.
Although we follow the structure of a filtration, the definitions in the following sections will be self-contained and will not require understanding the formalism of filtered probability spaces. Regardless, the definitions of the polynomial grammar provided here are consistent with the rules of conditional expectations over
filtrated

\sigma
-algebras, and this can be easily verified
[
ADPM12
, Chapter 1.1]
.

The raw variables of the SDP are the pseudo-expectations of “monomials” of this system, such as
\pE\E_{1}[v_{1}]
,
\pE\E_{1}[\E_{2}[v_{2}]]
,
\pE\E_{1}[v_{1}^{3}\E_{2}[v_{2}^{2}]]
, and
\pE\E_{1}[v_{1}^{2}(\E_{2}[v_{2}])^{3}(\E_{2}[v_{2}^{4}])^{2}]
.
The default constraints in the SDP are the usual non-negativity of square polynomials and the scaling
\pE 1=1
.

In a later subsection, we will introduce a syntactic extension of this proof system that allows us to reason about “nearly pointwise” properties of the random variables themselves, by reducing those properties to statements about their moments.

We also introduce notation for
conditional constraints
.
Let
p
be any polynomial in
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
and their expectations under
\E_{i}
,
\E_{i+1}
, …,
\E_{k}
.
Then
\{p\geqslant 0\}
means (intuitively) that
p\geqslant 0
for every value of
v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}
in the support of the distribution. More formally (and axiomatically for sum-of-squares), it asserts that

\fE_{1}\dots\fE_{i-1}pq^{2}\geqslant 0\,,

for any
q
which is a polynomial expression in
v_{1}
, …,
v_{i-1}
and/or their moments under
\fE_{1},\dots,\fE_{k}
which falls under the degree bound. This is more formally stated in
Section

4.2.5
.

4.2.1
Properties of the filtered expectation operator

The expectation at time 1 of the expectation at time 2 of some quantity is the same thing as the expectation at time 1 of the same quantity.
Symbolically, this can be expressed as
\fE_{1}\fE_{2}=\fE_{1}
.
When reasoning about the expectation of some expression at a particular level of the filtration, we will sometimes want that expression to include a reference to another expectation at the same or a preceding level of the filtration (for a canonical example of this, see
Proposition

4.11
).
In this case, the preceding-level expectation is actually a constant with respect to the level we are reasoning about.
Concretely in terms of expectation operators, this means that
\fE_{2}\fE_{1}=\fE_{1}
.
Both of these situations can be summed up in general as

\fE_{j}\fE_{i}=\fE_{\min(i,j)}\,.

Each expectation operator is associative with itself in multiplication:

\fE_{j}(p\fE_{j}q)=(\fE_{j}p)(\fE_{j}q)\,.

Finally, the expectation operator
\fE_{i}
commutes with multiplication by any polynomial of
v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}
:

\fE_{i}q(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1})p=q(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1})\fE_{i}p\qquad\forall q\in\mathbb{R}[v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}]\,.

4.2.2
Grammar

The set of allowable expressions is captured by a (redundant) context-free grammar. The language generated is then
truncated
up to a degree bound, and
quotiented
by a set of inequalities that split it into an equivalence class. These expressions can be expressed in a monomial basis, with the equivalences being encoded as inequalities in the SDP.

Definition 4.3

(Context-free grammar)
.

The context-free grammar of allowable expressions is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathsf{cfg}[0]

\displaystyle:=\poly(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})

\displaystyle\mathsf{ecfg}[i]

\displaystyle:=\fE_{1}\mathsf{cfg}[i]\mid\dots\mid\fE_{k}\mathsf{cfg}[i]\mid\mathsf{cfg}[i]

\displaystyle\mathsf{cfg}[i]

\displaystyle:=\poly(\mathsf{ecfg}[i-1])\,.

Here,
\poly(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})
refers to the set of all scalar multi-variate polynomials in the variables
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
, where
v_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for every
i\in[k]
.
The set of expressions
\mathsf{ecfg}[i]
allows you to optionally put any of the
k
expectation operators in front of any expression in
\mathsf{cfg}[i]
.
The set of expressions
\poly(\mathsf{ecfg}[i-1])
is the set of polynomials in any of the expressions in
\mathsf{ecfg}[i-1]
.

The term
\mathsf{cfg}
without a number refers to
\mathsf{cfg}[i]
for some large enough
i
depending on the context (usually the degree of the pseudo-distribution).

We define formally the notion of matrix polynomial used in the proofs.

Definition 4.4

(Matrix polynomial)
.

In compliance with the matrix SoS proof system introduce in
Section

4.1
, a matrix polynomial at level
i\in[k]
is given by a matrix
M_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{m_{1}}\times n^{m_{2}}}
such that,

(M_{i})_{j,k}=p_{j,k}\,,

where
p_{j,k}\in\mathsf{cfg}[i]
.

The set of all valid matrix polynomials for
\mathsf{cfg}[i]
is denoted as
\mathsf{mcfg}[i]
. If a matrix
M_{i}\in\mathsf{mcfg}[i]
has entries that are filtrated expectations of polynomials, such that it can be decomposed into a sum of rank-1 tensors, then such matrices may be equivalently “flattened” into tensors in the obvious way. This fact is often used during the analysis in
Section

5
, and is consistent with an equivalent representation followed under the equivalences implied by
Definition

4.5
.

The matrix polynomials (up to a degree bound) are, as a result, the set of expressions that (up to a degree bound) can be reasoned about in the SoS proof system.

4.2.3
Equivalences

The set of polynomials generated by
\mathsf{cfg}[i]
is to be treated as an equivalence class of polynomials under certain algebraic structure. This algebraic structure is reflected as a mixture of the structure generated by a
filtration
and an
algebra
(over a vector space).

Definition 4.5

(Equivalence classes of the
\mathsf{cfg}
-algebra)
.

Let
i\in[k]
be a level of filtration. Then, the filtered expectation
\fE_{i}
obeys the following equivalences when acting on scalar multi-variate polynomials:

Linearity of expectation

For any
p,q\in\mathsf{cfg}[k]
,
\fE_{i}\left(p+q\right)=\fE_{i}p+\fE_{i}q
.

Constant expectations are idempotent

For any
p\in\mathsf{mcfg}
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
,
\fE_{i}p=p
.

Ring laws for polynomials

For any
p,q,r\in\mathsf{cfg}[k]
, the following is true:

\fE_{i}\left(p(q+r)\right)=\fE_{i}(pq)+\fE_{i}(pr)\,,\,\fE_{i}((p+q)r)=\fE_{i}(pr)+\fE_{i}(qr)\,.

\fE_{i}\left(pq\right)=\fE_{i}(qp)\,.

\fE_{i}[(pq)r]=\fE_{i}[p(qr)]\,.

Towering property of filtrations

Given two filtrated expectations
\fE_{i}
and
\fE_{j}
, the following holds,

\fE_{i}\fE_{j}(p)=\fE_{j}\fE_{i}(p)=\fE_{\min(i,j)}(p)\,,

for any
p\in\mathsf{cfg}[l]
.

Filtrated associativity

Given two
p,q\in\mathsf{cfg}[i]
, the following holds,

\fE_{j}(p\fE_{j}q)=(\fE_{j}p)(\fE_{j}q)\,.

Filtrated commutativity

Given a polynomial
p\in\mathsf{cfg}[i]
and
q\in\mathsf{cfg}[i]
, such that
q\in\mathbb{R}[v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}]
, the following holds,

\fE_{i}q(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1})p=q(v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1})\fE_{i}p\,.

We will also allow vector, matrix, and tensor variables in the grammar, equipping it with a tensor product
\otimes
with the usual properties of a tensor algebra.

It will always be possible to simplify any expression in
\E_{1}\mathsf{cfg}[i]
for any
i
into a sum of “monomials”.
The SoS program will optimize the pseudo-expectations of these monomials.

4.2.4
Monomial basis & truncated filtered-polynomial algebras

Definition 4.6

(Monomial basis (modulo equivalences))
.

Let
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
be vector-valued variables in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

Let

\mathtt{mon}[{k}]:=\{1,v_{k},v_{k}^{\otimes 2},\dots\}\,.

•

For
i\in[k-1]
, let

\mathtt{mon}[{i}]:=\left\{1,v_{i},v_{i}^{\otimes 2},\dots\right\}\otimes\left(\{1\}\cup\left(\fE_{i+1}\mathtt{mon}[{i+1}]\right)\cup\left(\fE_{i+1}\mathtt{mon}[{i+1}]\right)^{\otimes 2}\cup\dots\right)\,,

where the operations
\otimes
,
\fE_{i+1}
, and
(\cdot)^{\otimes 2}
are applied elementwise when operating on sets.
For example,
\{a,b\}\otimes\{x,y\}=\{a\otimes x,a\otimes y,b\otimes x,b\otimes y\}
and
(\fE_{i+1}\{x,y\})^{\otimes 2}=\{(\fE_{i+1}x)^{\otimes 2},(\fE_{i+1}y)^{\otimes 2},(\fE_{i+1}x)\otimes(\fE_{i+1}y),(\fE_{i+1}y)\otimes(\fE_{i+1}x)\}
.

•

Let

\mathtt{mon}[{0}]:=\fE_{1}\mathtt{mon}[{1}]\,.

•

Let
\mathtt{closure}(\mathtt{mon}[{0}])
be the formal real vector space generated by all terms in
\mathtt{mon}[{0}]
.

Definition 4.7
.

The
degree
of an expression satisfies
\deg(\fE_{i+1}p)=\deg p
, and otherwise accumulates as usual with respect to addition, multiplication, and exponentiation.
Let
\mathsf{cfg}[i]^{\leqslant d}
and
\mathtt{mon}[{0}]^{\leqslant d}
be the intersections of
\mathsf{cfg}[i]
and
\mathtt{mon}[{0}]
with the set of expressions of degree at most
d
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Every expression in
\fE_{1}\mathsf{cfg}[i]
is equivalent under the relations in
Section

4.2.3
to some element of
\mathtt{closure}(\mathtt{mon}[{0}])
of the same degree.

This reduction to the monomial basis allows us to define a pseudo-expectation operator.

Definition 4.9

(Pseudo-Expectation of Filtered Expectations)
.

A degree-
d
pseudo-expectation of filtered distributions
\pE
is a linear functional on
\fE_{1}\mathsf{cfg}[d]^{\leqslant d}
,

\pE:\fE_{1}\mathsf{cfg}[d]^{\leqslant d}\to\mathbb{R}\,,

that satisfies the following constraints:

Scaling

:
\pE\fE_{1}1=1
.

Equivalances

:

\pE\fE_{1}p=\pE\fE_{1}q
for all expressions
p
and
q
that are equivalent to each other under the equivalence relations in
Section

4.2.3
.

Non-negativity of squares

:
\pE\fE_{1}p^{2}\geqslant 0
for all
i\in[k]
and
p\in\mathsf{cfg}[d/2]^{\leqslant d/2}
.

These pseudo-expectations can be optimized by semi-definite programs since the non-negativity of squares condition can be stated as the positive semi-definiteness of the block matrix whose blocks are
\pE pq^{\mathsf{T}}
for each
p,q\in\mathtt{mon}[{0}]^{\leqslant d}
up to the degree bound.

4.2.5
Filtered conditional inequalities in SoS proofs

For
p\in\mathsf{cfg}[d]
(or
\mathsf{mcfg}[d]
), we write

\left\{p\succeq 0\right\}

to mean

\fE_{1}qpq^{\mathsf{T}}\succeq 0

and furthermore

\pE\fE_{1}qpq^{\mathsf{T}}\succeq 0

for all expressions
q\in\mathsf{cfg}[d/2]
fitting under the degree bound of the pseudo-expectation.

This can be implemented as a constraint in a SDP that searches for a pseudo-distribution by writing the block matrix with blocks
\pE rps^{\mathsf{T}}
for each
r,s\in\mathtt{mon}[{0}]^{\leqslant d}
up to the degree bound.

Proposition 4.10

(Idempotence of
\E_{i}
for conditional constraints)
.

For every expression
p\in\mathsf{cfg}[k]
and every
i
,

\left\{p\succeq 0\right\}\;\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\;\left\{\fE_{i}p\succeq 0\right\}\,.

Proof.

Note that,

\displaystyle\left\{p\succeq 0\right\}\equiv\fE_{1}pqp^{\mathsf{T}}\succeq 0\,.

Similarly,

\left\{\fE_{i}p\succeq 0\right\}\equiv\fE_{1}\fE_{i}qpq^{\mathsf{T}}\succeq 0\,,

which is, by the towering property of filtrations, equivalent to

\fE_{1}\fE_{i}qpq^{\mathsf{T}}=\fE_{\min(1,i)}qpq^{\mathsf{T}}=\fE_{1}qpq^{\mathsf{T}}\succeq 0\,.

∎

4.2.6
Jensen’s inequality and non-negativity of variance

One of the core proofs that is expressible in this system is the fact that the variance is always nonnegative:

Proposition 4.11
.

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\left\{\fE_{j}v_{j}v_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}-(\fE_{j}v_{j})(\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}\succeq 0\right\}\,.

Proof.

In the proof below, all inequalities are to be interpreted conditionally (as in
Section

4.2.5
) and we omit the
\{\}
notation for brevity.

By
4.13
, we have
(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j})(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}\succeq 0
and by
Proposition

4.10
,
\fE_{j}(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j})(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}\succeq 0
, so

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\preceq\fE_{j}[(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j})(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}]

\displaystyle=\fE_{j}[v_{j}(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}-(\fE_{j}v_{j})(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}]

\displaystyle=\fE_{j}v_{j}(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}-\fE_{j}(\fE_{j}v_{j})(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle=\fE_{j}v_{j}(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}-(\fE_{j}v_{j})(\fE_{j}(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j}))^{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle=\fE_{j}v_{j}(v_{j}-\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}-(\fE_{j}v_{j})(0)^{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle=\fE_{j}[v_{j}v_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}-v_{j}(\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}]

\displaystyle=\fE_{j}v_{j}v_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}-\fE_{j}v_{j}(\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle=\fE_{j}v_{j}v_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}-(\fE_{j}v_{j})(\fE_{j}v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}\,.

∎

Proposition 4.12

(Jensen’s inequality for expectations of squares)
.

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\left\{(\fE_{j}p)^{2}\leqslant\fE_{j}p^{2}\right\}\,.

Proof.

The same proof as for
Proposition

4.11
works, just substituting
p
for
v_{j}
.
∎

4.2.7
Hermite coefficients

We will also introduce SoS variables
\mathscr{W}_{i}[A](\alpha)
for the Wiener-Hermite expansions (
Definition

3.33
) of each atom in
A\in\fE_{i+1}\mathtt{mon}[{i+1}]
with respect to the variable
v_{i}
, so that for all
\beta
up to the degree limit of the program,

\left\{\,\fE_{i}\left(A-\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha:|\alpha|=|\beta|}\mathscr{W}_{i}[A](\alpha)\,\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(v_{i}\mid\fE_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\right)\,\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(v_{i}\mid\fE_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})=0\,\right\}

is a constraint of the SoS program.
Said Wiener-Hermite expansions also have Wiener-Hermite expansions of their own at one level lower of the filtration:
\mathscr{W}_{i-1}[\mathscr{W}_{i}[A](\beta)](\alpha)
, for example.

4.2.8
Sum-of-squares proofs over filtered polynomials in the HES hierarchy

The proof system which uses the monomial basis defined in
Definition

4.6
(up to a certain degree) is a sum-of-squares proof system. This proof system certifies the non-negativity of polynomials in
\mathsf{cfg}[i]
via the familiar notion of a sum-of-squares proof, but where the polynomials may not just be over the variables
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
, but also over moments up to various levels in the filtration.

Fact 4.13

(Sum-of-squares proofs over
\mathsf{cfg}
and
\mathsf{mcfg}
)
.

Let,

\mathcal{A}=\left\{\{p_{i}\geqslant 0\},\{q_{j}=0\}\right\}_{i,j=1}^{m_{1},m_{2}}\,,

with
p_{i}\in\mathsf{cfg}[f_{i}]
,
q_{j}\in\mathsf{cfg}[g_{j}]
for every
i,j\in[m_{1}]\times[m_{2}]
and
f_{i},g_{j}\in[k]^{2}
. A degree-
d
sum-of-squares proof certifying the non-negativity of
h\in\mathsf{cfg}[i]
is given as,

h=\sumop\displaylimits_{i}s^{2}_{i}+\sumop\displaylimits_{j}a_{j}q_{j}+\sumop\displaylimits_{k}b_{k}^{2}p_{k}\,,

where
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{poly}(n)}
have degree
\leqslant d/2
,
\mathsf{deg}(a_{j}q_{j})\leqslant d
and
\mathsf{deg}(b_{k}^{2}p_{k})\leqslant d
.
More generally, for matrix polynomials
h\in\mathsf{mcfg}[i]
, a sum-of-squares proof is given as,

h=\sumop\displaylimits_{i}s_{i}s_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}+\sumop\displaylimits_{j}r_{j}q_{j}+\sumop\displaylimits_{k}t_{k}\bigotimesop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{r}p_{j}t_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\,,

with degree bounds as defined in
Section

4.1
and a semi-algebraic set
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
where
\{p_{j}\succeq 0\}_{j}
and
\{q_{j}=0\}_{j}
.

A critical fact to note is that while the monomial basis technically allows for proofs
pointwise
over the variables
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
, the HES SoS hierarchy introduced in
Equation

P.0
certifies a polynomial that
expects
over the entire filtration. Furthermore, all the constraints in the hierarchy are over
expected
polynomials at various levels of the filtration.

4.2.9
Insufficiency of joint expectation operator and equivalence with replicas

It may be tempting to attempt to simplify the above system and express everything in terms of the joint moments of
v_{1}
, …,
v_{k}
, so that there is only one expectation operator to deal with.

However, doing so discards the power to express what would otherwise be low-degree information: for example,
\E_{1}(v_{1}(\E_{2}v_{2})^{2})
cannot be written as any product of bounded-degree joint moments of
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.

One may see this by introducing a
replica
of
v_{2}
: another variable
u
which has the same joint moments with
v_{1}
as
v_{2}
does.
Then one can in fact write these expectations-of-polynomials-of-expectations as joint expectations in the replicas:
\E_{1}(v_{1}(\E_{2}v_{2})^{2})=\E_{1}\E_{2}\E_{u}v_{1}v_{2}u
.
Then the dependency graph between
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
, and
u
is a triangle, whereas if we expressed a nontrivial product of joint moments of
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
in terms of replicas, the resulting dependency graph would always be disconnected.

4.2.10
Soundness for actual distributions

Suppose that
\hat{v}_{1},\dots,\hat{v}_{k}
are drawn from an actual distribution of high-entropy steps that satisfies

\forall\hat{v}_{1},\dots,\hat{v}_{k}\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{D})\mathrel{.}\hat{p}\succeq 0\,,

where
\hat{p}
is an expression from
\widehat{\mathsf{cfg}}[k]
, which is the same as
\mathsf{cfg}[k]
but with the atoms
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
replaced with
\hat{v}_{1},\dots,\hat{v}_{k}
and
\fE_{1}
,
\fE_{2}
, …,
\fE_{k}
replaced with
\E_{\hat{v}_{1},\dots,\hat{v}_{k}}
,
\E_{\hat{v}_{2},\dots,\hat{v}_{k}}
, …,
\E_{\hat{v}_{k}}
respectively.

Then the pseudo-distribution defined by
\pE\fE_{1}q:=\E_{\hat{v}_{1},\dots,\hat{v}_{k}}\hat{q}
for all
q\in\mathsf{cfg}[k]
satisfies the corresponding pseudo-distribution constraint

\left\{p\succeq 0\right\}\,,

since for all
q\in\mathsf{cfg}[k]

\pE\fE_{1}[qpq^{\mathsf{T}}]=\E_{\hat{v}_{1},\dots,\hat{v}_{k}}\hat{q}\hat{p}\hat{q}^{\mathsf{T}}=\intop\nolimits\hat{q}\hat{p}\hat{q}^{\mathsf{T}}d\mu(\hat{v}_{1},\dots,\hat{v}_{k})\succeq 0\,,

since the integrand is always PSD.

4.2.11
Notational variants

We may sometimes write
\E_{\sigma}
or
\E_{\sigma_{i}}
to mean
\fE_{1}
with
\sigma=\sigma_{i}=(v_{1}+\dots+v_{i})/\sqrt{k}
, with the intended meaning that we average over all past iterates.

We may also write
\E_{v}
or
\E_{v_{i}}
to mean
\fE_{i}
, meaning that we average over only the
i
th iterate, conditioned on all the preceding ones.

Sometimes we write conditional expectation notation
\E[y\mid v_{i}]\,x
to mean
(\fE_{i+1}y)x
.

4.3
Almost-pointwise sum-of-squares proofs

An important concept in this proof system that is used extensively in
Section

5
is the existence of SoS proofs that work only up to some
bounded
degree. This degree depends on the problem being analyzed, and is reflected in the degree of the largest monomial in the grammar
\mathsf{cfg}
[i].

4.3.1
Sub-Weibull algebras

The HES SoS hierarchy creates joint moments for distributions at a particular distance from the sphere that are compliant with those of sub-Weibull distributions. These families of distributions form an algebra
[
Bak23
]
.

Definition 4.14

(Vector-valued sub-Weibull expressions)
.

A vector-valued expression
p(\sigma_{i})
is
(\nu,\theta)
-sub-Weibull with respect to
\sigma_{i}
if

\fE_{1}\left\lVert(p(\sigma_{i}))^{\otimes k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\nu^{k}k^{2\theta k}\,,

for all
k
.

To prove properties about the closure of sub-Weibull algebras requires access to a SoS friendly
functional
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

Proposition 4.15

(SoS functional Cauchy-Schwarz inequality)
.

Given two polynomials
p(\sigma_{i}),\,q(\sigma_{i})\in\mathsf{cfg}[k]
for any
i\in[k]
, the following holds,

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{4\mathsf{deg}(p)\mathsf{deg}(q)}\left(\fE_{1}p(\sigma_{i})q(\sigma_{i})\right)^{2}\leqslant\fE_{1}(p(\sigma_{i})^{2})\fE_{1}(q(\sigma_{i})^{2})\,.

Proof.

The proof follows by using the Hermite decomposition.

\displaystyle\left(\fE_{1}p(\sigma_{i})q(\sigma_{i})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=_{\text{by~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:encoding-hermite-decomps}}}\left(\fE_{1}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha,\beta}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}\mathsf{He}_{\beta}\hat{f}(\alpha)\hat{g}(\beta)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:sos-orthgonality-hermites}}}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}\sumop\displaylimits_{|\alpha|=|\beta|=j}d_{j}\hat{f}(\alpha)\hat{g}(\beta)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{Cauchy-Schwarz}}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}d_{i}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha,\beta,|\alpha|=|\beta|=j}\hat{f}(\alpha)\hat{f}(\beta)\right)\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}d_{j}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},|\alpha^{\prime}|=|\beta^{\prime}|}\hat{g}(\alpha^{\prime})\hat{g}(\beta^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:sos-orthgonality-hermites}}}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha,\beta}\hat{f}(\alpha)\hat{f}(\beta)\fE_{1}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}\mathsf{He}_{\beta}\right)\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\hat{g}(\alpha^{\prime})\hat{g}(\beta^{\prime})\fE_{1}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}^{\prime}\mathsf{He}_{\beta}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\fE_{1}p(\sigma_{i})^{2}\fE_{1}q(\sigma_{i})^{2}\,.

∎

The above proof can be straightforwardly adapted to the case that
p
and
q
are matrix polynomials by using the fact that the tensor product is multiplicative in any norm, along with the use of a
matrix
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (
Lemma

D.1
) as opposed to the scalar one used above.

Stated below is the definition of what it means to give sum-of-squares proofs over the HES hierarchy of the fact that some polynomial in the grammar has sub-Weibull tails (up to some fixed degree).

Definition 4.16

(Sum-of-squares proofs for
(\nu,\theta)
-sub-Weibull-ity)
.

A vector-valued expression
p(\sigma_{i})
is
(d,\nu,\theta,k_{\max})
-provably-sub-Weibull with respect to
\sigma_{i}
if there exists a degree-
d
SoS proof that

\{\text{HES constraints}\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{d}\left\{\fE_{1}\left\lVert(p(\sigma_{i}))^{\otimes k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\nu^{k}k^{2\theta k}\right\}

for all
k\leqslant k_{\max}
and
d\leqslant\deg(\pE)
.

Since sub-Weibull distributions form an algebra, it is possible to parameterize their closure under multiplication and addition. We give SoS proofs of this fact under the HES hierarchy, yielding the new sub-Weibull parameters (and up to what degree) this proof holds.

Lemma 4.17

(SoS proofs of sub-Weibull closure
[
Bak23
]
)
.

Let
p(\sigma_{i})
and
q(\sigma_{i})
be two vector valued expressions that are
(\nu_{1},\theta_{1})
and
(\nu_{2},\theta_{2})
sub-Weibull with respect to
\sigma_{i}
. Then,

\{\text{HES constraints}\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{4k\mathsf{deg(p)}\mathsf{deg}(q)}\fE_{1}\left\lVert\left(p(\sigma_{i})\otimes q(\sigma_{i})\right)^{\otimes k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant(\nu_{1}\nu_{2})^{k}(k^{2(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})k})\,,

and,

\{\text{HES constraints}\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{4k\mathsf{deg(p)}\mathsf{deg}(q)}\fE_{1}\left\lVert\left(p(\sigma_{i})+q(\sigma_{i})\right)^{\otimes k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\max(\nu_{1}^{k},\nu_{2}^{k})k^{\max(2(10+\theta_{1})k,\,2(10+\theta_{2})k)}\,,

for
k\leqslant k_{\max}
and
4k\mathsf{deg}(p)\mathsf{deg(q)}\leqslant\mathsf{deg}(\pE)
.
This implies that the vector valued expressions
\left\lVert\left(p(\sigma_{i})\otimes q(\sigma_{i})\right)^{\otimes k}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}
and
\left\lVert\left(p(\sigma_{i})+q(\sigma_{i})\right)^{\otimes k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
are also sub-Weibull with parameters that only depend on
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
and absolute constants, for any choice of
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

We show both parts separately. The first part follows with small modifications to the proofs given in
[
Bak23
, Lemma 2]
but the second requires a different approach that is SoS compliant. Throughout the proofs, the equivalences mentioned in
Section

4.2.3
are implicitly used.

1.

The proof for the first part follows directly by an application of the functional Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (
Proposition

4.15
).

\displaystyle\left(\fE_{1}\left\lVert\left(p(\sigma_{i})\otimes q(\sigma_{i})\right)^{\otimes k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}=\left(\fE_{1}\left(\left\lVert p(\sigma_{i})\otimes q(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)^{k}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\fE_{1}\left(\left\lVert p(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert q(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)^{k}\right)^{2}=\left(\fE_{1}\left\lVert p(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2k}\left\lVert q(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2k}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:sos-functional-cs}}}\fE_{1}\left\lVert p(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{4k}\fE_{1}\left\lVert q(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert^{4k}_{2}=\left(\fE_{1}\left\lVert p(\sigma_{i})^{\otimes 2k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\left(\fE_{1}\left\lVert q(\sigma_{i})^{\otimes 2k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant((\nu_{1}^{2}2^{4\theta_{1}})(\nu_{2}^{2}2^{4\theta_{2}}))^{k/2}=(\nu_{1}\nu_{2})^{k}(2^{2(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})k})

2.

This part follows by a standard triangle inequality (
Lemma

C.4
) in conjunction with the binomial expansion and functional CS inequality (
Proposition

4.15
).

\displaystyle\left(\fE_{1}\left\lVert\left(p(\sigma_{i})+q(\sigma_{i})\right)^{\otimes k}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}=\left(\fE_{1}\left\lVert p(\sigma_{i})+q(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2k}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:l1-to-lt}}}2^{2k}\left(\fE_{1}\left(\left\lVert p(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert q(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)^{k}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=2^{2k}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}\fE_{1}\left\lVert p(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2j}\left\lVert q(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2(k-j)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:l1-to-lt}}}k2^{2k}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}^{2}\left(\fE_{1}\left\lVert p(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2j}\left\lVert q(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2(k-j)}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:sos-functional-cs}}}k2^{2k}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}^{2}\fE_{1}\left\lVert p(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{4j}\fE_{1}\left\lVert q(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{4(k-j)}\right)

\displaystyle=k2^{2k}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}^{2}\fE_{1}\left\lVert p(\sigma_{i})^{\otimes 2j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\fE_{1}\left\lVert q(\sigma_{i})^{\otimes 2(k-j)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant k2^{2k}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}^{2}(\nu_{1}^{2j}(2j)^{2\theta_{1}2j})(\nu_{2}^{2(k-j)}(2(k-j))^{2\theta_{2}2(k-j)})\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{Assume WLOG $\nu_{1}\geqslant\nu_{2}$}}\sim k2^{2k}\cdot k\cdot\binom{k}{k/2}^{2}\nu_{1}^{2k}k^{2(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})k}\leqslant\nu_{1}^{2k}k^{2(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}+20)k}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{Assume WLOG $\theta_{1}\geqslant\theta_{2}$}}\nu_{1}^{2k}k^{2(2\theta_{1}+20)k}\,,

where it is used that the terms that contribute the most to the binomial sum are in the interval
j\in[k/2\pm O(\sqrt{k})]
. Then, using absolute constants (which can be sufficiently loose without any consequence) and some algebra, the desired bound follows.

Note that both bounds include squaring the polynomial, but by degree reduction (
Section

C.3
) these can be made to hold on the original polynomial.

∎

4.3.2
Near-pointwise filtered SoS proofs for Subweibull algebras

Lemma 4.18

(“Almost pointwise” proof)
.

If a vector-valued expression
p(\sigma_{i})
is
(d,\nu,\theta,d_{\max})
-provably-sub-Weibull with respect to
\sigma_{i}
, then the following conditional constraint is true of it up to a total degree
d_{\max}
of proof:

\left\{\left\lVert p(\sigma_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant d_{\max}^{2\theta}\nu\right\}_{\leqslant d_{\max}}

5
Joint Moments of the High-Entropy Step Constraints

We now give the key SoS proof for high-entropy step distributions: that there exist matrix representations of their moments that have low nuclear norm.
The main theorem of this section is
Theorem

5.8
, with a generalization for graph matrices in
Theorem

5.9
.

The most basic case of this is if a random variable
x
is drawn from the single-step distribution which is simply a Gaussian with variance
\Sigma
.
In this case, Isserlis’s lemma (
Lemma

3.11
) shows that the degree-
2\eta
moment matrix is equal to the sum of all mode-permutations of
\Sigma^{\otimes\eta}
.
If we represent a degree-
2\eta
homogeneous polynomial
p
canonically square-most as a mode-symmetric matrix
M
, then
\pE p=\left\langle M,V\right\rangle
when
V
is any representation of the degree-
2\eta
moments of
x
.
One of these representations is
(2\eta-1)!!\Phi^{\otimes\lfloor\eta/2\rfloor}\Phi^{\otimes\lceil\eta/2\rceil}
, where
\Phi\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}}
is defined by
\Phi_{(i,j)}:=\Sigma_{i,j}
, and this representation has nuclear norm
(2\eta-1)!!\left\lVert\Phi\right\rVert_{2}^{\eta}=(2\eta-1)!!\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{F}^{\eta}
.
The upshot of this is that by applying Holder’s inequality (
Lemma

D.5
) we bound
\pE p\leqslant(2\eta-1)!!\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{F}^{\eta}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}
by the
spectral norm
of
M
.

This analysis generalizes to multiple conditional steps through the use of the
moment-cumulant
formula (
Equation

3.17
), which expresses the joint moments of multiple variables as a sum of tensor products of their cumulants
7
7

7

The authors believe there exists a simpler formulation of the analysis in terms of Stein’s lemma instead of the moment-cumulant formula; we leave elaboration of this to possible future work.
.
The covariance matrix
\Sigma
is an example of such a cumulant.
We bound the 2-norm of each cumulant in its vector reshaping (
Section

5.6
) using concentration/isoperimetric properties exposed via their Hermite decompositions (
Section

5.4
) so as to show that the outer product of tensor products of cumulants has small nuclear norm.
Then we argue that even if we cannot construct a matrix of the correct dimensions as an outer product of tensor products, reshaping it into a matrix of the right dimensions will generally not increase the nuclear norm too much (
Section

5.5
).

Finally, we put it together into an induction over the conditional structure of high-entropy steps (
Section

5.7
).
In this induction, there is a tricky bad case when one attempts to add odd numbers of modes to both sides of the moment matrix: for example, in the single-step Gaussian case if we are considering second-order moments, in order to minimize nuclear norm, we would like to add a factor of
\Phi
instead of its matrix reshaping
\Sigma
.
It is always possible to reshape the maximal number of factors of
\Sigma
into
\Phi
if the matrix desired at the end has an even number of modes on at least one side, but if an
n^{2k+1}\times n^{2k+1}
matrix is desired, at least one factor of
\Sigma
must remain as a
\Sigma
.
This case is responsible for the extra factor of
n^{k/2}
that shows up in the power of
n
in
Theorem

5.8
, but we only have to incur this penalty once because after the first level of induction, we can maintain nuclear norm bounds on two different shapes of moment matrices—one that is square-most and one that is almost square-most—and in each case of the induction, pick whichever one would not force us to add an odd number of modes to both sides in order to get the next desired matrix shape.
The avoidance of this bad case is done in
Lemma

5.13
.

5.1
Matrix representations

We extend the matrix representation definitions in
Section

3.4.2
to delineate different degrees of freedom in reshaping the coefficient matrices and the moment matrices that jointly maintain the inner product between the two matrices as an invariant.

In a bit of an unprincipled but convenient maneuver, we will sometimes allow the matrix representations of pseudo-moments to interchange tensor modes that correspond to different variables.
