In order to preserve the correspondence between pseudo-expectation and the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product when we do this, we will have to add more symmetry restrictions to the matrix representations of the polynomials.

Definition 5.1
.

A matrix representation of a multivariate polynomial is
fully symmetric
if the matrix is mode-symmetric across all modes, including those modes that represent different variables (note that such a representation can only exist if the polynomial is itself symmetric with respect to interchange of its variables).

Definition 5.2
.

Given a pseudo-expectation operator
\pE
over several vector-valued variables
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
and a multiset
S
of those variables, a matrix
V
is a
fully permutable matrix representation
of its
S
-pseudo-moments if, for some
a
and
b
with
a+b=d
, there is a list of shapes
(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,(A_{\ell},B_{\ell})
, each satisfying the requirement that
S
is the multiset of elements of
A_{m^{\prime}}
and
B_{m^{\prime}}
counted with multiplicity, and a list of matrices
V^{(1)}
, …,
V^{(\ell)}
, so that
V=V^{(1)}+\dots+V^{(\ell)}
and for all sequences
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}
of multi-indices with
|\gamma_{m}|
equal to the multiplicity of
u_{m}
in
S
,

\sumop\displaylimits_{m^{\prime}\in[\ell]}\;\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\dots,i_{a})\in\operatorname{ind}(A_{m^{\prime}},\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k})\\
(j_{1},\dots,j_{b})\in\operatorname{ind}(B_{m^{\prime}},\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k})\\
\alpha_{m}+\beta_{m}=\gamma_{m}\,\forall m\in[k]\end{subarray}}V^{(m^{\prime})}_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{a}),(j_{1},\dots,j_{b})}=\pE_{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}}\prodop\displaylimits_{m\in[k]}u_{m}^{\gamma_{m}}\,,

where
a
is the number of elements in each
A_{m^{\prime}}
,
b
is the number of elements in each
B_{m^{\prime}}
and
\operatorname{ind}(A,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k})
is the set of all tuples
(i_{1},\dots,i_{a})
so that
i_{k}
is a valid index into the
k
th variable in
A
and
\alpha_{m}
counts the number of occurrences of each index associated with
u_{m}
among
(i_{1},\dots,i_{a})
.

Proposition 5.3
.

If
M
is a fully symmetric matrix representation of an
S
-homogeneous multivariate polynomial
p(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})
and
V
is a fully permutable matrix representation of the
S
-pseudo-moments of
\pE
, and
M
and
V
have the same dimensions,
then

\left\langle M,V\right\rangle=\pE_{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}}p(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})\,.

5.1.1
For powers of polynomials

Definition 5.4
.

A
c
-stacked permutable
matrix representation of the
S
-pseudo-moments of a pseudo-distribution is a fully permutable matrix representation of the
\underbrace{S\cup\dots\cup S}_{c}
-pseudo-moments of the same pseudo-distribution, with the additional restriction that
a
and
b
are both divisible by
c
and each shape
(A_{m^{\prime}},B_{m^{\prime}})
can be written as
(A_{m^{\prime}},B_{m^{\prime}})=(A_{(m^{\prime},1)}\circ\dots\circ A_{(m^{\prime},c)},B_{(m^{\prime},1)}\circ\dots\circ B_{(m^{\prime},c)})
, where
\circ
denotes tuple concatenation and for every
z\in[c]
, one of
A_{(m^{\prime},z)}
or
B_{(m^{\prime},z)}
contains
a/c
elements and the other contains
b/c
elements.

Proposition 5.5
.

If
M
is a fully symmetric matrix representation of an
\underbrace{S\cup\dots\cup S}_{c}
-homogeneous multivariate polynomial
p(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})
and
V
is a
c
-stacked permutable matrix representation of the
S
-pseudo-moments of
\pE
, and
M^{\otimes c}
and
V
have the same dimensions,
then

\left\langle M^{\otimes c},V\right\rangle=\pE_{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}}p(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})^{c}\,.

5.1.2
For graph matrices

Definition 5.6
.

Let
S_{1}
, …,
S_{c}
be multi-sets of formal variables with
a_{1}
, …,
a_{c}
elements respectively.
Let
S=S_{1}\cup\dots\cup S_{c}
.

An
(a_{1},\dots,a_{c})
-stacked single-flip-permutable
matrix representation of the
S
-pseudo-moments of a pseudo-distribution is a fully permutable matrix representation of its
S
-pseudo-moments,
with the additional restriction that each shape
(A_{m^{\prime}},B_{m^{\prime}})
can be written as
(A_{m^{\prime}},B_{m^{\prime}})=(A_{(m^{\prime},1)}\circ\dots\circ A_{(m^{\prime},c)},B_{(m^{\prime},1)}\circ\dots\circ B_{(m^{\prime},c)})
, where
\circ
denotes tuple concatenation and for every
z\in[c]
,

|A_{(m^{\prime},z)}|\in\{\lceil a_{z}/2+1/2\rceil,\lfloor a_{z}/2\rfloor,\lfloor a_{z}/2-1/2\rfloor\}

and
|B_{(m^{\prime},z)}|=a_{z}-|A_{(m^{\prime},z)}|
and
if
|A_{(m^{\prime},z)}|>a_{z}/2
, then letting
\ell
be the smallest number such that
|A_{(m^{\prime},z+\ell)}|\neq a_{z}/2
, if
\ell
exists we will have
|A_{(m^{\prime},z+\ell)}|<a_{z}/2
, and similarly, if
|A_{(m^{\prime},z)}|<a_{z}/2
, then letting
\ell
be the smallest number such that
|A_{(m^{\prime},z+\ell)}|\neq a_{z}/2
, if
\ell
exists we will have
|A_{(m^{\prime},z+\ell)}|>a_{z}/2
.

Definition 5.7
.

Let
(V,E)
be a hypergraph,
W\subseteq V
be a set of middle vertices, and
(e_{1},\dots,e_{c})
be an ordering of all the hyperedges in
E
.
We suppose that each hyperedge
e_{i}
is a multi-set which contains exactly
a_{i}
vertices (with multiplicity) that are not in
W
.

The set of
single-flipped graph matrix shapes
corresponding to
(V,E)
and
W
and the ordering
(e_{1},\dots,e_{c})
is the set of all graph matrix shapes
\alpha=(U_{\alpha},V_{\alpha},E,W)
with the property that for each hyperedge
e_{i}\in E
we have

|e_{i}\cap U_{\alpha}|\in\{\lceil a_{i}/2+1/2\rceil,\lfloor a_{i}/2\rfloor,\lfloor a_{i}/2-1/2\rfloor\}

and if
|e_{i}\cap U_{\alpha}|>a_{i}/2
, then letting
\ell
be the smallest number such that
|e_{i+\ell}\cap U_{\alpha}|\neq a_{i}/2
, if
\ell
exists we will have
|e_{i+\ell}\cap U_{\alpha}|<a_{i}/2
, and similarly, if
|e_{i}\cap U_{\alpha}|<a_{i}/2
, then letting
\ell
be the smallest number such that
|e_{i+\ell}\cap U_{\alpha}|\neq a_{i}/2
, if
\ell
exists we will have
|e_{i+\ell}\cap U_{\alpha}|>a_{i}/2
.

5.2
Nuclear norm bound on moments of sum of high-entropy steps

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
\sigma=v_{1}+\dots+v_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a sum of unit-norm high-entropy steps, such that each
v_{j}
is a high-entropy step conditioned on
v_{1},\dots,v_{j-1}
, with covariance matrices bounded by
\nu
in operator norm and having degree at most
d
in the previous iterates.
Let
u
be a unit-norm high-entropy step conditioned on
v_{1},\dots,v_{i}
and also with covariance matrix bounded by
\nu
in operator norm and having degree at most
d
in
v_{1},\dots,v_{i}
.

Let
p(\sigma,u)
be a homogeneous polynomial of degree
d_{\sigma}
in
\sigma
and degree
d_{u}
in
u
, with
d_{p}:=d_{\sigma}+d_{u}
.
Let
P
be its fully symmetric matrix representation.
Then

\E_{\sigma,u}p(\sigma,u)^{k}\leqslant i^{kd_{\sigma}}2^{kd(i+1)^{2}/2}(kd_{p}i)^{kd_{p}/2+2ki+2k}\nu^{kd_{p}/2}n^{k\lfloor d_{p}/2+1\rfloor/2}\left\lVert P\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}

by a low-degree sum-of-squares proof.

Proof.

Let
T
be the tensor reshaping of
P
.
First, let’s suppose that
k=1
.
Then, for every
M
a squaremost matrix representation of the moments
\E\sigma^{\otimes d_{\sigma}}\otimes u^{\otimes d_{u}}
,

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma,u}p\left(\sigma,u\right)

\displaystyle=\E\left\langle T,\sigma^{\otimes d_{\sigma}}\otimes u^{\otimes d_{u}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle P,M\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert P\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{1}\,,

where the last inequality was by
Lemma

D.5
.

By a multinomial expansion and
Lemma

5.13
, there exists such a matrix representation
M
satisfying

\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant i^{d_{\sigma}}2^{d(i+1)^{2}/2}d_{p}^{d_{p}/2+2i+2}\nu^{d_{p}/2}n^{\lfloor d_{p}/2\rfloor+1/2}\,.

This completes the argument.

Next, we discuss
k>1
.
First, we apply a multinomial expansion with
\sigma=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in[i-1]}v_{j}
and summing over multi-indices
\alpha
on
i
options, and then applying a weighted version of
Lemma

C.4
:

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma,u}p\left(\sigma,u\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=\E\left\langle T,\sigma^{\otimes d_{\sigma}}\otimes u^{\otimes d_{u}}\right\rangle^{k}

\displaystyle=\E\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{|\alpha|=d_{\sigma}}\binom{d_{\sigma}}{\alpha}\left\langle T,v^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes u^{\otimes d_{u}}\right\rangle\right)^{k}

\displaystyle\leqslant i^{(k-1)d_{\sigma}}\sumop\displaylimits_{|\alpha|=d_{\sigma}}\binom{d_{\sigma}}{\alpha}\E\left\langle T,v^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes u^{\otimes d_{u}}\right\rangle^{k}

\displaystyle=i^{(k-1)d_{\sigma}}\sumop\displaylimits_{|\alpha|=d_{\sigma}}\binom{d_{\sigma}}{\alpha}\left\langle T^{\otimes k},\E(v^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes u^{\otimes d_{u}})^{\otimes k}\right\rangle\,,

where the last equality is by the bilinearity of the tensor product.

By reshaping and then
Lemma

D.5
and
Proposition

5.5
, this quantity is

p\left(\sigma,u\right)^{k}\leqslant 2^{(k-1)d_{\sigma}}\sumop\displaylimits_{|\alpha|=d_{\sigma}}\binom{d_{\sigma}}{\alpha}\left\langle P^{\otimes k},M_{\alpha}\right\rangle\leqslant i^{(k-1)d_{\sigma}}\sumop\displaylimits_{|\alpha|=d_{\sigma}}\binom{d_{\sigma}}{\alpha}\left\lVert P\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}\left\lVert M_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{1}

where
M_{\alpha}
is any squaremost
k
-stacked permutable matrix representation (
Definition

5.4
) of
\E(v^{\otimes\alpha}\otimes u^{\otimes d_{u}})^{\otimes k}
.

By
Lemma

5.14
, there exist these representations
M_{\alpha}
satisfying

\left\lVert M_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant 2^{kdi^{2}/2}(c\eta)^{k\eta/2+2ki}\,\nu^{k/2}\,n^{k\lfloor\eta/2+1\rfloor/2}\,,

so

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma,u}p\left(\sigma,u\right)^{k}

\displaystyle\leqslant i^{(k-1)d_{\sigma}}\sumop\displaylimits_{|\alpha|=d_{\sigma}}\binom{d_{\sigma}}{\alpha}2^{kdi^{2}/2}(k\eta)^{k\eta/2+2ki}\,\nu^{k/2}\,n^{k\lfloor\eta/2+1\rfloor/2}\left\lVert P\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}

\displaystyle=i^{(k-1)d_{\sigma}}2^{kdi^{2}/2}(k\eta)^{k\eta/2+2ki}\,\nu^{k/2}\,n^{k\lfloor\eta/2+1\rfloor/2}\left\lVert P\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}\sumop\displaylimits_{|\alpha|=d_{\sigma}}\binom{d_{\sigma}}{\alpha}

\displaystyle=i^{(k-1)d_{\sigma}}2^{kdi^{2}/2}(k\eta)^{k\eta/2+2ki}\,\nu^{k/2}\,n^{k\lfloor\eta/2+1\rfloor/2}\left\lVert P\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}\cdot i^{d_{\sigma}}\,.

∎

5.2.1
Generalization for graph matrices

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
\sigma=v_{1}+\dots+v_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a sum of high-entropy steps, such that each
v_{j}
is a high-entropy step conditioned on
v_{1},\dots,v_{j-1}
, with covariance matrices bounded by
\nu
in operator norm.

Let
(V,E)
be a hypergraph,
W
be a set of middle vertices, and
(e_{1},\dots,e_{c})
be an ordering of all the hyperedges in
E
.
Let
q(\sigma)
be the polynomial represented by the graph matrix
P_{(U^{\prime},V^{\prime},E,W)}
for any choice of multi-sets
U^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime}
satisfying
U^{\prime}\cup V^{\prime}\cup W=V
, so that consequently,

q(\sigma)=\left\langle P_{(U^{\prime},V^{\prime},E,W)},\sigma^{\otimes|U^{\prime}|}(\sigma^{\otimes|V^{\prime}|})^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rangle\,.

Let
a_{i}
be the number of elements of
e_{i}
that are not in
W
and let
a=a_{1}+\dots+a_{c}
.
Then for constant
k
,

\E q(\sigma)^{k}\leqslant O(1)\nu^{ka}n^{k\lfloor a/2+1\rfloor/2}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in G}\left\lVert P_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}\,,

where
G
is the set of all single-flipped graph matrix shapes corresponding to
(V,E)
and
W
and
(e_{1},\dots,e_{c})
and
P_{\alpha}
is the graph matrix with shape
\alpha
.

Proof.

Assume
k=1
for now.
We have

\E q(\sigma)=\left\langle P,\E\sigma^{\otimes\lfloor a/2\rfloor}\sigma^{\otimes\lceil a/2\rceil}\right\rangle

where
P
is any
n^{\lfloor a/2\rfloor}
-by-
n^{\lceil a/2\rceil}
matrix that represents
q
.
It follows that for any set of matrices
M_{1},\dots,M_{m}
satisfying

\E\sigma^{\otimes\lfloor a/2\rfloor}\sigma^{\otimes\lceil a/2\rceil}=M_{1}+\dots+M_{m}\,,

it is true that

\E q(\sigma)=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in[m]}\left\langle P,M_{j}\right\rangle\,.

It then also follows that for any sequence
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{m}
of mode permutations,

\E q(\sigma)=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in[m]}\left\langle\pi_{j}(P),\pi_{j}(M_{j})\right\rangle\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in[m]}\left\lVert\pi_{j}(P)\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\left\lVert\pi_{j}(M_{j})\right\rVert_{1}\,.

By taking
P=P_{\alpha_{0}}
for some
\alpha_{0}\in G
satisfying
|e_{i}\cap U_{\alpha_{0}}|\in\{\lceil a_{i}/2\rceil,\lfloor a_{i}/2\rfloor\}
, and restricting each
\pi_{j}
to be a permutation that takes
P_{\alpha_{0}}
to some
P_{\alpha}
for some other
\alpha\in G
, the sum
\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\pi_{j}(M_{j})
becomes allowed to be any
(a_{1},\dots,a_{c})
-stacked single-flip permutable representation of the
a
th pseudo-moment of
\sigma
.

By taking a multinomial expansion and then applying
Lemma

5.15
, there exists such a
(a_{1},\dots,a_{c})
-stacked single-flip-permutable representation which satisfies

\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\left\lVert\pi_{j}(M_{j})\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant O(1)\nu^{a}n^{\lfloor a/2+1\rfloor/2}\,.

This completes the argument for
k=1
.
To handle
k>1
, we use the same technique as in the
k>1
part of
Theorem

5.8
, adding a layer of
k
-stacking on top of the
(a_{1},\dots,a_{c})
-stacking so as to apply a version of
Lemma

5.14
that has a layer of
Lemma

5.15
nested inside of it.
∎

5.3
Nuclear norm bounds on joint moments of high-entropy steps

Definition 5.10
.

Given a multiset
S=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
of random variables, its
S
-moments
are the collection of degree-
|S|
joint moments given by the tensor
\E\bigotimesop\displaylimits_{i\in k}x_{i}
.

Definition 5.11
.

The
(r,s)
-moment-nuclear norm
of a multi-set of vector-valued random variables
S=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
(where
k=r+s
) is the smallest nuclear norm of any
\mathbb{R}^{n^{r}\times n^{s}}
matrix representation of the
S
-moments.

More precisely, it is equal to

\left\lVert\E S^{(r,s)}\right\rVert_{1}:=\inf_{M\in\mathcal{M}}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{1}\,,

where
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1}
denotes the matrix nuclear norm and
\mathcal{M}
is the set of matrix representations (
Definition

3.15
) of the
S
-moments with co-domain
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes r}
and domain
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes s}
.

We will also write
\left\lVert\E[S^{(r,s)}\mid y_{1},\dots,y_{k^{\prime}}]\right\rVert_{1}
to denote
\left\lVert\E S^{(r,s)}\right\rVert_{1}
where the variables in
S
are interpreted as conditioned on the values of the variables
y_{1},\dots,y_{k^{\prime}}
.

Definition 5.12
.

A
rank-decomposing
SoS proof that
\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant C
conditioned on
v_{1},\dots,v_{j}
where
M
is a matrix indeterminate is an explicit decomposition
M=\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\E_{j}p_{i}q_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}
where each
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
are vector-valued indeterminates, such that
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\frac{1}{2}\E_{j}(\left\lVert p_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert q_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2})\leqslant C
by a SoS proof.

Lemma 5.13
.

Suppose that
S
is a multiset containing formal variables
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with multiplicities
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{k}
respectively.
Let
\eta=\eta_{1}+\dots+\eta_{k}
.

Suppose further that conditioned on the values of
u_{1},\dots,u_{i-1}
, each
u_{i}
is a Gaussian with covariance matrix
\Sigma_{i}
satisfying
\Sigma_{i}\preceq\nu_{i}\cdot\Id
and furthermore that the covariance matrix of
u_{i}
is a polynomial of degree at most
d
in
u_{1},\dots,u_{i-1}
, and let
\nu=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[k]}\nu_{i}^{\eta_{i}}
.

Then

\left\lVert\E S^{(\lfloor\eta/2\rfloor,\lceil\eta/2\rceil)}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant 2^{dk^{2}/2}\eta^{\eta/2+2k}\,\nu^{1/2}\,n^{\lfloor\eta/2+1\rfloor/2}

by a rank-decomposing sum-of-squares proof.

Proof.

This will be a proof by induction, although the inductive hypothesis will be slightly stronger than the lemma statement.

Let
S_{(j)}
be the multiset containing
u_{j},\dots,u_{k}
with multiplicities
\eta_{j},\dots,\eta_{k}
, let
\eta^{(j)}=\eta_{j}+\dots+\eta_{k}
, and let
\nu^{(j)}=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[k]\setminus[j-1]}\nu_{i}^{\eta_{i}}
.
Furthermore, let
r^{(j)}=\lfloor\eta^{(j)}/2\rfloor
and
s^{(j)}=\lceil\eta^{(j)}/2\rceil
.

The inductive hypothesis is that for every
j+1
, we have, by a rank-decomposing sum-of-squares proof,

\displaystyle\max\left(\left\lVert\E\left[S_{(j+1)}^{(r^{(j+1)},s^{(j+1)})}\mid u_{1},\dots,u_{j}\right]\right\rVert_{1},\left\lVert\E\left[S_{(j+1)}^{(r^{(j+1)}-1,s^{(j+1)}+1)}\mid u_{1},\dots,u_{j}\right]\right\rVert_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leqslant 2^{d(k-j)}\eta^{\eta^{(j+1)}+2(k-j)}(\nu^{(j+1)})^{1/2}n^{\lfloor\eta^{(j+1)}/2+1\rfloor/2}\,,

where we count
\left\lVert\E S_{(j+1)}^{\left(r,s\right)}\right\rVert_{1}
as
0
if
r
or
s
is negative.

In the base case, assuming without loss of generality that
\eta_{k}\neq 0
, then we have
S_{(k)}
is the multiset containing only
u_{k}
with multiplicity
\eta_{k}
.
We can compute the moments
\E\left[u_{k}^{\otimes\eta_{k}}\mid u_{1},\dots,u_{k-1}\right]
directly using Isserlis’s lemma (
Proposition

E.2
).
If
\eta_{k}
is odd, then all the degree-
\eta_{k}
moments are
0
and we are done.
If
\eta_{k}
is even, then Isserlis’s theorem tells us that
\E\left[u_{k}^{\otimes\eta_{k}}\mid u_{1},\dots,u_{k-1}\right]
is a sum over the
(\eta_{k}-1)!!
different mode permutations of
\Sigma_{k}^{\otimes\eta_{k}/2}
.
Letting
\Phi
be the vector reshaping of
\Sigma_{k}
, for the
\left\lVert\E\left[S_{(k)}^{(r^{(k)},s^{(k)})}\mid u_{1},\dots,u_{j}\right]\right\rVert_{1}
case we can choose the matrix representation that represents each summand as

(\Phi\Phi^{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes\lfloor\eta_{k}/4\rfloor}\otimes\Sigma_{k}^{\otimes(\eta_{k}/2-2\lfloor\eta_{k}/4\rfloor)}\,.

The nuclear norm of each summand is then, using
Lemma

D.5
to see that
\Tr\Sigma_{k}\leqslant\nu_{k}n
and
\left\lVert\Sigma_{k}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert\Sigma_{k}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\Tr\Sigma_{k}\leqslant\nu_{k}^{2}n
,

\left\lVert\Sigma_{k}\right\rVert_{F}^{2\lfloor\eta_{k}/4\rfloor}(\Tr\Sigma_{k})^{\eta_{k}/2-2\lfloor\eta_{k}/4\rfloor}\leqslant\nu_{k}^{\eta_{k}/2}n^{\lfloor\eta_{k}/4\rfloor}n^{\eta_{k}/2-2\lfloor\eta_{k}/4\rfloor}\leqslant\nu_{k}^{\eta_{k}/2}n^{\lfloor\eta_{k}/2+1\rfloor/2}\,.

Meanwhile, in the
\left\lVert\E\left[S_{(k)}^{(r^{(k)}-1,s^{(k)}+1)}\mid u_{1},\dots,u_{j}\right]\right\rVert_{1}
case, we can choose the matrix representations as

(\Phi\Phi^{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes\lfloor\eta_{k}/4-1/2\rfloor}\otimes\Sigma_{k}^{\otimes(\eta_{k}/2-1-2\lfloor\eta_{k}/4-1/2\rfloor)}\otimes\Phi\,.

The nuclear norm of each summand is then

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Sigma_{k}\right\rVert_{F}^{2\lfloor\eta_{k}/4-1/2\rfloor+1}(\Tr\Sigma_{k})^{\eta_{k}/2-1-2\lfloor\eta_{k}/4-1/2\rfloor}

\displaystyle\leqslant\nu_{k}^{\eta_{k}/2}n^{\lfloor\eta_{k}/4-1/2\rfloor+1/2}n^{\eta_{k}/2-1-2\lfloor\eta_{k}/4-1/2\rfloor}

\displaystyle\leqslant\nu_{k}^{\eta_{k}/2}n^{\eta_{k}/2-\lfloor\eta_{k}/4-1/2\rfloor-1/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\nu_{k}^{\eta_{k}/2}n^{\lfloor\eta_{k}/2+1\rfloor/2}\,.

In both cases, there are
(\eta_{k}-1)!!\leqslant\eta_{k}^{\eta_{k}/2}
summands and these were both rank-decomposing proofs, so the inductive hypothesis is satisfied in the base case.

In the inductive case, we mainly use
Lemma

5.27
.
We break it into cases depending on the value of
\eta_{j}
.
If
\eta_{j}\equiv 0\pmod{4}
, then in both the
S_{(j)}^{(r^{(j)},s^{(j)})}
and
S_{(j)}^{(r^{(j)}-1,s^{(j)})+1}
cases, we invoke
Lemma

5.27
with
\Delta r=\Delta s=\eta_{j}/2
on the respective cases of the inductive hypothesis.
Then the nuclear norm is at most
2^{d}\eta_{j}^{\eta_{j}/2+2}\nu_{j}^{\eta_{j}/2}n^{\lfloor\eta_{j}/2\rfloor/2}
times as large as the previous step, which since
\eta_{k}\equiv 0\pmod{2}
, is at most as large as the desired ratio

\displaystyle\frac{2^{d(k-j-1)}\eta^{\eta^{(j)}/2+2(k-j-1)}(\nu^{(j)})^{1/2}n^{\lfloor\eta^{(j)}/2+1\rfloor/2}}{2^{d(k-j)}\eta^{\eta^{(j+1)}/2+2(k-j)}(\nu^{(j+1)})^{1/2}n^{\lfloor\eta^{(j+1)}/2+1\rfloor/2}}

\displaystyle=2^{d}\eta^{\eta_{j}/2+2}\nu_{j}^{\eta_{j}/2}\frac{n^{\lfloor(\eta^{(j+1)}+\eta_{j})/2+1\rfloor/2}}{n^{\lfloor\eta^{(j+1)}/2+1\rfloor/2}}

\displaystyle=2^{d}\eta^{\eta_{j}/2+2}\nu_{j}^{\eta_{j}/2}n^{\eta_{j}/4}\,.

If
\eta_{j}\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, the argument works much the same way, except that in the
S_{(j)}^{(r^{(j)},s^{(j)})}
case we invoke
Lemma

5.27
with
\Delta r=\eta_{j}/2+1
and
\Delta s=\eta_{j}/2-1
on the inductive hypothesis for
S_{(j+1)}^{(r^{(j+1)}-1,s^{(j+1)}+1)}
, and in the
S_{(j)}^{(r^{(j)}-1,s^{(j)}+1)}
case, we invoke
Lemma

5.27
with
\Delta r=\eta_{j}/2-1
and
\Delta s=\eta_{j}/2+1
on the inductive hypothesis for
S_{(j+1)}^{(r^{(j+1)},s^{(j+1)})}
.
Then, compared with the
0\pmod{4}
case, we get the same expression for the bound on the new nuclear norm and also for the desired ratio, so the desired bound is still satisfied.

In the case that
\eta_{j}
is odd, if
\eta^{(j)}
is even then in both the
S_{(j)}^{(r^{(j)},s^{(j)})}
and
S_{(j)}^{(r^{(j)}-1,s^{(j)}+1)}
cases, we invoke
Lemma

5.27
with
\Delta r=\lceil\eta_{j}/2\rceil
and
\Delta s=\lfloor\eta_{j}/2\rfloor
on the respective cases of the inductive hypothesis.
If
\eta^{(j)}
is odd then in both the
S_{(j)}^{(r^{(j)},s^{(j)})}
and
S_{(j)}^{(r^{(j)}-1,s^{(j)}+1)}
cases, we invoke
Lemma

5.27
with
\Delta r=\lfloor\eta_{j}/2\rfloor
and
\Delta s=\lceil\eta_{j}/2\rceil
on the respective cases of the inductive hypothesis.
In both cases when
\eta_{j}
is odd, the new nuclear norm is at most
2^{d}\eta_{j}^{\eta_{j}/2+2}\nu_{j}^{\eta_{j}/2}n^{\lfloor\eta_{j}/2\rfloor/2}
times as large as the old one, and the desired bound on the ratio was for it to be at most

\displaystyle\frac{2^{d(k-j-1)}\eta^{\eta^{(j)}/2+2(k-j-1)}(\nu^{(j)})^{1/2}n^{\lfloor\eta^{(j)}/2+1\rfloor/2}}{2^{d(k-j)}\eta^{\eta^{(j+1)}/2+2(k-j)}(\nu^{(j+1)})^{1/2}n^{\lfloor\eta^{(j+1)}/2+1\rfloor/2}}

\displaystyle=2^{d}\eta^{\eta_{j}/2+2}\nu_{j}^{\eta_{j}/2}\frac{n^{\lfloor(\eta^{(j+1)}+\eta_{j})/2+1\rfloor/2}}{n^{\lfloor(\eta^{(j+1)})/2+1\rfloor/2}}

\displaystyle\geqslant 2^{d}\eta_{j}^{\eta_{j}/2+2}\nu_{j}^{\eta_{j}/2}n^{\lfloor\eta_{j}/2\rfloor/2}\,,

remembering that
\lfloor\eta_{j}/2\rfloor=(\eta_{j}-1)/2
since
\eta_{j}
is odd.
And that covers all the cases.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

Suppose that
S
is a multiset containing formal variables
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with multiplicities
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{k}
respectively.
Let
\eta=\eta_{1}+\dots+\eta_{k}
.

Suppose further that conditioned on the values of
u_{1},\dots,u_{i-1}
, each
u_{i}
is a Gaussian with covariance matrix
\Sigma_{i}
satisfying
\Sigma_{i}\preceq\nu_{i}\cdot\Id
and furthermore that the covariance matrix of
u_{i}
is a polynomial of degree at most
d
in
u_{1},\dots,u_{i-1}
, and let
\nu=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[k]}\nu_{i}^{\eta_{i}}
.

Then there exists a
c
-stacked permutable matrix representation (
Definition

5.4
) of
S
with domain
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes c\lfloor\eta/2\rfloor}
and co-domain
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes c\lceil\eta/2\rceil)}
, denoted
(S^{(\lfloor\eta/2\rfloor,\lceil\eta/2\rceil)})^{\mathrm{stack}(c)}
, so that

\left\lVert\E(S^{(\lfloor\eta/2\rfloor,\lceil\eta/2\rceil)})^{\mathrm{stack}(c)}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant 2^{cdk^{2}/2}(c\eta)^{c\eta/2+2ck}\,\nu^{c/2}\,n^{c\lfloor\eta/2+1\rfloor/2}

by a rank-decomposing sum-of-squares proof.

Proof.

This works very similarly to
Lemma

5.13
.
The inductive case will follow the same argument, but with all
c
stacked copies of a mode being grouped together, so that each formal variable in
S
actually represents a vector in
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes c}
rather than
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then each invocation of
Lemma

5.27
also applies to
n^{c}
-dimensional vector-valued variables instead of
n
-dimensional.
This ensures that whenever
Lemma

5.27
reshapes a matrix, it keeps all of the
c
copies of a particular mode on the same side of the matrix.

In the base case, it will no longer be possible to convert all but one copy of
\Sigma
into
\Phi
, since there may be some copies of
\Sigma
that tie together two modes from different blocks.
It may be possible to convert this
\Sigma
into a
\Phi
by moving one of the modes from being a row mode to a being a column mode in the same block, but this will not be possible if we’ve already used up all the row modes or column modes of the block.
In the end, we will get at most
c
copies of
\Sigma
, for a total nuclear norm of
(c\eta_{k}-1)!!\nu^{c\eta_{k}/2}n^{c\lfloor\eta_{k}/2+1\rfloor/2}
.

To formalize this, we may consider the
c
blocks as vertices with degree at most
\eta
in a graph, with
\Sigma
s and
\Phi
s being edges.
Each endpoint of each edge is labelled from the set of labels
\{\mathsf{row},\mathsf{col}\}
, and we are allowed to choose the labels for each endpoint.
Each vertex must be incident to
r^{(k)}
edge endpoints labelled
\mathsf{row}
and
s^{(k)}
endpoints labelled
\mathsf{col}
(or vice-versa) in the first case, or
r^{(k)}-1
and
s^{(k)}+1
(or vice-versa) in the second case.
An edge where both endpoints has the same label is an instance of
\Phi
and one where the two labels are different is an instance of
\Sigma
.
We add a “phantom edge” between pairs of vertices that both have odd degree, increasing the degree of each vertex by at most
1
to make them all even degree.
The graph is now a union of Eulerian graphs, and each component has an Eulerian cycle.

In the
(r^{(k)},s^{(k)})
case, we can follow each cycle, alternating between assigning
(\mathsf{row},\mathsf{row})
labels and
(\mathsf{col},\mathsf{col})
labels to each edge in the cycle.
Since each time the cycle enters a vertex, it must also leave it, this ensures that all but one vertex in the cycle gets an equal number of
\mathsf{row}
labels and
\mathsf{col}
labels.
The only edge that might be forced to have mismatched endpoints is the last edge in our cycle traversal.
There can only be one of these per connected component of the graph, so there are at most
c
of these
\Sigma
edges.

In the
(r^{(k)}-1,s^{(k)}+1)
case, we do the same thing but instead of always alternating between
(\mathsf{row},\mathsf{row})
and
(\mathsf{col},\mathsf{col})
, the first time we enter each vertex, we choose to exit using the same label that we entered it on.
∎

Lemma 5.15
.

Suppose that
S
is a multiset containing formal variables
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with multiplicities
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{k}
respectively.
Let
\eta=\eta_{1}+\dots+\eta_{k}
.

Suppose further that conditioned on the values of
u_{1},\dots,u_{i-1}
, each
u_{i}
is a Gaussian with covariance matrix
\Sigma_{i}
satisfying
\Sigma_{i}\preceq\nu_{i}\cdot\Id
and furthermore that the covariance matrix of
u_{i}
is a polynomial of degree at most
d
in
u_{1},\dots,u_{i-1}
, and let
\nu=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[k]}\nu_{i}^{\eta_{i}}
.

Then there exists a
(a_{1},\dots,a_{c})
-stacked single-flip-permutable matrix representation (
Definition

5.6
) of
S
with domain
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[c]}\lfloor a_{i}/2\rfloor}
and co-domain
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[c]}\lceil a_{i}/2\rceil}
, denoted
(S^{(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[c]}\lfloor a_{i}/2\rfloor,\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[c]}\lceil a_{i}/2\rceil)})^{\mathrm{stack}(a_{1},\dots,a_{c})}
, so that

\left\lVert\E(S^{(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[c]}\lfloor a_{i}/2\rfloor,\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[c]}\lceil a_{i}/2\rceil)})^{\mathrm{stack}(a_{1},\dots,a_{c})}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant O(1)\,\nu^{\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[c]}a_{i}/2}\,n^{\lfloor 1+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[c]}a_{i}/2\rfloor/2}

by a rank-decomposing sum-of-squares proof.

Proof.

The argument will be similar to
Lemma

5.14
.
Again, modes in different block will be grouped together, though this time not every grouping will result in a vector of the same dimension since unlike in
Lemma

5.14
, we can have
a_{1}\neq\dots\neq a_{c}
.

For the base case, we use almost the same construction as in
Lemma

5.14
.
The construction in
Lemma

5.14
results in at most one
\Sigma
per connected component of the graph.
We use the extra degree of freedom in
Definition

5.6
to flip a single mode of each block from a row mode to a column mode or vice-versa to convert those
\Sigma
s into
\Phi
s.

First, let us consider the case when all the entries of
\eta_{k}
are even.

We start by taking the graph from
Lemma

5.14
and labelling the vertices with labels in
[c]
according to the order imposed by
Definition

5.6
.
Let
I
be the set of vertices whose labels are the smallest among all the other vertices in the same connected component.

Note that we are able to convert one
\Sigma
into a
\Phi
at the cost of converting an adjacent
\Phi
into a
\Sigma
by swapping the
\mathsf{row}
/
\mathsf{col}
labels of those two edges at the two edge endpoints where they are adjacent.
This procedure alternates between creating a
\mathsf{row}\to\mathsf{col}
instance of
\Sigma
and a
\mathsf{col}\to\mathsf{row}
instance if you do it iteratively along a path.

For each connected component, using this procedure iteratively along the Eulerian cycle, we continue until we create a
\Sigma
that is incident to the vertex in
I
.
Note that the construction in
Lemma

5.14
only creates a
\Sigma
in a connected component that has an odd-length Eulerian cycle.
So if the
\Sigma
that we got was incident to the vertex in
I
via an endpoint labelled
\mathsf{col}
when we wanted one labelled
\mathsf{row}
for instance, we can instead create a
\Sigma
that is incident to
I
via a
\mathsf{row}
endpoint by iterating the same procedure though the entire Euclidean cycle again.
Then when it arrives back at the vertex in
I
, since the cycle was odd-length, by the alternating property of the procedure, it will now have the opposite label at the endpoint incident to
I
.

Therefore, we can configure the graph to have the
\Sigma
s all be incident to
I
and alternate so that, if
i_{j}
is the vertex with the
j
th smallest label in
I
, the
\Sigma
that is incident to
i_{j}
is incident via a
\mathsf{col}
endpoint if
j
is odd and a
\mathsf{row}
endpoint if
j
is even.
Up to this point, we have only permuted modes within blocks, which is always allowed by
Definition

5.6
.

Finally, we flip the endpoints of the
\Sigma
s incident to the vertices in
I
from being
\mathsf{col}
modes to
\mathsf{row}
modes or vice-versa, except for the vertex in
I
with the largest label if
|I|
is odd.
This turns all but one instance of
\Sigma
into a
\Phi
, and because it makes each block imbalanced by at most one mode and it alternates the direction of the imbalance, it satisfies the constraints of
Definition

5.6
.

Since at most one instance of
\Sigma
is left after this, this gives a nuclear norm of at most
(\left\lVert\eta_{k}\right\rVert_{1}-1)!!\nu_{k}^{\left\lVert\eta_{k}\right\rVert_{1}/2}n_{\vphantom{k}}^{\lfloor\left\lVert\eta_{k}\right\rVert_{1}/2+1\rfloor/2}
in the base case.

When some of the entries of
\eta_{k}
are odd, we use the same label-swapping procedure along the Eulerian cycles that contain the odd-degree vertices until the
\Sigma
has been moved to one of the “phantom edges” from the proof of
Lemma

5.14
, at which point the
\Sigma
disappears from the actual moment matrix we construct.

∎

5.4
Properties of Hermite polynomials

We make heavy use of the existence of weakly orthogonal Hermite polynomials, whose orthogonality is implied by the constraints that make the low-degree moments/cumulants of the high-entropy step consistent with those of a (normalized) Gaussian (
Equation

P.3
).

Definition 5.16
.

A
diagram
over a sequence of multi-indices
\alpha^{(1)},\dots,\alpha^{(k)}
is a graph where the
k
vertices correspond to the
k
multi-indices, where each edge between vertices
\alpha^{(i)}
and
\alpha^{(j)}
is labelled with
(a,b)
where
a
is an index in
\alpha^{(i)}
and
b
is an index in
\alpha^{(j)}
, and where each element of each multi-index is used exactly once in an edge label.

The diagram is
closed
if the graph is connected.

The diagram is
without loop
if there are no self-loops in the edge set.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
\kappa_{k}
denote the
k
th cumulant with respect to the the vector-valued random variable
y
.
Suppose that
\alpha
is a multi-index with
|\alpha|>0
.
Let
\beta
be the multi-index with indices given by the multi-set
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}
.
Then

\kappa_{k+1}(y_{i_{1}},\dots,y_{i_{k}},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y\mid\Sigma))\;=\;\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(y\mid\Sigma),\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y\mid\Sigma))\;=\;\E_{y}\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(y\mid\Sigma)\,\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y\mid\Sigma)\,.

These quantities are all equal to zero if
|\alpha|\neq k
.

In T-cumulant notation
[
Ter21
]
,

\kappa_{k+1}(y,\dots,y,\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y\mid\Sigma))\;=\;\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{k}(y\mid\Sigma),\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y\mid\Sigma))\,,

where
\mathsf{He}_{k}(y\mid\Sigma)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in[n]}\mathsf{He}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}(y\mid\Sigma)e_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{k}}
.

By multilinearity of the joint cumulant and a Wiener-Hermite decomposition,

\kappa_{k+1}(y,\dots,y,p(y))\;=\;\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{k}(y\mid\Sigma),p(y))

for any vector-valued polynomial
p
.

Proof.

By
[
Ter21
, Proposition 4.3]
,

\kappa_{k+1}(y_{i_{1}},\dots,y_{i_{k}},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y\mid\Sigma))=\sumop\displaylimits_{(V,E)\in\mathcal{G}}\prodop\displaylimits_{(u,v,(i,j))\in E}\Sigma_{i,j}\,,

where
\mathcal{G}
is the set of closed diagrams without loop over
\{i_{1}\}
, …,
\{i_{k}\}
, and
\alpha
.

By
[
Ter21
, Proposition 4.3]
,

\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(y\mid\Sigma),\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y\mid\Sigma))=\sumop\displaylimits_{(V,E)\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\prodop\displaylimits_{(u,v,(i,j))\in E}\Sigma_{i,j}\,,

where
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is the set of closed diagrams without loop over
\beta
and
\alpha
.

Since every diagram in
\mathcal{G}
is closed and each of the vertices corresponding to the singleton sets
\{i_{1}\}
, …,
\{i_{k}\}
must have degree 1, they must all have exactly one edge with the vertex corresponding to
\alpha
.
Therefore, there is exactly one diagram for each way to pair the elements of
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}
with the elements of
\alpha
(treating duplicate elements as distinct, so that there are
k!
diagrams), and the summand corresponding to each diagram is the product of
\Sigma_{i,j}
when
i\in\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}
is paired with
j\in\alpha
.

Since
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is the set of diagrams without loop, every
i\in\beta
must be paired with some unique
j\in\alpha
.
So again, there is one diagram for every way to pair the elements of
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}
with the elements of
\alpha
, with the same value of summands.

Since these two cumulants evaluate to the same sum over the same summands, they must be equal.
In both cases, the result is non-zero only if
|\alpha|=k
.

Finally,
\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(y\mid\Sigma),\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y\mid\Sigma))=\E_{y}\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(y\mid\Sigma)\,\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y\mid\Sigma)
because every Hermite of positive degree is zero-mean.
∎

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
\kappa
denote cumulants with respect to the the vector-valued random variable
y
.
Suppose that
\alpha
is a multi-index with
|\alpha|>0
.
Let
\gamma
be the multi-index with indices given by the multi-set
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}
.
Then

\kappa_{k+1}(y_{i_{1}},\dots,y_{i_{k}},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y\mid\Sigma),\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(y\mid\Sigma))\;=\;\kappa_{3}(\mathsf{He}_{\gamma}(y\mid\Sigma),\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y\mid\Sigma),\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(y\mid\Sigma))\,.

These quantities are all equal to zero if
|\alpha|+|\beta|+k
is not even.

Proof.

The argument is the same as in
Lemma

5.17
, just with one more vertex in the diagram.
∎

Lemma 5.19
.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Gaussian vector
y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
with
\Sigma\preceq\nu\cdot\Id_{n}
.
Let
\gamma
,
\alpha
, and
\beta
be multi-indices over
[n]
.
Then

\displaystyle\kappa_{3}(\mathsf{He}_{\gamma}(y\mid\Sigma),\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y\mid\Sigma),\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(y\mid\Sigma))=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\\
\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\text{partition of }\gamma\!\!\!\end{subarray}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime}\leqslant\alpha}\sumop\displaylimits_{\beta^{\prime}\leqslant\beta}\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{\gamma_{1}},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime}})\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{\gamma_{2}},\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime}})\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}},\mathsf{He}_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}})\,,

where we have elided some repetitive notation on the right.

Proof.

By
[
Ter21
, Proposition 4.3]
,

\kappa_{3}(\mathsf{He}_{\gamma},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha},\mathsf{He}_{\beta})=\sumop\displaylimits_{(V,E)\in\mathcal{G}}\prodop\displaylimits_{(u,v,(i,j))\in E}\Sigma_{i,j}\,,

where
\mathcal{G}
is the set of closed diagrams without loop over
\gamma
,
\alpha
, and
\beta
.
This set of diagrams can be partitioned by which of the edges coming out of
\gamma
go to
\alpha
and which go to
\beta
: that gives us the sum over partitions
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
of
\gamma
.
It can be further partitioned by which set of indices in
\alpha
are linked to
\gamma_{1}
and which set of indices in
\beta
are linked to
\gamma_{2}
: this gives us the sum over
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}
respectively.

Finally, once we have determined these values of
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
,
\alpha^{\prime}
, and
\beta^{\prime}
, to enumerate all the diagrams consistent with these choices, it suffices to enumerate all the ways that the elements of
\gamma_{1}
can be paired with the indices in
\alpha^{\prime}
and then take the product of
\Sigma_{i,j}
for each corresponding pair of
i\in\gamma_{1}
and
j\in\alpha^{\prime}
.
But this is the same as enumerating all closed diagrams without loop between
\gamma_{1}
and
\alpha^{\prime}
, hence this factor is equal to
\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{\gamma_{1}},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime}})
.
The same applies to the other two pairings, hence the final formula.
∎

Lemma 5.20
.

Fix a
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
PSD and
k\geqslant 0
.

Consider the vector space spanned by
\{\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}:|\alpha|=k\}
where
\alpha
ranges over multi-indices in
[n]
, whose dual vectors are spanned by
\{\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}^{\mathsf{T}}:|\alpha|=k\}
, and equipped with the
L^{2}(\Sigma)
inner product, the linear extension of

\left\langle\mathsf{He}_{\alpha},\mathsf{He}_{\beta}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}:=\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(x\mid{\Sigma})\,\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(x\mid{\Sigma})\,.

Let
\mathbf{H}^{(k)}
be the linear operator given by

\mathbf{H}^{(k)}=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in[n]^{k}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{He}_{I}\mathsf{He}_{I}^{\mathsf{T}}\,,

where
\mathsf{He}_{I}:=\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}
when
I
is a tuple of indices and
\alpha
is the multi-index constituted of the same indices.
Then

\left\lVert\mathbf{H}^{(k)}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\leqslant k!\,\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}\,.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{G}^{(k)}
be the linear operator given entrywise by

\mathbf{G}^{(k)}_{I,J}:=\left\langle\mathsf{He}_{I},\mathsf{He}_{J}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\,.

By weak orthonormality (
Section

3.5.2
),

\left\lVert\mathbf{H}^{(k)}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{G}^{(k)}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\,,

so we can just bound the latter.

By
Lemma

3.35
, we have

\mathbf{G}^{(k)}_{I,J}=\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi\in S_{k}}\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[k]}\Sigma_{I_{i},J_{\pi(i)}}\,.

We can decompose this matrix further as
\mathbf{G}^{(k)}=\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi\in S_{k}}\mathbf{G}^{(k,\pi)}
where

\mathbf{G}^{(k,\pi)}_{I,J}=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[k]}\Sigma_{I_{i},J_{\pi(i)}}\,.

Now we can invert the permutation
\pi
to get

\mathbf{G}^{(k,\pi)}_{I,\pi^{-1}J}=\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[k]}\Sigma_{I_{i},J_{i}}\,,

where
\pi^{-1}(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})=(j_{\pi^{-1}(1)},\dots,j_{\pi^{-1}(k)})
, and see therefore that

\mathbf{G}^{(k,\pi)}P_{\pi^{-1}}=\Sigma^{\otimes k}\,,

where
P_{\pi^{-1}}e_{J}=e_{\pi^{-1}J}
is a mode permutation matrix.
Since
P_{\pi^{-1}}
is an orthogonal matrix, we have

\left\lVert\mathbf{G}^{(k,\pi)}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}=\left\lVert\mathbf{G}^{(k,\pi)}P_{\pi^{-1}}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}=\left\lVert\Sigma^{\otimes k}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}=\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}\,.

And since
\mathbf{G}^{(k)}
is the sum of
k!
operators all whose operator norms are equal to
\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}
, its own operator norm is no more than
k!\,\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}
.
∎

Corollary 5.21
.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Gaussian vector
y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
with a SoS proof that
\Sigma\preceq\nu\cdot\Id_{n}
and let
p(y)
be a vector-valued polynomial.
Then

\sumop\displaylimits_{I\in[n]^{k}}\left\lVert\E_{y}\mathsf{He}_{I}(y)p(y)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant k!\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}\E_{y}\left\lVert p(y)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

by a SoS proof. As an immediate consequence,

\left\lVert\kappa_{2}\left(\mathsf{He}_{k}(y),p(y)\right)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant k!\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}\E_{y}\left\lVert p(y)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,,

where
\mathsf{He}_{k}(y)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in[n]}\mathsf{He}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}(y)e_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{k}}
and
\kappa_{2}(p,q)
is the second tensor cumulant (or T-cumulant
[
Ter21
]
; equivalently, the covariance) between vector-valued polynomials
p
and
q
with respect to the randomness of
y
.

Proof.

Apply
Lemma

5.20
, writing
\left\lVert\kappa_{2}(p(y),\mathsf{He}_{I}(y))\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
as

\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\kappa_{2}(p_{j}(y),\mathsf{He}_{I}(y))^{2}=\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\left\langle p_{j},\mathsf{He}_{I}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\leqslant k!\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{k}\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\left\lVert p_{j}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\,,

where
j
is summed over all indices of
p
as a vector and we applied
Lemma

D.2
as a SoS proof.
∎

Lemma 5.22
.

Consider the same vector space as in
Lemma

5.20
.

Let

\mathbf{G}^{(k)}_{\alpha,\beta}:=\left\langle\mathsf{He}_{\alpha},\mathsf{He}_{\beta}\right\rangle_{\ell^{2}(\Sigma)}

be the Gram matrix indexed by
\alpha
and
\beta
both multi-indices satisfying
|\alpha|=|\beta|=k
.

Let also

\mathbf{G}^{(k,\ell)}_{(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}),(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}:=\left\langle\mathsf{He}_{\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}},\mathsf{He}_{\beta+\beta^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{\ell^{2}(\Sigma)}

when
|\alpha|=|\beta|=k
and
|\alpha^{\prime}|=|\beta^{\prime}|=\ell
.

Let
\mathrm{sym}^{(k,\ell)}
be the symmetric matrix operating on the space whose coordinates are indexed by two multi-indices
(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
satisfying
|\alpha|=k
and
|\alpha^{\prime}|=\ell
, such that
\mathrm{sym}^{(k,\ell)}
symmetrizes between all pairs of lists whose concantenations contain the same multi-set of elements.
In other words,

\mathrm{sym}^{(k,\ell)}e_{\alpha}\otimes e_{\alpha^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{|\{(\beta,\beta^{\prime}):\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}=\beta+\beta^{\prime},|\beta|=k,|\beta^{\prime}|=\ell\}|}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\beta|=k,|\beta^{\prime}|=\ell\\
\beta+\beta^{\prime}=\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}\end{subarray}}e_{\beta}\otimes e_{\beta^{\prime}}\,,

Then

\mathrm{sym}^{(k,\ell)}\left(\mathbf{G}^{(k)}\otimes\mathbf{G}^{(\ell)}\right)\mathrm{sym}^{(k,\ell)}=\binom{k+\ell}{k}^{-1}\mathbf{G}^{(k,\ell)}\,.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.35
, we have

\mathbf{G}^{(k)}_{\alpha,\beta}=\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi\in S_{k}}\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[k]}\Sigma_{\alpha[i],\beta[\pi(i)]}\,,

where
\alpha[\cdot]
and
\beta[\cdot]
index into some arbitrary fixed ordering of the indices.

So

\left(\mathbf{G}^{(k)}\otimes\mathbf{G}^{(\ell)}\right)_{(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}),(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}=\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi\in S_{k}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\sigma\in S_{\ell}}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[k]}\Sigma_{\alpha[i],\beta[\pi(i)]}\right)\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[\ell]}\Sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}[i],\beta^{\prime}[\sigma(i)]}\right)\,,

And

\mathbf{G}^{(k,\ell)}_{(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}),(\beta,\beta^{\prime})}=\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi\in S_{k+\ell}}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in[k+\ell]}\Sigma_{(\alpha+\alpha^{\prime})[i],(\beta+\beta^{\prime})[\pi(i)]}\right)\,.

Symmetrizing the former expression leads to
\binom{k+\ell}{k}^{-1}
times the latter expression.
∎

Lemma 5.23
.

Let
p
be a degree-
d
vector-valued polynomial with Wiener-Hermite coefficients
\hat{p}(\alpha)
.
Let
B
be the matrix whose
\alpha
th column is
\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}
defined as a vector as in the previous lemmata and let
P
be the matrix whose
\alpha
th column is
\hat{p}(\alpha)
when
\alpha
is a multi-index.
Let
P^{\prime}
be the matrix whose
(\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime})
th column is
\hat{p}(\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime})
when both
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime\prime}
are multi-indices and neither are empty.
Then

\left\lVert(B\otimes B){P^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant(2^{d}-1)\,\left\lVert BP^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

Proof.

Let
B^{(k)}
and
P^{(k)}
be defined the same way as
B
and
P
except the multi-index
\alpha
must satisfy
|\alpha|=k
.
Similarly,
P^{(k,\ell)}
is defined the same way as
P^{\prime}
, except that
|\alpha^{\prime}|=k
and
|\alpha^{\prime\prime}|=\ell
.

Then

(B\otimes B){P^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{k,\ell\geqslant 1}(B^{(k)}\otimes B^{(\ell)}){P^{(k,\ell)}}^{\mathsf{T}}\,,

where
\oplus
here denotes a matrix concatenation.
By the weak orthogonality of the Hermites and the definition of the inner product involved in
B
(
Lemma

5.20
),

\left\lVert(B\otimes B){P^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\sumop\displaylimits_{k,\ell\geqslant 1}\left\lVert(B^{(k)}\otimes B^{(\ell)}){P^{(k,\ell)}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

By
Lemma

5.22
and the fact that
P^{(k,\ell)}_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}}
depends only on the sum
\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime}
,

\left\lVert(B\otimes B){P^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\sumop\displaylimits_{k,\ell\geqslant 1}\binom{k+\ell}{k}^{-1}\left\lVert B^{(k,\ell)}{P^{(k,\ell)}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,,

where
B^{(k,\ell)}
is the matrix whose
(\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime})
th column is
\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime}}
when
|\alpha^{\prime}|=k
and
|\alpha^{\prime\prime}|=\ell
.
Reparameterizing,

\left\lVert(B\otimes B){P^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\geqslant 1}\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell=1}^{k}\binom{k}{\ell}^{-1}\left\lVert B^{(\ell,k-\ell)}{P^{(\ell,k-\ell)}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

Note that
B^{(\ell,k-\ell)}
and
P^{(\ell,k-\ell)}
contain the same columns as
B^{(k)}
and
P^{(k)}
, but the former may contain repeats of the same column up to
\binom{k}{\ell}
times.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\lVert(B\otimes B){P^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{k\geqslant 1}\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell=1}^{k}\binom{k}{\ell}^{-1}\binom{k}{\ell}^{2}\left\lVert B^{(k)}{P^{(k)}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\geqslant 1}\left\lVert B^{(k)}{P^{(k)}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell=1}^{k}\binom{k}{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{k\geqslant 1}(2^{k}-1)\left\lVert B^{(k)}{P^{(k)}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

By
1-\infty
Holder’s inequality and the weak orthogonality of the Hermite polynomials,

\left\lVert(B\otimes B){P^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant(2^{d}-1)\,\left\lVert BP^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

∎

5.5
Matrix reshaping tools

Lemma 5.24
.

Suppose
v\in\mathbb{R}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{\otimes m}
and
C>0
is a constant.
Then there exists a set
I
and a decomposition

v=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}w_{i}\otimes z_{i}

such that
w_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and

\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}\left\lVert w_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=C\sqrt{n}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}\left\lVert z_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\frac{\sqrt{n}}{C}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

In particular,

\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}\frac{1}{2}(\left\lVert w_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert z_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2})=\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2C}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

Therefore if
\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant C^{2}
by a degree-
d
sum-of-squares proof, then there is a degree-
d
rank-decomposing sum-of-squares proof that
\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant C\sqrt{n}
, where
V\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
is the matrix reshaping of
v
.

Proof.

We take
I=[n]
and
w_{i}=\sqrt[4]{C^{2}/n}\,e_{i}
and
z_{i}=\sqrt[4]{n/C^{2}}\,(e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes\Id_{m})v
.
Then

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}w_{i}\otimes z_{i}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}\sqrt[4]{C^{2}/n}\;e_{i}\otimes\left[\sqrt[4]{n/C^{2}}(e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes\Id_{m})v\right]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}e_{i}\otimes\left[(e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes\Id_{m})v\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}(e_{i}^{\vphantom{T}}e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})\otimes\Id_{m}\right)\,v

\displaystyle=(\Id_{n}\otimes\Id_{m})\,v

\displaystyle=v

and by using the multiplicativity of norms over tensor products to introduce a tensor factor of
e_{i}
without changing the norm, and then the orthonormality of
\{e_{i}\}_{i}
to equate the sum of the square norms to the square norm of the sums,

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}\frac{1}{2}(\left\lVert w_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert z_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{C}\left\lVert(e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes\Id_{m})v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2C}\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}\left\lVert(e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes\Id_{m})v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2C}\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}\left\lVert e_{i}\otimes\left[(e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes\Id_{m})v\right]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2C}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[n]}e_{i}\otimes\left[(e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes\Id_{m})v\right]\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2C}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

∎

5.6
Cumulant nuclear norm bounds

Up to constants,
Lemma

5.25
is a special case of
Lemma

5.26
, but we leave the simpler argument in for readability.

Lemma 5.25
.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Gaussian vector
y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
with
\Sigma\preceq\nu\cdot\Id_{n}
and let
p\in\mathbb{R}[y]^{m}
be a vector-valued function of
y
.

Then

\left\lVert\kappa_{k+1}(\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{k},p)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant k!\,\nu^{k}\E_{y}\left\lVert p(y)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

by a sum-of-squares proof of degree
2k
in
\Sigma
and also degree
2k
times the largest degree of a
({\leqslant}k)
th order Wiener-Hermite coefficient of
p
in any other indeterminates.

Proof.

By
Lemma

5.17
then
Corollary

5.21
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\kappa_{k+1}(y,\dots,y,p)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left\lVert\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{k}(y),p)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant k!\nu^{k}\E_{y}\left\lVert p(y)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

∎

Lemma 5.26
.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Gaussian vector
y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
with
\Sigma\preceq\nu\cdot\Id_{n}
and let
p\in(\mathbb{R}[y]^{n})^{\otimes\ell_{1}}
and
q\in(\mathbb{R}[y]^{n})^{\otimes\ell_{2}}
be vector-valued polynomials in
y
with degree at most
d
.

Then for any
r\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor(k+|\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}|)/2\rfloor\}
,

\kappa_{k+2}(\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{k},p,q)

can be re-represented as a sum of terms of the form

\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in J}w_{j}z_{j}^{T}

for some index set
J
so that
w_{j}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes(\max(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})+k-r)}
and
z_{j}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes(\min(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})+r)}
and both are polynomials of degree at most
\lfloor(d+k)/2\rfloor
in the covariance
\Sigma
and

\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in J}\E_{y}\frac{1}{2}(\left\lVert w_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert z_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2})\leqslant(2^{d}-1)k^{k/2+1}\nu^{k/2}n^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor/2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\E_{y}(\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert q\right\rVert_{2}^{2})\,.

This inequality has a sum-of-squares proof.

Proof.

By the multilinearity of the joint cumulant and by taking Wiener-Hermite decompositions,

\displaystyle\kappa_{k+2}(y,\dots,y,p,q)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in[n]}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha,\beta}\kappa_{k+2}(e_{i_{1}}y_{i_{1}},\,\dots,\,e_{i_{k}}y_{i_{k}},\,\hat{p}(\alpha)\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y),\,\hat{q}({\beta})\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(y))

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in[n]}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha,\beta}\kappa_{k+2}(y_{i_{1}},\dots,y_{i_{k}},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(y),\mathsf{He}_{\beta}(y))\;\cdot\;e_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{k}}\otimes\hat{p}(\alpha)\otimes\hat{q}({\beta})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{I\in[n]^{k}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha,\beta}\kappa_{3}(\mathsf{He}_{I},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha},\mathsf{He}_{\beta})\;\cdot\;e^{\otimes I}\otimes\hat{p}(\alpha)\otimes\hat{q}({\beta})\,,

where in the last equality we have used
Lemma

5.18
and introduced the notational convenience
e^{\otimes I}:=e_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes e_{i_{k}}
when
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
.

At this point we will decompose the cumulant
\kappa_{3}(\mathsf{He}_{I},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha},\mathsf{He}_{\beta})
into terms capturing the different interactions between
I
,
\alpha
, and
\beta
, then group those terms together based on how many variables in each of the blocks are interacting with each other block.
This will allow us to find hidden rank-1 terms with smaller norm than if you had simply added up the norms of their components.

By
Lemma

5.19
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{k+2}(y,\dots,y,p,q)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{I\in[n]^{k}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha,\beta}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(I_{1},I_{2})\\
\text{partition of }I\end{subarray}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime}\leqslant\alpha}\sumop\displaylimits_{\beta^{\prime}\leqslant\beta}\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{I_{1}},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime}})\,\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{I_{2}},\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime}})\,\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}},\mathsf{He}_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}})\,e^{\otimes I}\otimes\hat{p}(\alpha)\otimes\hat{q}({\beta})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell\in\{0,\dots,k\}}\binom{k}{\ell}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\sumop\displaylimits_{I_{1}\in[n]^{\ell}}\sumop\displaylimits_{I_{2}\in[n]^{k-\ell}}\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{I_{1}},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime}})\,\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{I_{2}},\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime}})\,\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}},\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}})\,e^{\otimes(I_{1}+I_{2})}\otimes\hat{p}(\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime})\otimes\hat{q}({\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}})\,.

For each
\ell
, the above sum over
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime},I_{1},I_{2}
can be re-represented as the matrix

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}}\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}},\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}})\,\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{I_{1}\in[n]^{\ell}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime}}\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{I_{1}},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime}})\,e^{\otimes I_{1}}\otimes\hat{p}(\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime})\right)\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{I_{2}\in[n]^{k-\ell}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\beta^{\prime}}\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{I_{2}},\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime}})\,e^{\otimes I_{2}}\otimes\hat{q}({\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}})\right)^{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle:=\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}}\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}},\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}})\,\left(L^{(\ell,\alpha^{\prime\prime})}\right)\left(R^{(\ell,\beta^{\prime\prime})}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle=\E_{y}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}L^{(\ell,\alpha^{\prime\prime})}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(y)\right)\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}R^{(\ell,\beta^{\prime\prime})}\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}(y)\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\,,

such that
L^{(\ell,\alpha^{\prime\prime})}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes(\ell_{1}+\ell)}
and
R^{(\ell,\beta^{\prime\prime})}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes(\ell_{2}+k-\ell)}
.

Assume without loss of generality that
\ell_{1}\leqslant\ell_{2}
(otherwise we can exchange
p
with
q
).
Assume also without loss of generality that
\ell_{1}+\ell\geqslant\max(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})+k-r
(otherwise we have
\ell_{2}+(k-\ell)\geqslant\min(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})+r
and we can apply the next step to
\sumop\displaylimits_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}R^{(\ell,\beta^{\prime\prime})}\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}(y)
rather than
\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}L^{(\ell,\alpha^{\prime\prime})}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(y)
) and let

\Delta\ell:=\ell_{1}+\ell-(\max(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})+k-r)\geqslant 0\,.

Note that since
k-r\geqslant k/2-|\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}|/2=k/2-(\ell_{2}-\ell_{1})/2
by definition of
r
, we have
\Delta\ell=(\ell_{1}-\ell_{2})+\ell-(k-r)\leqslant\ell-k/2-(\ell_{2}-\ell_{1})/2\leqslant k/2-(\ell_{2}-\ell_{1})/2\leqslant k/2
.

Now by
Lemma

5.24
applied to
\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}L^{(\ell,\alpha^{\prime\prime})}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(y)
, there is a decomposition

\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in J^{(\ell)}}x^{(\ell)}_{j}\otimes y^{(\ell)}_{j}=\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}L^{(\ell,\alpha^{\prime\prime})}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(y)\,.

such that
x^{(\ell)}_{j}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes(\max(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})+k-r)}
and
y^{(\ell)}_{j}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes\Delta\ell}
and

\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in J^{(\ell)}}\left\lVert x^{(\ell)}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\sqrt{n^{\Delta\ell}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}L^{(\ell,\alpha^{\prime\prime})}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(y)\right\rVert_{2}^{2},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in J^{(\ell)}}\left\lVert y^{(\ell)}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\sqrt{n^{\Delta\ell}}\,.

So we will take our final decomposition as

w_{(\ell,j)}:=(k\nu)^{(k-2\ell)/4}\,x^{(\ell)}_{j}\qquad\text{and}\qquad z_{(\ell,j)}:=(k\nu)^{(2\ell-k)/4}\,y^{(\ell)}_{j}\otimes\sumop\displaylimits_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}R^{(\ell,\beta^{\prime\prime})}\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}(y)

and we have

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,j}\left\lVert w_{(\ell,j)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,j}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}}\left\lVert x^{(\ell)}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}L^{(\ell,\alpha^{\prime\prime})}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(y)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\sumop\displaylimits_{I_{1}\in[n]^{\ell}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime}}\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{I_{1}},\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime}})\left(e^{\otimes I_{1}}\otimes\hat{p}(\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime})\right)\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(y)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

By
Corollary

5.21
, introducing the replica
y^{(1)}
to represent the copy of
y
being expected over in the cumulant (as in
Section

4.2.9
),

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,j}\left\lVert w_{(\ell,j)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\ell!\nu^{\ell}\E_{y^{(1)}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(y^{(1)})\hat{p}(\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime})\mathsf{He}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(y)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

At this point, we have a lot of summations that we would like to interpret as matrix multiplications.
To this end, let
\overline{B}_{\ell}
be the matrix whose
I_{1}
th column is
\mathsf{He}_{I_{1}}
when
I_{1}\in[n^{\ell}]
, let
B
be the matrix whose
\alpha
th column is
\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}
when
\alpha
is a multi-index, and let
P^{(2)}
be the matrix whose
(\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime})
th column is
\hat{p}(\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime})
when both
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime\prime}
are non-empty multi-indices.
Then, recalling that the second joint cumulant between two Hermite polynomials is equal their the inner product, we can write the previous expression as

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,j}\E_{y}\left\lVert w_{(\ell,j)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\sumop\displaylimits_{I_{1}\in[n]^{\ell}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime}}\left\langle\overline{B}_{\ell}e^{\otimes I_{1}},Be_{\alpha^{\prime}}\right\rangle\left(e^{\otimes I_{1}}\otimes\left(P^{(2)}(e_{\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes e_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}})\right)\right)(Be_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}})^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\sumop\displaylimits_{I_{1}\in[n]^{\ell}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(\left(\left(e^{\otimes I_{1}}{e^{\otimes I_{1}}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\overline{B}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}Be_{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)\otimes\left(P^{(2)}(e_{\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes e_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}})\right)\right)(Be_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}})^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,,

where we commuted the inner product through since it is a scalar, and rewrote the inner product using a transpose.
Next, we recognize that the sum over
I_{1}
is an identity matrix, and then use the multilinearity of tensor products and the linearity of matrix multiplication to group together the elementary basis vectors in order to find another identity matrix from the sum over
\alpha^{\prime\prime}
:

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,j}\E_{y}\left\lVert w_{(\ell,j)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(\left(\overline{B}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}Be_{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)\otimes\left(P^{(2)}(e_{\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes e_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}})\right)\right)(Be_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}})^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(\left(\overline{B}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}B\right)\otimes P^{(2)}\right)\left(e_{\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes e_{\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes e_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\right)e_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}B^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(\left(\overline{B}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}B\right)\otimes P^{(2)}\right)\left(e_{\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes e_{\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes B^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

Now we use the fact that the Frobenius norm is invariant under tensor/matrix reshapings, and bring the first mode on the left over to the right, and find our last identity matrix .

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,j}\E_{y}\left\lVert w_{(\ell,j)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha^{\prime}}P^{(2)}\left(e_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}(e_{\alpha^{\prime}}B^{\mathsf{T}}\overline{B}_{\ell})\otimes B^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\left\lVert P^{(2)}\left((B^{\mathsf{T}}\overline{B}_{\ell})\otimes B^{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\,\Tr P^{(2)}(B\otimes B)^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\overline{B}_{\ell}\overline{B}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}\otimes\Id\right)(B\otimes B){P^{(2)}}^{\mathsf{T}}

By
Lemma

5.20
,
\left\lVert\overline{B}_{\ell}\overline{B}_{\ell}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\leqslant\ell!\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{\ell}\leqslant\ell!\nu^{\ell}
, so applying
Lemma

D.2
, we find

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,j}\E_{y}\left\lVert w_{(\ell,j)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\;\ell!\nu^{\ell}\left\lVert(B\otimes B){P^{(2)}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

Finally, by
Lemma

5.23
,
\left\lVert(B\otimes B){P^{(2)}}^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant(2^{d}-1)\,\left\lVert BP^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
, where
P
is the matrix whose
\alpha
th column is
\hat{p}(\alpha)
, and by
Theorem

3.34
,
\left\lVert BP^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\E\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, so

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,j}\E_{y}\left\lVert w_{(\ell,j)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\,(2^{d}-1)\ell!\,\nu^{\ell}\left\lVert BP\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k-2\ell}n^{\Delta\ell}}\,(2^{d}-1)k^{\ell}\nu^{\ell}\E\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k}n^{\Delta\ell}}\,(2^{d}-1)\E\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

A very similar calculation with the
z
vectors yields

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,j}\E_{y}\left\lVert z_{(\ell,j)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,j}\E_{y}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{2\ell-k}}\left\lVert y^{(\ell)}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}R^{(\ell,\beta^{\prime\prime})}\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}(y)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\E_{y}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{2\ell-k}n^{\Delta\ell}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}R^{(\ell,\beta^{\prime\prime})}\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}(y)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\E_{y}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{2\ell-k}n^{\Delta\ell}}\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\sumop\displaylimits_{I_{2}\in[n]^{k-\ell}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\beta^{\prime}}\kappa_{2}(\mathsf{He}_{I_{2}},\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime}})\left(e^{\otimes I_{2}}\otimes\hat{q}(\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime})\right)\mathsf{He}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}(y)\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

Now we apply the same matrix interpretation and manipulations as for the
w
vectors to find

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,j}\E_{y}\left\lVert z_{(\ell,j)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{2\ell-k}n^{\Delta\ell}}k^{(k-\ell)}\nu^{k-\ell}(2^{d}-1)\E\left\lVert q\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell}\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k}n^{\Delta\ell}}\,(2^{d}-1)\E\left\lVert q\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

Therefore,

\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell,j}\E_{y}\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lVert w_{(\ell,j)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert z_{(\ell,j)}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\leqslant(2^{d}-1)k\sqrt{(k\nu)^{k}n^{\Delta\ell}}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\E(\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert q\right\rVert_{2}^{2})\,.

Since
\Delta\ell\leqslant k/2
and
\Delta\ell
is an integer, we have the result.
∎

5.7
Induction over steps

Lemma 5.27
.

Suppose that there exists a rank-decomposing SoS proof that

\left\lVert\E[S^{(r,s)}\mid y]\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant C

for some multiset
S
of random variables and some Gaussian vector variable
y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
with
\Sigma\preceq\nu\cdot\Id
.

Suppose that the joint moments of
S
conditioned on
y
are degree-
d
polynomials in
y
.

Then for every
k
and every
(\Delta r,\Delta s)
satisfying
\Delta r+\Delta s=k
where at least one of
\Delta r
and
\Delta s
is even, there is a rank-decomposing SoS proof that

\left\lVert\E_{y}(S\cup\{\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{k}\})^{(r+\Delta r,s+\Delta s)}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant 2^{d}k^{k/2+2}\nu^{k/2}n^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor/2}C\,.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume that there is only one term
M=pq^{\mathsf{T}}
with
p\in(\mathbb{R}[y]^{n})^{\otimes r}
and
q\in(\mathbb{R}[y]^{n})^{\otimes s}
in the rank-decomposing SoS proof establishing the upper bound
\left\lVert\E[S^{(r,s)}\mid y]\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant C
, so that
\frac{1}{2}(\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert q\right\rVert_{2}^{2})\leqslant C
.
Then sublinearity of the nuclear norm is enough to extend the argument to an arbitrary number of terms.

By the law of conditional expectation,

\E_{y}S\cup\{\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{k}\}=\E_{y}\;(\E[S\mid y])\otimes\{\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{k}\}\,.

Then by our assumption,

\E_{y}S\cup\{y,\dots,y\}=\E_{y}\;p\otimes q\otimes\{y,\dots,y\}\,.

By the moment-cumulant formula,

\E_{y}\;p\otimes q\otimes\{y,\dots,y\}\propto\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\\
\text{partition of $\{p,q,y,\dots,y\}$}\end{subarray}}\prodop\displaylimits_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}(B)\,,

where here
\propto
means that they have equal mode symmetrizations.
Since
\kappa(y,y)=\E_{y}(y\otimes y)
and the cumulant of any other number of
y
s by themselves is 0, for all the non-zero terms of that sum, every block of the partition that doesn’t contain
p
or
q
must be exactly
\{y,y\}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\E_{y}\;p\otimes q\otimes\{y,\dots,y\}\propto

\displaystyle\left[\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b\\
k-a-b\in 2\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}(\E_{y}y\otimes y)^{\otimes(k-a-b)/2}\otimes\kappa_{a+1}(\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{a},p)\otimes\kappa_{b+1}(\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{b},q)\right]

\displaystyle{}+\left[\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\\
k-a\in 2\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}(\E_{y}y\otimes y)^{\otimes(k-a)/2}\otimes\kappa_{a+2}(\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{a},p,q)\right]\,.

Now we just have to find re-representations of these terms that satisfy the nuclear norm requirement.
We will do so one summand at a time.

Let’s start with the terms where
p
and
q
are in the same cumulant block.
Let
\Delta r^{\prime}=2\lfloor\min(\Delta r,k-a)/2\rfloor
and
\Delta s^{\prime}=k-a-\Delta r^{\prime}
, noting that
\Delta s^{\prime}
must be even since
k-a
and
\Delta r^{\prime}
are both even and that
(\Delta r-\Delta r^{\prime})+(\Delta s-\Delta s^{\prime})=a
.
By
Lemma

5.26
, there is a representation of
\kappa_{a+2}(\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{a},p,q)
as a matrix
\sumop\displaylimits_{j}w_{j}z_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
with
w_{j}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes r}\otimes(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes(\Delta r-\Delta r^{\prime})}
and
z_{j}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes s}\otimes(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes(\Delta s-\Delta s^{\prime})}
and

\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\E_{y}\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lVert w_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert z_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\leqslant(2^{d}-1)a^{a/2+1}\nu^{a/2}n^{\lfloor a/2\rfloor/2}C\,.

Then our matrix representation for
(\E_{y}y\otimes y)^{\otimes(k-a)/2}\otimes\kappa_{a+2}(\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{a},p,q)
is, in rank-decomposing form,

\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\left(\left\lVert\E_{y}y\otimes y\right\rVert_{2}^{(\Delta s^{\prime}-\Delta r^{\prime})/4}\left(\E_{y}y\otimes y\right)^{\otimes\Delta r^{\prime}/2}\otimes w_{j}\right)\left(\left\lVert\E_{y}y\otimes y\right\rVert_{2}^{(\Delta r^{\prime}-\Delta s^{\prime})/4}\left(\E_{y}y\otimes y\right)^{\otimes\Delta s^{\prime}/2}\otimes z_{j}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}

:=\sumop\displaylimits_{j}p^{\prime}_{j}q^{\prime}_{j}{}^{\mathsf{T}}\,.

Then we have, using the fact that
\left\lVert\E_{y}y\otimes y\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\nu^{2}n
,

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\E_{y}\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lVert p^{\prime}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert q^{\prime}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant(\nu^{2}n)^{(\Delta r^{\prime}+\Delta s^{\prime})/4}(2^{d}-1)a^{a/2+1}\nu^{a/2}n^{\lfloor a/2\rfloor/2}C

\displaystyle=(\nu^{2}n)^{(k-a)/4}(2^{d}-1)a^{a/2+1}\,\nu^{a/2}n^{\lfloor a/2\rfloor/2}C

\displaystyle\leqslant(2^{d}-1)k^{k/2+1}\,\nu^{k/2}n^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor/2}C\,.

Since there are at most
k
different values of
a
, in total the terms with
p
and
q
in the same cumulant block make a nuclear norm contribution of
(2^{d}-1)k^{k/2+2}\,\nu^{k/2}n^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor/2}C
.

Now consider the terms where
p
and
q
are in different cumulants.
Without loss of generality, assume
\Delta r-a\geqslant\Delta s-b
(otherwise we just swap the roles of
(r,a)
and
(s,b)
in this argument), noting that this implies
a\leqslant\Delta r
since
a+b\leqslant k=\Delta r+\Delta s
.
Let
\Delta r^{\prime}=2\lfloor\min(\Delta r-a,k-a-b)/2\rfloor
and
\Delta s^{\prime}=k-a-b-\Delta r^{\prime}
, noting that
\Delta s^{\prime}
must be even since
k-a-b
and
\Delta r^{\prime}
are both even and that
\Delta s^{\prime}\leqslant\Delta s-b
since
\Delta s^{\prime}=k-a-b-\Delta r^{\prime}\leqslant k-b-\Delta r=\Delta s-b
.
By
Lemma

5.25
, there is a representation of
\kappa_{a+1}(\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{a},p)
as a vector
v\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes(r+a)}
and by a combination of
Lemma

5.25
and
Lemma

5.24
, there is a representation of
\kappa_{b+1}(\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{b},q)
as a matrix
\sumop\displaylimits_{j}w_{j}z_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
such that
w_{j}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes(b-\Delta s+\Delta s^{\prime})}
and
z_{j}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes s}\otimes(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\otimes(\Delta s-\Delta s^{\prime})}
and

\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant a!\nu^{a}\E_{y}\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\frac{1}{2}(\left\lVert w_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert z_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2})\leqslant\sqrt{b!}\,\nu^{b/2}n^{(b-\Delta s+\Delta s^{\prime})/2}\sqrt{2C},\,

Then our matrix representation for
(\E_{y}y\otimes y)^{\otimes(k-a-b)/2}\otimes\kappa_{a+1}(\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{a},p)\otimes\kappa_{b+1}(\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{b},q)
is, in rank-decomposing form,
\sumop\displaylimits_{j}p^{\prime}_{j}q^{\prime}_{j}{}^{\mathsf{T}}
with

\displaystyle p^{\prime}_{j}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt[4]{a!\nu^{a}2C}}(\nu^{2}n)^{(\Delta s^{\prime}-\Delta r^{\prime})/8}\left(\E_{y}y\otimes y\right)^{\otimes\Delta r^{\prime}/2}\otimes w_{j}\otimes v

\displaystyle q^{\prime}_{j}

\displaystyle:=\sqrt[4]{a!\nu^{a}2C}(\nu^{2}n)^{(\Delta r^{\prime}-\Delta s^{\prime})/8}\left(\E_{y}y\otimes y\right)^{\otimes\Delta s^{\prime}/2}\otimes z_{j}\,.

Then we have, using the fact that
\left\lVert\E_{y}y\otimes y\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\nu^{2}n
,

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lVert p^{\prime}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert q^{\prime}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\sqrt{a!}\,\nu^{a/2}(\nu^{2}n)^{(\Delta r^{\prime}+\Delta s^{\prime})/4}\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lVert w_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\sqrt{(2C)^{-1}}\E_{y}\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert z_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}{\sqrt{2C}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\sqrt{a!}\,\nu^{a/2}\sqrt{2C}(\nu^{2}n)^{(\Delta r^{\prime}+\Delta s^{\prime})/4}\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lVert w_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert z_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{a!}\,\nu^{a/2}\sqrt{2C}(\nu^{2}n)^{(k-a-b)/4}\sumop\displaylimits_{j}\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lVert w_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert z_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{a!}\,\nu^{a/2}\sqrt{2C}(\nu^{2}n)^{(k-a-b)/4}\cdot\sqrt{b!}\,\nu^{b/2}n^{(b-\Delta s+\Delta s^{\prime})/2}\sqrt{2C}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{a!b!}\,\nu^{k/2}n^{(k-a+b-2\Delta s+2\Delta s^{\prime})/4}C

\displaystyle\leqslant 2k^{k/2}\nu^{k/2}n^{(k-a+b-2\Delta s+2\Delta s-2b)/4}C

\displaystyle=2k^{k/2}\nu^{k/2}n^{(k-a-b)/4}C

\displaystyle\leqslant 2k^{k/2}\nu^{k/2}n^{2\lfloor k/2\rfloor/4}C\,,

where the last inequality is because
k-a-b
is an even integer that is at most
k
.
Since there are at most
k^{2}/2
different values of
a
and
b
, in total the terms with
p
and
q
in the separate cumulant block make a nuclear norm contribution of
k^{k/2+2}\nu^{k/2}n^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor/2}C
.

Therefore, by adding together the nuclear norm contributions of the two types of terms, we have a matrix representation for

\E(S\cup\{\underbrace{y,\dots,y}_{k}\})^{(r+\Delta r,s+\Delta s)}
with a rank-decomposing nuclear norm bound of
2^{d}k^{k/2+2}\nu^{k/2}n^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor/2}C
.
∎

5.8
Upper bound for
\left\lVert\E[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\,\mid\,v_{i-1}]\right\rVert^{q}_{q}
under high-entropy

We show that the operator norm HES constraint in conjunction with the fact that
\Tr\left[\E\left[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\,|\,\mid v_{i-1}\right]\right]=1
implies that for
q>1
, the Schatten
q
-norm of the conditional covariance is bounded appropriately.

Proposition 5.28

(Bounded Schatten
q
-norm of conditional covariance)
.

Let
V
be a symmetric matrix-valued variable.
Then,

\left\{\vphantom{\frac{1}{1}}\Tr V=1\,,\;0\preceq V\preceq c\,\Id\right\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\left\{\vphantom{\frac{1}{1}}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{q}^{q}\leqslant c^{q-1}\right\}\,.

Proof.

This follows by an application of a
(1,\infty)
-Matrix Hölder’s inequality (
Lemma

D.5
) as follows,

\displaystyle\left\lVert V\right\rVert^{q}_{q}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[V^{q}\right]=\left\langle V^{q-1},V\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lVert V^{q-1}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{q-1}\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant c^{q-1}\cdot 1=c^{q-1}\,.

Note:
While
q-1
is
fractional
, one can use the standard trick of converting to integer powers in conjunction with the encoding of the operator norm constraint via a slack-matrix to make the assertion that

\left\lVert V^{q-1}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}=\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{q-1}\,,

has a sum-of-squares proof using with the radical
X=V^{1/(p-1)}
and the fact that
q-1=\frac{1}{p-1}
(
Appendix

E
, Radicals of the polynomial grammar).
∎

6
Low-Degree Polynomial Approximation of the Top Eigenspace of the Hessian

In this section we analyze the spectrum of the Hessian of the spherical spin-glass Hamiltonian on points that are generated by HES processes. In particular, we provide low-degree sum-of-squares certificates that certify the value of the low-degree moments of the empirical distribution of the eigenvalues of the Hessian up to lower order corrections (
Lemma

6.1
). We then use these sum-of-squares certificates about the low-degree spectral moments of this Hessian along with the bounds provided in
Section

5
to certify the amount the value of the Hamiltonian can increase in a single step under
any
HES process (
Lemma

6.4
).

Having certified the maximum energy increase of the Hamiltonian under any HES process, we also provide a specific low-degree matrix polynomial which arbitrarily approximates a projector into the top part of the bulk spectrum of the Hessian (
Corollary

6.7
). This low-degree polynomial also happens to be positive semi-definite with bounded operator norm, and is used extensively in the feasibility argument provided in
Section

7
.

A crucial part of the argumentation that certifies how much the energy can increase under any HES process is estimating the rate at which the low-degree spectral moments of the Hessian converge to the desired value (which is the operator norm). Using some involved Catalan combinatorics in conjunction with the basic analytic properties of the Lambert-
W
function allows for a fairly precise estimate as a function of
\varepsilon
(
Corollary

6.3
). This rate of convergence governs the degree of the underlying sum-of-squares certificates over the HES SoS hierarchy that are used in the proofs in this section.

6.1
Certifiable approximate Wignerianity of the Hessian
