Subag
[
Sub18
]
showed that the eigenspectrum of the Hessian converges exponentially quickly to the semicircle law, with a scaling factor of
\sqrt{\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{2}^{2})}
.

Here we give a SoS-certifiable approximate version of the same statement and argument. To make this statement fit in the low-degree sums-of-squares framework, we relax the requirement somewhat so that we certify only the
low-degree moments
of the semicircle law by bounding the Schatten norms of the Hessian.
Although we do not do this, the bounds are strong enough to be interpreted as equivalent to conditional pointwise bounds

\left\{\,|\,\|\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\|_{2q}^{2q}-nC_{q}(\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}))^{q}|\leqslant Cn^{0.5+2qd_{H}\lambda}\right\}

for any large enough
C
as long as the downstream proofs using it do not exceed a certain degree limit depending on
C
and the degree of the pseudo-distribution.

This involves a careful combinatorial calculation to compute the expectation of
\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\right\rVert_{2q}^{2q}
as a polynomial in
\sigma
over the randomness of the instance.
This turns out to be provably close to a function of
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, so in effect, the expectation does not depend on
\sigma
when
\sigma
is known to fall on a sphere.
The calculation counts the number of non-backtracking walks that come out of the trace power calculation for
\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\right\rVert_{2q}^{2q}
.

Due to heavy concentration of the trace powers of a random matrix
[
For23
,
CMŚS07
]
, there is a lot of slack between the bound we need and the bound that’s actually true for the fluctuations of
\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\right\rVert_{2q}^{2q}
around its expectation.
Graph matrix norm bounds
[
AMP21
]
together with
Theorem

5.9
suffice to certifiably control these fluctuations with subexponential probability.

Lemma 6.1

(Certifiable Approximate Wignerianity)
.

Let
C_{q}
refer to the
q
th Catalan number. Then for each
p
and
q
positive integer, for every
\lambda>0
, with probability
1-O(\exp(-n^{\lambda}))
over the randomness of the instance, there are SoS proofs whose degree depends only on
p
and
q
and
d_{H}
demonstrating that for all high-entropy step distributions over
\sigma
satisfying the constraint
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}=\zeta
for some
\zeta
with stepwise covariances bounded by
c
,

\E_{\sigma}\left(\left\lVert\tfrac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\right\rVert_{2q}^{2q}-nC_{q}(\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}))^{q}\right)^{2p}\leqslant O(n^{2p(0.5+2qd_{H}\lambda)})\,,

\E_{\sigma}\left(\Tr(\tfrac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma))^{2q-1}\right)^{2p}\leqslant O(n^{2p(0.5+2qd_{H}\lambda)})\,.

Proof.

In this argument, the big-
O
notation
O(\cdot)
will refer to asymptotics with respect to
n
, with
q
,
p
,
d_{H}
, and
\nu
treated as constants.

We begin by writing the expectation (over the randomness of the instance) of the
2q
-th Schatten norm of
\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)
as a polynomial in
\sigma
, and show that this polynomial is close to
nC_{q}(\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}))^{q}
.
Later we will write the
2p
th moment of
2q
th Schatten norm of
\tfrac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)
as a polynomial in both
\sigma
and the gaussian tensors
g
(as in
Definition

2.1
), to show concentration (in both
\sigma
and
g
) around the expectation.

For convenience, we define

T_{\alpha}=\frac{\gamma_{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!}\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi\in S_{|\alpha|}}g_{\alpha_{\pi(1)},\dots,\alpha_{\pi(|\alpha|)}}\,,

where in an abuse of notation,
\alpha
is used as both a multi-index and an (arbitrarily) ordered tuple of its constituent indices.

Then

\tfrac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)_{i,j}=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha|\leqslant d_{H}-2\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}T_{\alpha\cup\{i,j\}}\sigma^{\alpha}\,,

where, again in another abuse of notation, we treat
\alpha
as both a multi-index and a multi-set.

The trace of the
2q
-th power of the Hessian can now be written as, letting
\mathcal{C}_{2q}
be the set of closed walks of length
2q
over
[n]
, where
w(i)
is the
i
th vertex in the walk when
w\in\mathcal{C}_{2q}
and we take
w(0):=w(2q)
, as defined in
Section

3.6.1
,

\displaystyle\Tr[(\tfrac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma))^{2q}]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{w\in\mathcal{C}_{2q}}\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{2q}\left(\tfrac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\right)_{w(i-1),w(i)}

(6.1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{q}}\sumop\displaylimits_{w\in\mathcal{C}_{2q}}\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{2q}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\alpha|\leqslant d_{H}-2\end{subarray}}T_{\alpha\cup\{w(i-1),w(i)\}}\sigma^{\alpha}

(6.2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{q}}\sumop\displaylimits_{w\in\mathcal{C}_{2q}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{2q}\\
|\alpha_{i}|\leqslant d_{H}-2\;\end{subarray}}\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{2q}T_{\alpha_{i}\cup\{w(i-1),w(i)\}}\sigma^{\alpha_{i}}\;

(6.3)

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{signal}+\mathsf{noise}\,,

(6.4)

where
\mathsf{signal}
is the part of that polynomial that is simultaneously even in
\sigma
and degree-
0
when expressed in the Hermite basis (equivalently, having non-zero expected value) as a polynomial in
g
, and
\mathsf{noise}
is everything else.

Signal term

First, let us define, using notation from
Section

3.6.1
to represent Dyck-shaped walks,

\displaystyle\mathsf{dyck}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n^{q}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{q}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\omega\in{}_{\tau}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}\\
|\alpha_{i}|\leqslant d_{H}-2\\
\alpha_{1}\neq\dots\neq\alpha_{q}\end{subarray}}\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{q}T_{\alpha_{i}\cup\{W(\tau,\omega)_{i-1},W(\tau,\omega)_{i}\}}^{2}\sigma^{2\alpha_{i}}\,,

where the notation
\alpha_{1}\neq\dots\neq\alpha_{q}
is used to indicate that none of the
\alpha_{i}
are equal to each other.

Let
\mathsf{supersignal}
denote the degree-0 portion of
\mathsf{dyck}
when expressed in the basis in
g
.
We will show that

\mathsf{signal}=(1+O(1/n))\mathsf{supersignal}=(1\pm O(1/n))C_{q}(\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}))^{q}\,.

We start by showing that

\mathsf{signal}=(1+O(1/n))\mathsf{supersignal}\,.

First, note that by linearity of the Hermite decomposition (in this case equivalent to linearity of expectation), each of the summands has its own degree-0 term, additive with each other, all of which are non-negative and within a
O(1)
factor of each other since the magnitude of the degree-0 part of each summand is determined only by
q
,
\nu
, and the cardinalities of the indices in
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}
and
\omega
or
w
.
And the summands in
\mathsf{dyck}
are a subset of those that contribute to
\mathsf{signal}
, specifically the subset where the walk
w
is Dyck-shaped and
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}
whenever the
j
is the falling step corresponding to
i
which is a rising step in the Dyck-shaped walk and all
\alpha
’s are otherwise distinct.

Therefore it suffices to show that the number of summands in
\mathsf{dyck}
corresponding to a certain even monomial
\sigma^{2\beta}
is within a factor of
(1-O(1/n))
of the total number of summands that contribute nontrivially to the same monomial in
\mathsf{signal}
.

First, we argue that most of the nonzero terms in the expectation are where
\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{2q}\}
is a multiset where all elements are even cardinality.
Since we fixed
2\beta=\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{2q}
, there is no factor of
n
in the combinatorics of how that
2\beta
is divided into the terms
\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{2q}
, so any factor of
n
difference will come from the choice of the walk
w
.
But the condition that the different factors of
T_{\alpha_{i}\cup\{w(i-1),w(i)\}}
must multiply together into an even polynomial is a strong one. Consider any specific pairing of values of
i
such that paired up values have the same index in
T
: if
i
and
j
are paired up so that
\alpha_{i}\cup\{w(i-1),w(i)\}=\alpha_{j}\cup\{w(j-1),w(j)\}
while
\alpha_{i}\neq\alpha_{j}
, then that implies
(\{w(i-1),w(i)\}\triangle\{w(j-1),w(j)\})\triangle(\alpha_{i}\triangle\alpha_{j})=\emptyset
, which fixes at last two of the choices of
w(i-1)
,
w(i)
,
w(j-1)
, and
w(j)
. Meanwhile the equality condition means that if two of those choices remain unfixed, then they must be equal to each other.
In the end, this is at least one factor of
n
fewer choices than if
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}
instead.

So now it suffices to show that, for a specific choice of
\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\alpha_{q}^{\prime}
such that
\beta=\alpha_{1}^{\prime}+\dots+\alpha_{q}^{\prime}
, it is the case that most of the terms where
\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{2q}\}=2\{\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\alpha_{q}^{\prime}\}
as a multiset equality are in the situation where
w
is a Dyck-shaped walk whose pairs of rising and falling steps correspond to the pairs of
\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{2q}\}
.

To see this, note that for a walk to be closed and traverse each edge an even number of times, it must necessarily have at most
q+1
distinct vertices since it has at most
q
distinct edges.
But in order for it to not be a Dyck-shaped walk, it must repeat at least one edge without retracing its steps to in order to get back to that edge, meaning that the graph of traversed edges contains a non-trivial cycle, meaning that there are at most
q
distinct vertices in the walk.

Therefore there are
O(1/n)
as many non-Dyck-shaped closed walks as there are Dyck-shaped ones, since enumerating the Dyck-shaped walks involves making
q+1
arbitrary choices of vertices from
[n]
instead of
q
choices.

And we cannot have mismatches in the pairings between
\alpha_{i}
s and the pairings between rising and falling steps in the Dyck-shaped walk without introducing additional constraints where at least two of the vertices of the walk are forced to be equal to some of the indices in the
\alpha_{i}
s.

Finally we show that

\mathsf{supersignal}=(1\pm O(1/n))C_{q}(\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}))^{q}\,.

Evaluating the Hermite basis with respect to
g
(equivalently, the Gaussian moments),

\displaystyle\E_{g}\mathsf{supersignal}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{q}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\tau}\sumop\displaylimits_{\omega}\sumop\displaylimits_{\;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}\;}\zeta_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q},\omega}\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{q}\sigma^{2\alpha_{i}}\;\frac{(|\alpha_{i}|+2)!}{(\alpha_{i}\cup\{W(\tau,\omega)_{i-1},W(\tau,\omega)_{i}\})!}\gamma_{|\alpha_{i}|+2}^{2}\,,

where
\zeta_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q},\omega}
is the product of the double factorials of one less than double the number of occurrences of each index
i\in[n]
among
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}
and
\omega
(this factor coming from the Gaussian moments or Hermite basis).

Now, since this will necessarily be an even symmetric polynomial, it decomposes into a sum of products of even power sums, by Newton’s identities.
We will argue that we only need to care about the terms that are powers of
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, because the other terms will be asympotically smaller by a SoS proof.
This follows from the canonical matrix representation of the polynomial
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2j}^{2j}
having spectral norm 1, which implies by
Theorem

5.8
that
\E_{\sigma}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2j}^{2j}\leqslant O(n^{-j/2})<O(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2j}/n)
for
j\geqslant 2
, and from an argument that the coefficient in front of any term with a factor of
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2j}^{2j}
is within a constant factor of the coefficient in front of the same term with that factor replaced by
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2j}
.
And this is true because the number of summands corresponding to each choice of
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}
doesn’t depend on what those choices are and the summands themselves do not depend on
n
.

This allows us to conclude that we only lose a multiplicative factor of
(1-1/n)
by redefining
\zeta_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q},\omega}=1
regardless of the choices of indices involved.
And a similar argument allows us to substitute
(\alpha_{i}\cup\{W(\tau,\omega)_{i-1},W(\tau,\omega)_{i}\})!
with
\alpha_{i}!
again with only a loss of a factor of
(1-1/n)
.

Finally, we can carry through the calculation, using
3.38
in the second equation after the summands lose dependence on
\tau
and
\omega
:

\displaystyle\E_{g}\mathsf{supersignal}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+O(1/n))}{n^{q}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\tau}\sumop\displaylimits_{\omega}\sumop\displaylimits_{\;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}\;}\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{q}\sigma^{2\alpha_{i}}\frac{(|\alpha_{i}|+2)!}{\alpha_{i}!}\gamma_{|\alpha_{i}|+2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+O(1/n))}{n^{q}}C_{q}n(n-1)^{q}\sumop\displaylimits_{\;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{q}\;}\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{q}\sigma^{2\alpha_{i}}\frac{(|\alpha_{i}|+2)!}{\alpha_{i}!}\gamma_{|\alpha_{i}|+2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+O(1/n))}{n^{q}}C_{q}n(n-1)^{q}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha}\sigma^{2\alpha}\frac{(|\alpha|+2)!}{\alpha!}\gamma_{|\alpha|+2}^{2}\right)^{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+O(1/n))}{n^{q}}C_{q}n(n-1)^{q}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{r=2}^{d_{H}}\sumop\displaylimits_{|\alpha|=r-2}\sigma^{2\alpha}\;\frac{r!}{\alpha!}\gamma_{r}^{2}\right)^{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+O(1/n))}{n^{q}}C_{q}n(n-1)^{q}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{r=2}^{d_{H}}r(r-1)\gamma_{r}^{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2(r-2)}\;\right)^{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+O(1/n))}{n^{q}}C_{q}n(n-1)^{q}\left(\sqrt{\nu^{\prime\prime}\left(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)}\right)^{2q}

\displaystyle=n\left(1\pm O\left(\tfrac{1}{n}\right)\right)C_{q}\left(\sqrt{\nu^{\prime\prime}\left(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)}\right)^{2q}\,,

where the third-to-last equality uses the fact that
\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{2}^{2m}=\sumop\displaylimits_{|\alpha|=m}\frac{m!}{\alpha!}x^{2\alpha}
.

Odd moments

Because all terms of
\Tr(\tfrac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma))^{2q-1}
are odd in
g
, its expectation over
g
is
0
and therefore all terms are noise terms.
The argument follows the same outline as the noise term for the even moments.

Noise terms

We proceed by applying graph matrix norm bounds (
Definition

3.44
and
Definition

3.45
) to the polynomial
\mathsf{noise}
.

The broad idea is that
\Tr(\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma))^{2q}
is a sum over
2q
-length walks
w_{1},\dots,w_{2q}=w_{0}
over the indices of
\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)
of the entries of
\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)
indexed by edges of the walk
(w_{i},w_{i+1})
. The noise terms are formed of those walks where at least one entry of
\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)
appears an odd number of times, or the higher-order Hermite terms of the walks where each entry appears an even number of times.
Each entry of
\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)
is a polynomial
p_{i,j}
in
\sigma
, so to expand the term associated with a single walk into the monomial basis of
\sigma
, we end up with a sum over all different possible choices of
2q
multi-indices (one per edge in the walk).
So just like in the argument for the signal term, we label the
i
th multi-index of the walk as
\alpha_{i}
for
i\in[2q]
, and we label the
i
th node of the walk as
w_{i}
.

We write the evaluation of this polynomial as an inner product between a coefficient matrix and the moment matrix of
\sigma
.
We split up the coefficient matrix into a sum over “shape matrices” in order to use graph matrix norm bounds on the spectral norms of those shape matrices, and use
Theorem

5.9
to control the nuclear norm of the moment matrix.
Each shape matrix contains only some of the terms of the expansion of
\mathsf{noise}
into the monomial basis, specifically those where the
\alpha_{i}
and the
w_{i}
have a particular “index shape” (pattern of repeated indices and degrees).
Each shape matrix is further specified by which indices end up as row indices (to be denoted
U_{\kappa}
for a shape
\kappa
) of the final coefficient matrix and which are column indices (
V_{\kappa}
).

We split
\mathsf{noise}
into a sum of graph matrices as

\mathsf{noise}(\sigma)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\kappa}\frac{c_{\kappa}}{n^{q}}\left\langle M_{\kappa},\left(\sigma^{\otimes\deg(U_{\kappa})}\right)\left(\sigma^{\otimes\deg(V_{\kappa})}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rangle\,,

where the sum is over generalized shapes
\kappa
,
U_{\kappa}
and
V_{\kappa}
are the generalized index shapes of
\kappa
, and
\deg(U_{\kappa})
is the sum of the degrees (the total degree) of
U_{\kappa}
, for some constants
c_{\kappa}
which do not depend on
n
.
The relevant shapes can be constructed as follows, using definitions from
Section

3.7.1
:

1.

All variables of the same type, with ground set
[n]
.

2.

Generalized index shapes
U_{\kappa}
consisting of variables of total degree up to
2q(d_{H}-2)
, and
V_{\kappa}
similarly consisting variables of total degree up to
2q(d_{H}-2)
, with possibly some variables shared with
U_{\kappa}
(although the same variable might have different degrees in
U_{\kappa}
and
V_{\kappa}
).

3.

Middle index shapes
W_{\kappa}
consisting of variables of total degree up to
4q
.

4.

The shape
\kappa
is further specified by parameters
\widehat{\alpha}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\alpha}_{2q}
, each of which is a multiset of up to
d_{H}-2
elements of
V(V_{\kappa})\cup V(U_{\kappa})
(possibly with repeats), and
\widehat{w}_{1},\dots,\widehat{w}_{2q}
, each of which is an element of
V(\kappa)
(again with possible repeats).

5.

The hyperedges
E(\kappa)
consist of
\widehat{\alpha}_{i}\cup\{\widehat{w}_{i-1},\widehat{w}_{i}\}
for each
i\in[2q]
, where
\widehat{w}_{0}
is taken to be another name for
\widehat{w}_{2q}
, subject to the constraint that the total number of occurences in
E(\kappa)
of any given vertex in
V(\kappa)
is equal to the degree of that variable.
The random variable associated with this edge is
T^{\mathrm{sym}}(\alpha_{i},w_{i-1},w_{i})
, and it is a Gaussian scaled by some factor which is bounded by a number that depends only on
q
and
d_{H}
and
\nu
(in particular, not on
n
).

If the same edge appears more than once over the different values of
i
, then that edge is labelled with the number of times
\ell
it appears, and its corresponding value is
H_{\ell}(T^{\mathrm{sym}}(\alpha_{i},w_{i-1},w_{i}))
, where
H_{\ell}
is the
\ell
th Hermite polynomial.

6.

Due to the decomposition into Hermite polynomials, for every shape containing an edge with cardinality larger than
1
, the shape with the same indices and edges except with that edge having cardinality decremented by
2
(even if that cardinality becomes
0
as a result) is also a shape included in our consideration (and doing this edge-removing operation increases the factor
c_{\kappa}
of the result by a factor of 1 plus the new cardinality of that edge after 2 was substracted).

7.

In order to avoid including any
\mathsf{signal}
terms, we exclude the shape that has no hyperedges at all.

Now by

Theorem

5.9
,

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma}\mathsf{noise}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leqslant O(1)\sumop\displaylimits_{\kappa\in K}n^{p-2p\lceil\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[2q]}a_{i}(\kappa)/2\rceil/2}\left\lVert\frac{1}{n^{q}}M_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{2p}\,,

(6.5)

where
K
is the set of graph matrix shapes in our expansion that are single-flipped (
Definition

5.7
) and
a_{i}(\kappa)
is the number of non-middle vertices incident to the
i
th hyperedge of
\kappa
so that
a_{i}(\kappa)=|\hat{\alpha}_{i}|
, the hyperedges ordered according to
Item

5
.

To obtain the bound we wanted, specifically
\E_{\sigma}\mathsf{noise}^{2p}<o(n^{2p})
, it now suffices to show that
\left\lVert\tilde{M}_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}<o(n^{q+\lceil 1+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[2q]}a_{i}(\kappa)/2\rceil/2})
for all
\kappa\in K
with high probability.

To apply a graph matrix norm bound, first we understand all of the possible hypergraphs that could make up our shape
\kappa
.
As in
Section

3.7.1
, let
S_{\min}
be the minimum-size set of vertices that separates
U_{\kappa}
from
V_{\kappa}
(since all our vertices are the same type, they all have the same weight), including any vertices in
U_{\kappa}\cap V_{\kappa}
.
Let also
W_{\mathrm{iso}}
be the set of isolated vertices (vertices not incident to any hyperedges).
We argue that

|V(\kappa)|-|S_{\min}|+|W_{\mathrm{iso}}|<2q+1+\lceil\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[2q]}a_{i}(\kappa)/2\rceil
.

To understand this, we can start from the extreme case with a shape
\kappa_{0}
where all vertices in
\widehat{\alpha}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\alpha}_{2q}
and
\widehat{w}_{1},\dots,\widehat{w}_{2q}
are distinct and included in each multiset with cardinality at most 1.
Then
|V(\kappa_{0})|=2q+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{2q}a_{i}(\kappa_{0})
where the
2q
is from the middle vertices
\widehat{w}_{1},\dots,\widehat{w}_{2q}
and
S_{\mathrm{iso}}
is empty.
By the definition of single-flipped graph matrix shape (and taking
a_{i}:=a_{i}(\kappa_{0})
), the
i
th hyperedge has
\lceil a_{i}/2+1/2\rceil
or
\lfloor a_{i}/2\rfloor
or
\lfloor a_{i}/2-1/2\rfloor
vertices in
U_{\kappa_{0}}
and
a_{i}-|e_{i}\cap U_{\kappa_{0}}|
in
V_{\kappa_{0}}
.
Therefore
\min(|e_{i}\cap U_{\kappa_{0}}|,|e_{i}\cap V_{\kappa_{0}}|)\geqslant\lfloor a_{i}/2-1/2\rfloor
and so since a vertex separator must separate
e_{i}\cap U_{\kappa_{0}}
from
e_{i}\cap V_{\kappa_{0}}
, at least
\lfloor a_{i}/2-1/2\rfloor
of the vertices in
e_{i}
are part of any minimum vertex separator
S_{\min}
.
Furthermore, if
|e_{i}\cap U_{\kappa_{0}}|\neq|e_{i}\cap V_{\kappa_{0}}|\neq a_{i}/2
, then the next hyperedge
e_{j}
in the cycle that has an imbalance in
|e_{i}\cap U_{\kappa_{0}}|
vs
|e_{i}\cap V_{\kappa_{0}}|
has that imbalance in the opposite direction, forcing either a middle vertex in
W_{\kappa_{0}}
to also be part of this minimum vertex separator or for the vertex separator to stay on the same side (either all
U_{\kappa_{0}}
or all
V_{\kappa_{0}}
).
The latter will be cheaper, at the average cost of up to
1/2
of a vertex per hyperedge (except for the last one, if the total number of modes is odd).
Therefore,
|S_{\min}|\geqslant\lfloor\sumop\displaylimits_{i}a_{i}/2\rfloor
.
Overall,
|V(\kappa_{0})|-|S_{\min}|+|W_{\mathrm{iso}}|\leqslant 2q+\lceil\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[2q]}a_{i}/2\rceil
, which is the bound we wanted.

The remaining shapes are formed from these extreme
\kappa_{0}
by merging vertices together and removing even numbers of duplicate copies of identical hyperedges.
Merging two vertices together has the effect of reducing
|V(\kappa)|
by 1, and it cannot reduce
S_{\min}
by more than 1, so the merging operation itself cannot cause
|V(\kappa)|-|S_{\min}|+|W_{\mathrm{iso}}|
to increase.
The identical-hyperedge-removing operation can cause
|W_{\mathrm{iso}}|
to increase without affecting
|V(\kappa)|
or
|S_{\min}|
, but we argue that for each element of
W_{\mathrm{iso}}
, there must have been at least one merging operation that decreased
|V(\kappa)|
without changing
|S_{\min}|
.
Since our construction of a minimum vertex separator did not use any vertices in
W
, any middle vertex that is merged would decrease
|V(\kappa)|
but not decrease
|S_{\min}|
.

We can lower-bound the number of vertices in
W
that were merged by looking at the connected components of the graph when it is restricted to only the vertices in
W
.
Any merging/removal of hyperedges in the original graph matrix shape imply the merging/removing of the corresponding edges in this restricted graph
(V,E)
with
V=W
.
Each removal of a pair of identical edges can only increase the number
C
of connected components in this restricted graph by
1
, so
C-1\leqslant q-|E|/2
.
Unless there are no edges at all in the graph (which is impossible by
Item

7
),
|W_{\mathrm{iso}}|\leqslant C-1
.
And finally, since there is at least one component with edges and every vertex has even degree so at least one component is not a tree,
|E|\geqslant|V|-C+1
.
Therefore,
|V|\leqslant|E|+C-1\leqslant 2q-C+1\leqslant 2q-|W_{\mathrm{iso}}|
.
Therefore, at least one vertex of
W
must have been merged for every element of
|W_{\mathrm{iso}}|
and the inequality
|V(\kappa)|-|S_{\min}|+|W_{\mathrm{iso}}|\leqslant 2q+\lceil\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[2q]}a_{i}/2\rceil
holds for all shapes
\kappa\in K
.

By
Theorem

3.51
, we then have with probability at least
1-\varepsilon^{\prime}
for each
\kappa
,

\left\lVert M_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\leqslant O(1)n^{q+\lceil\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[2q]}a_{i}(\kappa)/2\rceil/2}\left(O(1)\left(|S_{\min}|\log n-\log\varepsilon^{\prime}\right)\right)^{2qd_{H}}\,.

By a union bound,
Equation

6.5
, and taking
\varepsilon^{\prime}=e^{-n^{\lambda}}
, we have with probability
1-O(e^{n^{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma}\mathsf{noise}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leqslant O(1)\sumop\displaylimits_{\kappa\in K}n^{p-2p\lceil\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[2q]}a_{i}(\kappa)/2\rceil/2}\left\lVert\frac{1}{n^{q}}M_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}^{2p}

\displaystyle\leqslant O(n^{p+4pqd_{H}\lambda})\,.

∎

6.2
Certifiable Taylor expansion

We state an elementary combinatorial lemma using Hölder’s inequality that transparently shows that
Lemma

6.1
implies an upper bound on the certified value of the hessian under the maximum entropy constraint.

Lemma 6.2

(Hölder’s inequality for Hessian certificate)
.

Given the certificate in
Lemma

6.1
the following holds by a sum-of-squares proof:

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left\langle\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),\E_{v}\left[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma\right]\right\rangle\right)^{2pq(p-1)}\leqslant\left((1-\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^{2}))2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\frac{1}{k}\right)^{2pq(p-1)}+o_{n}(1)\,.

Proof.

The proof follows by a simple combinatorial argument to choose
p
large enough, so that
C_{p}\approx(1-\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^{2}))
, followed by an application of Hölder’s inequality.
First, note that,

\displaystyle(C_{p/2}\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{p/2})^{2q(p-1)}=\left(\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})C_{p/2}^{1/p}\right)^{2pq(p-1)}

\displaystyle\sim_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:catalan-stirling}}}\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{2pq(p-1)/2}\left(\left(\frac{4^{p/2}}{\sqrt{\pi}\left(\frac{p}{2}\right)^{3/2}}\right)^{1/p}\right)^{2pq(p-1)}

\displaystyle=\left(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\right)^{2pq(p-1)}\left(\left(\frac{2^{3}}{p^{3}(\pi)}\right)^{1/2p}\right)^{2pq(p-1)}

By the maximum entropy constraint,

\left\lVert\E_{v}[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma]\right\rVert^{q}_{q}\leqslant\left(\frac{1+o_{n}(1)}{\delta n}\right)^{q-1}\,,

and using Hölder’s inequality in conjunction with
Lemma

C.11
then yields,

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left\langle\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),\E_{v}\left[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma\right]\right\rangle\right)^{2pq(p-1)}=\frac{1}{k^{2pq(p-1)}}\E_{\sigma}\left(\left[\left\langle\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),\E_{v}\left[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma\right]\right\rangle\right]\right)^{2pq(p-1)}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k^{2pq(p-1)}}\E_{\sigma}\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\right\rVert^{2pq(p-1)}_{p}\left\lVert\E_{v}\left[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma\right]\right\rVert^{2pq(p-1)}_{q}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k^{2pq(p-1)}}\E_{\sigma}\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\right\rVert^{2pq(p-1)}_{p}\left(\frac{1+o_{n}(1)}{\delta n}\right)^{2p(q-1)(p-1)}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k^{2pq(p-1)}}\left((n\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{p/2}C_{p/2})^{2q(p-1)}+o(n^{2q(p-1)})\right)\left(\frac{1+o_{n}(1)}{\delta n}\right)^{2p(q-1)(p-1)}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k^{2pq(p-1)}}\frac{n^{2q+2p-2q}}{n^{2pq-2q}}\left(\frac{\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{pq(p-1)}(C_{p/2})^{2q(p-1)}}{\delta^{2p(q-1)(p-1)}}\right)+o_{n}(1)

\displaystyle=

{}_{\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1}\frac{1}{k^{2pq(p-1)}}\left(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\right)^{2pq(p-1)}\left(\frac{1}{(\delta\sqrt{\pi})^{1/p}(p/2)^{3/(2p)}}\right)^{2pq(p-1)}+o_{n}(1)

We first invoke a series argument (ignoring contributions second-order and higher as
p
will be large) to expand the denominator terms as,

\displaystyle(\delta\sqrt{\pi})^{1/p}=1+\frac{\log(\delta\sqrt{\pi})}{p}+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle(p/2)^{3/2p}=1+\frac{3\log(p/2)}{2p}+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)\,.

We would like the approximation term to be
1-\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon)^{2}\approx\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^{2})}
. Equating the denominator (and ignoring contributions larger than second-order),

\displaystyle 1+\varepsilon+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)=1+\frac{3\log(p/2)}{2p}+\frac{\log(\delta\sqrt{\pi})}{p}+O\left(\frac{\log(p/2)\log(\delta\sqrt{\pi})}{p^{2}}\right)\,.

The first-order solution to the above equation is given by the so-called Lambert-W function
[
Wei02
]
and is,

p(\varepsilon,\delta)=\frac{-3W\left(\frac{-4}{3\pi^{1/3}}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta^{2/3}}\right)}{2\varepsilon}\,.

The choice of
\delta=A(\varepsilon)=O(\varepsilon)
comes from
Corollary

6.7
yielding a final value for
p
as,

p(\varepsilon)=-\frac{3W\left(\frac{-4}{3\pi^{1/3}}\cdot O(\varepsilon^{1/3})\right)}{2\varepsilon}\,.

Note that as
\varepsilon<<1
we can do a Taylor expansion of the Lambert-W function around
0
,

W_{0}(z)=\sumop\displaylimits_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-n)^{n-1}}{n!}z^{n}=z-z^{2}+\frac{3}{2}z^{3}-\frac{8}{3}z^{4}+\dots\,,

yielding,

p(\varepsilon)=-\frac{3}{2\varepsilon}W\left(\frac{-4}{3\pi^{1/3}}\cdot O(\varepsilon^{1/3})\right)=\frac{2}{\pi^{1/3}}O(\varepsilon^{-2/3})-O(\varepsilon^{-1/3})+O(1)\,.

Close to
0^{-}
, the Lambert function is linear in the first-order and, therefore, monotone. This implies that one can scale by a constant factor (smaller than
O(\varepsilon^{-2/3})
) so that
p(\varepsilon)
is an integer and assert that,

\frac{2}{\pi^{1/3}}O(\varepsilon^{-2/3})-O(\varepsilon^{-1/3})+O(1)=p(\varepsilon)\geqslant 2\,,

for a sufficiently small
\varepsilon
. This allows for the conclusion that the Hölder conjugate
q\geqslant 1
, and that,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{(\delta\sqrt{q})^{1/p}(p/2)^{3/2p}}\right)^{2pq}

\displaystyle=1-\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^{2})\implies

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left\langle\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),\E_{v}\left[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma\right]\right\rangle\right)^{2pq}\leqslant\left((1-O(\varepsilon))2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\frac{1}{k}\right)^{2pq}+o_{n}(1)\,.

∎

While it is possible to achieve the upper bound above with
some
choice of
\E[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma]
, as our constraint system restricts this to be a PSD matrix, we must write a small modification of the lemma above to work for an “adjustment” of the hessian (where it is shifted to be PSD with high probability).

Corollary 6.3

(Large correlation of conditional covariance with “shifted” Hessian)
.

Given the certificate in
Lemma

6.1
, the following holds by a sum-of-squares proof:

\E_{\sigma}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left\langle\left(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\Id\right)+\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),\E[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma]\right\rangle\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{k}2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\left(2-\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^{2})\right)+o_{n}(1)\,.

Proof.

We do a binomial expansion on the shifted Hessian and use one more application of Stirling’s approximation of the factorial to get a uniform upper bound on the Catalan numbers, leading to a
slightly
different parameterization of the Lambert-W function to obtain the right choice of
(p(\varepsilon),q(\varepsilon))
-Hölder conjugates that correspond to the desired approximation.

We simplify the proof used above by using the radical
A=\E[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma]^{1/(p-1)}
and using it with an application of
Lemma

D.6
(with
B=\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)+2\nu^{\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\Id_{n}
) and obtain,

\displaystyle\left(\E_{\sigma}\left(\left\langle\left(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\Id\right)+\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),\E[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma]\right\rangle\right)\right)^{p}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leqslant_{\text{Jensen with $p$=even}}\E_{\sigma}\left(\Tr[BA^{p-1}]\right)^{p}\leqslant_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:matrix-p-q-holders}}}\left(\Tr[A^{p}]\right)^{p-1}\E_{\sigma}\Tr[B^{p}]

\displaystyle=(\Tr[\E[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma]^{p/(p-1)}])^{p-1}\E_{\sigma}\Tr[B^{p}]=_{q=p/(p-1)}\left(\Tr[\E[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma]^{q}]\right)^{p-1}\E_{\sigma}\Tr[B^{p}]

\displaystyle=\left(\left\lVert\E[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma]\right\rVert_{q}^{q}\right)^{p-1}\E_{\sigma}\Tr[B^{p}]\leqslant_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:q-norm-cond-cov}}}\left(\frac{1}{(\delta n)^{q-1}}\right)^{p-1}\E_{\sigma}\Tr[B^{p}]=_{1/p+1/q=1}\frac{1}{\delta n}\E_{\sigma}\Tr[B^{p}]

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{Binomial expansion}}\frac{1}{\delta n}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{k=2,\text{even}}^{p}\binom{p}{k}(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2})^{p-k}\E_{\sigma}\Tr\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\right)^{k}\right]+(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2})^{p}n+o(n)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:hessian-schatten-norm}}}\frac{1}{\delta n}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\{2,4,\dots,p\}}\binom{p}{k}(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2})^{p-k}\left(\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{k/2}C_{k/2}+o(n)\right)+(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2})^{p}n+o(n)\right)

\displaystyle=(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2})^{p}\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\left\{\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\{2,4,\dots,p\}}\binom{p}{k}2^{-k}C_{k/2}\right\}+1\right)+o_{n}(1)

\displaystyle=_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:catalan-stirling}}}(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2})^{p}\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\left\{{\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{\pi}}}\sumop\displaylimits_{k\in\{2,4,\dots,p\}}\binom{p}{k}\frac{1}{k^{3/2}}\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)\right)\right\}+1\right)+o_{n}(1)\,,

where we have used semi-algebraic degree reduction (one-sided) of the form as shown in
Lemma

C.7
in the calculation above.

By a calculation similar to
[
Spe14
, Section 5.4]
, using estimates similar to those in
[
Spe14
, Eq. 5.45 Eq. 5.49 & Eq. 5.50]
we have that, for
k^{\prime}=\frac{p+100\sqrt{\ln(p)}}{2}
,

\sumop\displaylimits_{s\in\{k^{\prime},p\}}\binom{p}{s}\frac{1}{s^{3/2}}\leqslant\Theta\left(\frac{2^{p}}{p^{5001}}\right)\,,

using the fact that for the range of
s
above,
\frac{1}{s^{3/2}}\leqslant O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)
. Similarly, by symmetry,

\sumop\displaylimits_{s\in\{0,p-k^{\prime}\}}\binom{p}{s}\frac{1}{s^{3/2}}\leqslant\Theta\left(\frac{2^{p}}{p^{4999}}\right)\,,

with the bound of
\frac{1}{s^{3/2}}\leqslant 1
for the desired range. Since the largest contributions come from the middle terms in the binomial sum, those are bounded as,

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{s\in\{p-k^{\prime},k^{\prime}\}}\binom{p}{s}\frac{1}{s^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{p^{3/2}}+o(1/p^{3/2})\right)\sumop\displaylimits_{s\in\{p-k^{\prime},k^{\prime}\}}\binom{p}{k}\sim\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{p^{3/2}}\binom{p}{p/2}\sumop\displaylimits_{s=\frac{p+c\sqrt{\ln(p)}}{2}\in\{k^{\prime},p-k^{\prime}\}}e^{-(c^{2}/2)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{p^{3/2}}\binom{p}{p/2}\sqrt{p}\intop\nolimits_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-c^{2}/2}}{2}dc=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{p^{3/2}}\binom{p}{p/2}\sqrt{p}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\,

where we used the fact that for
s\in\{p-k^{\prime},k^{\prime}\}
it is the case that,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\left(\frac{p-100\sqrt{\ln(p)}}{2}\right)^{3/2}}=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{(p-100\sqrt{\ln(p)})^{3/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{p^{3/2}\left(1-\frac{100\sqrt{\ln(p)}}{p^{3/2}}\right)}=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{p^{3/2}}\left(1+O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\ln(p)}}{p^{3/2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{p^{3/2}}+O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\ln(p)}}{p^{3}}\right)\,,

for every
p
. Using the Stirling approximation for
\binom{p}{p/2}
again leads to,

\binom{p}{p/2}\sqrt{p}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\sim\frac{2^{p}}{\sqrt{p}}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sqrt{p}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}=2^{p}\,.

A similar analysis can be used to show that the terms above strictly dominate the remaining
o\left(\frac{1}{k^{3/2}}\right)
terms, which continue to contribute on the same order and
\sim
is used to denote precise equality
up to first order
terms in the approximation.

Putting the above bounds altogether leads to the final bounds on the inner-product as,

\displaystyle\left(\E_{\sigma}\left(\left\langle\left(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\Id\right)+\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),\E[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma]\right\rangle\right)\right)^{p}\leqslant(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2})^{p}\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\frac{8}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{2^{p}}{p^{3/2}}+1\right)+o_{n}(1)

\displaystyle\implies_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:ideal-reduction-2p}}}\E_{\sigma}\left\langle\left(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\Id\right)+\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),\E[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma]\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leqslant 2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\frac{8}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{2^{p}}{p^{3/2}}+1\right)+o_{n}(1)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leqslant 2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\frac{8}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{2^{p}}{p^{3/2}}+1\right)\right)^{1/p}+o_{n}(1)\,,

and the goal is to set
p(\varepsilon)
so that,

\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\left(\frac{8}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{2^{p}}{p^{3/2}}+1\right)\right)^{1/p}=(2-\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon)^{2})\,.

Note that,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)^{1/p}\left(1+\left(\frac{2C^{1/p}}{p^{3/2p}}\right)^{p}\right)^{1/p}\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)^{1/p}\left(2\left(\frac{2C^{1/p}}{p^{3/2p}}\right)^{p}\right)^{1/p}=2\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right)^{1/p}\left(\frac{C}{p^{3/2}}\right)^{1/p}=2\left(\frac{(2C)^{1/p}}{\delta^{1/p}p^{3/2p}}\right)\,,

and so the final desired ratio is,

1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+O(\varepsilon^{2})=(\delta)^{1/p}\left(\frac{p}{2C}\right)^{3/2p}\,,

at which point an analysis for the Lambert-W function identical to the one in
Lemma

6.2
gives the desired choice of
p(\varepsilon)=O(\varepsilon^{-2/3})
.
∎

Lemma 6.4

(Taylor Series expansion under maximum-entropy)
.

Given a program iterate
\sigma
and increment
v
from a polynomial grammar satisfying the axioms of the HES SoS relaxation (up to an appropriate constant degree), the following statement holds by a SoS proof:

\E_{v,\sigma}\left(\frac{1}{n}\left(H\left(\sigma+\frac{v}{\sqrt{k}}\right)-H(\sigma)\right)\right)\leqslant(1-O(\varepsilon)-o_{n}(1))\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\frac{1}{k}+O\left(\frac{1}{k^{3}}\right)+o_{n}(1)\,,

(6.6)

with high probability over the randomness of the input.

Proof.

The proof for this follows by combining the following three facts:

1.

The low-degree
p
-Schatten norm SoS certificate of the hessian
\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)
with an appropriately large choice of
p
(
Lemma

6.1
). This conjoined with a Holder’s inequality yields the desired bound.

2.

The gradient term is zero since the first cumulant is zero.

3.

The low-degree SoS certificates that, for all
p\in[3,d_{H}]
, the contributions of
\frac{1}{j!k^{j/2}n}\langle\nabla^{r}H(\sigma_{j}),v^{\otimes r}\rangle
(
Theorem

5.9
) are
\frac{O(\varepsilon^{2})}{k}
. In particular, when
\left\lVert P\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}=n^{\lceil d_{p}/2+1\rceil/2}
(as is the case when each of its coefficients/entries is random, independent, and on the order of
\sqrt{n}
),
k=1
and
\nu=\frac{1}{\delta n}
, we have

\E_{\sigma,u}p(\sigma,u)\leqslant O(d_{p})^{O(d_{p}+i+d)}(\nu n)^{d_{p}/2}n\,.

This immediately implies, with the appropriate choice of
\nu
(and a choice of step precision
\varepsilon
that is
exponentially
small in the desired global precision) that,
\frac{1}{n}\E_{\sigma,u}[p(\sigma,u)]\leqslant\frac{O(\varepsilon^{2})}{k}
.

The Taylor expansion of the
H(\sigma+\frac{v}{\sqrt{k}})
around
\sigma
is given as,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}H\left(\sigma+\frac{v}{\sqrt{k}}\right)=\frac{1}{n}H(\sigma)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}n}\left\langle\nabla H(\sigma),v\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2kn}\left\langle\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),vv^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rangle+\sumop\displaylimits_{r=3}^{d_{H}}\frac{1}{k^{r/2}r!n}\langle\nabla^{r}H(\sigma),v^{\otimes r}\rangle\,.

Applying the conditional expectation operators as well,

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma,v}\left(\frac{1}{n}H\left(\sigma+\frac{v}{\sqrt{k}}\right)-\frac{1}{n}H(\sigma)\right)=\sumop\displaylimits_{j=2}^{d_{H}}\frac{1}{j!k^{j/2}n}\pE_{\sigma,v}\langle\nabla^{r}H(\sigma),v^{\otimes r}\rangle\,,

where we used the fact that the gradient is zero. To isolate the contribution of the derivatives, we will apply
Lemma

C.7
in conjunction with a pseudo-Jensen inequality for even powers. Since there are only
k
steps, only the hessian term will survive while the others will contribute no more than
O(1/k^{3/2})
per step.

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{j=2}^{d_{H}}\frac{1}{j!k^{j/2}n}\E_{\sigma,v}\langle\nabla^{r}H(\sigma),v^{\otimes r}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2k}\E_{\sigma}\left\langle\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),\E_{v}\left[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma\right]\right\rangle+O\left(\frac{1}{k^{3/2}}\right)+o_{n}(1)\,.

We now bound the hessian term.

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{k}\E_{\sigma}\left[\left\langle\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),\E_{v}\left[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma\right]\right\rangle\right]\right)^{2pq(p-1)}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:pseudo-jensen-even}}}\E_{\sigma}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left\langle\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),\E_{v}\left[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma\right]\right\rangle\right)^{2pq(p-1)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left((1-O(\varepsilon))2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\frac{1}{k}\right)^{2pq(p-1)}+o_{n}(1)\,.

Applying
Lemma

C.7
yields,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\left(\E_{\sigma}\left[\left\langle\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma),\E_{v}\left[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\mid\sigma\right]\right\rangle\right]\right)\leqslant\left(\left((1-O(\varepsilon))2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\frac{1}{k}\right)^{2pq(p-1)}+o_{n}(1)\right)^{1/(2pq(p-1))}

\displaystyle=(1-O(\varepsilon))2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\frac{1}{k}\left(1+\frac{o_{n}(1)}{\left((1-O(\varepsilon))2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\frac{1}{k}\right)^{2pq(p-1)}}\right)^{1/(2pq(p-1))}

\displaystyle=_{\text{Taylor expansion}}(1-O(\varepsilon))2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\frac{1}{k}\left(1+\frac{o_{n}(1)}{2pq(-1)\left((1-O(\varepsilon))2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\frac{1}{k}\right)}+o_{n}(1)\right)

\displaystyle=(1-O(\varepsilon))2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\frac{1}{k}\left(1+o_{n}(1)\right)=\left(1-O(\varepsilon)-o_{n}(1)\right)2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\frac{1}{k}\,.

A similar calculation as above for the higher-order derivative certificates provided by
Theorem

5.9
concludes the argument.
∎

We demonstrate that the global optimization stated in the axioms of the program (which is feasible as shown in
Section

7.1
) in conjunction with the upper bound on the (pseudo-expected) increase of the energy at every step concludes a lower bound as well.

Lemma 6.5

(Local maximization via maximum-entropy and global maximization)
.

Given the maximum entropy constraints and the (feasible) constraint of global (hamiltonian) maximization, the following lower bound holds by a SoS proof:

\E_{k,\dots,i}\left[H\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{i}\frac{v_{j}}{\sqrt{k}}\right)-H\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{i-1}\frac{v_{j}}{\sqrt{k}}\right)\right]\geqslant\frac{1}{k}(1-\varepsilon)\nu^{\prime\prime}(i/k)^{1/2}-O(\varepsilon^{2})-O\left(\frac{1}{k^{3/2}}\right)-o_{n}(1)\,.

Proof.

Note that the bounds provided in
Proposition

C.10
are dual sided, and the certificate for the hessian in
Lemma

6.1
controls the deviation. Therefore, combining
Proposition

C.10
and
Lemma

6.1
immediately yields that,

n\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{k/2}C_{k/2}-o(n)\leqslant\E_{\sigma}\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\right\rVert^{k}_{k}\leqslant n\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{k/2}C_{k/2}+o(n)\,.

Given the assumption of global optimality (under moments) which is made possible because of feasibility, that is,

\E\left[H\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}\frac{v_{i}}{\sqrt{k}}\right)\right]\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)\max_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1)}H(\sigma)\sim_{\text{by~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:taylor}}}(1-\varepsilon)\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime\prime}(q)^{1/2}dq+O(\varepsilon)^{2}+o_{n}(1)\,,

and the bound on the higher-order derivatives certified by sum-of-squares to be
O(1/k^{3/2})+o_{n}(1)
(
Theorem

5.9
) allows one to conclude by simple linear manipulation that,

\E\left[H\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{k}\frac{v_{j}}{\sqrt{k}}\right)\right]-\E\left[H\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{k-1}\frac{v_{j}}{\sqrt{k}}\right)\right]\geqslant\frac{1}{k}(1-\varepsilon)\left(\nu^{\prime\prime}(1)^{1/2}\right)-O(\varepsilon^{2})-O\left(\frac{1}{k^{3/2}}\right)-o_{n}(1)\,,

where the contribution is as is because one subtracts all steps but the last one to get the relevant contribution from the Hessian.
∎

6.3
Matrix Bernstein approximation bound via analytic sparsity

Note that the
true
Hessian is distributed as
\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert)^{1/2}\mathsf{GOE}(n)
with high probability. Consequently, it is traceless. However, in order to analyze the rounding scheme introduced in the companion paper, it is critical show that the Bernstein polynomial has trace
\approx 1
. To do this, we need to take the Bernstein polynomial of the
shifted
Hessian
2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\Id_{n}+\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)=p\left(\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\right)
and invoke
Lemma

6.6
(proved below). This allows one to conclude that, for an appropriate choice of the ramp (fixed in
Corollary

6.7
), the trace of the (appropriately) normalized (divided by
1/(\delta n)
) Bernstein polynomial can be made
\approx 1
.

Lemma 6.6

(Equivalence with semicircle moments)
.

Let
X(\sigma)=M(\sigma)/(2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2})^{1/2})
for a symmetric matrix
M(\sigma)
with entries that are low-degree polynomials in
\sigma
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a pseudo-distribution over high-entropy step distributions over
\sigma
satisfying the conclusions of
Lemma

6.1
and let
p
be a degree-
d
polynomial.
Then, for all positive integer
r
,

\{\text{HES Constraints}\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\E_{\sigma}\left(\left[\Tr\frac{1}{n}p(X(\sigma))\right]-n\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[p(x)\right]\right)^{2r}\leqslant o(n^{2r})\,\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2r}

where
\mu_{sc}
is the Wigner semicircle law given by
\mu_{sc}(x)=\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}
.

Proof.

We write
p(\cdot)
in the monomial basis, and then apply a Cauchy-Schwarz inequality on the summands to separate the constants from the SoS program variables (which are functions of
\sigma
). This is followed by an application of
Lemma

6.1
in concurrence with
Lemma

C.4
. We will crucially use the facts that the moment variable
r=O_{\varepsilon}(1)
and that the degree of the polynomial
d=O_{\varepsilon}(1)
.

First, we define

\displaystyle y_{i}(\sigma):=\Tr\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}X(\sigma)\right)^{i}\right]-n\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[x^{i}\right]\,.

Then, for all bounded
i
and bounded even
j
,

\E\left[y_{i}(\sigma)^{j}\right]\leqslant o(n^{j})\,,

(6.7)

which follows directly from
Lemma

6.1
.

Expressing
p(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}c_{i}x^{i}
and using linearity of the trace and expectation immediately gives

\displaystyle\E\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}\Tr[p(X(\sigma))]-\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}[p(x)]\right)^{2r}\right]

\displaystyle=\E\left[\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}c_{i}\Tr\left[(\frac{1}{n}X(\sigma))^{i}\right]-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}c_{i}\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}[x^{i}]\right)^{2r}\right]

\displaystyle=\E\left[\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}c_{i}y_{i}(\sigma)\right)^{2r}\right]\,.

Applying the strategy of first isolating the terms containing the program variables
\left(\{y_{i}(\sigma))\}_{i=0}^{d}\right)
from the constant terms that come from the coefficients
\{c_{i}\}_{i=0}^{d}
via an application of the Cauchy-Schwarz (CS) inequality yields,

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}c_{i}y_{i}(\sigma)\right)^{2r}\right]=\E_{\sigma\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(\left\langle\mathbf{c},\mathbf{y(\sigma)}\right\rangle^{2}\right)^{r}\right]\overset{\mathsf{CS}}{\leqslant}\pE_{\sigma\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}c_{i}^{2}\right)^{r}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}y^{2}_{i}(\sigma)\right)^{r}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}c_{i}^{2}\right)^{r}\E_{\sigma\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}y_{i}^{2}(\sigma)\right)^{r}\right]=\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2r}_{2}\E_{\sigma\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\sumop\displaylimits_{k_{0}+\dots+k_{d}=r}\binom{r}{k_{0},\dots,k_{d}}y^{2k_{0}}_{0}(\sigma)\cdots y^{2k_{d}}_{d}(\sigma)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:l1-to-lt}}}\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2r}_{2}\E_{\sigma\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[d^{r-1}\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}y_{i}^{2r}\right]\leqslant\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{2}^{2r}d^{r}o(n^{2r})=o(n^{2r})\left\lVert p\right\rVert_{2}^{2r}\,.

(6.8)

Since
d,r=O_{\varepsilon}(1)
, this yields that,

\displaystyle\E\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}\Tr[p(X(\sigma))]-\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}[p(x)]\right)^{2r}\right]\leqslant\left\lVert p\right\rVert^{2r}_{2}f(\varepsilon)o(n^{2r})\,,

for some bounded function
f(\varepsilon)
, concluding the proof.
∎

Alternatively, a better dependency on the constants can be obtained using
Lemma

C.13
, however, it is irrelevant to the use case.

Corollary 6.7

(Large Hilbert-Schmidt Correlation between Bernstein Polynomial and Hessian)
.

Given the Bernstein matrix polynomial
B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}
as defined in
Proposition

A.2
, it is the case that for any high-entropy step distribution
\mathcal{D}
,

\{\text{HES Constraints}\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\E_{\sigma\sim\mathcal{D}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\left[\langle B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(X),X\rangle\right]-\psi\right)^{2p}\leqslant o_{n}(1)\,,

(6.9)

where
a\Id_{n}\preceq X\preceq b\Id_{n}
and
\psi\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
.

Proof.

To compute the correlation with the matrix Bernstein polynomial that approximates the ramp function, we will use the definition of the Bernstein polynomial followed by linearity (again) in conjunction with the bounds above.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left[\left\langle B^{L_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{d}(X),X\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\Tr\left(X\cdot B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}_{d}(X)\right),

By
Lemma

6.6
,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]}\right)^{2p}\E_{\sigma\sim\mathcal{D}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\left[\langle B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(X),X\rangle\right]-\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[x\cdot B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]\right)^{2p}\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]}\right)^{2p}o_{n}(1)\leqslant o_{n}(1)\,,

provided that
\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]=C(\varepsilon)
and
p=O_{\varepsilon}(1)
, where the former will follow from
Lemma

B.1
with the appropriate choice of constants made later.

This reduces the goal to showing that,

\frac{\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[x\cdot B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]}{\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]}\geqslant 1-\varepsilon\,,

(6.10)

for an appropriate choice of
d(\varepsilon)
. Since this does not involve any SoS program variable, it need not be a SoS proof.

By the definition in
Appendix

A
and appropriately rescaling the interval
\left[-2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2},2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}\right]\to[-1,1]
and denoting the re-scaled area of the semi-circle containing the valid fraction of eigenvectors as

\displaystyle\phi:=\frac{A(\varepsilon)}{2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}}\,,

we have the following,

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}0\,,&x\in\left[-1,\,1-\phi\right),\\
\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}(x-(1-\phi))\,,&x\in\left[1-\phi,\,1-\phi+\phi^{2}\right],\\
1\,,&x\in\left(1-\phi+\phi^{2},\,1\right],\end{array}\right.

where
A(\varepsilon)
is chosen later. The Weierstrass approximation (
Theorem

3.29
) gives,

\displaystyle\|B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)-L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}(x)\|\leqslant\varepsilon\,,\forall x\in[-1,1]\,,

provided
\mathsf{deg}(B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(\cdot))\geqslant O\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{3}}\right)
.

Average correlation between
x
and
B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)
under
\mu_{sc}

We now compute the scalar proxy for the correlation between the hessian and the matrix Bernstein polynomial under the semi-circle measure. This will be normalized appropriately using
Lemma

B.1
.

\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[x\cdot B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]=\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1}x\cdot B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\geqslant\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1-\phi}x\left(L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}+\varepsilon\right)\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}dx+\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1}x\left(L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}(x)-\varepsilon\right)\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}dx

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1-\phi}xd\mu_{sc}(x)-\varepsilon\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1}xd\mu_{sc}(x)+\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}x\cdot\left(\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}(x-(1-\phi))\right)d\mu_{sc}(x)+\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}^{1}xd\mu_{sc}(x)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\varepsilon\left(\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1-\phi}xd\mu_{sc}(x)-\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1}xd\mu_{sc}(x)\right)}_{\mathsf{I}(\varepsilon,\phi)}+\underbrace{\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}x\cdot\left(\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}(x-(1-\phi))\right)d\mu_{sc}(x)}_{\mathsf{II}(\phi)}+\underbrace{\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}^{1}xd\mu_{sc}(x)}_{\mathsf{III}(\phi)}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{-2\varepsilon}{3\pi}\left((1-(1-\phi)^{2})^{3/2}+(1-(1-\phi)^{2})^{3/2}\right)+\frac{2}{3\pi}\left(1-(1-\phi+\phi^{2})^{2}\right)^{3/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3\pi}\left[(1-(1-\phi+\phi^{2})^{2})^{3/2}-2\varepsilon\phi^{3/2}(2-\phi)^{3/2}\right]

where the first inequality follows from using the closeness of the Bernstein polynomial to the
L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}
function, and the final inequality follows from the observation that
\mathsf{II}(\phi)\geqslant 0
and the use of
Proposition

3.41
to evaluate
\mathsf{I}(\varepsilon,\phi)
and
\mathsf{III}(\phi)
.

The final lower bound

Combining the upper bound on
\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]
via
Lemma

B.1
with the lower bound on the correlation term computed above yields,

\displaystyle\frac{\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[x\cdot B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]}{\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\frac{2}{3\pi}\left[(1-(1-\phi+\phi^{2})^{2})^{3/2}-2\varepsilon\phi^{3/2}(2-\phi)^{3/2}\right]}{1+\varepsilon-\mathsf{CDF}(1-\phi)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{2}{3\pi}\left[(2\phi+2\phi^{3}-3\phi^{2}-\phi^{4})^{3/2}-2\varepsilon\phi^{3/2}(2-\phi)^{3/2}\right]}{\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+o(\phi^{3/2})}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\frac{2}{3\pi}2^{3/2}\phi^{3/2}\left((1-4\phi)^{3/2}-\frac{2}{2^{3/2}}\varepsilon(2-\phi)^{3/2}\right)}{\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+o(\phi^{3/2})}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}\left(\left(1-\frac{3}{2}4\phi+O(\phi^{2})\right)-2\varepsilon\right)}{\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+o(\phi^{3/2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-20\phi-2\varepsilon}{1+o(\phi)}\,.

For completeness, we also demonstrate an upper bound of (roughly) the same quantitative strength on the normalized correlation between
x
and
B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)
under
\mu_{sc}
. To do this, we utilize a lower bound on the normalization term given in
Lemma

B.1
and compute an upper bound on
x\cdot B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)
using the uniform upper bound of
\phi^{2}
on the ramp part.

The final upper bound

A similar computation as above yields,

\displaystyle\frac{\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[x\cdot B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]}{\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]}\leqslant\frac{\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1}x\cdot\left(L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}(x)+\varepsilon\right)d\mu_{sc}(x)}{(1+2\varepsilon)\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2})+\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1-\phi}x\cdot 0d\mu_{sc}(x)+\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}\frac{x}{\phi^{2}}\cdot\left(x-(1-\phi)\right)d\mu_{sc}(x)+\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}^{1}xd\mu_{sc}(x)+\varepsilon\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[x\right]}{(1+2\varepsilon)\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2})+\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}xd\mu_{sc}(x)+\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}^{1}xd\mu_{sc}(x)}{(1+2\varepsilon)\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2})+\varepsilon}=\frac{\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1}xd\mu_{sc}(x)}{(1+2\varepsilon)\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2})+\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{2}{3\pi}\left(1-(1-\phi)^{2}\right)^{3/2}}{(1+2\varepsilon)\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2})+\varepsilon}=\frac{\frac{2}{3\pi}\left(\phi(2-\phi)\right)^{3/2}}{(1+2\varepsilon)\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2})+\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\frac{2}{3\pi}2^{3/2}\phi^{3/2}}{(1+2\varepsilon)\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2})+\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{(1+3\varepsilon)+O(\phi)+o(\phi)}\,.

A choice of
A(\varepsilon)=O(\varepsilon)
suffices for the desired approximation ratios, yielding
\phi=\frac{O(\varepsilon)}{2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})^{1/2}}
.
∎

7
Feasibility

In this section we will demonstrate that a randomized version of Subag’s Hessian ascent algorithm (
Algorithm

2
) can be cast as a HES process (
Lemma

7.1
). Doing this demands showing that the conditional covariance matrix, which basically projects in the
\delta
n-dimensional subspace corresponding to the top part of the eigenspectrum of the Hessian, has bounded operator norm and is close to a matrix-low degree polynomial. This low-degree matrix polynomial will be the Bernstein matrix polynomial introduced in
Section

6.3
.

As a minor technical detail, since the HES process on the unit sphere is described as an appropriately scaled set of small Gaussian steps, we will need a lemma that converts between the moments of the real Haar measure on the unit sphere and scaled Gaussian moments (
Lemma

7.2
).

7.1
Feasibility via a near-optimal randomized algorithm

To show feasibility we will work with
Algorithm

2
, which is a variant of Subag’s algorithm (
Algorithm

1
). Fix
0<\varepsilon<1
and
A(\varepsilon)
.

v_{1}\sim\mathcal{S}^{A(\varepsilon)n-1}(1)
.

w_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}v_{1}
.

while

i\in\{2,\dots,k\}

do

v_{i}\sim\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{eigen_{A(\varepsilon)n-1}}(\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(w_{i-1}))}(1)

w_{i}=w_{i-1}+\frac{v_{i}}{\sqrt{k}}
.

i\leftarrow i+1
.

end

while

Output
v_{k}
.

Algorithm 2

Randomized Hessian-Ascent
[
Hua22
]

The only difference between
Algorithm

1
and
Algorithm

2
is that
x
is chosen uniformly at random from the unit sphere on the
\delta n
-dimensional subspace corresponding to the top eigenvectors of the Hessian projected orthogonal to
v_{i-1}
as
\nabla^{2}_{\perp}H(v_{i-1})
. Critically,
Algorithm

2
continues to be nearly optimal for the same reason as the original algorithm is
[
Sub21
, Lemma 3 & Theorem 4]
in conjunction with the fact that the first step can be arbitrary. A more detailed explanation for the equivalence between these two algorithms can be found in
[
Hua22
, Section 3.2.7]
.

Consequently, showing feasibility amounts to showing that
Algorithm

2
satisfies, at every iteration
i\in[k]
, the constraints of the SoS program at the corresponding cumulant SoS variable. Demonstrating this is done by showing that:

1.

The uniform distribution on the unit-sphere satisfies the conditional maximum-entropy constraints imposed on valid pseudo-distributions at every iterate
i\in[k]
.

2.

The cumulative norm increments have the same norms
and
are incrementally orthogonal. This is trivially true by construction and is omitted in the formal proof.

3.

The conditional pseudo-expectation of the quadratic form of the hessian under
v_{i}
is at least
(1-\varepsilon)2\nu^{\prime\prime}(q_{i})
.

The statements above are proved formally in
Lemma

7.1
below.

Lemma 7.1

(Feasibility of the SoS program)
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<1
and
A(\varepsilon)
, the following facts hold:

1.

For
i=1,\dots,k
,

\displaystyle\left\{\widetilde{\kappa}_{{j}}\left[{v_{i}\,\mid\,v_{i-1}}\right]=o_{n}(1)\right\}_{1,\dots,i-2}\,,j\neq 2\,,

\displaystyle\left\{\left\lVert\E[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\,\mid\,v_{i-1}]\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\leqslant\frac{1+o_{n}(1)}{A(\varepsilon)n}\right\}_{\mid 1,\dots,i-2}\,.

2.

For
i=1,\dots,k
,

\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mathcal{S}^{A(\varepsilon)n-1}(\mathsf{eigen}\frac{1}{n}(\nabla^{2}_{\perp}H(w_{i-1})))}[x^{\mathsf{T}}\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}_{\perp}H(w_{i-1})x]\geqslant 2\nu^{\prime\prime}(q)^{1/2}-\varepsilon\,.

Proof.

Note that every probability distribution is a pseudo-distribution, since
\E_{x}[1]=1
(by normalization of probability) and
\E_{x}[p^{2}(x)]\geqslant 0
for any polynomial
p
since it can be viewed as an inner product between non-negative quantities. Therefore, we set
\pE=\E_{x}
to show that the maximum-entropy constraints bounds are satisfied.
[
Sub21
, Lemma 3]
immediately implies that the expected quadratic form of the hessian is arbitrarily close to the largest eigenvalue under the uniform distribution on the sphere of the top
A(\varepsilon)n
-dimensional subspace of the Hessian orthogonal to the current iterate.

The
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}
constraint

Proving this constraint amounts to showing that the operator norm of the covariance of
v_{i}
conditioned on
v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1}
is bounded from above by
\frac{1}{A(\varepsilon)n}
.
Since
v_{i}\sim\mathcal{S}^{A(\varepsilon)n-1}(\mathsf{eigen}(\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}_{\perp}H(w_{i-1})))
, note that conditioned on
v_{1},\dots,v_{i-2}
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\E[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\,\mid\,v_{i-1}]\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\E_{v\sim\mathcal{S}^{A(\varepsilon)n-1}(\mathsf{eigen}(\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}_{\perp}H(w_{i-1})))}\left[vv^{\mathsf{T}}\right]\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}

\displaystyle=_{\text{Rotational Invariance of Haar-Measure}}\left\lVert\E_{v^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1^{A(\varepsilon)n}\oplus\,0^{(1-A(\varepsilon))n})}\left[v^{\prime}v^{\prime\mathsf{T}}\right]\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}

\displaystyle=_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:denom-conc} with }q=1}\left\lVert\frac{1-o_{n}(1)}{A(\varepsilon)n}\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Id_{A(\varepsilon)n}\oplus 0_{(1-A(\varepsilon))n})}\left[xx^{\mathsf{T}}\right]\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-o_{n}(1)}{A(\varepsilon)n}\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}1&\\
&\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 7.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{$.$}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{$.$}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$.$}\mkern 1.0mu}&\\
&&1\\
&&&0&\\
&&&&\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 7.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{$.$}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{$.$}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$.$}\mkern 1.0mu}&\\
&&&&&0\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\leqslant\frac{1+o_{n}(1)}{A(\varepsilon)n}\,,

where in the above calculation
v^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1^{A(\varepsilon)n}\oplus 0^{(1-A(\varepsilon))n})
refers to a unit vector chosen uniformly at random from the
A(\varepsilon)n
-dimensional sphere where the last
(1-A(\varepsilon))n
-coordinates are
0
.

The
\kappa_{j}[v_{i}\mid v_{i-1}]
constraint

To show this constraint is satisfied, we follow a similar strategy. Note that,
\forall j\neq 2
, the following holds for every entry of the
j
-th conditional cumulant tensor,

\displaystyle\kappa_{j}[v_{i}\mid v_{i-1}]

\displaystyle=_{\text{by moment-cumulant formula}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}(k_{1},\dots,k_{j})}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\prodop\displaylimits_{b\in\pi}\E\left[\prodop\displaylimits_{a\in b}v_{i_{a}}\right]

\displaystyle=_{\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:denom-conc} and rotational invariance}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}(k_{1},\dots,k_{j})}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\prodop\displaylimits_{b\in\pi}\frac{1-o_{n}(1)}{\left(A(\varepsilon)n\right)^{|b|}}\E\left[\prodop\displaylimits_{a\in b}v^{\prime}_{i_{a}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}(k_{1},\dots,k_{j})}\frac{(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!(1-o_{n}(1))}{(A(\varepsilon)n)^{j}}\prodop\displaylimits_{b\in\pi}\E\left[\prodop\displaylimits_{a\in b}v^{\prime}_{i_{a}}\right]\,,

where in the above calculation we again used
3.25
. Now, since this is a sum of the form,

\sumop\displaylimits_{\pi}\frac{(1-o_{n}(1))}{(\delta n)^{j}}a_{\pi}\,,

we can conclude that,

\left\lVert\kappa_{j}[v_{i}\mid v_{i-1}]-\kappa_{j}\left[\mathcal{N}(0,\Id_{A(\varepsilon)n\oplus 0_{(1-A(\varepsilon))n}})\right]\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant o_{n}(1)\,.

The approximate-LDP constraint

We now show that the original algorithm’s updates satisfy the approximate-LDP constraint in the program by demonstrating that the choice of polynomial corresponding to a Bernstein polynomial of sufficiently large (but constant) degree has the following properties:

1.

It permits a (weakly) orthogonal decomposition using the Hermite-Weiner fourier basis, by virtue of being square integrable on
\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1)
.

2.

By
Proposition

A.2
, it is arbitrarily close in Frobenius norm to the projector into the top
A(\varepsilon)n
-dimensional eigenspace of
\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)
.

Note that any
f\in\ell_{2}(\Sigma)
can be expressed as,

f(x)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\left\langle f,\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(x\mid\Sigma)\right\rangle_{\ell^{2}(\Sigma)}\mathsf{He}_{\alpha}(x\mid\Sigma)\,.

Note that for
x=\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{i-1}v_{j}
with
\left\lVert v_{j}\right\rVert^{2}=1
and
f=p_{d}:=B^{d}_{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}\left(c_{i}\Id_{n}+\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\right)
with the Fourier decomposition taken element-wise in the matrix polynomial, it is the case that,

\displaystyle\left\lVert f_{ij}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}:=\langle f_{ij}(x),f_{ij}(x)\rangle_{\ell^{2}(\Sigma)}=\intop\nolimits_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)}\frac{1}{\delta n}B^{d}_{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}\left(c_{i}(\Id_{n})_{ij}+\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(x)_{ij}\right)dg(x)<\infty\,.

Since the algorithm samples uniformly at random from the
A(\varepsilon)n
-dimensional sphere corresponding to the top eigenspace of
\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)
, we can then apply
Lemma

7.2
to conclude that the appropriately scaled conditional covariance of this algorithm is basically a projector into this subspace times the identity matrix, and a triangle inequality then immediately implies feasibility as demonstrated below:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\E\left[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mid w_{i-1}\right]-p_{d}(w_{i-1})\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\left\lVert\E\left[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mid w_{i-1}\right]-\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha,|\alpha|\leqslant d}\langle\E\left[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mid w_{i-1}\right],\mathsf{He}_{d}\rangle_{\ell^{2}(\Pi_{\delta n})}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\E\left[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mid w_{i-1}\right]-\frac{1}{\delta n}B^{d}(c_{i}\Id_{n}+\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma))\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{triangle inequality}}\left\lVert\E\left[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mid w_{i-1}\right]-\frac{1}{\delta n}\Pi_{\delta n}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}+\frac{1}{\delta n}\left\lVert\Pi_{\delta n}-\frac{1}{\delta n}B^{d}(c_{i}\Id_{n}+\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma))\right\rVert^{2}_{F}

\displaystyle=_{\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:matrix-bernstein-linear-ramp}}}\left\lVert\E\left[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mid w_{i-1}\right]-\Pi_{\delta n}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}+o_{n}(1)

\displaystyle=_{\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:denom-conc}}}\left\lVert\frac{1-o_{n}(1)}{\delta n}\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\,\Pi_{\mathsf{eigen}\left(\nabla^{2}_{\perp}H(w_{i-1})\right)}\right)}[(xx^{\mathsf{T}})]-\frac{1}{\delta n}\Pi_{\delta n}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+o_{n}(1)

\displaystyle=_{\E[xx^{\mathsf{T}}]=\Pi_{\delta n}}\left\lVert\frac{1-o(n(1))}{\delta n}\Pi_{\delta n}-\frac{1}{\delta n}\Pi_{\delta n}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+o_{n}(1)

\displaystyle=\left\lVert-\frac{o_{n}(1)}{\delta n}\Pi_{\delta n}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+o_{n}(1)=o_{n}(1)+o_{n}(1)=o_{n}(1)\,,

where we used the fact that the covariance of the Gaussian is given by an orthogonal transformation into the top
\delta n
-dimensional subspace of
\nabla^{2}H(w_{i-1})
, which in this case is just
\Pi_{\delta n}=\Pi_{\mathsf{eigen}\left(\nabla^{2}_{\perp}H(w_{i-1})\right)}
.
For every
q\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\E[(v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes q}\mid w_{i-1}]

\displaystyle=_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:denom-conc}}}\frac{1-o_{n}(1)}{\left(\delta n\right)^{q}}\E_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Id_{A(\varepsilon)n}\oplus 0_{(1-A(\varepsilon))n})}\left[(xx^{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes q}\right]

\displaystyle=_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:isserlis}}}(q-1)!!\,\mathrm{sym}\left[\left(\E[xx^{\mathsf{T}}]\right)^{\otimes q}\right]

\displaystyle=(q-1)!!\,\mathrm{sym}\left[\left((1-o_{n}(1))\E[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mid w_{i-1}]\right)^{\otimes q}\right]

\displaystyle=(1-o_{n}(1))(q-1)!!\,\mathrm{sym}\left[\left(\E[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mid w_{i-1}]\right)^{\otimes q}\right]\,.

Rewrite the conditional covariance as
\E[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mid w_{i-1}]=p(w_{i-1})+B
where
B
is a matrix with
o_{n}(1)
Frobenius norm (as shown above). This yields that,

\E[(v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes q}\mid w_{i-1}]=(1-o_{n}(1))(q-1)!!\,\mathrm{sym}\left[\left(p(w_{i-1})+B\right)^{\otimes q}\right]\,,

and, therefore,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\E[(v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes q}\mid w_{i-1}]-p(w_{i-1})^{\otimes q}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant(q-1)!!\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[q],j=q-i}\mathrm{sym}\left(B^{\otimes i}\otimes p(w_{i-1})^{\otimes j}\right)\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{$\mathrm{sym}$ is contractive}}(q-1)!!\left\lVert\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[q],j=q-i}B^{\otimes i}\otimes p(w_{i-1})^{\otimes j}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:l1-to-lt}}}q^{2}(q-1)!!\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in[q],j=q-i}\left\lVert B^{\otimes i}\otimes p(w_{i-1})^{\otimes j}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:scalar-am-gm} + \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:matrix-bernstein-linear-ramp}}}\frac{q^{3}(q-1)!!}{\delta^{q}}\frac{1}{n^{q}}\,.

Soundness & Optimality of Taylor Expansion

The last step is to invoke soundness for all distributions (
Section

4.2.10
) which implies that the corresponding pseudo-distribution comprising the low-degree moments of the “Subag distribution” implicit in
Algorithm

2
satisfies the entropy constraints. Invoking
[
ASZ20
, Corollary 59]
in conjunction with a Taylor series argument immediately implies that the hessian has the largest contribution, and the final energy is lower-bounded by
\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{k}\nu^{\prime\prime}(q_{i})^{1/2}-\varepsilon\cdot k(\varepsilon)-O\left(\frac{1}{k(\varepsilon)^{1/2}}\right)
, which is arbitrarily close to
\intop\nolimits_{0}^{1}\nu^{\prime\prime}(q)^{1/2}dq
with the appropriate choice of
k(\varepsilon)
. The upper bound follows from
Lemma

3.7
, as no configuration can have value greater than the true maximum.
∎

7.2
Equivalence of rescaled spherical and Gaussian moments

We state and prove a small lemma that allows us to freely go between the low-degree moments of the uniform measure on the real unit sphere and an appropriate rescaling of the comparable Gaussian moments.

Lemma 7.2

(Equivalence of re-scaled gaussian and uniform moments)
.

Given
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Id_{n})
and
v\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1))
, the following holds,

\E_{v}[(vv^{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes q}]=\frac{(1-O_{n}(1/n))}{n^{q}}\E_{x}[(xx^{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes q}]\,.

(7.1)

Proof.

The proof will use the rotational invariance of both distributions and an explicit entry-by-entry comparison between the rescaled Gaussian moment matrix and the real Haar distribution moment matrix.

\displaystyle\E_{x}[(xx^{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes q}]=\sumop\displaylimits_{a_{1},\dots,a_{2q}=1}^{n}\E_{x}[x_{a_{1}}\cdots x_{a_{2q}}](e_{a_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{a_{q}})(e_{a_{q+1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{a_{2q}})^{\mathsf{T}}\,.

By exactly the same argument,

\displaystyle\E_{v}[(vv^{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes q}]=\sumop\displaylimits_{a_{1},\dots,a_{2q}=1}^{n}\E_{v}[v_{a_{1}}\cdots v_{a_{2q}}](e_{a_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{a_{q}})(e_{a_{q+1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{a_{2q}})^{\mathsf{T}}\,.

It is elementary to see from
Lemma

3.10
and the definition of the
\Gamma
-function that the
2m_{i}
-th moment of
x_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is,

\E_{x_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}[x^{2m_{i}}_{i}]=(2m_{i}-1)(2m_{i}-3)\cdots(3)(1)=\frac{(2m_{i})!}{2^{m_{i}}m_{i}!}=\frac{\Gamma(m_{i}+1/2)2^{m_{i}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\,.

(7.2)

Symmetry implies that the odd moments of the
1
-marginals of the uniform distribution are
0
. By

[
Fol01
, Theorem, Pg. 2]
, the even moments are

\displaystyle\E_{v}[v_{a_{1}}\cdots v_{a_{2q}}]

\displaystyle=\E_{v}\left[\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{\ell}(v_{i}^{2})^{m_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{\ell}(v_{i}^{2})^{m_{i}}\right)d\sigma=\frac{\intop\nolimits_{v\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1)}v^{\alpha}d\sigma}{\intop\nolimits_{v\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1)}d\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\Gamma(m_{1}+1/2)\Gamma(m_{2}+1/2)\cdots\Gamma(m_{\ell}+1/2)\left(\Gamma(1/2)\right)^{n-\ell}}{\Gamma(q+n/2)}\frac{\Gamma(n/2)}{2\Gamma(1/2)^{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\E_{x}[x_{a_{1}}\cdots x_{a_{2q}}]}{2^{q}}\frac{\Gamma(n/2)}{\Gamma(q+n/2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\E_{x}[x_{a_{1}}\cdots x_{a_{2q}}]}{n(n+2)(n+4)\cdots(n+2q-2)}\,,

(7.3)

where
2(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{\ell})=2q
with
m_{i}
denoting the number of times an index
a_{i}\in[n]
appears in
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{2q}\}
and
\ell
being the number of distinct indices.
Combining
Section

7.2
and
Equation

7.2
immediately yields that,

\E_{v}[v_{a_{1}}\cdots v_{a_{2q}}]=\frac{(1-O_{n}(1/n))}{n^{q}}\E_{x}[x_{a_{1}}\cdots x_{a_{2}q}]\,,

(7.4)

for every
a_{1},\dots,a_{2q}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
∎

8
Local iterative algorithms vs. HES SoS hierarchy

In this section, we provide three concrete examples of inputs from a “modified” spherical spin glass ensemble, and then give proofs for the first two (and a sketch for for the latter) of the sub-optimality of local iterative algorithms on these (such as Hessian ascent
[
Sub21
]
and its randomized generalization (
Algorithm

2
)) along with arguments for why the HES SoS relaxation provides certificates with better values. The last example, in particular, motivates the conjecture that the HES SoS relaxation is capable of “disentangling” arbitrary correlations in the input, provided they are sufficiently smooth.

8.1
Invariance with respect to the spherical ideal

The first example is one that “fools” the randomized version (
Algorithm

2
) of the Hessian ascent
[
Sub21
]
algorithm by a scaling of the degree-2 component.

Example 8.1

(Homogeneous degree scaling)
.

Let the Hamiltonian
H
be,

H(\sigma)=\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{100}_{2}H_{2}(\sigma)+H_{4}(\sigma)\,,

for some choice of
\alpha>0
. Then, the algorithm given in
Algorithm

2
is provably suboptimal, but the HES SoS relaxation does better.

Proof.

A proof sketch for the claim above is given, whereby the geometry and scaling of the top
\delta n
-dimensional eigenspace of the Hessian is calculated, and then it is demonstrated that
Algorithm

2
initially follows the
incorrect
direction, thereby accumulating an incorrect amount of energy before it changes direction. However, the HES relaxation is able to use the Schatten
p
-norm of the collective Hessian to “distinguish” between the space where the large (and dominant) amount of signal from the top part of the spectrum resides, while the rank-
1
terms contribute signal that is suppressed by the moment scalings implied by the HES constraints.

Eigenspectrum of the Hessian

When
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(1)
, note that
H(\sigma)
is indistinguishable from an instance of a spherical spin glass Hamiltonian.
The Hessian of
H(\sigma)
is,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)=

\displaystyle\,\,\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{100}_{2}\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H_{2}(\sigma)+\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H_{4}(\sigma)+100\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{98}\frac{1}{n}H_{2}(\sigma)\Id_{n}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{9800\alpha\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{96}\frac{1}{n}H_{2}(\sigma)\sigma\sigma^{\mathsf{T}}+100\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{98}\left(\sigma\left(\nabla\frac{1}{n}H_{2}(\sigma)\right)^{\mathsf{T}}+\frac{1}{n}\nabla H_{2}(\sigma)\sigma^{\mathsf{T}}\right)}_{\text{rank-1 terms}}\,.

By
[
Sub21
, Proof of Lemma-3]
, it is clear that,

\alpha\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{100}\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H_{2}(\sigma)\sim\sqrt{2\alpha_{2}}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{50}\mathsf{GOE}(n)\,,\text{ and},\,\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H_{4}(\sigma)\sim\sqrt{12}\mathsf{GOE}(n)\,.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle H_{2}(\sigma)\leqslant\sup_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2})}H_{2}(\sigma)=_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:frsb-equivalent}}}\sqrt{2}\intop\nolimits_{0}^{\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}dx=\sqrt{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\,.

This immediately implies that
all
the (redundant) eigenvalues of
100\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{98}_{2}\frac{1}{n}H_{2}(\sigma)\Id_{n}
are no more than
100\sqrt{2}\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{100}_{2}
.

Suboptimality of the rank-
1
components

Suboptimality of the rank-
1
terms is a consequence of the analysis of Subag
[
Sub18
,
Sub21
]
but can also be rigorously justified by noticing that they have large alignment with the gradient which leads to larger steps (under the HES constraints) and sub-optimal gain of energy after a certain number of steps
8
8

8

This can be made rigorous via an analysis based on a Taylor expansion argument which gives a precise iterate
i
after which it becomes suboptimal to follow the gradient term.
.

Threshold for suboptimality of local algorithm
