We show the sub-optimality of the variant of Subag’s algorithm stated in
Algorithm

2
. Note that, since
v\sim\mathcal{S}^{\mathsf{eigen}_{\delta n}(\nabla^{2}H(\sigma))}(1)_{\perp\sigma}
, by construction,

\displaystyle\left\langle 9800\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{96}\frac{1}{n}H_{2}(\sigma)\sigma\sigma^{\mathsf{T}}+100\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{98}\left(\sigma\left(\nabla\frac{1}{n}H_{2}(\sigma)\right)^{\mathsf{T}}+\frac{1}{n}\nabla H_{2}(\sigma)\sigma^{\mathsf{T}}\right),vv^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=0+\left\langle 9800\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\sigma\left(\frac{1}{n}H_{2}(\sigma)\right)^{\mathsf{T}},vv^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rangle+0=0+0+9800\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\left\langle\frac{1}{n}H(\sigma),v\right\rangle\langle\sigma,v\rangle=0\,.

Consequently, the algorithm chooses the next step uniformly at random from the unit sphere in the space spanned by the top
\delta n
-dimensional eigenspace of
\alpha\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}_{\perp\sigma}H_{2}(\sigma)+\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}_{\perp\sigma}H_{4}(\sigma)+100\alpha\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{98}_{2}\frac{1}{n}H_{2}(\sigma)\left(\Id_{n}-\sigma\sigma^{\mathsf{T}}\right)
, where the Hessians have been projected orthogonal to
\sigma
. Both the Hessians are symmetric with independent Gaussian coefficients, and therefore, by
[
Sub21
, Proof of Lemma 3]
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{100}\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}_{\perp\sigma}H_{2}(\sigma)+\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}_{\perp\sigma}H_{4}(\sigma)+100\alpha\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{98}_{2}\frac{1}{n}H_{2}(\sigma)\left(\Id_{n}-\sigma\sigma^{\mathsf{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(\sqrt{2\alpha_{2}}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{50}\right)\mathsf{GOE}(n-1)\boxplus\sqrt{12}\mathsf{GOE}(n-1)+100\alpha\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{98}\frac{1}{n}H_{2}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\sim\left(\sqrt{2\alpha_{2}}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{50}\right)\mathsf{GOE}(n-1)\boxplus\sqrt{12}\mathsf{GOE}(n-1)+100\alpha\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{98}\frac{1}{n}H_{2}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\sim_{\text{free convolution~\lx@bibunits@resetglobal\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{mingo2017free}{}{}]}}}\left(\sqrt{2\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{100}+12}\right)\mathsf{GOE}(n-1)+100\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{98}\frac{1}{n}H_{2}(\sigma)\,.

Now, note that, for
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}
sufficiently small, as the top
\delta n
-dimensional eigenbases of
\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}_{\perp\sigma}H_{2}(\sigma)
and
\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}_{\perp\sigma}H_{4}(\sigma)
will be mostly orthogonal (which can be verified using the fact that symmetric
\mathsf{GOE}
matrices are orthogonally invariant), one can see that the local algorithm will (with high probability) move in the direction of
\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}_{\perp\sigma}H_{4}(\sigma)
. However, as
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\to 1
, the dominating signal will come (for sufficiently large
\alpha_{2}>0
) from
\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}_{\perp\sigma}H(\sigma)
.

Robustness of the HES relaxation

The HES SOS relaxation will use the following low-degree extension,

\tilde{H}(\sigma)=\alpha_{2}H_{2}(\sigma)+H_{4}(\sigma)\,,

which is simply equivalent to the original spherical spin-glass Hamiltonian. Consequently,
Theorem

1.1
implies that the HES SoS hierarchy outputs certificates with value better than direct Hessian ascent.
∎

Note
:
While the above argument demonstrates that the HES relaxation is automatically robust to the sort of perturbed Hamiltonian shown, a small modification to the local algorithm to quotient the input Hamiltonian by the ideal
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2p}=\left(\frac{i}{k}\right)^{p}
(which is just the
p
-th power of the length of the current step
\sigma_{i}
) fixes this example.

8.2
Global information: The direct-sum case

We now use projectors
\{\Pi,\Pi^{\perp}\}
to project into subspaces, and then define different degree components to act in complementary subspaces. This leads to examples of Hamiltonians which confuse the Hessian ascent algorithm in a way that doesn’t seem to be fixable with
local
information, since one would require global knowledge of a path in the
future
(on average) to prevent the algorithm from getting “stuck” in the wrong subspace.

Example 8.2

(Disjoint-subspace projector)
.

Let the hamiltonian
H
be,

H(\sigma)=\alpha_{1}H_{2}(\Pi\sigma)+\alpha_{2}H_{4}(\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)\,.

Then, there exists a choice of
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\geqslant 0
which causes
Algorithm

2
to be provably suboptimal, but the HES SoS relaxation does better.

Proof.

We will normalize contributions to the energy in the both sub-spaces, so that the “history” of energy gains from the degree-
2
component can
never
fool the HES SoS hierarchy to
only
follow the degree-
2
steps, but the Hessian ascent
[
Sub21
]
algorithm (and its randomized version) will always follow the degree-
2
component initially, thereby making it suboptimal.

Eigenspectrum of the Hessian

It is clear to see that the Hessian of the as-is Hamiltonian is,

\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)=\frac{1}{n}\left(\alpha_{2}\Pi\nabla^{2}H_{2}(\Pi\sigma)\Pi+\alpha_{4}\Pi^{\perp}H_{4}(\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)\Pi^{\perp}\right)\,,

and that it has a spectral law given by,

\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma)\sim\sqrt{2\alpha_{2}}\mathsf{GOE}(\Tr[\Pi])\oplus\left\lVert\Pi^{\perp}\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\sqrt{12\alpha_{4}}\mathsf{GOE}(\Tr[\Pi^{\perp}])\,.

Normalization of contributions to
\Pi
&
\Pi^{\perp}

We now choose
\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4}\geqslant 0
, such that, the contribution from the degree-2 part forces the Hessian ascent algorithm into the
\Pi
-subspace, thereby, only following the degree-2 signal. Note that, near the origin (at the
i
-th step), we want

\sqrt{2\alpha_{2}}>>\left\lVert\Pi^{\perp}\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\sqrt{12\alpha_{4}}\,,

which holds for a choice of
\alpha_{4}<<\frac{1}{\left\lVert\Pi^{\perp}\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}\frac{\alpha_{2}}{6}
. A concrete choice of
\alpha_{4}=\frac{1}{\left\lVert\Pi^{\perp}\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}\frac{\alpha_{2}}{12}
suffices to obstruct the local algorithm (since it will be mislead within the first few steps).

Robustness of the SoS HES relaxation

The previous choice of normalization motivates the following extended hamiltonian in the ball,

\tilde{H}(\sigma)=\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}H_{2}(\Pi\sigma)+\alpha_{4}H_{4}(\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)\,.

After some algebra, it is easy to see that the Hessian, ignoring rank-
1
terms, is given as,

\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}(\sigma)=\frac{1}{n}\left(\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\Pi\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}_{2}(\Pi\sigma)\Pi+\alpha_{4}\Pi^{\perp}\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}_{4}(\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)\Pi^{\perp}+\alpha_{2}\tilde{H}_{2}(\Pi\sigma)\Id_{n}\right)\,.

Assuming that
\sigma
is the
i
-th step, the eigenspectrum of the above Hessian (modulo the rank-
1
contributions) is,

\displaystyle\left\{\sqrt{2\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}\mathsf{GOE}(\Tr[\Pi])\oplus\sqrt{12\alpha_{4}\left\lVert\Pi^{\perp}\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}\mathsf{GOE}(\Tr[\Pi^{\perp}])\right\}+\alpha_{2}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{i}\frac{(j-i)}{n}\left\langle\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}_{2}(\Pi\sigma_{j-1}),(\Pi v_{j})(\Pi v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rangle\right)\,,

where we intrinsically used the fact that the contributions of the higher-order derivatives are suppressed under the HES distributions. Given the normalizations above, this can be simplified further to purely depend on
\alpha_{2}
as,

\left\{\sqrt{2\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}\mathsf{GOE}(\Tr[\Pi])\oplus\sqrt{\alpha_{2}}\mathsf{GOE}(\Tr[\Pi^{\perp}])\right\}+\alpha_{2}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{i}\frac{(j-i)}{n}\left\langle\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}_{2}(\Pi\sigma_{j-1}),(\Pi v_{j})(\Pi v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rangle\right)

Since
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}<\frac{1}{2}
for the first half of the iterates and the energy from the
\alpha_{2}\tilde{H}_{2}(\Pi\sigma)\Id_{n}
term contributes equally to
\Pi
and
\Pi^{\perp}
(while being independent of
v
), it is clear that the SoS HES certificates for the Hessian will be dominated by the signals from the
\Pi^{\perp}
space.

Suboptimality of local algorithm

Since the local algorithm can only see the as-is extended Hamiltonian, for the first few steps it will
only
follow the
\Pi
-subspace (because of the reasoning above). Note that this implies that the algorithm will go into the wrong subspace for a constant fraction of the time, and output a solution strictly worse than the HES relaxation.
∎

Note:
The only missing part of the argumentation presented is that the certifiable threshold rank condition of
Theorem

1.1
is proxied for using the extremal point in the support of the bulk of the spectrum. Given that the eigenspectrum involves a block-decomposed sum of (independent)
\mathsf{GOE}
matrices and
Lemma

6.1
already certifies the
p
-Schatten norm of matrices that are Wigner-like, this is a reasonable proxy. To see why, note that,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}(\sigma)\right\rVert^{p}_{p}

\displaystyle=\|\frac{1}{n}\left\{\sqrt{2\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}\mathsf{GOE}(\Tr[\Pi])\oplus\sqrt{\alpha_{2}}\mathsf{GOE}(\Tr[\Pi^{\perp}])\right\}+

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{i}\frac{(j-i)}{n}\left\langle\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}_{2}(\Pi\sigma_{j-1}),(\Pi v_{j})(\Pi v_{j})^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rangle\right)\Id_{n}\|^{p}_{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{p}}\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{p}\left(\alpha_{2}\tilde{H}_{2}(\sigma)\right)^{p-k}n\left\lVert\sqrt{2\alpha_{2}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}\mathsf{GOE}(\Tr[\Pi])\oplus\sqrt{\alpha_{2}}\mathsf{GOE}(\Tr[\Pi^{\perp}])\right\rVert^{k}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{p-1}}\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{p}\left(\alpha_{2}\tilde{H}_{2}(\sigma)\right)^{p-k}\left(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert^{2k}_{2}\left\lVert\alpha_{2}\Pi\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}_{2}(\Pi\sigma)\Pi\right\rVert^{k}_{k}+\left\lVert\alpha_{4}\Pi^{\perp}\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}_{4}(\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)\Pi^{\perp}\right\rVert^{k}_{k}\right)\,,

at which point one can invoke
Lemma

6.1
to bound the Schatten norms of each of the individual
\mathsf{GOE}
components and then use the HES constraint on the moment matrices of
\sigma
to apply a
(p,q)
-Hölder’s inequality as in
Corollary

6.3
.

8.3
More involved HES SoS proofs

For this example, a SoS proof of stepwise boundedness will require upper bounding the value gained over all of a set of possible choices that the algorithm could’ve made in previous steps.
Here, the Hamiltonian will involve the direct sum of two usual spin glass Hamiltonians in different subspaces, along with an interaction term between the two subspaces.
These terms will be set up so that the optimal choice will be to evenly balance
\sigma
between the two subspaces at every step, but showing an upper bound will require considering every possible choice of how to split
\sigma
between the two subspaces at each step.

This will only be a very rough sketch of a possible argument, depending on the following assumption:

Assumption 8.3
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{(n/2)\times(n/2)}
and
C\in\mathbb{R}^{(n/2)\times(n/2)}
be random dense symmetric-matrix-valued polynomials of
\sigma
and
\tau
respectively, and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{(n/2)\times(n/2)}
be a random dense asymmetric-random-valued matrix polynomial of both
\sigma
and
\tau
.
In this way,
A
,
B
, and
C
will be GOE-distributed with respect to the randomness of the polynomial coefficients and a HES distribution over
(\sigma,\tau)
.
Let
a
,
b
, and
c
, be the expected operator norms of
A
,
B
, and
C
, which may depend on
\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
and
\left\lVert\tau\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
.

There exists some function
f(\mu^{2},a,b,c)
that expresses the with-high-probability maximum over
(u,v)
satisfying
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=1
and
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\mu^{2}
of

\left(u\;\;v\right)M\left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}u\\
v\end{array}\!\!\right):=\left(u\;\;v\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&B\\
B^{\mathsf{T}}&C\end{array}\right)\left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}u\\
v\end{array}\!\!\right)

There also exist
g_{X}(\mu^{2},a,b,c)
for
X\in\{A,B,C\}
that express the with-high-probability values of
u^{\mathsf{T}}Au
,
2u^{\mathsf{T}}Bv
, and
v^{\mathsf{T}}Cv
, respectively, for the aforementioned maximizing
(u,v)
.

The assumption is that there are HES SoS proofs that capture the fact that the quantities described by
f(\mu^{2},a,b,c)
and
g_{X}(\mu^{2},a,b,c)
actually concentrate strongly at the true values of those functions.

Furthermore, there is
b^{2}/(ac)
large enough so that
f(\mu^{2},a,b,c)
is maximized at
\mu^{2}=1/2
.

One might hope for the existence of this HES SoS proof because there are combinatorial arguments that compute the spectral moments of block-structured Wigner-type random matrices via a trace power method
[
Zhu20
]
which has structure very similar to the proof of
Lemma

6.1
.

Example 8.4

(Shared-subspace projector)
.

Let the Hamiltonian
H
be,

H(\sigma)=\alpha_{4}H_{4}(\Pi\sigma)+\alpha_{6}H_{6}(\Pi\sigma,\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)+\alpha_{8}H_{8}(\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)\,,

where
H_{6}(\rho,\tau)=\left\langle g_{6},\rho^{\otimes 2}\otimes\tau^{\otimes 4}\right\rangle
for
g_{6}
an order-6 tensor of i.i.d. unit Gaussian entries. Then, there exists a choice of
\alpha_{4},\alpha_{6},\alpha_{8}\geqslant 0
which causes
Algorithm

2
to be provably suboptimal, but the HES SoS relaxation does better with
8.3
.

Proof sketch.

The argument for the failure of the local algorithm will be very similar as for the previous example, so we focus here on the success of the HES hierarchy, especially on the stepwise upper bound on the extended Hamiltonian.
Let the extension be

\tilde{H}(\sigma)=\alpha_{4}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{4}H_{4}(\Pi\sigma)+\alpha_{6}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}H_{6}(\Pi\sigma,\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)+\alpha_{8}H_{8}(\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)\,.

Then its Hessian is

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\tilde{H}(\sigma)={}

\displaystyle\alpha_{4}\left(\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{4}\nabla^{2}H_{4}(\Pi\sigma)+12\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}H_{4}(\Pi\sigma)\Id\right)

\displaystyle+2\alpha_{6}H_{6}(\Pi\sigma,\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)\Id

\displaystyle+\alpha_{6}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left(2\nabla_{1}^{2}H_{6}(\Pi\sigma,\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)+12\nabla_{2}^{2}H_{6}(\Pi\sigma,\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)+16\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}H_{6}(\Pi\sigma,\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)\right)

\displaystyle+\alpha_{8}\nabla^{2}H_{8}(\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)+\text{[rank-1 terms]}\,,

where
\nabla_{1}
refers to the gradient in the first argument of a multi-argument function and similarly for
\nabla_{2}
, and the rank-1 terms make negligible contribution over an HES distribution.

Now we fit this into
8.3
, characterizing each of these terms by their norm and whether they are part of
A
,
B
,
C
, or constant with respect to
v_{i}
.
We halve the contributions to
B
due to the fact that
B
needs to appear twice.

Term

Norm

Type

\alpha_{4}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{4}\nabla^{2}H_{4}(\Pi\sigma)

\sqrt{6}\,\alpha_{4}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{4}\left\lVert\Pi\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

A

12\alpha_{4}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}H_{4}(\Pi\sigma)\Id

12\alpha_{4}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}H_{4}(\Pi\sigma)

constant

2\alpha_{6}H_{6}(\Pi\sigma,\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)\Id

2\alpha_{6}H_{6}(\Pi\sigma,\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)

constant

2\alpha_{6}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\nabla_{1}^{2}H_{6}(\Pi\sigma,\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)

\sqrt{2}\alpha_{6}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\Pi^{\perp}\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{4}

A

12\alpha_{6}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\nabla_{2}^{2}H_{6}(\Pi\sigma,\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)

\sqrt{12}\alpha_{6}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\Pi\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\Pi^{\perp}\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

C

16\alpha_{6}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}H_{6}(\Pi\sigma,\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)

\sqrt{8}\alpha_{6}\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\Pi\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{1}\left\lVert\Pi^{\perp}\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{3}

B

\alpha_{8}\nabla^{2}H_{8}(\Pi^{\perp}\sigma)

\sqrt{28}\left\lVert\Pi^{\perp}\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{6}

C

At this point, we invoke
8.3
and unfurl it over
i
steps to get a formula for the total expected value of the Hamiltonian
H(\sigma_{i})\approx\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in[i]}v_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\nabla^{2}H(\sigma_{j-1})v_{j}
at the end of
i
steps, as a function of
\left\lVert\Pi v_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
for each
j\in[i]
.

Showing that this formula is maximized when
\left\lVert\Pi v_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=1/2
for all
j
will involve a “payment plan” argument where, while the value gain from the
H_{8}
term is immediate, the value gain from the
H_{4}
and
H_{6}
terms are spread out over all future steps via the
\Id
terms in the Hessian.
The task will be to show that the extra value from the “payment plans” when taking
\left\lVert\Pi v_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}>1/2
fails to offset the future losses to the
H_{8}
term, since the
H_{8}
term scales with a relatively high power of
\left\lVert\Pi^{\perp}\sigma\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
, and similarly but conversely when taking
\left\lVert\Pi v_{j}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}<1/2
.
∎

Note:
The parameters in the examples above (the subspaces projected to) are carefully balanced (for instance,
\{\Pi,\Pi^{\perp}\}
are chosen so that
\Tr[\Pi]=\Tr[\Pi^{\perp}]
) to make the argument easier, but we expect that the argument generalizes to unbalanced choices as well, in part by using the
R
-transform from free probability theory
[
Spe19
]
to more precisely calculate spectral distributions of sums of matrices. Given this flexibility of choice one has to “fool” the local algorithm in ways that the SoS HES relaxation seems to be robust to, it leads to a natural conjecture - That the SoS HES relaxation can “peel” out correlations between the entries (when drawn from some sub-gaussian distribution), provided these correlations are arbitrarily well-approximated by a low-degree polynomial. This conjecture is formally stated in
1.6
.

9
Discussion

9.1
Open problems

9.1.1
Hardness equivalence between LDPs and
O(1)
-degree SoS?

In the introduction, we described the desire in the literature to relate various meta-algorithmic families, such as LDPs and SoS hierarchies. To demonstrate two-way reductions between these families for the family of random optimization problems of interest, it is not enough to merely show that the (for instance) SoS HES hierarchy can match the various LDP algorithms in use on the family of problems, one must also demonstrate that any SoS hierarchy cannot do
better
in terms of outputting better solutions (under the utilization of some reasonable rounding schemes).

Partly, the “hardness” half of this task would involve demonstrating that, under the presence of various so-called overlap-gap properties
[
CGPR19
,
GJW20
,
CLSS22
,
HS22
,
JMSS23
]
(OGPs), there exist
no

O(1)
-degree SoS relaxations that perform above a certain algorithmic threshold
[
HS22
, Eq. (1.9) & Eq. (2.5)]
[
JMSS23
, Corollary 6.9]
. Even more ambitiously, one would perhaps attempt to rule out the possibility of
\poly(n)
sized semi-definite programs (SDPs) in the presence of OGPs altogether.

However, as it stands, this seems to be a rather daunting technical challenge and a good first step in this direction would be to demonstrate the overlap-concentration of (certain families of) low-degree polynomial algorithms
[
JMSS23
, Conjecture 7.1]
on mean-field spin glasses and sparse random Max-CSPs.

9.1.2
SoS algorithms for the SK model & bounded-degree CSPs under fRSB?

There is a precise way to round the parameterized HES distribution, so that the SoS relaxation can be adapted to work when the solution domain is the discrete hypercube. This requires certifying a bound on a Hessian with a low-degree potential function added, and defining the SoS program over a newly parameterized HES distribution
[
AC15
,
Mon19
,
Pan13a
]
. Doing this would require generalizing the graph matrix framework
[
AMP21
]
to the setting of random matrices that are conjugated by diagonal matrices with
non
i.i.d. entries. This is a significant technical challenge, but ideas from asymptotic freeness introduced in the free probability theory literature in conjunction with appropriately certifiable properties (such as unitary-invariance) should allow the current HES SoS relaxation template to work for spin glasses on the hypercube as well.

While an AMP
[
Mon19
]
algorithm and Hessian ascent-type algorithm
[
JSS24
]
are already known for the SK model, it would be interesting to know if a SoS algorithm using some parameterized family of certificates can be made to work in the setting of
all
bounded degree CSPs.
Adapting the current SoS program for the sparse setting, however, would require surmounting the formidable technical challenge of bounding the operator norms of
extremely
sparse graph matrices.

9.1.3
SoS certificate complexity of the Ghirlanda-Guerra identities?

Note that the success of the HES SoS relaxation given in our work crucially relies on the information about the distribution of the eigenspectrum of the Hessian and its scaling equivalence matching the optimal value given by the Chen and Sen formula (
Lemma

3.7
). More precisely, the precise optimal value is known to us by knowledge that is
external
to the SoS solver. A natural question then is whether one can certify key mathematical properties of many spin glass models that are known to be the reason why the ground state configurations reside in an ultrametric space. As mentioned before, due to a landmark result of Panchenko
[
Pan10
,
Pan13a
]
, it is known that ultrametricity is implied by the so-called Ghirlanda-Guerra (GG) identities
[
GG98
]
. As a result, a first natural question along this direction is asking whether there even
exist
SoS certificates to check whether an instance of certain spin glass models satisfies some low-degree weakening of the GG identities, written over
some
parameterizable step distribution.

Open Question 9.1

(SoS Certificate for “weakened” Ghirlanda-Guerra Identities of Low-Degree Moments of the Gibbs Distribution)
.

Does there exist a SoS relaxation with degree parametrized by the support of the overlap CDF
\mu
(and
n
) as defined in the Parisi functional
\mathcal{P}_{\beta}(\mu)
that can certify whether an instance
H_{n}(.)
of a mean-field spin glass (approximately) satisfies the GG identities on the
low degree
moments of some parameterizable distribution (to proxy those of the Gibbs distribution)? Are there other weakenings of the GG identities that a SoS relaxation can certify?

Note that it is unclear how to even state this problem in the sum-of-squares proof system, let alone find a certificate for it, as the Ghirlanda-Guerra identities are a property satisfied by the Gibbs measure of (typical) instances of spin glass models. However, if it is possible to understand the low-degree cumulants of the Gibbs precisely, one may state a relaxed version of this property over the low-degree moments of the Gibbs distribution. An answer to the question above sheds light on the power of the SoS proof system to “recognize” when solutions to a problem instance satisfy a certain geometric structure (albeit under a different relaxation), which in this case is an ultrametric structure. In a regime (if any) in which certificates of low-complexity exist, one can conceivably show the existence of a SoS algorithm that relies
even less
on external knowledge about the problem structure.

9.2
Future directions

9.2.1
Equivalences between algorithm classes

By subsuming local iterative algorithms on problems that previously defied a SoS approach, the HES hierarchy could make progress toward unification of meta-algorithmic families such as local algorithms
[
CGPR19
,
CLSS22
]
and the low-degree polynomial family (which include AMP and certain spectral algorithms)
[
Hop18b
,
GJW20
]
. This advances a goal of Schramm to classify these approaches under an
algorithmic universality

[
Sch20
]
.

9.2.2
Other spin glass ansatze

The pseudo-distribution structure of the HES hierarchy allows for statistical mixtures of HES distributions that capture near-extrema (up to a particular precision) when solutions exhibit a particular overlap structure. This is compatible with the structure described by the Mezard-Parisi ansatz
[
MP01
,
Pan15
,
Pan16
,
COP19
]
, involving non-constructive statistical mixtures of hierarchically-organized solutions.
A prototypical example of a problem whose solutions are described by the Mezard-Parisi ansatz is the
\mathsf{MAX}
-
\mathsf{CUT}
of random
d
-regular graphs for
fixed

d\in\mathbb{N}
.
The structural similarity suggests that the HES hierarchy could optimize these
\mathsf{MAX}
-
\mathsf{CUT}
instances in the fRSB regime
[
Con18
,
Con19
]
.

9.2.3
Bridging the HES and Lasserre Hierarchies

We observe that
Lemma

1.3
suggests that the HES approach might generally work when there are no large spectral weights in the higher-degree components of the input polynomial.
The existence of these heavy spectral directions would suggest that techniques based on reweighing or lifting into higher-dimensional spaces would be non-trivial
[
HSSS16
,
MSS16
]
.
It would be interesting to bridge the gap between these two regimes.

Appendix A
Univariate Matrix Bernstein Polynomials

In this section, we define the
matrix
bernstein basis polynomials over symmetric matrices with eigenvalues in a compact interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
. The matrix Bernstein polynomial
B^{f}_{d}(M)
of degree
d
approximating a continuous function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
will have the same desirable properties as in the scalar regime when the underlying inputs are symmetric matrices, including a low-degree SoS proof of positivity over the Loewner order (
Appendix

B
).

Definition A.1

(Matrix Bernstein Basis Polynomials)
.

Given a degree
d\geqslant 0
, the
d+1
matrix Bernstein basis polynomials on the interval
I=[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
are defined as,

B_{d,i}=\binom{d}{i}\frac{(M-a\Id)^{i}(b\Id-M)^{d-i}}{(b-a)^{d}}\,,

(A.1)

where
M
is a symmetric matrix such that
a\Id\preceq M\preceq b\Id
.

Given a step-function
S_{\alpha}:I=[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
with the step at
\alpha\in(a,b)
, we define the linear ramp function
L_{\alpha,\gamma}:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
as a continuous approximation of the step-function as follows,

L_{\alpha,\gamma}(x)=\begin{cases}0&x\in[a,\alpha-\gamma]\,,\\
x&x\in(\alpha-\gamma,\alpha+\gamma)\,,\\
1&x\in[\alpha+\gamma,b]\,.\end{cases}

(A.2)

Typically,
\gamma
will be chosen to be significantly smaller than
\alpha
. For the Hessian
\nabla^{2}_{\perp}H(\cdot)
, the linear ramp function approximates its top
\delta n
-dimensional eigenspace with the following choice:
a=-2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\cdot)^{1/2}
,
b=2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\cdot)^{1/2}
,
\alpha=2\nu^{\prime\prime}(\cdot)^{1/2}-2\varepsilon/3
and
\gamma=\varepsilon/3
. The choice of
\varepsilon
and
\delta
correspond to
[
Sub21
, Lemma 3]
.

Proposition A.2

(Matrix Bernstein Approximation of
L_{\alpha,\gamma}
)
.

Let
I=[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
. Then, given a linear-ramp function
L_{\alpha,\gamma}:I\to\mathbb{R}
, the Bernstein polynomial
B^{L_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{d}(\cdot)
defined over symmetric matrices
M
with
a\Id\preceq M\preceq b\Id
that approximates it is given as,

B^{L_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{d}(M):=\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}L_{\alpha,\gamma}\left(a+\frac{j(b-a)}{d}\right)\binom{d}{i}\frac{(M-a\Id)^{j}(b\Id-M)^{d-j}}{(b-a)^{d}}\,.

(A.3)

Furthermore,
B^{L_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{d}
has the following properties:

1. Symmetric Positive Semi-Definiteness:

B^{L_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{d}(M)\succeq 0
.

2. Operator Norm Bound:

B^{L_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{d}(M)\preceq\Id
.

3. Close in Frobenius Norm:

Let
\Pi_{\alpha+\gamma,b}
be the orthogonal projector to the subspace of
M
with eigenvalues in
[\alpha+\gamma,b]
and
p=\frac{B^{L_{\alpha},\gamma}_{d}(M)}{\delta^{\prime}n}
. Then, for any
d\geqslant\frac{8\nu^{\prime\prime}(q)^{1/2}}{\varepsilon^{3}}
,

\left\lVert p-\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}n}\Pi_{\alpha+\gamma,b}\right\rVert_{F}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{2\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\delta^{\prime 2}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,,

where,

\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}=\max\left\{\varepsilon^{2},\frac{3}{4}(\delta-\delta^{\prime\prime})\right\}\,.

Proof.

We prove each item in sequence.

1. Symmetric Positive Semi-Definiteness:

This follows immediately from the following observations:

•

The value of the scaled linear ramp function
\binom{d}{i}\frac{L_{\alpha,\gamma}(a+j(b-a)/d)}{(b-a)^{d}}\geqslant 0
for every
j\in[d]
since the function is non-negative and
b>a
.

•

By invoking
Lemma

B.2
with
p_{1}(M-a\Id)=(M-a\Id)^{j}
and
p_{2}(b\Id-M)=(b\Id-M)^{d-j}
, we have that
p_{1}(\cdot)p_{2}(\cdot)
is a symmetric and positive definite matrix since
a\Id\preceq M\preceq b\Id
.

•

A positive linear sum of symmetric and positive semi-definite matrices is a symmetric positive semi-definite matrix.

2. Operator Norm Bound:

Diagonalizing the symmetric matrix immediately implies that,

B^{L_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{d}(M)=O\begin{bmatrix}B^{L_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{d}(\lambda_{1})&&\\
&\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 7.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{$.$}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{$.$}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$.$}\mkern 1.0mu}&\\
&&B^{L_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{d}(\lambda_{n})\end{bmatrix}O^{\mathsf{T}}\,,

(A.4)

where the Bernstein polynomials applied to the eigenvalue are as defined in
Equation

3.21
. Then, by
Definition

3.28
,

\displaystyle B^{L_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{d}(\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}L_{\alpha,\gamma}(a+j(b-a)/d)\binom{d}{j}\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}-a}{b-a}\right)^{j}\left(\frac{b-\lambda_{i}}{b-a}\right)^{d-j}\,,

\displaystyle\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}\binom{d}{j}\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}-a}{b-a}\right)^{j}\left(\frac{b-\lambda_{i}}{b-a}\right)^{d-j}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}\binom{d}{j}y^{j}(1-y)^{d-j}=(y+(1-y))^{d}

\displaystyle=1\,,

where we used the facts that
L_{\alpha,\gamma}(a+j(b-a)/d)\leqslant 1
for every
\lambda_{i}\in[a,b]
and the substitution
y=\frac{\lambda_{i}-a}{b-a}\in[0,1]
. The last equality follows by the binomial formula. This immediately implies that,

B^{L_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{d}(M)\leqslant O\Id O^{\mathsf{T}}=\Id\,.

3. Close in Frobenius Norm:

We apply
[
Sub21
, Lemma 3]
repetitively to obtain
0<\delta^{\prime\prime}<\delta^{\prime}<\delta<1
corresponding to the choices of
0<\varepsilon/3<2\varepsilon/3<\varepsilon<1
respectively. Then, applying
Theorem

3.29
for
f=L_{\alpha,\gamma}
with the choice of parameters
a,b,\gamma
stated above, we have,

\displaystyle\left\lVert p-\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}n}\Pi_{\alpha+\gamma,b}\right\rVert_{F}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}n}\sqrt{\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{\delta^{\prime\prime}n}\varepsilon^{2}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=\delta^{\prime\prime}n+1}^{\delta^{\prime}n}(1/2+\varepsilon)^{2}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=\delta^{\prime}n+1}^{\delta n}(1/2+\varepsilon)^{2}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=\delta n}^{n}\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}n}\left(\sqrt{(\delta^{\prime\prime}n)\varepsilon^{2}+(\delta^{\prime}-\delta^{\prime\prime})n\left(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon\right)^{2}+(\delta-\delta^{\prime})n\left(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon\right)^{2}+(1-\delta)n\varepsilon^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}n}\left(\sqrt{(1-\delta-\delta^{\prime\prime})\varepsilon^{2}n+(\delta-\delta^{\prime\prime})\left(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon\right)^{2}n}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}n}\left(\sqrt{(1-2\delta^{\prime\prime})\varepsilon^{2}n+\left(\frac{\delta-\delta^{\prime\prime}}{4}\right)n+\left(\frac{\delta-\delta^{\prime\prime}}{2}\right)\varepsilon n}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}n}\left(\sqrt{(1-2\delta^{\prime\prime})\varepsilon^{2}n+\frac{3n}{4}(\delta-\delta^{\prime\prime})}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}n}\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon^{2}n+\frac{3n}{4}(\delta-\delta^{\prime\prime})}\right)\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{2\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\delta^{\prime 2}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,,

where we used the definition of the Frobenius norm, in conjunction with a rewrite of the projector diagonalized into the eigenbasis of
B^{L_{\alpha,\gamma}}_{d}(M)
and the fact that
\varepsilon<1
.

∎

Appendix B
Analytic Sparsity Proofs

B.1
Normalizing the Bernstein polynomial under the semi-circle measure

Lemma B.1

(Average Area of Bernstein Polynomial under
\mu_{sc}
)
.

For the semi-circle measure
d\mu_{sc}
supported on
[-1,1]
and
B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)
as defined in
Equation

A.3
with a choice of
L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}(x)
given in
Section

6.3
, the following holds,

\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]\leqslant 1+\varepsilon-\mathsf{CDF}(1-\phi)\,,

and,

\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]\geqslant(1+2\varepsilon)\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2})+\varepsilon\,.

Proof.

We compute the area of the semi-circle where the Bernstein polynomial “lights up” to normalize the correlation appropriately. To avoid unnecessary complications, we will use the uniform lower bound of
0
and uniform upper bound of
\phi^{2}
on the terms, leading us to a lower and upper bound of the correct
leading
order.

The upper bound

We being by computing the upper bound by using the uniform upper bound of
\phi^{2}
on the ramp part,

\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]=\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1}B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}dx\leqslant\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\left(L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}(x)+\varepsilon\right)\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}dx

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1}L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}(x)d\mu_{sc}(x)+\varepsilon\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1}d\mu_{sc}(x)

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1-\phi}0\cdot d\mu_{sc}(x)+\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}\left(x-(1-\phi)\right)d\mu_{sc}(x)+\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}^{1}d\mu_{sc}(x)+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leqslant 0+\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}\phi^{2}\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}d\mu_{sc}(x)+\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}^{1}d\mu_{sc}(x)+\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1}d\mu_{sc}(x)+\varepsilon=(1+\varepsilon)-\mathsf{CDF}(1-\phi)\,.

To evaluate the above, we take the Taylor expansion of
\mathsf{CDF}(1-\phi)
around
1
(using the fact that
\phi(\varepsilon)<<1
) and have,

\displaystyle 1-\mathsf{CDF}(1-\phi)

\displaystyle=1-\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(1-\phi)\sqrt{1-(1-\phi)^{2}}}{\pi}+\frac{\arcsin{1-\phi}}{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Taylor expansion}}{=}1-\left(1-\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2})\,,

where
o(\phi^{5/2})
denotes terms of higher power than
5/2
and utilizes the fact that
\phi(\varepsilon)<<1
.

The lower bound

The lower bound follows a similar computation, using the uniform lower bound of
0
on the ramp part,

\displaystyle\E_{x\sim\mu_{sc}}\left[B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\right]=\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1}B^{L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}}(x)\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\geqslant\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1-\phi}\left(L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}+\varepsilon\right)\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}dx+\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1}\left(L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}-\varepsilon\right)\frac{2}{\pi}dx

\displaystyle=\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}\left(x-(1-\phi)\right)d\mu_{sc}(x)+\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi+\phi^{2}}^{1}d\mu_{sc}+\varepsilon\left(\intop\nolimits_{-1}^{1-\phi}d\mu_{sc}(x)-\intop\nolimits_{1-\phi}^{1}d\mu_{sc}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\mathsf{CDF}(1-\phi+\phi^{2})+\varepsilon\left(\mathsf{CDF}(1-\phi)-0-1+\mathsf{CDF}(1-\phi)\right)

\displaystyle=1-\mathsf{CDF}(1-\phi+\phi^{2})+2\varepsilon(\mathsf{CDF}(1-\phi)-\frac{1}{2})

\displaystyle=1-\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{(1-\phi(1-\phi))\sqrt{1-(1-\phi(1-\phi))^{2}}}{\pi}+\frac{\arcsin{1-\phi(1-\phi)}}{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+2\varepsilon\left(\frac{1}{2}\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}(1-\phi)^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2}(1-\phi)^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2}(1-\phi^{5/2})+2\varepsilon\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geqslant(1+2\varepsilon)\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{3\pi}\phi^{3/2}+O(\phi^{5/2})+o(\phi^{5/2})+\varepsilon\,,

where fourth equality uses a similar taylor expansion as the one used in computing the upper bound.
∎

B.2
Auxiliary lemmata

We state and prove some helper lemmata, used to reason about matrix Bernstein polynomials and low-degree SoS proofs.

Lemma B.2

(Products of univariate non-negative symmetric matrix polynomials)
.

Given univariate matrix polynomials of symmetric matrices
p_{1},p_{2}:M_{n}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
with
\mathsf{deg}(p_{1}),\mathsf{deg}(p_{2})\leqslant d
and
0\preceq p_{1}(M)
,
0\preceq p_{2}(M)
for every
M\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, the matrix polynomial
p_{1}(M)p_{2}(M)
is symmetric and non-negative in the Loewner order with
\mathsf{deg}(p_{1}p_{2})\leqslant 2d
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

B.3
,
p_{1}(M)p_{2}(M)
has non-negative non-zero eigenvalues. It remains to check that
p_{1}(M)p_{2}(M)
is symmetric, as
Proposition

B.3
then also implies that
p_{1}(M)p_{2}(M)
is a symmetric PSD matrix. This follows by expressing the matrix polynomials explicitly over the monomial basis with their real coefficients
\{a_{i}\}_{i=0}^{d}
and
\{b_{i}\}_{i=0}^{d}
.

\displaystyle p_{1}(M)p_{2}(M)

\displaystyle=\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}a_{i}M^{i}\right)\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}b_{j}M^{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j=0}^{d}a_{i}b_{j}M^{i}M^{j}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}a_{i}b_{j}M^{i+j}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}a_{i}b_{j}(M^{\mathsf{T}})^{i}(M^{\mathsf{T}})^{j}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}a_{i}b_{j}(M^{i})^{\mathsf{T}}(M^{j})^{\mathsf{T}}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}a_{i}b_{j}(M^{j}M^{i})^{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}a_{i}b_{j}(M^{i}M^{j})^{\mathsf{T}}=\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{d}a_{i}b_{j}M^{i}M^{j}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}=\left(p_{1}(M)p_{2}(M)\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\,.

Note that
\mathsf{deg}(p_{1}p_{2})\leqslant 2d
also follows from the reasoning above.
∎

Stated below is an elementary proposition which shows that the products of matrices that commute preserves the Lowener order.

Proposition B.3

(Non-Zero Eigenspectrum Equivalence of conjugated PSD matrices)
.

Let
A
and
B
by symmetric PSD matrices in
M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
. Then,

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}(AB)=\lambda_{i}(A^{1/2}BA^{1/2})\,,

(B.1)

for all non-zero eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(\cdot)
.
Furthermore, if
AB=(AB)^{\mathsf{T}}
, then
AB
is a symmetric PSD matrix.

Proof.

Since
A
is PSD,

\displaystyle AB

\displaystyle=A^{1/2}A^{1/2}B\,.

For every non-zero eigenvector
u_{i}
of
A^{1/2}BA^{1/2}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
, the vector
v_{i}=A^{1/2}u_{i}
is an eigenvector with eigenvalue
u_{i}
as,

\displaystyle AB(A^{1/2}u_{i})=ABv_{i}

\displaystyle=A^{1/2}(A^{1/2}B)v_{i}=A^{1/2}(A^{1/2}BA^{1/2})u_{i}

\displaystyle=A^{1/2}\lambda_{i}u_{i}=\lambda_{i}(A^{1/2}u_{i})\,.

For every
w\in\mathsf{ker}(A^{1/2})
that is also a non-zero eigenvector of
AB
, we can choose
w^{\prime}=A^{1/2}Bw
as an eigenvector of
A^{1/2}BA^{1/2}
with the same eigenvalue. If there is some vector
w\in\mathsf{ker}(A^{1/2})\cap\mathsf{ker}(B)
, then it is a zero eigenvector of both
AB
and
A^{1/2}A^{1/2}B
.

It remains to show that
A^{1/2}BA^{1/2}
is PSD. This follows by the elementary observation that, for every
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle v^{\mathsf{T}}(A^{1/2}BA^{1/2})v=\left\lVert B^{1/2}w\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\geqslant 0\,,

where
w=A^{1/2}v
. This implies that the non-zero eigenvalues of
AB
are non-negative.

If, furthermore,

\displaystyle AB=(AB)^{\mathsf{T}}=B^{\mathsf{T}}A^{\mathsf{T}}=BA\,,

the spectral theorem for symmetric matrices immediately implies that
AB
is a symmetric PSD matrix.
∎

Lemma B.4

(Asymptotic evaluation of Catalan Numbers)
.

For all
q>0
, the Catalan number
C_{q}
can be equivalently expressed as,

C_{q}=\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{q}\right)\right)\left(\frac{4^{q}}{\sqrt{\pi}q^{3/2}}\right)\,.

(B.2)

Proof.

The proof follows by a simple application of the Stirling bounds for the factorial function on the definition of the Catalan numbers.

\displaystyle C_{q}

\displaystyle:=\binom{2q}{q}\frac{1}{q+1}=\frac{(2q)!}{(q!)^{2}(q+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{4\pi q}\left(\frac{2q}{e}\right)^{2q}\left(1+\Theta\left(\frac{1}{q}\right)\right)}{(q+1)\left(\sqrt{2\pi q}\left(\frac{q}{e}\right)^{q}\left(1+\Theta\left(\frac{1}{q}\right)\right)\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{2q}\left(1+\Theta\left(\frac{1}{q}\right)\right)}{\sqrt{\pi}\sqrt{q}(q+1)\left(1+\Theta\left(\frac{1}{q}\right)\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4^{q}}{\sqrt{\pi}q^{3/2}}\frac{1+\Theta\left(\frac{1}{q}\right)}{\left(1+\Theta\left(\frac{1}{q}\right)\right)^{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{q}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{q}\right)\right)\left(\frac{4^{q}}{\sqrt{\pi}q^{3/2}}\right)\,.

∎

Appendix C
Sum-of-Squares Proofs for Commutative Analytic Inequalities

C.1
Pseudo-Jensen inequalities

Note
: Each of the lemmata proved below about pseudo-distributions can
also
be used to give a SoS proof for polynomials of moments that are of even degree, provided they are valid elements of the polynomial grammar (
Section

4.2.4
), meaning that these proofs can also be point-wise over the atoms of the HES SoS hierarchy.

Lemma C.1

(Pseudo-Jensen Inequality for arbitrary functions)
.

Given a real polynomial
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
with degree
\leqslant d
and a pseudo-distribution
\mathcal{D}
of degree
r>2d
over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the following holds,

\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(f(x)\right)^{2}\right]\geqslant\left(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)]\right)^{2}\,.

(C.1)

Proof.

We prove the statement using a “variance” like computation.

\displaystyle 0\leqslant\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(f(x)-\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2}-2f(x)\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)]+\left(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)^{2}]-2\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)\pE_{\mathcal{D}}f(x)\right]+\left(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)]\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)^{2}]-2\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)]+\left(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)]\right)^{2}=\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)^{2}]-\left(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)]\right)^{2}\,,

where the first inequality uses the definition of a pseudo-distribution and the second-last equality uses the fact that the pseudo-expectation operator outputs a real number and is a linear operator.
∎

Remark

Extending the above proof to hold for even degree monomials is straightforward and follows by setting
f(x)=g(x)^{p}
when attempting to prove pseudo-Jensen for
g(x)^{2p}
, thereby yielding
\left(\pE[g(x)^{p}]\right)^{2}\leqslant\pE[g(x)^{2p}]
.

Lemma C.2

(Pseudo-Jensen inequality for even powers)
.

Given a real polynomial
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
with degree
\leqslant d
, an even number
2p>0
and a pseudo-distribution of degree
>4dp
, the following holds,

\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p}\right]\geqslant\left(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)\right]\right)^{2p}\,.

Proof.

We prove this by induction, and use
Lemma

C.3
in conjunction with a direct application of
Lemma

C.1
to prove the inductive step.

Base case

The case when
p=1
simply reduces to
Lemma

C.1
.

Inductive step

We assume the statement is true up to
2\leqslant 2p-2=2(p-1)
. For the case of
2p\geqslant 4
, we obtain the following,

\displaystyle\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p}\right]-\left(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)\right]\right)^{2p}

\displaystyle=\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p}\right]-\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2}\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)\right]^{2p-2}+\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2}\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)\right]^{2p-2}-\left(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)\right]\right)^{2p}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2}\left(f(x)^{2p-2}-\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)\right]^{2p-2}\right)\right]}_{\mathsf{I}}+\underbrace{\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)\right]^{2p-2}}_{=\,\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[(f(x)^{p-1})^{2}\right]\geqslant 0}\underbrace{\left(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2}\right]-\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)]^{2}\right)}_{\geqslant 0\text{ by~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:matrix-pseudo-jensen}}}\,.

To evaluate
\mathsf{I}
we will apply
Lemma

C.3
in conjunction with the inductive hypothesis. First, note that,

\displaystyle\mathsf{I}

\displaystyle=\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)^{2p}]-\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2}\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)]^{2p-2}\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\iff\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)^{2p}]\geqslant\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2}\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)]^{2p-2}\,.

Now,

\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)^{2p}]\geqslant_{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:reverse-cauchy-schwarz} with }s=1}\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2}\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)^{2p-2}]\geqslant_{\text{by I.H.}}\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2}\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)\right]^{2p-2}\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma C.3

(Factored Even Powers)
.

Given an even number
2p>0
and a number
s>0
, such that
1\leqslant 2s\leqslant p
, the following holds,

\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p}\right]\geqslant\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2s}\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p-2s}\right]

Proof.

We prove this by induction. For the base case, we consider
p=1
.

Base case

When
p=1
and
2s=1
, the following

\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2}\right]\geqslant\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)\right]=\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)\right]^{2}\,,

holds by
Lemma

C.1
.

Inductive step

We assume the statement is true up to
2p-2=2(p-1)
. For the case of
2p
, we obtain the following by generalizing the calculation in
Lemma

C.1
,

\displaystyle 0\leqslant

\displaystyle\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p-4s}\left(f^{2s}(x)-\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f^{2s}(x)\right]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p}\right]-\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p-2s}\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2s}\right]\right)-\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2s}\right]\left(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p-2s}\right]-\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p-4s}\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2s}\right]\right)\,.

Note that, since
s\geqslant 1
, we have that
2p-2s\leqslant 2(p-1)
which allows us to invoke the inductive hypothesis and conclude that,

\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p-2s}\right]-\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p-4s}\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2s}\right]\geqslant 0\,.

Since
\mathsf{deg}(f(x)^{2s})=\mathsf{deg}((f(x)^{s})^{2})\leqslant 2dp
, we immediately have that
\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[f(x)^{2s}]\geqslant 0
and this implies,

\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p}\right]-\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p-2s}\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2s}\right]\geqslant\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2s}\right]\left(\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p-2s}\right]-\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2p-4s}\right]\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x)^{2s}\right]\right)\geqslant 0\,.

∎

C.2
\ell_{1}
-to-
\ell_{t}
norm inequality

Stated below is an elementary
1
-to-
t
-norm inequality that permits a very simple Sum-of-Squares proof of
O(t)
.

Lemma C.4

(
\ell_{1}
-to-
\ell_{t}
norm inequality, Mild generalization of
[
Hop18a
, SoS triangle inequality]
)
.

Given
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
and
t=2^{k}
for some
k>0
, the following holds,

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{t}(a_{1}+\dots+a_{m})^{t}\leqslant m^{t-1}(a_{1}^{t}+\dots+a_{m}^{t})\,.

Proof.

The proof is by induction in conjunction with
[
Hop18a
, Proposition (squaring SoS proofs)]
.

Base case (
t=2
)

In this case, we set
a=a_{1}
and
b=a_{2}+\dots+a_{m}
, and observe that,

(a_{1}+\dots+a_{m})^{2}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}^{2}+2\sumop\displaylimits_{i<j}a_{i}a_{j},,

and that,

m(a_{1}^{2}+\dots+a_{m}^{2})-(a_{1}+\dots+a_{m})^{2}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i<j}(a_{i}-a_{j})^{2}\,.

The above immediately implies that,

(a_{1}+\dots+a_{m})^{2}\leqslant m(a_{1}^{2}+\dots+a_{m}^{2})\,,

where the SoS proof is clearly degree-
2
.

Inductive step

For the inductive step, we assume the following,

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{t/2}(a_{1}+\dots+a_{m})^{t/2}\leqslant m^{t/2-1}(a_{1}^{t/2}+\dots+a_{m}^{t/2})\,.

By
[
Hop18a
, Proposition (squaring SoS proofs)]
, this immediately implies that,

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{t}(a_{1}+\dots+a_{m})^{t}\leqslant m^{t-2}(a_{1}^{t/2}+\dots+a_{m}^{t/2})^{2}\,.

Using the base case with
t=2
and
a^{\prime}_{1}=a_{1}^{t/2},\dots,a^{\prime}_{m}=a_{m}^{t/2}
, this immediately yields the following,

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{2}(a_{1}^{t/2}+\dots+a_{m}^{t/2})^{2}\leqslant m(a_{1}^{t}+\dots+a_{m}^{t})\,,

which finishes the proof.
∎

C.3
Semi-algebraic degree reduction inequalities

Note
: Once again, the statements below of
Proposition

C.9
,
Proposition

C.10
and
Lemma

C.11
can all be made to work as SoS proofs for polynomials of moments in the grammar for the underlying HES SoS relaxation.

Lemma C.5

(Quadratic degree reduction)
.

Let
c>0
be a constant.
There is a SoS proof that if
(x-y)^{2}\leqslant c^{2}
then
x-y\leqslant c
.

Proof.

(x-y)^{2}\leqslant c^{2}
is equivalent to
c^{2}-(x-y)^{2}\geqslant 0
, which is equivalent by factoring to
[c-(x-y)][c+(x-y)]\geqslant 0
.

We can add
[c-(x-y)]^{2}
to both sides to get
[c-(x-y)][2c]\geqslant[c-(x-y)]^{2}\geqslant 0
.
Dividing both sides by
2c
, we get
c\geqslant(x-y)
.
∎

Note:
The above can also be amended to immediately conclude that
x+y\geqslant-c
, and the same conclusion follows about the “two-sidedness” of all semi-algebraic degree reduction inequalities.

Lemma C.6
.

Let
c>0
be a constant.
There is a SoS proof that if
(x-y)^{4}\leqslant c^{4}
then
x-y\leqslant c
.

Proof.

(x-y)^{4}\leqslant c^{4}
is equivalent to
c^{4}-(x-y)^{4}\geqslant 0
, which is equivalent by factoring to
[c-(x-y)][c^{3}+c^{2}(x-y)+c(x-y)^{2}+(x-y)^{3}]\geqslant 0
.

We can add
[c-(x-y)]^{2}[c+(x-y)]^{2}
to both sides to get
[c-(x-y)][2c^{3}+2c^{2}(x-y)]\geqslant[c-(x-y)]^{2}[c+(x-y)]^{2}\geqslant 0
.

We can then add
2[c-(x-y)]^{2}c^{2}
to both sides to get
[c-(x-y)][4c^{3}]\geqslant 2[c-(x-y)]^{2}c^{2}\geqslant 0
.
Dividing both sides by
4c^{3}
, we get
c\geqslant(x-y)
.
∎

Lemma C.7
.

Let
c>0
be a constant.
There is a SoS proof that if
(x-y)^{2p}\leqslant c^{2p}
then
x-y\leqslant c
.

Proof.

(x-y)^{2p}\leqslant c^{2p}
is equivalent to
c^{2p}-(x-y)^{2p}\geqslant 0
, which is equivalent by factoring to
[c-(x-y)][c^{2p-1}+c^{2p-2}(x-y)+c^{2p-3}(x-y)^{2}+\dots+(x-y)^{2p-1}]\geqslant 0
. It suffices to recurisvely generalize the case for degree-
2
and degree-
4
.
∎

Proposition C.8
.

Suppose that
x_{i}^{2}\leqslant y_{i}^{2}
and
y_{i}\geqslant 0
by sum-of-squares proofs for all
i
.
Then there is a degree-
3
sum-of-squares proof that

\sumop\displaylimits_{i}x_{i}\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{i}y_{i}

and

-\sumop\displaylimits_{i}x_{i}\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{i}y_{i}\,.

Proof.

The inequality
x_{i}^{2}\leqslant y_{i}^{2}
can be factored and summed to get

\sumop\displaylimits_{i}(y_{i}-x_{i})(y_{i}+x_{i})\geqslant 0\,.

Add
(y_{i}-x_{i})(y_{i}-x_{i})
, which is non-negative, to find

\sumop\displaylimits_{i}(y_{i}-x_{i})y_{i}\geqslant 0

Since
y_{j}\geqslant 0
, we know that

(y_{i}-x_{i})y_{i}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j\neq i}y_{j}\right)\geqslant 0\,.

This implies by a weighted AM-GM that

(y_{i}-x_{i})\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n-1}}y_{i}^{2}+\sqrt{n-1}y_{j}^{2}\right)\geqslant 0\,,

where
n
is the number of possible values of
i
.
This implies

(y_{i}-x_{i})\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j}y_{j}^{2}\sqrt{n-1}\right)\geqslant 0\,.

Finally, sum the preceding equation over
i
.
This is the form of a SoS proof that demonstrates that either
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}x_{i}\leqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{i}y_{i}
or
y_{i}=0
for all
i
.
The latter case implies that
x_{i}=0
for all
i
as well.
The
-\sumop\displaylimits_{i}x_{i}
case follows symmetrically.
∎

One again, note that each of the lemmata proved below about pseudo-distributions can
also
be used to give a SoS proof for polynomials of moments that are of even degree, provided they are valid elements of the polynomial grammar (
Section

4.2.4
), meaning that these proofs can also be point-wise over the atoms of the HES SoS hierarchy.

Proposition C.9

(Quadratic degree reduction with pseudo-expectations)
.

Given non-SoS program variables
y=an,c(n)=o(n)
for
a\geqslant 1
a constant, a non-SoS program variable
c(n)=o(n)
, and a low-degree SoS proof that,

\pE_{x}\left(x-y\right)^{2}\leqslant c(n)^{2}\,,

there are low-degree SoS proofs that,

\displaystyle\pE_{x}(x^{2})\leqslant y^{2}+2yc(n)+c^{2}(n)=y^{2}+o(n^{2})\,,

(C.2)

\displaystyle\pE_{x}(x^{2})\geqslant y^{2}-o(n^{2})\,,

(C.3)

\displaystyle\pE_{x}(x)\leqslant y+o(n)\,,

(C.4)

\displaystyle\pE_{x}(x)\geqslant y-o(n)\,.

(C.5)

Proof.

We first use quadratic ideal reduction to show the linear bounds, and then use those to obtain the quadratic bounds.

Linear bounds

By the pseudo-Jensen inequality (
Lemma

C.1
) and the given SoS proof,

(\pE_{x}(x-y))^{2}=\left(\pE_{x}\left[x\right]-y\right)^{2}\leqslant\pE_{x}(x-y)^{2}\leqslant c(n)^{2}\,.

Then, by
Lemma

C.5
on
(\pE_{x}(x)-y)^{2}\leqslant c(n)^{2}
, we have that,

\pE_{x}(x-y)\leqslant c(n)\,.

(C.6)

Using the fact that
(\pE_{x}(y-x))^{2}\leqslant\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2}=\pE_{x}(x-y)^{2}\leqslant c(n)^{2}
, one can again use
Lemma

C.5
to conclude that,

y-\pE_{x}(x)\leqslant o(n)\implies\pE_{x}(x)\geqslant y-o(n)\,.

Quadratic bounds

Now,

\pE_{x}(x-y)^{2}=\pE(x^{2})+y^{2}-2y\pE_{x}(x)\leqslant c(n)^{2}\,.

Using the implication in
Equation

C.6
and the given bound,

\pE_{x}[x^{2}]\leqslant c(n)^{2}-y^{2}+2y(c(n)+y)=y^{2}+2yc(n)+c(n)^{2}\leqslant y^{2}+o(n^{2})\,.

Using the fact that
\pE_{x}(x-y)^{2}\geqslant 0
and
\pE_{x}(x)\geqslant y-o(n)
,

\displaystyle\pE_{x}(x-y)^{2}=\pE_{x}(x^{2})+y^{2}-2y\pE_{x}(x)\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\implies\pE(x^{2})\geqslant 2y\pE_{x}(x)-y^{2}\geqslant 2y(y-o(n))-y^{2}=y^{2}-o(n^{2})\,.

∎

Proposition C.10

(Odd pseudo-moment bounds from degree Reductions)
.

Given non-SoS program variables
y=an,c(n)=o(n)
for
a\geqslant 1
a constant, a pseudo-distribution of degree
\geqslant 4p+2
, and low-degree SoS proofs that,

\pE_{x}[(x-y)^{2j}]\leqslant o(n^{2j})\,,\,\forall 1\leqslant j\leqslant 2p+1\,,

there exist low-degree SoS proofs that,

\displaystyle\pE_{x}(x-y)^{2j+1}\leqslant o(n^{2j+1})\,,\,\forall 1\leqslant j\leqslant p

(C.7)

\displaystyle\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\leqslant o(n^{2j+1})\,,\,\forall 1\leqslant j\leqslant p\,.

(C.8)

Proof.

We begin the following observation using the pseudo-Jensen inequality (
Lemma

C.1
) and the given bound over even pseudo-moments,

0\leqslant\left(\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)^{2}=\left(\pE_{x}(x-y)^{2j+1}\right)^{2}\leqslant\pE_{x}(x-y)^{4j+2}\leqslant o(n^{4j+2})\,,\forall\,1\leqslant j\leqslant 2p-1\,.

We then apply a similar argument as in
Lemma

C.5
to
0\leqslant\left(\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)^{2}\leqslant o(n^{4j+2})
as follows,

\displaystyle o(n^{4j+2})-\left(\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)^{2}\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\implies\left(o(n^{2j+1})-\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)\left(o(n^{2j+1})+\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\implies\left(o(n^{2j+1})-\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)\left(o(n^{2j+1})+\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)+\left(o(n^{2j+1})-\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)^{2}\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\implies\left(o(n^{2j+1})-\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)\left\{\left(o(n^{2j+1})+\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)+\left(o(n^{2j+1})-\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)\right\}\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\implies\left(o(n^{2j+1})-\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)o(n^{2j+1})\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\implies\left(o(n^{2j+1})-\pE_{x}(y-x)^{2j+1}\right)\geqslant 0\,,

which yields the desired bound.
∎

Lemma C.11

(Pseudo-moment bounds via inductive degree reductions)
.

Given non-SoS program variables
y=an,c(n)=o(n)
for
a\geqslant 1
a constant, a pseudo-distribution with degree
\geqslant(4p+2)\cdot\mathsf{deg}(x)
and a low-degree SoS proof that,

\pE_{x}(x-y)^{2j}\leqslant c(n)^{2j}=o(n^{2j})\,,\,\forall 1\leqslant j\leqslant 2p+1

there are low-degree SoS proofs that,

\displaystyle y^{2p}-o(n^{2p})\leqslant\pE_{x}(x^{2p})\leqslant y^{2p}+o(n^{2p})\,,

(C.9)

\displaystyle y^{2p+1}-o(n^{2p+1})\leqslant\pE_{x}(x^{2p+1})\leqslant y^{2p+1}+o(n^{2p+1})\,.

(C.10)

Proof.

We prove this by appealing to strong induction. The base case corresponds to
Proposition

C.9
. The assumption will be guaranteed for the particular use case by the certificates provided in
Lemma

6.1
.

Base case

For
j=1
, the base case is immediately implied by

Proposition

C.9
which gives the following bounds,

\displaystyle\pE_{x}(x^{2})\leqslant y^{2}+2yc(n)+c^{2}(n)=y^{2}+o(n^{2})\,,

\displaystyle\pE_{x}(x^{2})\geqslant y^{2}-o(n^{2})\,,

\displaystyle\pE_{x}(x)\leqslant y+o(n)\,,

\displaystyle\pE_{x}(x)\geqslant y-o(n)\,.

Inductive hypothesis

Assume that,

y^{2j}-o(n^{2j})\leqslant\pE_{x}(x^{2j})\leqslant y^{2j}+o(n^{2j})\,,\forall\,j\in\{2,3,4,\dots,p-1\}\,,

and

y^{2j+1}-o(n^{2j+1})\leqslant\pE_{x}(x^{2j+1})\leqslant y^{2j+1}+o(n^{2j+1})\,,\forall\,j\in\{1,2,3,\dots,p-1\}\,.

Inductive step

We will demonstrate lower and upper bounds for the case that
j=p
for both the even (
2j=2p
) and odd (
2j+1=2p+1
) case. We will need the former (even) for the latter (odd), and therefore, we begin with the even case. By the definition of a pseudo-distribution and the certificates given in the theorem condition,

0\leqslant\pE_{x}(x-y)^{2p}\leqslant c(n)^{2p}=o(n^{2p})\,.

expanding the LHS yields,

\displaystyle\pE_{x}(x-y)^{2p}

\displaystyle=\pE\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{2p}(-1)^{2p-j}\binom{2p}{j}x^{j}y^{2p-j}\right)

\displaystyle=y^{2p}+\pE_{x}\left[x^{2p}\right]+\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{2p-1}(-1)^{2p-j}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}\pE_{x}[x^{j}]

\displaystyle=_{(-1)^{2p-j}=(-1)^{j}}y^{2p}+\pE_{x}\left[x^{2p}\right]+\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{2p-1}(-1)^{j}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}\pE_{x}[x^{j}]\,.

For the lower bound, we use the expansion of the LHS with the inductive hypothesis and positivity,

\displaystyle y^{2p}+\pE_{x}\left[x^{2p}\right]+\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{2p-1}(-1)^{j}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}\pE_{x}[x^{j}]\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\implies\pE_{x}\left[x^{2p}\right]\geqslant-y^{2p}-\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{2p-1}(-1)^{j}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}\pE_{x}[x^{j}]

\displaystyle\implies\pE_{x}[x^{2p}]\geqslant-y^{2p}-\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\{2,4,\dots,2p-2\}}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}\pE_{x}[x^{j}]+\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\{1,3,\dots,2p-1\}}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}\pE_{x}[x^{j}]

\displaystyle\implies\pE_{x}[x^{2p}]\geqslant_{\text{ind. hyp.}}-y^{2p}-\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\{2,4,\dots,2p-2\}}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}(y^{j}+o(n^{j}))+\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\{1,3,\dots,2p-1\}}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}(y^{j}-o(n^{j}))

\displaystyle\implies\pE_{x}[x^{2p}]\geqslant-y^{2p}-y^{2p}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{2p-1}(-1)^{j}\binom{2p}{j}-\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{2p-1}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}o(n^{j})

\displaystyle\implies\pE_{x}[x^{2p}]\geqslant-y^{2p}-y^{2p}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{2p}(-1)^{j}\binom{2p}{j}-2\right)-(1+a)^{p}o(n^{2p})

\displaystyle\implies\pE_{x}[x^{2p}]\geqslant_{(1-1)^{2p}=0}-y^{2p}+2y^{2p}-o(n^{2p1})=y^{2p}-o(n^{2p})\,.

For the upper bound, we combine the expansion of the LHS with the inductive hypothesis and the upper bound given by the condition,

\displaystyle\pE[x^{2p}]

\displaystyle\leqslant o(n^{2p})-y^{2p}-\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{2p-1}(-1)^{j}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}\pE_{x}\left[x^{j}\right]

\displaystyle=o(n^{2p})+\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1,3,\dots,2p-1}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}\pE_{x}[x^{j}]-\sumop\displaylimits_{j=2,4,\dots,2p-2}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}\pE_{x}[x^{j}]-y^{2p}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{\text{ind. hyp.}}o(n^{2p})+\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1,3,\dots,2p-1}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}(y^{j}+o(n^{j}))-\sumop\displaylimits_{j=2,4,\dots,2p-2}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}(y^{j}-o(n^{j}))-y^{2p}

\displaystyle=o(n^{2p})-y^{2p}-y^{2p}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{2p-1}(-1)^{j}\binom{2p}{j}+\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{2p-1}\binom{2p}{j}y^{2p-j}o(n^{j})

\displaystyle=o(n^{2p})-y^{2p}-y^{2p}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{2p}(-1)^{j}\binom{2p}{j}-2\right)+o(n^{2p})\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{2p-1}\binom{2p}{j}a^{2p-j}

\displaystyle\leqslant_{(1-1)^{2p}=0}o(n^{2p})-y^{2p}+2y^{2p}+(1+a)^{2p}o(n^{2p})\leqslant y^{2p}+o(n^{2p})\,.

Using the bounds,

-o(n^{2p+1})\leqslant\pE_{x}(x-y)^{2p+1}\leqslant o(n^{2p+1})\,,

guaranteed by
Proposition

C.10
and the bounds for the case that
j=2p
as proved in the above (even) induction step, a similar computation gives the desired result for the odd case.
∎

C.4
Strong convexity for
x\to x^{q}
via linear interpolation

Stated below is a low-degree SoS proof that the function
f:x\to x^{q}
for
q\in(1,2]
is strongly convex when
p
is dyadic as the Hölder conjugate. This proof is presented for the purpose of modularization and is “lifted” to a proof of
trace
strong convexity for the same function in the companion paper, and ends up being crucial in the rounding analysis.

Lemma C.12

(Strong convexity of
x\to x^{q}
)
.

Let the function
f:x\to x^{q}
with
\mathsf{dom}(f)\in\left[0,\frac{1}{(A(\varepsilon)n)}\right]
and
q=\frac{2^{k}}{2^{k}-1}
for some
k\geqslant 1
. Then,
\forall x\neq y\in\mathsf{dom}(f)
,

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\{y^{q}-x^{q}\geqslant q(y-x)x^{q-1}+C(y-x)^{2}\}\,,

where
C=\frac{(q-1)}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}
.

Proof.

We want to show (with
p/(p-1)=q\implies 1/(q-1)=(p-1)
) that,

y^{q}-x^{q}\geqslant q(y-x)x^{q-1}+\frac{(q-1)}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}(y-x)^{2},\,\forall q\in(1,2].

Let
x=a^{1/(q-1)}
and
y=b^{1/(q-1)}
. Then,

b^{p}-a^{p}\geqslant q(b^{p-1}-a^{p-1})a+\frac{(q-1)}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}(b^{p-1}-a^{p-1})^{2}\,.

Dividing both sides by
(b-a)
(assuming
b>a
WLOG):

\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{p}b^{i-1}a^{p-i}\geqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{p-1}b^{j-1}a^{p-1-j}\left(qa+\frac{(q-1)}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}(b^{p-1}-a^{p-1})\right)\,.

We now linearly interpolate between the summands in the LHS by breaking each into a weighted sum of two terms as,

\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{p}b^{i-1}a^{p-i}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{p-2}\left[\left(1-\frac{j}{p-1}\right)(b^{j}a^{p-1-j})+\frac{j+1}{p-1}(b^{j+1}a^{p-2-j})\right]\,,

where we use the fact that
p
is even and
p-1
is odd. Rewriting the sum in the LHS and RHS from zero indices yields the following final form,

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{p-2}\left[\left(1-\frac{i}{p-1}\right)(b^{i}a^{p-1-i})+\frac{i+1}{p-1}(b^{i+1}a^{p-2-i})\right]\geqslant\sumop\displaylimits_{j=0}^{p-2}b^{j}a^{p-2-j}\left(aq+\frac{(q-1)}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}(b^{p-1}-a^{p-1})\right)\,.

Re-arranging the terms immediately yields that,

\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{p-2}b^{j}a^{p-2-j}\left[\left(\frac{p-1-j}{p-1}\right)a+\left(\frac{j+1}{p-1}\right)b-\left(aq+\frac{(q-1)}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}(b^{p-1}-a^{p-1})\right)\right]\geqslant 0\,,

which, with a bit of algebra becomes equivalent to showing that,

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{p-2}b^{j}a^{p-2-j}\left[\left(1-\frac{j}{p-1}\right)a+\left(\frac{j+1}{p-1}\right)b-qa-\frac{(q-1)}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}(b^{p-1}-a^{p-1})\right]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{p-2}b^{j}a^{p-2-j}\left[a\left(1-j(q-1)\right)+b((j+1)(q-1))-aq-\frac{(q-1)}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}(b^{p-1}-a^{p-1})\right]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{p-2}b^{j}a^{p-2-j}\left[(q-1)(b(j+1)-aj)+a-aq-(q-1)\left(\frac{b^{p-1}-a^{p-1}}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{p-2}b^{j}a^{p-2-j}\left[-a(q-1)+(q-1)(j(b-a)+b)-(q-1)\left(\frac{b^{p-1}-a^{p-1}}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=(q-1)\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{p-2}b^{j}a^{p-2-j}\left[(b-a)+j(b-a)-\left(\frac{b^{p-1}-a^{p-1}}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=_{\text{divide by }(b-a)}(q-1)\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{p-2}b^{j}a^{p-2-j}\left[(j+1)-\frac{1}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{p-2}b^{i}a^{p-2-i}\right)\right]

Now, clearly
j+1\geqslant 1
. Furthermore, since,
x=a^{p-1}
and
b=y^{p-1}
with the given constraints that
a^{p-1}\leqslant\frac{1}{\delta n}
and
b^{p-1}\leqslant\frac{1}{\delta n}
,

\displaystyle a,b\leqslant_{\text{semi-algebraic degree reduction by~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:ideal-reduction-2p}}}\frac{1}{(\delta n)^{1/(p-1)}}\,.

Note that
p-1
is odd, so technically one needs to apply a slightly different version of
Lemma

C.7
for the case that
y=0
,
c>0
,
x>0
and there is an
odd
power. Factoring
x^{p-1}-c^{p-1}=(x-c)\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{p-2}x^{i}c^{p-2-i}
, separating the terms with even powers as being squared, and then taking
xc
as a factor for the odd terms (yielding sums of even squares for the factored terms) yields a SoS proof for odd-degree ideal reduction in the desired special case. The concluding step in the strong convexity argument is to use this semi-algebraic degree reduction and observe that,

\displaystyle b^{i}a^{p-2-i}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{(\delta n)^{i/(p-1)}}\frac{1}{(\delta n)^{(p-2-i)/(p-1)}}=\frac{1}{(\delta n)^{(p-2)/(p-1)}}=\frac{1}{(\delta n)^{1-1/(p-1)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\delta n)^{1-(q-1)}}=\frac{1}{(\delta n)^{2-q}}

\displaystyle\implies-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{p-2}b^{i}a^{p-2-i}\geqslant-(p-1)\frac{1}{(\delta n)^{2-q}}

\displaystyle\implies\frac{-1}{(p-1)(\delta n)^{q-2}}\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{p-2}b^{i}a^{p-2-i}\geqslant\frac{1}{(p-1)}\frac{1}{(\delta n)^{q-2}}\frac{-(p-1)}{(\delta n)^{2-q}}=1\,.

The above set of inequalities in conjunction with the facts that
a,b\geqslant 0
and
q>1
finishes the proof.
∎

C.5
AM-GM-type inequality

We state and prove a version of the AM-GM inequality and briefly comment on how to straight-forwardly extend it to a non-commutative version for symmetric matrices.

Lemma C.13

(Scalar AM-GM inequality)
.

Given any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{even}}
and real-valued variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
, the following holds,

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{4k}\left\{\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}\leqslant\frac{1}{k}\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}x^{k}_{i}\right\}\,.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
k
.

Base case
k=2

For the case that
k=2
, the inequality follows by the non-negativity of
\frac{1}{4}(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}
in conjunction with
Lemma

C.4
.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}(x_{1}+x_{2})^{2}-x_{1}x_{2}=\frac{1}{4}(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2})+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}x_{2}-x_{1}x_{2}=\frac{1}{4}(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2})-\frac{1}{2}x_{1}x_{2}=\frac{1}{4}(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}\geqslant 0\,.

Furthermore, invoking
Lemma

C.4
with
m=2
and
t=2
gives,

\frac{1}{4}(x_{1}+x_{2})^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{4}2^{1}(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2})=\frac{1}{2}(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2})\,.

Combing the above two inequalities yields the desired result.

Inductive hypothesis

Assume the inequality holds for even powers up to
k=2^{m}
,

x_{1}\cdots x_{k}\leqslant\frac{1}{k}(x_{1}^{k}+\dots+x^{k}_{k})\,.

Inductive step

Set
k^{\prime}=2^{m+1}=2k
. Now,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{2k}x^{2k}_{i}=\frac{\frac{\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}x^{2k}_{i}}{k}+\frac{\sumop\displaylimits_{j=k+1}^{2k}x^{2k}_{j}}{k}}{2}

\displaystyle\geqslant_{\text{by~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:l1-to-lt}}}\frac{\frac{\left(x^{k}_{1}+\dots+x^{k}_{k}\right)^{2}}{k^{2}}+\frac{\left(x_{k+1}^{k}+\dots+x_{2k}^{k}\right)^{2}}{k^{2}}}{2}

\displaystyle\geqslant_{\text{by inductive step}}\frac{(x_{1}\cdots x_{k})^{2}+(x_{k+1}\cdots x_{2k})^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\geqslant_{\text{by~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:l1-to-lt} with $a_{1}=x_{1}\cdots x_{k}$, $a_{2}=x_{k+1}\cdots x_{2k}$}}\left(\frac{x_{1}\cdots x_{k}+x_{k+1}\cdots x_{2k}}{2}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\left(x_{1}\cdots x_{k}+x_{k+1}\cdots x_{2k}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geqslant_{\text{by $\frac{1}{4}(a+b)^{2}\geqslant ab$}}(x_{1}\cdots x_{k})(x_{k+1}\cdots x_{2k})=x_{1}\cdots x_{2k}\,.

In the event that the even number is not a power of
2
, we reduce to the case that it is by adding “dummy” variables that have a specific value. Let
k=2m
and let
k^{\prime}
be the smallest number that is a power of
2
such that
k^{\prime}>k
. Then, set
x^{k}_{k+1}=\dots=x^{k}_{k^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{k}\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}{x^{k}_{i}}=\alpha
and
y_{i}=(x_{i}^{1/k^{\prime}})^{k}
as a polynomial of radicals of
x_{i}
. By straightforward algebraic manipulation,

\displaystyle\alpha^{k^{\prime}}=\left(\frac{1}{k}\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}x^{k}_{i}\right)^{k^{\prime}}=\left(\frac{\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}x^{k}_{i}}{k^{\prime}}\right)^{k^{\prime}}=\left(\frac{\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}y_{i}^{k^{\prime}}}{k^{\prime}}\right)^{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\geqslant_{\text{by power-of-2 version}}(y_{1}\cdots y_{k^{\prime}})^{k^{\prime}}=y^{k^{\prime}}_{1}\cdots y^{k^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left(x^{k}_{1}\cdots x^{k}_{k}\right)\left(x^{k}_{k+1}\cdots x^{k}_{k^{\prime}}\right)=\left(x^{k}_{1}\cdots x^{k}_{k}\right)\alpha^{k^{\prime}-k}

\displaystyle\implies\alpha^{k}\geqslant\left(x^{k}_{1}\cdots x^{k}_{k}\right)\implies_{\text{by~\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:ideal-reduction-2p} with $y=0$ and $c=\varepsilon$}}\,\alpha\geqslant x_{1}\cdots x_{k}+\varepsilon\,.

∎

Appendix D
Sum-of-Squares proofs for Non-Commutative Analytic Inequalities

D.1
Matrix Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

We begin by stating a straightforward proof of a matrix Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, which works on symmetric matrices where the entries are low-degree polynomials in the SoS program variables.

Lemma D.1

(Matrix Cauchy-Schwarz)
.

Given two symmetric matrices
A,B\in M_{n}(p(\sigma))
where
p\in\mathbb{R}_{\leqslant d}[\sigma_{1},\dots\sigma_{n}]
, the following holds:

\left\{A=A^{\mathsf{T}},B=B^{\mathsf{T}}\right\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\left\{\langle A,B\rangle^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\left\lVert B\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\right\}\,.

Proof.

The proof follows by simply “flattening” the matrices into a
n^{2}
-dimensional vector, and applying the SoS proof for the standard Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

\displaystyle\left\lVert A\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\left\lVert B\right\rVert^{2}_{F}-\langle A,B\rangle^{2}=\Tr[A^{\mathsf{T}}A]\Tr[B^{\mathsf{T}}B]-\left(\Tr[A^{\mathsf{T}}B]\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{n}A^{2}_{i_{1},j_{1}}\right)\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{n^{2}}B^{2}_{i_{2},j_{2}}\right)-\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{n}\langle\mathsf{col}_{j}(A),\mathsf{col}_{j}(B)\rangle\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{1},j_{1}=1}^{n}A^{2}_{i_{1},j_{1}}\right)\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i_{2},j_{2}=1}^{n^{2}}B^{2}_{i_{2},j_{2}}\right)-\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i,j=1}^{n}A_{ij}B_{ij}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{(i_{1},j_{1})\preceq(i_{2},j_{2})}A_{i_{1},j_{1}}B_{i_{2},j_{2}}-A_{i_{2},j_{2}}B_{i_{1},j_{1}}\right)^{2}\geqslant 0\,.

∎

D.2
The
2\text{-}\infty\text{-}2
matrix Hölder’s inequality

We state low-degree SoS proofs for Matrix versions of Hölder inequalities with
(2,\infty,2)
and
(1,\infty)
conjugate pairs. The general form of Hölder’s inequality for matrices is typically proved using the weak majorization of singular values, however, these proofs are not low-degree SoS proofs.

Lemma D.2

((
2,\infty,2
)-Matrix Hölder’s Inequality)
.

Given two symmetric matrices
A,B\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, the following holds,

\{0\preceq B,B=\lambda\Id-CC^{\mathsf{T}}\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{4\mathsf{deg}(x^{\mathsf{T}}Bx)}\Tr[ABA^{\mathsf{T}}]\leqslant\lambda\left\lVert A\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\,.

Proof.

We begin by giving a
\mathsf{deg}(x^{\mathsf{T}}Bx)
SoS proof that,

\Tr[ABA^{\mathsf{T}}]\leqslant\Tr[A(\lambda\Id)A^{\mathsf{T}}]=\lambda\Tr[AA^{\mathsf{T}}]=\lambda\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

Observe the fact that
B=\lambda\Id-CC^{\mathsf{T}}
by the system of axioms and, therefore, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle x^{\mathsf{T}}(A(\lambda\Id)A^{\mathsf{T}}-ABA^{\mathsf{T}})x

\displaystyle=\lambda x^{\mathsf{T}}(AA^{\mathsf{T}})\Id x-(A^{\mathsf{T}}x)^{\mathsf{T}}B(A^{\mathsf{T}}x)

\displaystyle=\lambda y^{\mathsf{T}}y-y^{\mathsf{T}}By=\lambda\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}_{2}-y^{\mathsf{T}}By

\displaystyle=\lambda\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}_{2}-(\lambda\left\lVert y\right\rVert^{2}_{2}-y^{\mathsf{T}}CC^{\mathsf{T}}y)

\displaystyle=\left\lVert C^{\mathsf{T}}y\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\geqslant 0\,,

where we used the fact that
0\leqslant\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}=\lambda\Id-CC^{\mathsf{T}}
by our axioms. We now show that
\Tr[ABA^{\mathsf{T}}]\leqslant\lambda\Tr[AA^{\mathsf{T}}]=\lambda\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
permits a low-degree SoS proof.

\displaystyle\lambda\Tr[AA^{\mathsf{T}}]-\Tr[ABA^{\mathsf{T}}]

\displaystyle=\lambda\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}(AA^{\mathsf{T}})e_{i}\right)-\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}(ABA^{\mathsf{T}})e_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\left(A(\lambda\Id)A^{\mathsf{T}}-ABA^{\mathsf{T}}\right)e_{i}\right)\geqslant 0\,.

∎

Lemma D.3

(Asymmetric (
2,\infty,2
)-Matrix Hölder’s Inequality)
.

Given three real matrices
A,B,X
, the following holds:

\{XX^{T}=\lambda^{2}\Id-CC^{\mathsf{T}}\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{6}\{(\Tr[A^{\mathsf{T}}XB])^{2}\leqslant\lambda^{2}\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\}\,.

Proof.

By one step of matrix Cauchy-Schwarz,

(\Tr[A^{\mathsf{T}}XB])^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\left\lVert BX\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

Now,

\left\lVert BX\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\Tr BXX^{T}B^{T}=\lambda^{2}\Tr BB^{T}-\Tr BCC^{T}B^{T}\leqslant\lambda^{2}\Tr BB^{T}=\lambda_{2}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

∎

Lemma D.4

((
2,\infty,2,\infty
)-Matrix Hölder’s Inequality)
.

Given three real matrices
A,B,X
, the following holds:

\{XX^{T}=\lambda^{2}\Id-CC^{\mathsf{T}}\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{8}\left\{(\Tr[AXBX])^{2}\leqslant\lambda^{4}\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\right\}\,.

Consequently,

\{XX^{T}=\lambda^{2}\Id-CC^{\mathsf{T}}\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{12}\left\{\pm\Tr[AXBX]\leqslant\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}(\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2})\right\}\,.

Proof.

By one step of matrix Cauchy-Schwarz,

(\Tr[AXBX])^{2}\leqslant\left\lVert AX\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\left\lVert BX\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\,.

Now,

\left\lVert BX\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\Tr BXX^{T}B^{T}=\lambda^{2}\Tr BB^{T}-\Tr BCC^{T}B^{T}\leqslant\lambda^{2}\Tr BB^{T}=\lambda_{2}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}

and similarly for
\left\lVert AX\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
.

For the consequence, we apply
Proposition

C.8
along with the SoS proof that

\lambda^{4}\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\frac{\lambda^{4}}{4}(\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2})+\frac{\lambda^{4}}{2}\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leqslant\frac{\lambda^{4}}{4}(\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}+\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2})^{2}\,,

where we applied AM-GM to
\frac{\lambda^{4}}{2}\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
and then factored the result.
∎

D.3
The
1\text{-}\infty
matrix Hölder’s inequality

Lemma D.5

((
1,\infty
)-Matrix Hölder’s Inequality)
.

Given two symmetric matrices
A,B\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, the following holds,

\{A=\lambda\Id-CC^{\mathsf{T}},0\preceq B=DD^{\mathsf{T}}\}\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}_{4}\langle A,B\rangle\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{1}\,,

when
B
is either PSD (as stated in the axioms above)
or
permits a rank-decomposing SoS proof (as defined in
Definition

5.12
) that
\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant C
.

Proof.

The proof for this is similar to the proof of
Lemma

D.2
. Note that,

\langle A,B\rangle=\Tr[A^{\mathsf{T}}B]\leqslant\Tr[\lambda\Id B]=\lambda\Tr[B]\,.

A SoS proof for this is as follows,

\displaystyle\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{{}_{\mathsf{op}}}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{1}-\langle A,B\rangle

\displaystyle=\lambda\Tr[B]-\Tr[A^{\mathsf{T}}B]=\Tr[(\lambda\Id B-A^{\mathsf{T}}B)]=\Tr[(\lambda\Id-A^{T})B]

\displaystyle=\Tr[(\lambda\Id-(\lambda\Id-CC^{\mathsf{T}})^{\mathsf{T}})B]=\Tr[(CC^{\mathsf{T}})^{\mathsf{T}}B]

\displaystyle=\Tr[CC^{\mathsf{T}}DD^{\mathsf{T}}]=\Tr[C(D^{\mathsf{T}}C)^{\mathsf{T}}D^{\mathsf{T}}]=\Tr[D^{\mathsf{T}}C(D^{\mathsf{T}}C)^{\mathsf{T}}]

\displaystyle=\Tr[((D^{\mathsf{T}}C)^{\mathsf{T}})^{\mathsf{T}}(D^{\mathsf{T}}C)^{\mathsf{T}}]=\left\lVert(D^{\mathsf{T}}C)^{\mathsf{T}}\right\rVert^{2}_{F}\geqslant 0\,.

In the rank-decomposing setting, we use a generalization of
Lemma

D.2
to the three-matrix case, along with the definition of
\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant C
having a rank-decomposing proof.
∎

D.4
Matrix Hölder’s inequality for dyadic norms

The following is the Schatten norm version of Hölder’s inequality reformulated to be a polynomial inequality.
We recover the usual form by upper-bounding the inner product of
A^{p-1}
with
B
by the product of the
p
-norm of
B
with the
q
-norm of
A^{p-1}
, which is equal to
(\tr A^{p})^{(p-1)/p}
.

Lemma D.6
.

Let
A
and
B
be symmetric matrix variables.
Let
p=2^{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\left\{(\tr A^{p-1}B)^{p}\leqslant(\tr A^{p})^{p-1}(\tr B^{p})\right\}\,.

Proof.

We show this inductively.
The base case where
p=1
is trivial.
The inductive hypothesis tells us that for
X
and
Y
symmetric matrix variables,

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\left\{(\tr X^{p/2-1}Y)^{p/2}\leqslant(\tr X^{p/2})^{p/2-1}(\tr Y^{p/2})\right\}.

Going back to the lemma statement, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
\left\langle A^{p/2},A^{p/2-1}B\right\rangle\leqslant\left\lVert A^{p/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\left\lVert A^{p/2-1}B\right\rVert_{F}^{2}
, which we can write by trace cyclicity as

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\left\{(\tr A^{p-1}B)^{p}\leqslant(\tr A^{p})^{p/2}(\tr A^{p-2}B^{2})^{p/2}\right\}.

Now we can invoke the inductive hypothesis with
X:=A^{2}
and
Y:=B^{2}
to find

\mathrel{|}\joinrel\mkern-0.5mu\mathrel{-}\left\{(\tr A^{p-2}B^{2})^{p/2}\leqslant(\tr A^{p})^{p/2-1}(\tr B^{p})\right\}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix E
Semi-Definite Encodings: High-Entropy Steps and Cumulants

E.1
Encoding high-entropy step constraints into the SDP

We briefly describe how to encode the maximum-entropy constraints into the semi-definite program by introducing the required auxiliary variables and using the definition of hierarchical pseudo-distributions.

Radicals of the polynomial grammar

We “radicalize” various elements of the polynomial grammar in many proofs above. To see why this is reasonable, note that, by introducing families of dummy variables
\mathsf{dum}_{i}\succeq 0
one may split the (non-negative) fractional power
q=\frac{a}{b}
into integer powers, which is a standard technique
[
BBH+12
,
LS17
]
.

\displaystyle\{\mathsf{dum}_{i}\succeq 0\}\,,

\displaystyle\left\{\mathsf{dum}_{i}^{b}=\fE_{i}\left[v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\right]\right\}\,,
